JUDGMENT

Introduction

1. This is my Judgment in two actions, both of which arise from a fraud against a group of
         companies known as the “NMC Group”, the parent company of which was NMC Health
         plc (“NMC plc”), an English company.

2. In one action, which has come to be referred to as the “JA Action”, the Claimants (the
         “JA Claimants”) are (a) two companies in the NMC Group, being (i) NMC Healthcare
         Limited (“NMCH”) and (ii) NMC Holding Limited (“Holding”), and (b) their Joint
         Administrators, being (iii) Mr Richard Dixon Fleming and (iv) Mr Benjamin Thom Cairns
         (the “JAs”), both of Alvarez & Marsal Europe LLP (“A&M Europe”). (NMCH and Holding
         act by the JAs.) The JA Claimants bring claims in their own right. NMCH also brings claims
         as assignee of the claims of the so-called “ADGM Companies”, being 34 companies
         which were in the NMC Group, and of their former Joint Administrators.

3. At the start of the trial, there were three Defendants in the JA Action: two former
         executives in the NMC Group, (i) Dr Bavaguthu Raghuram Shetty (“Dr Shetty”) and (ii) Mr
         Prasanth Manghat (“Mr Manghat”), and (iii) the Bank of Baroda (“Baroda”), an Indian
         bank. Towards the end of the trial, the JA Claimants and Baroda reached a settlement,
         and the JA Claimants discontinued their claims against it.

4. In response to the claims, Dr Shetty makes claims against the other Defendants, and
         Baroda similarly makes claims against Dr Shetty and Mr Manghat (and, before the
         settlement, brought various counterclaims against the JA Claimants). When the claims
         against Baroda were discontinued, I told the parties that I would not decide these matters
         in this Judgment, and that I would hear further argument about them after I had delivered
         it and in light of my decisions in it.

5. The claims in the JA Action fall into two categories: (i) claims brought under the United
         Arab Emirates (“UAE”) Federal Law No. 5 of 1985 Concerning the Issuance of the Civil
         Transactions Law (the “Civil Code”) and other UAE statutory provisions, to which I shall
         refer as the “Civil Claims”; and (ii) the claims under the Abu Dhabi Global Market
         (“ADGM”) Insolvency Regulations 2022 (“IR 2022”), to which I shall refer as the
         “Insolvency Claims”. The Civil Claims are brought by NMCH (in its own capacity and as
         assignee of the ADGM Companies’ Civil Claims) and by Holding. The Insolvency Claims
         are brought by the JAs and NMCH (as assignee of the ADGM Companies’ former Joint
         Administrators’ Insolvency Claims). All the Claims are denied.

6. The second action (the “ADCB Action”) is brought against Mr Manghat by Abu Dhabi
         Commercial Bank PJSC (“ADCB”), a bank incorporated in the UAE. It makes claims under
         the Civil Code, particularly Articles 282 and 285, and under Article 84 of the UAE Federal

COURT OF FIRST INSTANCE JUDGMENT
ADGMCFI-2022-111 – ABU DHABI COMMERCIAL BANK PJSC V PRASANTH MANGHAT

LISTED IN SCHEDULE 1 TO THE ADMINISTRATION APPLICATION
ADGMCFI-2022-299 – NMC HEALTHCARE LIMITED (IN ADMINISTRATION) (SUBJECT TO A DEED OF COMPANY ARRANGEMENT) AND OTHERS V BAVAGUTHU RAGHURAM
SHETTY AND OTHERS

                                                                                                                                                                      16

                                                                                       05 November 2026 04:43 PM

         Law No. 2 of 2015 Concerning Commercial Companies (as amended by UAE Federal-
         Decree Law No. 26 of 2020) (the “CCL 2015”). Mr Manghat denies the claims.

7. It is not in dispute that the NMC Group was a victim of massive and sophisticated fraud
         between 2 April 2012 and 9 April 2020 (to which I shall refer as the “Relevant Period”). In
         essence, it had two main elements: (i) the misappropriation of assets of the NMC Group,
         and (ii) the misrepresentation of its finances in its financial statements by way of
         overstating its net assets and its true turnover. NMC plc was put into administration by
         order of the English High Court on 9 April 2020, and NMCH, Holding and the ADGM
         Companies were put into administration by order of this Court on 27 September 2020.
         The core issues that I have to decide are whether Dr Shetty and Mr Manghat were parties
         to the fraud. Dr Shetty contends that he knew nothing of it, and that he was the victim of
         a fraud carried out by Mr Manghat and other senior managers of the NMC Group. Mr
         Manghat disputes his role in the fraud, and he contends that Dr Shetty perpetrated it and
         that it was for Dr Shetty’s benefit.

8. In the ADCB Action, in broad summary ADCB alleges inter alia that Mr Manghat knowingly
         participated in the fraud, particularly in an aspect of it that it calls the “Loan Recycling
         Scheme”, whereby borrowings taken out by the NMC Group to cover the
         misappropriations were not disclosed in the Group’s financial statements; and that Mr
         Manghat gave ADCB false assurances about the NMC Group’s financial position.

9. At the Appendix to this Judgment is a glossary of the abbreviated terms that I use in it.

The proceedings

10. The ADCB Action was brought on 10 May 2022. The background to it is that, on 2
         December 2020, ADCB had brought proceedings in England against Dr Shetty, Mr
         Manghat and four other shareholders and senior executives of the NMC Group. Dr Shetty,
         Mr Manghat and some other defendants successfully challenged the jurisdiction of the
         English Court, and the English proceedings were stayed against those defendants in April
         2022. Essentially, ADCB pursues in this Court against Mr Manghat alone the complaints
         that it had brought in the English proceedings.

11. The JA Action was brought on 15 December 2022.

12. There have been many protracted interlocutory applications in these proceedings, but I
         need mention only some of them.

Concurrent trials

13. First, by an application of 14 September 2023 (the “Manghat Directions Application”),
         Mr Manghat sought orders that the claims against him in the two proceedings be “subject
         to co-ordinated case management”, and that there be “a concurrent trial” of them. By a
         judgment of 29 December 2023 ([2023] ADGMCFI 0025), I granted the Manghat Directions

SHETTY AND OTHERS

                                                                                                                                                                      17

                                                                                       05 November 2026 04:43 PM

         Application, and the Actions have been conducted accordingly, both before and at the
         trial. In particular, I directed that the evidence in each Action should stand as evidence
         in them both.

The preliminary issues

14. Secondly, as I have said, the JA Claimants bring claims under the IR 2022. More
         specifically, they made claims against Dr Shetty, Mr Manghat and Baroda for fraudulent
         trading under section 251 of the IR 2022, and against Dr Shetty and Mr Manghat for
         wrongful trading under section 252 of the IR 2022. The allegations pursued against Dr
         Shetty and Mr Manghat relate to conduct between 2012 and 2020 and the allegations
         pursued against Baroda related to conduct between 2013 and 2020. As I shall explain,
         NMCH, Holding and the ADGM Companies were registered in the ADGM only in
         September 2020, shortly before they were put into administration, and liability for
         fraudulent and wrongful trading was first introduced into ADGM law in 2015 by the ADGM
         Insolvency Regulations 2015 (“IR 2015”), the predecessor of the IR 2022.

15. Dr Shetty and Baroda advanced what came to be termed “Retrospectivity Defences” to
         these Insolvency Claims: that sections 251 and 252 of IR 2022 do not apply to anything
         that they were alleged to have done (i) before the JA Claimants were incorporated in the
         ADGM, or (ii) before the IR 2015 were enacted and came into force. They also contended
         that they had insufficient connection between the Insolvency Claims and the ADGM for
         this Court to entertain them.

16. These arguments were determined by way of preliminary issues. By a judgment of 8 July
         2024 ([2024] ADGMCFI 0007), I rejected the Retrospectivity Defences, determining the
         issues as follows:

              “a. Issue 1: Can an order be made under sections 251 and 253 of the [IR 2022] in
              respect of the fraudulent carrying on of the business of a company prior to the
              time at which that company was continued in the ADGM? Yes.

              b. Issue 2: Can an order be made under section 252 of the [IR 2022] in respect of
              the wrongful carrying on of the business of a company prior to the time at which
              that company was continued in the ADGM? Yes.

              c. Issue 3: Can a claim be brought under section 251 and/or section 252 in
              respect of the fraudulent and/or wrongful carrying on of business before the date
              when sections 251 and section 252 first came into effect in the ADGM (...
              pursuant to the [IR 2015], which was the predecessor of the [IR 2022])? Yes.

              d. Issue 4: Can a claim successfully be brought under section 251 and/or section
              252 absent a sufficient connection between the defendant and the ADGM? Yes;
              and

              e. If not, assuming (for present purposes only) the facts pleaded by the Claimants,
              including in their proposed Re-Re-Amended Particulars of Claim, to be true,
              would there be a sufficient connection between the relevant Defendant and the
              ADGM? This does not arise, but if it did, I would answer, Yes”.

17. Dr Shetty and Baroda appealed against my determinations, but their appeal was
         dismissed by the Court of Appeal on 30 December 2024 ([2024] ADGMCA 0001).

Submissions on UAE law

18. Thirdly, the proceedings give rise to issues of UAE law. In the JA Action, the Civil Claims
         are governed by UAE law, but the Insolvency Claims are governed by ADGM law. In the
         ADCB Action, the claims are governed by UAE law.

19. In a judgment of 10 March 2025 ([2025] ADGMCFI 0004), I refused applications by ADCB
         and the JA Claimants to adduce evidence of UAE law. Dr Shetty and Baroda had argued
         that instead the Court should deal with the UAE law issues by way of legal submissions
         under rule 117 of the ADGM Court Procedure Rules 2016 (the “CPR”), and I accepted their
         argument.

20. This does not change the task of the ADGM Court when determining questions of UAE
         law: it is to determine how they would be interpreted and applied by the onshore Courts
         of the UAE: see NMC Healthcare Limited (in administration) and Associated Companies
         v Dubai Islamic Bank PJSC & Others [2023] ADGMCFI 0017 at para. 24.

21. The JA Claimants and ADCB presented joint submissions on UAE law. Mr Manghat and
         Baroda presented submissions in response, and the JA Claimants and ADCB made
         further submissions in reply. Dr Shetty did not present submissions on UAE law, but, as I
         shall explain, he adopted those of Baroda on some points.

ADCB’s application to join Dr Shetty

22. Fourthly, by an application dated 3 May 2024, ADCB applied for permission to join Dr
         Shetty as a defendant in the ADCB Action. By a judgment dated 30 July 2024, [2024]
         ADGMCFI 0010, and an Order dated 30 July 2024, I refused the application, saying (at
         paras. 86 and 87 of my judgment): “The problems in accommodating the joinder of Dr
         Shetty in the ADCB claim within the timetable to the March 2026 trial are a result of ADCB
         allowing the trial date and the procedural timetable to be directed without indicating that
         it would, or might, seek to join Dr Shetty as a defendant. While that is the context in which
         I decide the Joinder Application it is not in itself a reason for refusing it. I do not lightly
         refuse the Joinder Application: there is an obvious attraction in having these proceedings
         resolve not only issues between ADCB and Mr Manghat but also the complaints of both
         ADCB and Mr Manghat against Dr Shetty. … In the end, I have concluded (i) that it would
         be unfair to Dr Shetty to require him to add the demands of these proceedings to the
         already demanding programme to prepare for trial in March 2026, and (ii) that to grant the

The Bin Buttis settlement agreement

24. The last pre-trial direction that I should mention concerns the position about documents
         relating to a settlement agreement reached by the JAs in relation to Mr Khaleefa Butti
         Omair Yousef Almuhairi (“KBBAM”) and HE Saeed Mohamed Butti Mohamed Khalfan Al
         Qebaisi (“SAQ”), KBBAM’s uncle, who together were referred to as the “Bin Buttis”. On
         27 July 2021, KBBAM and the so-called “Implicated Litigants” entered into restructuring
         procedures in the Abu Dhabi Bankruptcy Court (the “ADBC”) pursuant to UAE Federal
         Decree-Law No. 9 of 2016 on Bankruptcy. The Implicated Litigants included SAQ in his
         personal capacity and in his capacity as the ultimate sole owner of Centurion
         Investments (“Centurion”), a sole proprietorship based in Abu Dhabi, and Infinite
         Investments LLC (“Infinite”), a company registered in Abu Dhabi, in which the Bin Buttis
         each held half the shares.

25. The NMC Group, or more specifically NMC plc, made a claim in the bankruptcy, but, as I
         explain in more detail at para. 170 below, by February 2022 it had reached a settlement
         agreement covering any claims against KBBAM, SAQ and others; and the agreement was
         ratified by the ADBC. The JA Claimants had in their control documents relating to the
         proceedings in the bankruptcy that were certainly relevant to issues in the JA Action, but
         they were prohibited by the ADBC from making them available for inspection in these
         proceedings.

26. On 24 December 2025, Judge Ali Bakhit Al Katheri issued a decision in the ADBC that
         allowed a partial and limited relaxation of the prohibitions. He permitted the Defendants
         in the JA Action limited access to the settlement agreement and other documents, solely
         for the purposes of the JA Action and in order to defend them, the permission expressly
         being given on the basis that this Court would treat them confidentially and issue an order
         accordingly. The decision stipulated restrictions on the permitted access, including that
         the number of persons so granted access was limited to nine persons, being up to three
         persons for each Defendant, the so-called “Access Individuals”. On 11 February 2026, I

The pleadings

27. As I said in my judgment in Union Properties PJSC & Anor v Trinkler & Partners Ltd & Others
         [2026] ADGMCFI 0010, “it has long been established that (in the words of Thesiger LJ in
         Davy v Garrett (1878) 7 Ch D 473) fraud and comparable allegations ‘must be distinctly
         alleged and as distinctly proved’”. I cited this summary of the proper approach from the
         judgment of Cockerill J in King v Stiefel [2021] EWHC 1045 (Comm) at para. 25:

               “i) The Court should bear in mind that cogent evidence is required to justify a
              finding of fraud or other discreditable conduct, reflecting the court's conventional
              perception that it is generally not likely that people will engage in such conduct.

              ii) Pleadings of fraud should be subjected to close scrutiny and it is not possible
              to infer dishonesty from facts that are equally consistent with honesty.

              iii) However, in view of the common feature of fraud claims that the Defendant
              will, if the underlying allegation is true, have tried to shroud his conduct in
              secrecy, the Court should adopt a ‘generous’ approach to pleadings”.

28. In his speech in Three Rivers District Council v Bank of England [2001] UKHL 16 (at paras.
         185 and 186), Lord Millett distinguished two requirements where fraud is alleged: that an
         allegation of this kind be unequivocally pleaded and the principle that it be properly
         particularised.

29. I fully recognise those principles and their importance. That said, it is necessary to keep
         a sense of proportion: Lord Millett’s first requirement is not an excuse for digging for
         ambiguity in the literal wording of a plea where the pleader’s meaning is clear, nor is his
         second requirement a justification for demanding unnecessary particularisation. As was
         said by Saville LJ in British Airways Pension Trustees Ltd v Sir Robert McAlpine & Sons
         Ltd (1995) 11 Const LJ 365 at p. 367 (in an observation cited by Lord Hope in the Three
         Rivers case at para. 49): “The basic purpose of pleadings is to enable the opposing party
         to know what case is being made in sufficient detail to enable that party properly to
         prepare to answer it. To my mind it seems that in recent years there has been a tendency
         to forget this basic purpose and to seek particularisation even when it is not really
         required. This is not only costly in itself, but is calculated to lead to delay and to
         interlocutory battles in which the parties and the court pore over endless pages of
         pleadings to see whether or not some particular point has or has not been raised or
         answered, when in truth each party knows perfectly well what case is made by the other
         and is able properly to prepare to deal with it”. See too the judgment of Lord Woolf MR
         in McPhilemy v Times Newspapers Limited and Others [1999] EMLR 751.

30. The pleadings in both these Actions are long, necessarily but rather inconveniently so.
         Nevertheless, the Defendants made criticisms (both in their own pleadings and in their
         submissions) that the allegations against them are not adequately pleaded. I shall
         consider the more significant complaints of this kind in the course of this Judgment, but
         I do not engage with all of them where I consider the Claimants’ pleading is fully
         adequate.

31. Moreover, as Cockerill J said, it is characteristic of fraud cases of this kind that the victim
         is not always able to spell out in full detail how it was done and the exact role of those
         involved in it. Therefore, for example, I am not impressed by Mr Manghat’s plea in his
         defence in the JA Action that he “will rely on the paucity of particulars specifically alleging
         his involvement in support of his case that he was not involved in the fraud”, not least
         because, as I conclude, he was involved in hiding or destroying relevant documentation
         when the fraud started to come to light: see para. 435 below.

The trial

32. The trial took place between 23 March 2026 and 3 July 2026. I had directed that it be
         conducted mainly in person in the ADGM, but circumstances in the Gulf region after
         about 28 February 2026 prevented that. It was therefore conducted remotely.

33. The representation was as follows:

         c. Dr Shetty was represented by Mr Kajetan Wandowicz.

         d. Mr Manghat was represented by Mr Patrick Hennessey.

35. Mr Manghat’s difficulties were aggravated because (i) he had limited access to his

36. The Court did what it could to mitigate these and other difficulties facing Mr Manghat. For

Mr Manghat’s post-hearing submissions application

38. Understandably in the circumstances, Mr Manghat did not make opening submissions in
         writing. Further, he did not present written closing submissions as directed by the Court
         during the trial, despite Mr Hennessey indicating from time to time that he was about to
         serve them. In contrast, the written closing submissions of the other parties (including
         Baroda) ran in total to well over 2,000 pages.

39. More than two months after the hearing was concluded, on 17 September 2026, the Court
         received an email from Mr Hennessey that Mr Manghat still planned to file written closing
         submissions. I responded that he would need to apply for permission to make further
         submissions after the hearing.

40. On 5 October 2026, more than three months after the hearing had finished, Mr Manghat
         applied to the Court for permission to file written closing submissions. His witness
         statement in support of the application attributed responsibility for the delay to Mr
         Hennessey, who was said to have been ill. Closing submissions should have been filed
         before the oral closings, and nothing explains or excuses Mr Manghat’s failure to give the
         Court and the other parties any earlier indication that he intended to apply to file
         submissions after the end of the hearing.

41. Mr Manghat attached to his witness statement a document by way of submissions (the

42. To put it mildly, this was unsatisfactory. Mr Manghat’s conduct here is in stark contrast
         to the consideration that other parties showed to him before and throughout the trial.
         Further, by the time that Mr Manghat made his application, preparation of the Judgment
         was pretty well advanced and the considerable inconvenience and the delay that an
         application of this kind causes need not be explained. Unsurprisingly given the nature of
         his contentions, the Post-Hearing Submissions criticise other parties and their
         witnesses, in particular Dr Shetty. It would have been particularly unfair to Dr Shetty to
         take account of Mr Manghat’s criticisms without giving him a chance to reply. That would,
         of course, have caused further delay and expense. Mr Manghat could not have
         complained if his application were refused, and I altogether disregarded the Post-Hearing

43. However, the practical answer to the difficulties created by Mr Manghat seems to me to

         them.

44. On the other hand, I have considered the points made in Mr Manghat’s Post-Hearing

         purport to engage with the substance of the case against Mr Manghat, they are
         characterised by broad and largely inconsequential generalisations. In my judgment,
         their main impact is to emphasise that Mr Manghat has no answer to the specific

47. More generally, much of the Post-Hearing Submissions emphasise the difficulties that Mr
         Manghat has had dealing with the demands of the litigation, such as those in respect of
         amended pleadings and disclosure, before and during the trial. As it was put, “Mr
         Manghat’s unusual and demanding position cannot be ignored or downplayed”. I have
         already acknowledged this, but it does not, in itself, answer the allegations of ADCB or
         the JA Claimants. Other considerations must also be kept in mind.

48. First, while Mr Manghat complains that he is “required, in one trial, to answer two
         separate, but overlapping and interconnected claims; brought by two, theoretically
         independent, but openly collaborate Claimants”, it must not be forgotten that it was Mr
         Manghat who applied for concurrent trials.

49. Secondly, Mr Manghat complains that: “To add insult to injury, whether by accident or
         design, Mr Manghat has been further disadvantaged because claims have not been
         advanced against other alleged participants in and/or beneficiaries of the fraud”. Of
         course, it was for ADCB and the JA Claimants to decide whom they should sue, and when
         ADCB applied to join Dr Shetty as a defendant in the ADCB Action, Mr Manghat declined
         to support the application, but he maintained a neutral position. Further, it would have
         been open to Mr Manghat to apply to join others to the proceedings had he been so
         advised. Perhaps more importantly, on the face of it, the proceedings would have been
         the more demanding on Mr Manghat (and others) if there had been more defendants.

50. Next, Mr Manghat now makes complaints about procedural matters that he could have
         raised, but did not raise, before the trial. To take just one example, he complains about
         the late disclosure and production of documents relating to the settlement with the Bin
         Buttis, but he did not apply under rule 86 of the CPR for further or specific disclosure to
         have them produced earlier at any of the case management conferences or at the pre-
         trial review.

51. The Court has, since Mr Manghat ceased to act with solicitors, granted him all possible
         assistance to prepare and present his case. Not least, he has now been afforded
         generous time to marshal his arguments, in that I am taking account of his very late Post-
         Hearing Submissions. He has not identified any specific prejudice that he has suffered as
         a result of having limited representation and assistance, nor did he explain how he might
         have presented his case significantly differently if he had had more resources available
         to him.

The documentary evidence

52. An enormous number of documents were disclosed, and the electronic trial bundles that
         were prepared for the trial were correspondingly large. However, the documentary record
         is not as complete as it might have been: as I shall explain, documents held by the NMC
         Group were destroyed or hidden in early 2020.

53. The ADGM procedural regime does not include a provision, corresponding to paragraph
         27.2 of the English Practice Direction 32, that documents in agreed trial bundles are
         admissible unless otherwise ordered by the Court or a notice of objection has been given.
         In order to manage the trial, by an Order of 3 February 2026 following the pre-trial review,
         I directed that a document should be treated as being in evidence at the trial only if it was
         (a) specifically referred to (and, where applicable, identified by bundle reference) in a
         statement of case, a witness statement, an expert report or submissions on matters of
         UAE law; (b) specifically referred to in a party’s written opening submissions (including
         any schedules to the opening submissions); (c) put to a witness in examination in chief,
         cross-examination or re-examination; (d) included in a chronology, dramatis personae,
         or other trial document which the Court has directed be prepared or permitted to be
         referred to at trial; or (e) otherwise permitted by the Court.

54. During the trial, including in closing submissions, various suggestions were made from
         time to time that the disclosure of each of the parties was inadequate. I was not
         impressed by those points, not least because generally I was not invited to draw any
         specific inference from the supposed deficiency in disclosure. It suffices to say that these
         complaints have not affected any of my conclusions.

55. Rule 110 of the CPR provides that: “A party shall be deemed to admit the authenticity of
         a document disclosed to him under Part 13 (disclosure and inspection of documents)
         unless he serves notice that he wishes the document to be proved at trial”. On 27 June
         2025, Dr Shetty served a notice (the “Rule 110 Notice”) that he required the JA Claimants
         to prove “any document said to bear the signature of Dr Shetty, to the extent relied upon
         by [the JA Claimants] against Dr Shetty as evidence that Dr Shetty in fact signed,
         approved, authorised, or otherwise consented to the document or its contents”. The JA
         Claimants submitted that the Rule 110 Notice was not sufficient to displace the
         presumption of authenticity under the CPR, and that the documents relied upon by the
         JA Claimants bearing Dr Shetty’s signature are to be taken to be authentic.

56. I do not accept the JA Claimants’ submission. The JA Claimants seek to rely in support of
         their case against Dr Shetty on many documents, spanning a period of some eight years,
         which, on their face, bear his signature, either a ‘wet’ signature or an electronic signature.
         Throughout the proceedings, Dr Shetty has made clear that, in many cases, he does not
         know whether what appears to be his signature is: (i) an electronic signature applied
         without his knowledge or consent; (ii) a signature that he wrote on a document placed
         before him, typically in a large pile of documents, without appreciating what the
         document was or its terms or purpose; (iii) a signature on a document that he might have
         signed in blank for use by NMC Group executives when required; or (iv) a signature that
         was forged by some other person. Given the number of documents apparently signed by
         him on which the JA Claimants rely, Dr Shetty’s position seems to me readily
         understandable, and the Rule 110 Notice appropriate to defeat an argument that what
         appears to be Dr Shetty’s signature on a document, in itself, conclusively establishes the
         “authenticity” of the document, not least because it is not clear quite what is meant by

         “authenticity” in this context: I note that, in their closing submissions, the JA Claimants
         do not commit themselves as to whether a deemed admission of “authenticity” would
         preclude an argument that an electronic signature was applied without Dr Shetty’s
         consent.

57. In support of their complaint about the Rule 110 Notice, the JA Claimants contended first
         that it does not identify what documents are in dispute. I consider that there is nothing in
         this point: its purpose and scope is clear.

58. Secondly, it was said that a notice of this kind is not an appropriate way to make an
         allegation of fraud or forgery. Again, this is beside the point: Dr Shetty is not making an
         allegation that his signature was forged on any particular document, and that his
         signature was obtained by fraud in any particular case: understandably, he says that he
         does not remember, and is not in a position to know, how his signature was obtained in
         any particular case. He simply asserts his right to have the JA Claimants discharge their
         burden of proving the case against him.

59. This also answers another point made by the JA Claimants: that Dr Shetty made an
         unsuccessful application to adduce expert evidence about handwriting and whether his
         signature had been forged on occasions. By an Order of 25 March 2025, I refused his
         application because it did not properly identify the issues to which the evidence should
         go, but I gave him liberty to restore his application provided the issues were properly
         identified and a draft report was produced. Dr Shetty did not restore the application, and
         the JA Claimants complain that nevertheless “Dr Shetty now invites the Court to make
         findings of forgery”. I do not accept that characterisation of Dr Shetty’s position.

WhatsApp exchanges

60. The documentary evidence included WhatsApp exchanges, and, as will be apparent from
         my Judgment, some of them are important to the cases against Dr Shetty and Mr
         Manghat. Many of these exchanges were disclosed late. When he opened Mr Manghat’s
         case and in his closing submissions, Mr Hennessey expressed concern that Mr Manghat,
         who understandably did not recall the exchanges and had not had access to his own
         WhatsApp account, had not had the time or resources to satisfy himself that this
         evidence was fairly presented: that there were not significant omissions in the disclosure
         and in what was presented in evidence, and that the extracts relied upon were presented
         in context and without their meaning being distorted.

61. I accept that this concern must be given due weight when I am asked to rely on WhatsApp
         messages to support submissions against Mr Manghat, and indeed against Dr Shetty. I
         cannot believe that the WhatsApp evidence upon which I have relied in reaching my
         conclusions is vulnerable to problems of the kind to which Mr Hennessey referred. Nor is
         there any specific reason to think that the disclosure is materially incomplete to the
         disadvantage of either of the remaining Defendants.

62. Mr Hennessey also submitted that, when considering the WhatsApp messages, I should
         recognise what he called the “informality” of such communications: “It is much more of
         a medium that is used among friends and in a group format, which tends itself to sort of
         jokiness and hyperbole”. I accept that this is typically a characteristic of WhatsApp
         messaging, but that can, at times, make them the more telling as evidence. In this case,
         the importance of these messages lies in the substance of what was being said, rather
         than manner of expression.

The witnesses of fact

63. The JA Claimants called three witnesses of fact, all of whom gave oral evidence and were
         cross-examined:

         a. Mr Julian Jones, who is a managing director in Alvarez & Marsal Disputes &
                  Investigations LLP, a part of A&M Europe. Mr Jones had led a forensic investigation
                  team that examined the fraud and other aspects of the history of the NMC Group.
                  He presented material about how the fraud was operated.

         b. Mr Richard Dixon Fleming, much of whose evidence was unchallenged. I later (at
                  paras. 162 – 180) consider the suggestion put to him in cross-examination that the
                  JAs might have pursued claims against other potential defendants to recover
                  losses from the fraud.

         c. Lord Clanwilliam (Patrick Meade), who was a non-executive director of NMC plc
                  from 8 March 2012 until 11 April 2020. In his closing submissions, Mr Hennessey
                  submitted that Lord Clanwilliam’s “recollection was a curious thing” in that he
                  could not remember some matters at all and “very unusually there were some
                  things he could remember incredibly specifically”, and that, while he was a “fair”
                  witness, he was not “particularly reliable in his recollection”. I do not find it at all
                  strange that Lord Clanwilliam’s memory was in some ways patchy. I regard him as
                  an honest witness, and where he was able to recall matters, in my judgment, his
                  evidence was reliable.

64. In Mr Manghat’s Written Submissions and in his Post-Hearing Submissions it was
         explained that the JAs’ investigations and their attempts to reconstruct a financial record
         of the NMC Group were based on incomplete information and assumptions about
         missing documentation. Mr Jones said, for example, “I don’t think that we ever got the
         complete record”, and Mr Fleming accepted that the JAs’ investigations were continuing.
         However, when Mr Jones was cross-examined by Mr Hennessey, he was not challenged
         on specific points or particular assumptions. Moreover, much of Mr Jones’ evidence was
         superseded by, and largely endorsed by, the forensic accounting evidence of expert
         witnesses. To my mind, Mr Manghat’s criticisms do not materially detract from Mr Jones’
         evidence.

Cross-examination

77. I directed a timetable for the trial, restricting the time allowed for oral submissions and
         cross-examination of both factual and expert witnesses. Counsel therefore had to curtail
         their cross-examination accordingly. For example, the JA Claimants and ADCB did not
         have time to put to Dr Shetty, Mr Manghat or some of the Baroda witnesses all their
         allegations of wrongdoing: they had to put their case by reference to examples. I am
         satisfied that this neither prejudiced the JA Claimants and ADCB, nor prevented the
         Defendants from presenting their cases fully. In particular, as I shall further explain, I
         reject Mr Wandowicz’s submission that Dr Shetty was not given a proper opportunity to
         answer the JA Claimants’ case that Dr Shetty would still be liable to them even if he was
         not party to the fraud.

The failure to call witnesses

78. In some circumstances, the Court may draw inferences that are adverse to a party from
         the fact that he did not call evidence from a witness who was available to him. The
         question whether any inference of this kind should be drawn “depends entirely on the
         context and particular circumstances”: Royal Mail Group Ltd v Efobi [2021] UKSC 33 at
         para. 41 per Lord Leggatt. I was invited to draw such adverse inferences in this case,
         particularly by Dr Shetty and Mr Manghat against the JA Claimants and ADCB. This does
         not seem to me a case in which it is appropriate to do so.

79. In this context, Dr Shetty referred to evidence from the JAs’ Progress Reports that
         interviews had been conducted by or for the JAs with directors of the NMC plc and
         “individuals in NMC finance and IT departments”. It was submitted that I should infer (i)
         that board members would have supported Dr Shetty’s contentions “as to his approach
         to management, the degree of his day-to-day involvement in the business, and his
         reliance upon his inner circle”; and (ii) that finance employees would have support Dr
         Shetty’s case about how the finance department functioned and about “free use of Dr
         Shetty’s financial structures and signatures”.

80. I am not impressed by these points. Mr Wandowicz acknowledged that Lord Clanwilliam
         was “a good witness who tried to assist the Court” and submitted that “[t]he principal

82. In support of his contention that Dr Shetty was responsible for the fraud, Mr Manghat

Proof of fraud

Cogent evidence

86. I have referred (at para. 27 above) to the requirement that fraud must be distinctly proved,
         and I cited Thesiger LJ in Davy v Garrett (loc. cit.) and the observations of Cockerill J in
         King v Stiefel (loc. cit.). In Fiona Trust & Holding Corp v Privalov [2010] EWHC 3199
         (Comm) at para.1438, I said this:

                 “It is well established that ‘cogent evidence is required to justify a finding
                 of fraud or other discreditable conduct’: per Moore-Bick LJ in Jafari-Fini v
                 Skillglass Ltd [2007] EWCA Civ 261 at para.73. This principle reflects the
                 court's conventional perception that it is generally not likely that people
                 will engage in such conduct: ‘where a claimant seeks to prove a case of
                 dishonesty, its inherent improbability means that, even on the civil burden
                 of proof, the evidence needed to prove it must be all the stronger’, per Rix
                 LJ in Markel v Higgins [2009] EWCA 790 at para. 50. The question remains
                 one of the balance of probability, although typically, as Ungoed-Thomas J

SHETTY AND OTHERS

                                                                                                                                                                      33

                                                                                       05 November 2026 04:43 PM

                 put it in In re Dellow's Will Trusts [1964] 1 WLR 415, 455 (cited by Lord
                 Nicholls in In re H [1996] AC 563 at p.586H), ‘The more serious the
                 allegation the more cogent the evidence required to overcome the
                 unlikelihood of what is alleged and thus to prove it’. Associated with the
                 seriousness of the allegation is the seriousness of the consequences, or
                 potential consequences, of the proof of the allegation because of the
                 improbability that a person will risk such consequences: see R(N) v Mental
                 Health Review Tribunal (Northern Region) [2005] EWCA 1605 para. 62,
                 cited in Re Doherty [2008] UKHL 33 para. 27 per Lord Carswell”.

87. This principle is well-established, and the allegations of dishonesty and wrongdoing
         against Dr Shetty and Mr Manghat are certainly very serious. However, the principle is not
         to be applied mechanically. In his opening submissions, Mr Wandowicz illustrated this
         aptly by reference to the judgment of Bryan J in JSC BM Bank v Kekhman [2018] EWHC
         791 (Comm), a case in which it was “common ground that there were extensive frauds
         and deceits perpetrated by [the company’s] employees over an extended period of time”
         (at para. 58). It is self-evident that this colours the conventional view that persons are not
         likely to engage in fraud: as Bryan J noted, “That is of relevance when considering [the]
         likelihood and the probability or improbability of someone in Mr Kekhman’s position, and
         indeed Mr Kekhman himself, knowing of, and being involved in, such fraud when
         considering the evidence before the Court as to the degree of involvement of Mr Kekhman
         in the business” (loc. cit. at para. 58). Here, the position is starker: it is not disputed either
         that there was an extensive fraud over some years in the NMC Group, or that at least one
         of Dr Shetty and Mr Manghat was party to it.

88. The need for cogent evidence is a procedural requirement and therefore a matter for the
         lex fori. Accordingly, I adopt it when considering the Civil Claims, notwithstanding that
         they are governed by UAE law, as well as in relation to the Insolvency Claims.

Fraud: motive

89. Mr Wandowicz argued that Dr Shetty had no rational motive for misappropriating funds
         from the NMC Group, being enormously wealthy by 2012, and having won respect and
         recognition both in India and in the UAE. He was about to turn 70, and there was no reason
         that he should have risked his wealth and reputation by turning to fraud. As it is, as a result
         of the collapse of the NMC Group, he has been the subject of a series of personal claims
         in respect of borrowings of the NMC Group for which he gave personal guarantees, and I
         understand that other proceedings against him are pending.

90. I shall consider these arguments further when I assess the allegations about Dr Shetty’s
         involvement in the fraud. Here, I only introduce the relevance of motive in cases of this
         kind, and refer to the judgment of Calver J in ED&F Man Capital Markets v Come Harvest
         Holdings Ltd [2022] EWHC 229 (Comm). He said this (at paras. 71 (iii) and 71(iv)):

SHETTY AND OTHERS

                                                                                                                                                                      34

                                                                                       05 November 2026 04:43 PM

                   “Although not strictly a requirement for such a claim, motive ‘is a vital
                   ingredient of any rational assessment’ of dishonesty: Bank of Tokyo-
                   Mitsubishi UFJ Ltd v Baskan Gida Sanayi Ve Pazarlama AS [2009] EWHC
                   1276 (Ch) at para. 858 per Briggs J. By and large dishonest people are
                   dishonest for a reason; while establishing a motive for conspiracy is not a
                   legal requirement, the less likely the motive, the less likely the intention to
                   conspire unlawfully: Group Seven Ltd v Nasir [2017] EWHC 2466 (Ch) at
                   para. 440 per Morgan J.

                   Assessing a party's motive to participate in a fraud also requires taking into
                   account the disincentives to participation in the fraud; this includes the
                   disinclination to behave immorally or dishonestly, but also the damage to
                   reputation (both for the individual and, where applicable, the business)
                   and the potential risk to the ‘liberty of the individuals involved’ in case they
                   are found out: Tokyo-Mitsubishi UFJ Ltd v Baskan Gida Sanayi Ve
                   Pazarlama AS [2009] EWHC 1276 (Ch) at paras. 858, 865 per Briggs J”.

91. While I agree with this, and I add these observations. First, it must be recognised that it
         might not always be possible for a victim of fraud to know the motive of those involved in
         it.

92. Secondly, motive is not to be understood in a narrow sense. The question is really
         whether there is a plausible explanation for the alleged behaviour.

93. Thirdly, a connected point: the authorities about motive must be assessed in light of the
         decision of the United Kingdom Supreme Court in Ivey v Genting Casinos (UK) Ltd [2017]
         UKSC 67, which was decided after the decision in the Bank of Tokyo-Mitsubishi UFJ Ltd
         decision which Calver J cited. I consider the judgment in the Ivey case in more detail
         below (at paras. 271 – 274). It establishes that a person’s honesty or dishonesty is not
         determined by reference to whether he acts contrary to his own standards of morality,
         but whether there is a plausible and rational explanation for him not acting in accordance
         with objective standards of honesty; when considering a person’s motive, therefore, the
         Court is not necessarily looking at whether there is an explanation for doing what he
         himself considers to be wrong.

94. Lastly, the question of motive does not only impose a burden upon a claimant in a case
         such as this. It is also proper to take it into account when assessing the defendants’
         cases about how others behaved. In this case, perhaps the most significant question
         about motive arises when considering whether Mr Manghat and others might have had
         any plausible reason improperly to divert the NMC Group’s funds to make acquisitions
         for Dr Shetty without letting him know that they were enriching him.

The parties and associated persons

Dr Shetty and the origins of the NMC Group

95. Dr Shetty is an Indian national, having been born in Karnataka, India in 1942. He acquired
         some early experience in pharmaceuticals as a distributor and sales representative, and
         in 1973, he immigrated to the UAE and worked selling medicines. In 1976, he married Dr
         Chandrakumari Shetty in Bangalore, and she joined him in Abu Dhabi.

96. In 1975, Dr Shetty established in Abu Dhabi a healthcare centre, with a pharmacy, a
         dental clinic and a laboratory. He did so in partnership with HE Abdulla Humaid Al
         Mazrouei (“HE Al Mazrouei”), who had an interest of around 60% in the enterprise. They
         went on to acquire further pharmacies and hospital businesses, and in 1981 Dr Shetty’s
         business interests expanded into pharmaceutical distribution. These enterprises were
         the foundation of the NMC Group, which in due course grew to become the largest
         healthcare provider in the UAE, operating over 200 hospitals and other medical facilities,
         predominantly (but not exclusively) in the UAE and across the Middle East.

97. Until 2008, Dr Shetty operated through various sister companies and partnerships. On 29
         April 2008, NMCH was incorporated as a holding company for the various operations.

98. In 2010, the NMC Group sought external financing and funds by way of a private
         placement, but it had difficulties in raising external finance. KBBAM assisted the NMC
         Group to do so. On 30 January 2011, Centurion bought shares in NMCH. The
         shareholdings then came to be owned by Dr Shetty as to 30%, by HE Al Mazrouei as to
         30% and by Centurion as to 40%. By April 2011, the directors were the Bin Buttis and Dr
         Shetty. (In the proceedings, the term “Principal Shareholders” was used to refer
         together to Dr Shetty and the Bin Buttis. I shall use it in this Judgment.)

99. Shortly thereafter, HE Al Mazrouei disposed of his shareholding to SAQ, KBBAM and
         Infinite. Thus, the shares came to be held by: Dr Shetty as to 30%, SAQ as to 43%, KBBAM
         as to 15% and Infinite as to 12%.

100. The changes in the ownership of the NMC Group led to dispute between Dr Shetty and HE
         Al Mazrouei. Early in 2012, HE Al Mazrouei brought civil proceedings in the UAE against,
         amongst others, Dr Shetty, Mr Manghat (who, as I shall explain, had become NMCH’s
         Chief Financial Officer (“CFO”) in 2011) and Centurion, and criminal proceedings in the
         UAE against Dr Shetty, Mr Manghat and others. Proceedings were also brought in India.
         As a result of the UAE criminal proceedings, a travel ban was imposed upon Dr Shetty and
         Mr Manghat. According to the evidence before me, it was determined in 2013 that the
         dispute was a civil matter, the criminal proceedings were terminated and the travel ban
         lifted. The civil proceedings were settled in 2014: on 23 July 2014, Dr Shetty issued a
         series of post-dated cheques payable to HE Al Mazrouei.

101. On 20 July 2011, NMC plc was incorporated in England, in preparation for an Initial Public
         Offering (the “IPO”). Its original directors were Dr Shetty, the Bin Buttis and Infinite. In
         March 2012, four further directors were appointed: on 8 March 2012, Mr Mark Tompkins,
         who was the Chairman of NMC plc from 8 March 2012 to 26 March 2020, Mr Justin Jewitt
         and Lord Clanwilliam, and on 19 March 2012, Ms Heather Lawrence. Mr Binay Shetty, Dr
         Shetty’s son, was the Chief Operating Officer. Further directors, including Mr Binay
         Shetty, were appointed in 2014.

102. On 28 March 2012, NMC plc became the ultimate parent company of NMCH and the
         other companies in the NMC Group, and it remained the ultimate parent of the operating
         companies until 25 March 2022, when the NMC Group was restructured (see para. 128
         below), and of NMCH until 22 December 2023. Ernst & Young LLP (“EY”) worked with
         NMCH’s management on the IPO, and they prepared a Project Long Form Report and a
         Financial Reporting Procedures Report (the “FRP Report”), both dated 2 April 2012. On
         2 April 2012, pursuant to the IPO, 33.05% of NMC plc’s shares were listed on the London
         Stock Exchange, the other 66.95% being retained by Dr Shetty (20.32%) and the Bin Buttis
         and Infinite (together, 46.63%). As Mr Manghat explained, and I accept, the IPO not only
         raised money from shareholders, but also made it easier to raise bank debt because
         lenders would take comfort from the listed status of NMC plc and its audited financial
         accounts.

103. Dr Shetty held senior positions in the NMC Group. From its incorporation in 2011 until
         2020, he was a director of NMC plc. He was its CEO from 20 July 2011 until 8 March 2017,
         its Executive Vice-Chairman from 31 December 2014 until 8 March 2017, and its Non-
         Executive Vice-Chairman from 8 March 2017 until 16 February 2020. Dr Shetty was also:
         (i) a director/manager of NMCH from 28 April 2008 to 20 June 2018; (ii) a director/manager
         of Holding from 12 May 2011 to 7 November 2018; and (iii) a director/manager of most of
         the ADGM Companies.

104. The relationship that Dr Shetty and the Bin Buttis had with NMC plc and between
         themselves was governed by relationship agreements that they entered into with NMC
         plc on 2 April 2012 and on 15 November 2014 (together the “Relationship Agreements”).
         They provided inter alia that Dr Shetty and SAQ would each conduct all transactions with
         any member of the NMC Group at arm’s length and on normal commercial terms (and
         that they would use their best endeavours to procure that their respective “Associates”
         as defined therein, would do so). The Relationship Agreements provided that these terms
         would continue to bind Dr Shetty and SAQ for as long as they or their “Associates”
         remained the direct or indirect beneficial owners of at least 15% of the entire issued share
         capital of NMC plc.

The Bin Buttis

105. The Bin Buttis are UAE nationals and were wealthy, prominent and well-connected
         businessmen. They held substantial shareholdings in NMC plc until it was put into
         administration. SAQ was appointed as: (i) a director of NMCH from at least 16 February
         2011 to 20 June 2018; (ii) a director of Holding from 12 May 2011 to 7 November 2018; and
         (iii) a non-executive director of NMC plc from 20 July 2011 to 24 February 2014. KBBAM
         was appointed as: (i) a director of NMCH from at least 7 April 2011 to 20 June 2018; (ii) a
         director of Holding from at least 12 May 2011 to 7 November 2018 at the latest; (iii) the
         Executive Vice Chairman of NMC plc from 20 July 2011 to 25 March 2014; and (iv) a
         director of NMC plc from 28 June 2017 to 14 February 2020.

106. As I have said, on 27 July 2021 bankruptcy proceedings were commenced before the
         ADBC in relation to the Bin Buttis and their related entities.

NMC plc and its subsidiaries

107. As Mr Manghat pleaded in the JA Action and was not disputed, “[NMC plc] was the holding
         company, but … the group was run and operated out of [NMCH] in the UAE, with de
         minimis activities and no full-time employees in the UK”. Therefore, under the new
         structure, NMCH remained an intermediate holding company of the operating
         subsidiaries, and it managed the NMC Group’s banking and other financial operations in
         Abu Dhabi. The financial organisation comprising, at least from 2014, a Treasury Team
         and an Accounts and Reporting Team. NMCH’s shares were held by Holding, an
         intermediate holding company, and Holding was in turn wholly owned by NMC plc.

108. At around the time of the IPO, there were some twenty companies in the NMC Group,
         including NMC plc, Holding and NMCH. The other companies fell into two categories: the
         healthcare division, including pharmaceuticals, and the trading division, which
         distributed medical and pharmaceutical supplies.

109. The large number of operating companies in the healthcare division was explained by Mr
         Manghat in his defence in the JA Action, his explanation was not disputed and I accept it:
         “The NMC Group structure was such that subsidiaries were required to be incorporated
         for each hospital, because it was not possible for regulatory reasons to open branches,
         and each medical practice would need a separate insurance policy which was easier to
         arrange for a dedicated subsidiary company. However … the NMC Group was managed
         and operated at Group level, from and by [NMCH]. The subsidiary companies did not have
         separate Board meetings”.

110. The trading division included a subsidiary of NMCH called NMC Trading LLC and its own
         subsidiaries, Mr Manghat described it in an email of 4 September 2014 as “the trading
         and services arm” of NMC plc with “exclusive rights to engage in the importing, marketing

         and distribution of over 50,000 products from leading brands including [Fast Moving

111. NMC plc, being the listed parent company of the NMC Group, was required to publish,
         and throughout the Relevant Period until 30 June 2019 did publish, consolidated and
         statutory financial statements at six monthly periods at 30 June and 31 December in each
         year. The consolidated statements, and NMC plc’s statements, were audited by EY. For
         its part, NMCH prepared annual financial statements in respect of the NMC Group that it
         controlled for the years ending 31 December 2012 until 31 December 2018, and these
         were audited by EY’s associated entity, Ernst & Young Middle East (“EYME”). The
         difference between EY and EYME is not important to what I have to decide, and I shall
         refer to them without distinction as “EY”.

112. By the end of 2014, the NMC Group had acquired four further companies, and thereafter
         the NMC Group continued to expand through acquisitions in the UAE and elsewhere.

113. In 2015, NMCH obtained a syndicated loan facility of US$ 825 million, which was partly
         used to refinance existing debt, but also provided funds for a major programme of further
         acquisitions. In 2015, the NMC Group acquired seven companies or corporate groups,
         including companies outside the Gulf region, in Spain and Italy.

114. In 2016 and 2017, the NMC Group continued its programme of expansion: in 2016, it
         acquired six more companies or groups, in the UAE, Denmark and Brazil. On 14
         December 2016, NMC plc had a rights issue, which raised US$ 322 million: those funds
         were used to part-finance the acquisition on 13 February 2017 of Al Zahra PVT Hospital
         Company Limited (“Al Zahra”), which came to be called NMC Royal Hospital LLC (but in
         this Judgment, I shall refer to it throughout as Al Zahra). In 2017, it also made acquisitions
         in the Kingdom of Saudi Arabia and Oman.

115. By 2017, differences had apparently arisen between Dr Shetty and the Bin Buttis. In 2016,
         Dr Shetty had entered into discussions about transferring to Centurion all his shares in
         NMC plc, BRS Ventures & Holdings Limited (“BRS Ventures & Holdings”), UAE Exchange
         Centre LLC (“UAE Exchange”) and associated companies for US$ 2 billion, and a term
         sheet dated 28 December 2016 was signed that recorded the “principal terms and
         conditions” for the purchase. That deal did not go ahead, for reasons that, according to
         Mr Manghat, he did not know. Discussions continued about a different deal, and it was
         proposed that Dr Shetty should sell most of his shares in NMC plc to Centurion. However,
         the regulatory body of the UK, the Panel on Takeovers and Mergers, allowed Dr Shetty to
         transfer to Centurion no more than 5% of the issued shares of NMC plc. On 31 May 2017,
         Dr Shetty entered into the so-called “2017 MOUs”: he was to transfer just under 5% of
         the issued shares to the Bin Buttis, and to divest himself of a further 10% to be held on
         trust for them; and the Bin Buttis were to have control of the board of NMC plc. This
         arrangement involved Mr Binay Shetty resigning as a director, his resignation being
         announced on 28 June 2017. In exchange, Dr Shetty was to acquire the Bin Buttis’ shares

116. In September 2017, NMC plc joined the UK’s Financial Times Stock Exchange 100 index
         (the “FTSE 100”).

117. In 2018, the NMC Group raised further debt by way of a syndicated loan of US$ 2 billion
         in February 2018 (the “2018 Syndicated Loan”), a US$ 450 million convertible bond issue
         in April 2018 and a US$ 400 million sukuk financing arrangement. According to its
         financial statements and a company announcement, the purpose of the 2018 Syndicated
         Loan was to refinance debt, to make further acquisitions and to provide headroom for
         future needs. The stated purpose of the convertible bond issue and the sukuk
         arrangement was to repay part of a US$ 1 billion bridging facility that had been arranged
         at the time of the 2018 Syndicated Loan.

118. By an agreement of 4 November 2018, NMCH entered into a term loan facility agreement
         with Baroda for a loan of AED 367 million (the “AED 367 Million Facility”), repayable in
         24 quarterly instalments commencing in March 2020 (or, if earlier, 24 months after the
         “Utilisation Date” as defined therein).

119. In 2018, the NMC Group’s acquisitions included not only the acquisition by NMCH of
         some nineteen entities in the UAE and elsewhere, but also the direct acquisition by NMC
         plc of a 70% interest in BIVF Management Services LLC, an American investment
         sometimes referred to as Boston IVF, and a 49% interest in Fakih IVF LLC. In 2019, NMCH
         made two further acquisitions, Exeter Orthopedic Spinal Center LLC and Fertimed SA.

120. On 17 December 2019, Muddy Waters Capital LLC (“Muddy Waters”), an American
         investment company that specialises in investigations to identify corporate failings,
         published a report on NMC plc (the “First Muddy Waters Report”), identifying what it
         called “numerous red flags” in its consolidated accounts, and expressing “serious
         doubts” about NMC plc’s financial statements and “concerns about fraudulent asset
         values and theft of company assets”. It said this: “We are unsure how deep the rot at NMC
         goes, but we do not believe that its insiders or financials can be trusted”.

121. Unsurprisingly, NMC plc’s share price immediately suffered a significant fall.
         Undoubtedly, the First Muddy Waters Report would have had an immediate impact on
         NMC plc’s lenders: Mr Zo’Mot’s evidence of his concern was not challenged, and Mr El
         Khaldi considered it so serious that he told his colleagues at ADCB that they should not
         communicate with the NMC Group until further notice.

122. On 19 December 2019, NMC plc refuted the criticisms in the First Muddy Waters Report,
         and on 23 December 2019, it announced the appointment of an “Independent Review
         Committee”, under the chairmanship of Mr Jonathan Bomford and including Lord
         Clanwilliam, to investigate its allegations. It sought advice from Glaser Weil LLP and, on

123. On 10 February 2020, Muddy Waters issued a further report, in which it identified

124. On 27 February 2020, at the request of the board of NMC plc, its shares were suspended

         US$ 2.7 billion of debt had not been previously disclosed or approved by the board, and

The administrations

125. On 2 April 2020, ADCB applied to the English High Court for an order that NMC plc be put
         into administration. On 9 April 2020, the English High Court made an Administration
         Order, and the JAs, together with Mr Mark Firmin, also of A&M Europe, were appointed as
         its Joint Administrators.

126. Before September 2020, NMCH and Holding were incorporated in onshore Abu Dhabi,
         and NMCH had operating subsidiaries variously incorporated in Abu Dhabi, Dubai and
         Sharjah. On 14 and 15 September 2020, NMCH, Holding and the ADGM Companies were
         re-registered under the ADGM Companies Regulations 2015 (as amended), as I described
         in my judgment in these proceedings of 8 July 2024, [2024] ADGMCFI 0007, esp. at paras.
         27ff. On 27 September 2020, I made an Order to put NMCH, Holding and the ADGM
         Companies into administration, and I appointed the JAs as their Joint Administrators: see
         my judgment of 6 October 2020, [2020] ADGMCFI 0008. Not all the subsidiary companies
         of NMCH went into administration (nor into other insolvency procedures, either in the
         ADGM or elsewhere).

127. By my Order of 27 September 2020 and my judgment of 6 October 2020, [2020] ADGMCFI
         0008, I also permitted the JAs to enter into an Administrative Funding Facility (“AFF”)
         under section 109A of the IR 2015.

128. On 21 September 2021, NMCH and the ADGM Companies (but not Holding) each entered
         into a Deed of Company Arrangement (“DOCA”, and respectively the “NMCH DOCA”
         and the “Related Party DOCAs”) for the restructuring of the business of the NMC Group
         on the so-called “Restructuring Effective Date” (the “RED”), which was 25 March 2022.
         (The NMCH DOCA was amended on 27 October 2021.) The administration of the 34
         ADGM Companies, and the JAs’ appointments as their Joint Administrators, ended on 25

         March 2022. NMCH and Holding remain in administration and the JAs remain their Joint
         Administrators.

129. In a judgment dated 4 April 2022, NMC Healthcare Limited v Noor Capital PSC [2022]
         ADGMCFI 0003, I summarised the overall scheme of the DOCA arrangements as follows:

                    “In broad outline, the scheme of the proposed DOCAs was to allow
                   creditors to submit claims for proof, such proofs being in the
                   administration of the relevant debtor company as well as under the
                   relevant DOCA; and proving creditors were to share rateably in the value
                   of the Group through instruments of entitlement in the restructured Group.
                   A new company in the Group was to take over the subsidiary companies
                   and through them the operating businesses, principally through transfers
                   of shares owned by NMCH. (Under section 81 of the IR, the administrator
                   of a DOCA may transfer shares in the company with the written consent of
                   the owner of the shares or the Court's leave.) Thus, the subsidiary
                   companies were to continue as going concerns. NMCH was to continue in
                   administration in order to collect and distribute assets so as to achieve the
                   best result for its creditors as a whole” (at para. 21).

130. In a judgment dated 15 April 2026, Secure Capital Equipment LLC v NMC Healthcare
         Limited [2026] ADGMCFI 0011, I added to this summary: “[U]nder the scheme, the
         creditors of the NMC Companies were treated as ‘Group Creditors’ and were to share in
         the assets of the NMC Group: in that way, the structure of the separate companies in the
         NMC Group was overridden. The scheme was put into effect through two companies:
         NMC Holdco SPV Ltd (‘Holdco’), the holding company of the new structure that had been
         incorporated to acquire substantially all the assets of the NMC Companies; and NMC
         Opco SPV Ltd ("Opco"), the newly incorporated operating company of the Holdco Group.
         The creditors of the NMC Group were to participate in the new structure by being issued
         with so-called ‘DOCA Creditor Entitlements’” (at para. 26). The NMCH DOCA defines the
         term “DOCA Creditor Entitlements” to mean the EPM Entitlements and the Litigation
         Entitlements. The EPM Entitlements are commonly referred to as “Exit Instruments”,
         and I shall adopt that term.

131. It is the JA Claimants’ case that, under the DOCA arrangements, the ADGM Companies
         assigned to NMCH any claims that they or the JAs in their capacity as their Joint
         Administrators had against, inter alia, Dr Shetty or Mr Manghat. Dr Shetty (and, before the
         settlement, Baroda) disputed the validity of the assignments, and I consider this issue at
         paras. 233ff.

132. The NMCH DOCA sets out the procedure for “Group Creditors” (as defined therein) to
         make claims, and the Related Party DOCAs were materially similar. Under clauses 6.2 to
         6.5 of the NMCH DOCA, Group Creditors were entitled to submit proofs of debt, which
         the JAs, in their capacities as “Deed Administrators” (as defined therein), were entitled

         to accept or reject (in whole or in part). Where a proof of debt was rejected, a Group
         Creditor could seek an adjudication under clauses 6.6 and 6.7 of the NMCH DOCA (and,
         in some circumstances a Group Creditor could being court proceedings in respect of a
         rejected proof of debt under clause 6.8).

133. Dr Shetty submitted a proof of debt against NMCH, which was rejected by the Deed
         Administrators in full. Dr Shetty exercised his right to have his rejected claim determined
         by an independent adjudicator pursuant to clause 6.6(b) of the NMCH DOCA. The
         adjudication process is subject to confidentiality restrictions, and I need say nothing
         about it except that Dr Shetty is not an “Admitted Group Creditor” (as defined therein in
         the NMCH DOCA).

134. Mr Manghat did not submit any proof of debt claim and is not an Admitted Group Creditor.

135. On the RED, 25 March 2022, ADCB and other Admitted Group Creditors received Exit
         Instruments. In exchange, they waived their creditor claims. Accordingly, clause 13.1 of
         the NMCH DOCA provided that, on the RED, Holdco would issue Exit Instruments to the
         creditor: this “Holdco Issuance” (as defined therein) was deemed to discharge the claim
         pro tanto. Therefore, the recipient creditor’s claims were discharged in an amount equal
         to the value of the Exit Instruments. Clause 13.5 provided that the distribution of the Exit
         Instruments was to be treated as settling all claims and liabilities to the extent provided
         for in clause 13.1. The Related Party DOCAs had similar provisions, except that they
         provided that claims would be fully released and extinguished (and not discharged pro
         tanto).

136. Dr Shetty had many commercial interests other than those in the NMC Group. They

         a. Neopharma LLC (“Neopharma”), a company incorporated in Abu Dhabi in 2003,
                 which was a pharmaceutical manufacturer. According to Dr Shetty, he had a 90%
                 interest in it, the other 10% being owned by Dr Abdul Rahim Jaffar. Mr Manghat was
                 its Finance Manager (or, as the JA Claimants plead, “acted as Chief Financial
                 Officer for Neopharma”) from 2003 to 2009, and its CFO from 2009 to 2011.

         b. Nexgen Pharma FZ LLC (“Nexgen”), another pharmaceuticals company, which was
                 incorporated in Dubai. Dr Shetty owned a 45% interest in Nexgen, through his
                 interest in Neopharma, the other owner being Hetero FZCO, an Indian
                 pharmaceuticals company founded in 1993 by Dr Bandi Parthasaradhi Reddy. Mr
                 Manghat was named as a manager on Nexgen’s trade licence and he was a
                 signatory on its bank accounts, but he denies being involved in its “day-to-day
                 operations and management”.

ADCB

139. ADCB is a major UAE bank. It is majority owned (as to some 60%) by the government of
         Abu Dhabi through Mubadala Investment Company PJSC.

140. On 1 May 2019, ADCB merged with Union National Bank PJSC (“UNB”), another UAE-
         based bank, through a UAE statutory merger. UNB was dissolved, and all its assets, rights
         and liabilities were transferred to ADCB. On the same day, 1 May 2019, ADCB also
         acquired the UAE bank, AHB, which continues to operate as a separate Islamic bank.

141. At the time of the IPO in April 2012, ADCB had little financial exposure to the NMC Group,
         providing only a relatively small working capital facility. However, in 2002, UNB had
         entered into a facility arrangement (the “UNB Facility”) with New Medical Centre Trading
         LLC (“New Medical Centre”), which was renewed from time to time, NMCH becoming
         the borrower in 2011. On 1 May 2019, upon merger of the banks, ADCB became the lender
         of the UNB Facility, and it was renewed by ADCB on 29 August 2019.

142. After the IPO, ADCB’s exposure increased considerably. Its primary exposure was by way
         of a series of syndicated facility agreements with NMCH in the years from 2013 to 2017.
         It also increased the limit on the working capital facility: for example, over 2014 to 2015,
         it increased the limit from AED 70.3 million to AED 295 million. As I shall describe in more
         detail at paras. 339 – 340 below, in 2018 and 2019, ADCB extended major lending

         facilities to the NMC Group. When the NMC Group was put into administration in 2020,
         ADCB was its largest creditor.

143. After the First Muddy Waters Report, NMCH defaulted on its obligations under its
         agreements with ADCB. More specifically, on 26 February 2020, it defaulted on its
         obligation under the 2018 Syndicated Loan to make a payment of some US$ 70 million,
         and in March 2020 it defaulted on other obligations under it. On 2 April 2020, NMCH
         defaulted on an obligation under the 2018 Syndicated Loan to pay some US$ 223 million,
         which led ADCB to serve a notice of default so that the full amount outstanding under the
         2018 Syndicated Loan (including interest) immediately accrued due and payable.

144. As I have said, on 2 April 2020, ADCB issued its petition in the English High Court for NMC
         plc to be put into administration. On 14 April 2020, ADCB announced that it had made a
         criminal complaint to the UAE authorities against, among others, Dr Shetty and Mr
         Manghat.

Mr Manghat

145. Mr Manghat is an Indian national, having been born in Kerala, India, in 1974. In 2001, he
         qualified as a chartered accountant, and was first employed at Kotak Mahindra Bank, an
         Indian bank. He met Dr Shetty in December 2002 through his brother, Mr Promoth
         Manghat, who was then employed as the Finance Manager of UAE Exchange. Dr Shetty
         invited Mr Manghat to work for him, or his companies, and he moved to the UAE to do so
         in January 2003. In about August 2003, he became the Finance Manager of Neopharma.
         While so employed, at Dr Shetty’s request, Mr Manghat did some work for NMCH and in
         2009 he became a part-time financial adviser of NMCH.

146. In 2011, Mr Manghat was appointed as the CFO of NMCH, being so appointed from 1 May
         2011. He remained the CFO of the NMC Group until December 2014.

147. From 1 January 2015, he became the Deputy CEO of the NMC Group, having been
         appointed as its Acting Deputy CEO in October 2014, and he was promoted to be its CEO
         from 8 March 2017, a position that he held until 2020. (Apparently, Dr Shetty had planned
         that he should be succeeded as CEO by his son, Mr Binay Shetty. According to Mr
         Manghat, Mr Binay Shetty told him that he did not want the position. However, that might
         be, in the event Mr Manghat was appointed.) During this period, Mr Manghat held other
         positions in the NMC Group, including as a director of NMC plc from 26 June 2014 until
         26 February 2020, and a director of NMCH from 20 June 2018 until 26 February 2020.

148. After his appointment as Deputy CEO and later as CEO, Mr Manghat continued to be
         closely involved with the NMC Group’s financial affairs. The minutes of a board meeting
         of NMC plc on 19 October 2014 record that “Prasanth Manghat is promoted to the
         position of Deputy CEO retaining oversight of financial matters and taking responsibility
         for all operational matters of the Group”.

149. The evidence shows that as Deputy CEO:

         a. Mr Manghat frequently made presentations to the NMC plc board on matters
                 relating to the bank facilities, the financial strategy and the overall financial
                 position. For example, the minutes of a meeting on 16 February 2015 and 2 June
                 2015 record him reporting on the financial implications of proposed acquisitions;
                 and the minutes of meetings on 20 April 2015 and 25 August 2015 record him
                 reporting on “M&A and Strategic Financial matters”.

         b. Mr Manghat negotiated the terms of the NMC Group’s banking facilities and signed
                 loan agreements.

150. After his appointment as CEO, Mr Manghat continued to have oversight and control of the
         NMC finances. For example:

         a. At a board meeting of NMC plc on 5 February 2018 (which was not attended by Mr
                 Prashanth Shenoy, then the CFO but not a director), Mr Manghat presented a paper
                 on the “new US$ 2 bn Group Debt Financing Facilities” (the 2018 Syndicated Loan).
                 In cross-examination, Mr Manghat said that he was asked to make this presentation
                 because “I was involved anyway in the finance”.

         b. He negotiated and signed these banking facilities, none of which was disclosed in
                 NMC Group’s financial statements: (i) a term sheet dated 29 March 2017 for
                 US$100 million bridging finance for Al Zahra with Abu Dhabi Islamic Bank PJSC; (ii)
                 a term sheet dated 1 April 2017 with Noor Bank for Al Zahra for a AED 367 million
                 Shari’a facility; and (iii) a term loan facility dated 4 July 2018 with First Abu Dhabi
                 Bank PJSC for NMCH for up to AED 200 million. (As ADCB demonstrated in its
                 closing submissions, and Mr Hennessey did not dispute, WhatsApp exchanges
                 prove Mr Manghat’s close involvement in arranging these facilities.)

151. This picture is corroborated by the evidence of Mr Zo’Mot, who said that: “Certainly from
         my perspective Mr Manghat ran NMC from October 2014 onwards; taking the lead on all
         NMC financing requests to ADCB, managing all key meetings, and providing key updates
         to ADCB in relation to NMC”.

152. On 26 February 2020, Mr Manghat was removed as a director of NMC plc and NMCH. He
         also then ceased to work for NMC plc, although he pleads that his contract of
         employment was not formally terminated. On 22 March 2020, he ceased to be on the
         board of Holding.

153. On 29 February 2020, Mr Manghat travelled with his wife and family from the UAE to India.
         His wife, Mrs Manghat, and their two children returned to the UAE on 11 March 2020. Mr
         Manghat travelled to London to consult lawyers, but while he was there, COVID-19 travel
         restrictions were introduced. Whilst in London, Mr Manghat was advised that, if he went
