         to the UAE, he would face arrest. He travelled to India, and he has not since returned to
         the UAE.

154. As a result of a complaint made by ADCB in April 2020, Mr Manghat’s assets in the UAE
         have been frozen, as have those of his wife and other family members. He and Mrs
         Manghat are also subject to travel restrictions, and therefore Mr Manghat has felt unable
         to visit the UAE and his wife has been unable to leave the country: they have been apart
         for some years. Mr Manghat has seen little of their children.

155. Mr Manghat says that he also suffered financial loss as a result of the fraud because he
         retained his shareholding in NMC plc and indeed increased it in 2018 and 2019 when the
         share price was falling. He pleads in the ADCB Action that he did so on the advice of J P
         Morgan and Barclays “in order to give confidence to the market”. He also says that in early
         2020, when the fraud was coming to light, he kept his shares; and that his shareholding,
         worth some US$ 13.5 million on the basis of the shares’ value when acquired, are now of
         negligible value. In contrast, he contends, Dr Shetty and the Bin Buttis sold shares in early
         2020, and reduced their combined shareholding in NMC plc, which had totalled some
         59% in March 2019, to some 26% of the shares in February 2020. Mr Manghat’s
         arguments are that this supports his contentions that:

         a. Dr Shetty and the Bin Buttis were the (or the principal) beneficiaries of the fraud,
                   and enriched themselves, knowing that the fraud, which had inflated the value of
                   the shares, was about to be exposed; and

         b. had he known about the fraud, he would not have increased his shareholding but
                   would, like Dr Shetty and the Bin Buttis, have disposed of his shares.

156. The factual basis of these allegations are not admitted by other parties and Mr Manghat
         did not prove them by proper evidence. However, the contentions about the
         shareholdings of Dr Shetty and the Bin Buttis were pleaded by ADCB before, in the course
         of the trial, I ordered the allegations in ADCB’s Particulars of Claim to which they related
         be struck out. That does not prevent Mr Manghat from relying on ADCB’s struck-out
         pleading: Thevarajah v Riordan [2015] EWCA Civ 41 (esp. at para. 33) establishes that this
         is the position in the ADCB Action, and it seems to me to follow that Mr Manghat can also
         rely on the statements made by ADCB as hearsay evidence in relation to the JA Claimants’
         claims. I shall therefore consider Mr Manghat’s contentions on the assumption that the
         factual basis for them is made out.

157. I am not persuaded of Mr Manghat’s first contention. There is no reason to suppose that
         decisions by Dr Shetty and the Bin Buttis to sell NMC plc shares in early 2020 were driven
         by knowledge that fraud was about to be discovered, rather than by concern that the
         value of the shares would be adversely affected by the allegations of Muddy Waters,
         whether they were right or wrong.

158. I accept that Mr Manghat’s second contention might provide some modest support for
         his denial that he was party to the fraud or knew of it. However, in this case, it is
         overshadowed by the specific allegations made against Mr Manghat that I shall examine.

NMCH’s other financial management

159. As well as Mr Manghat, the NMC Group’s senior management included the following
         executives:

         a. Mr Suresh Krishnamoorthy, who was Head of Management Information and
                   Corporate Planning from 2011 to 2014, Finance Manager from 2014 to 2015 and
                   succeeded Mr Manghat as the CFO on 1 January 2015. He remained the CFO until
                   31 August 2017.

         b. Mr Prashanth Shenoy, who was the Deputy CEO responsible for mergers and
                   acquisitions from mid-2016 to August 2017, and the CFO from 1 September 2017
                   to 24 March 2020.

         c. Mr Suresh Kumar, who was Head of Banking and Trade from 2011 to 2016 and the
                   Deputy CFO from 20 November 2016 until 26 February 2020 with responsibility
                   for Treasury and Corporate Finance.

         d. Mr Deepak Ghosh, who joined NMCH in 2007 and was the Head of Corporate
                   Accounting at the time of the IPO. He was the Deputy CFO from 20 November
                   2016 to 26 February 2020 with responsibilities for Accounting and Corporate
                   Reporting.

         e. Mr Praveen Kumar, who worked as a Manager in the NMC Group’s Treasury and
                   Corporate Finance Department from August 2007 until 26 February 2020.

160. An organisational chart in the FRP Report at the time of the IPO shows Mr Suresh
         Krishnamoorthy, Mr Suresh Kumar and Mr Deepak Ghosh reporting to Mr Manghat as the
         CFO. In cross-examination, Mr Manghat accepted that this organogram accurately
         represented the organisation of the finance structure of NMCH at the time of the IPO,
         including his role overseeing it.

161. Within NMCH’s financial organisation, the Treasury Team was directed and supervised
         by Mr Suresh Kumar, and the Accounts and Reporting Team by Mr Deepak Ghosh. Mr
         Suresh Kumar and Mr Deepak Ghosh reported to the CFO: to Mr Krishnamoorthy from 1
         January 2015 and then to Mr Shenoy from 1 September 2017.

Other proceedings and mitigation

162. Mr Fleming gave evidence that the companies in the NMC Group and the JAs brought
         proceedings and reached settlements against other persons; and that they had decided

         in some cases not to pursue possible claims. I refer to these matters because Mr

         potential remedies against others, the NMC Group had not mitigated its loss.

163. Mr Manghat did not plead a defence of this kind, but Mr Manghat’s Written Submissions
         contended that it is clear, and the JA Claimants should have known, that the NMC Group
         had potential remedies against UAE Exchange, Neopharma and (as I understand it)
         Nexgen. Mr Hennessey did not develop these arguments in his closing submissions, but
         I do not understand them to have been abandoned.

164. Baroda pleaded in its defence that any recovery by the JA Claimants should be reduced
         because of the fault of the JA Claimants and some of the ADGM Companies in “failing to
         bring claims against other wrongdoers who perpetrated the fraud”, and it referred
         specifically to them not pursuing claims against the Bin Buttis. Further, despite the
         settlement, I shall refer to other matters that Ms Windle put to Mr Fleming in cross-
         examination.

NMC plc’s proceedings against EY

165. NMC plc brought proceedings against EY in the English Commercial Court in respect of
         their role as auditors of the NMC Group’s financial statements. They were tried between
         May and October 2025, but on 29 January 2026, before judgment was given, a settlement
         was reached by agreement, whereby the claim was resolved without admission of
         liability. As I understand it, it was not suggested that the settlement was not a reasonable
         one for NMC plc and its Administrators to conclude.

UAE Exchange

166. In Mr Manghat’s Written Submissions, it was contended that there was evidence that the
         NMC Group had transferred substantial sums to UAE Exchange, and he invited the
         inference that the “failure to pursue this avenue was a deliberate decision motivated by
         political considerations and/or, more disturbingly, an undeclared conflict of interest”. He
         argued that UAE Exchange’s parent company, Finablr, having been acquired by Prism
         Group AG (now called Wizz Financial), would have had funds, or funds would have been
         available, to satisfy any claim that the NMC Group might bring.

167. The basis for the allegation about political considerations is an announcement by Finablr
         that Prism Group AG acquired it in a consortium that included “Abu Dhabi’s Royal
         Strategic Partners”. As for the allegation of a conflict of interest, Mr Hennessey put to Mr
         Fleming in his cross-examination that he (and presumably Mr Cairns) had an undeclared
         conflict of interest that influenced the JAs’ decision with regard to investigating or
         pursuing a claim against UAE Exchange. The suggestion, as I understood it, was that, after
         they were appointed as the JAs, A&M Europe acted for Prism Group AG in respect of its
         acquisition of Finablr and thereafter. Mr Fleming explained that A&M Europe has

         (unsurprisingly) robust processes to identify and prevent conflicts of interest. The
         allegation was a serious one, but it was not properly explained or developed, and I see no
         proper basis for it. I reject it.

168. I also reject the argument that it would have been reasonable for companies in the NMC
         Group or the JAs to have pursued claims against UAE Exchange. Mr Fleming stated that
         UAE Exchange was insolvent when it was acquired by Prism Group AG, and it was sold for
         GBP 1. Mr Manghat’s argument supposed that its purchaser would have met claims
         against it: there is no reason to think that it would have done so.

Neopharma and Nexgen

169. Mr Manghat’s contention about Neopharma and Nexgen was that they had received
         “substantial illegitimate payments from NMC”. He appeared to make a similar allegation
         about Nexgen. It was not clear to me from the cross-examination of Mr Fleming quite how
         these allegations are put. However, I accept the JA Claimants’ submission that
         Neopharma and Nexgen would almost certainly be unable to meet any judgment against
         them, and for this reason alone, it would not have been sensible to pursue proceedings
         against either of them. They were companies involved in what was called the “Sham
         Suppliers Scheme”, and they apparently defaulted on their debts in 2020. Indeed, on 31
         March 2020, Neopharma’s creditors were asked to agree to a “standstill” in view of the
         financial difficulties that it faced, and in July 2020, it reported to Baroda and other banks
         a loss of over AED 998 million in the 2020 financial year. Although Mr Manghat observed
         that Neopharma has apparently not filed for insolvency, there is no proper reason to
         doubt A&M Europe’s assessment that there was no realistic chance of any meaningful
         recovery from Neopharma or Nexgen.

The Bin Buttis

170. There is no dispute that the Bin Buttis are bankrupt. In July 2021, bankruptcy proceedings
         were brought against KBBAM in the ADBC. The NMC Group made a claim in the
         bankruptcy, but by February 2022 (relatively shortly before the RED for the restructuring
         of the NMC Group on 25 March 2022), it reached a settlement covering any claims against
         KBBAM, SAQ and others, and it was ratified by the ADBC.

171. Ms Windle cross-examined Mr Fleming about claims that the JA Claimants or the ADGM
         Companies might have had against the Bin Buttis, and she questioned him about
         evidence that he had given in the English proceedings against EY about the claims that
         might have been brought. Mr Fleming explained that he understood that the merits of the
         claims in UAE law were uncertain, and more generally why the settlement was
         considered to be in the best interests of creditors, not least because it enabled the
         restructuring under the DOCAs to proceed.

172. I accept Mr Fleming’s evidence that the settlement agreement “was essential for the
         orderly restructuring of the NMC Group and was in the best interests of creditors”: the
         prospects of recoveries in the bankruptcy clearly would not have justified the risk to the
         restructuring plans if no agreement was reached. I do not understand Dr Shetty or Mr
         Manghat to dispute it. Nor do I understand either of them to pursue an argument that the
         JA Claimants or any of the ADGM Companies failed to mitigate their loss by settling
         claims against the Bin Buttis as they did. If they do, I would reject it.

Claims against banks

173. On 6 January 2022, Sayacorp BSC (“Sayacorp”), a Bahraini bank which was formerly
         known as First Energy Bank BSC and the Chairman of which was at one time KBBAM,
         brought proceedings in this Court against NMCH and other companies of the NMC Group
         after the JAs had rejected a proof of debt filed by Sayacorp on the grounds of set-off
         against a claim against Sayacorp. NMCH and the JAs (who had been added as
         defendants to Sayacorp’s claim) brought a counterclaim. The trial was fixed for April
         2024, but on 26 March 2024, the proceedings were settled. I can see no basis on which it
         might be argued that the settlement was not a reasonable one for NMCH and the JAs to
         make, or that they failed to mitigate their loss in entering into it.

174. Ms Windle cross-examined Mr Fleming as to why proceedings had not been brought
         against Emirates Islamic Bank PJSC (“EIB”), through which payments had been made to
         Dr Shetty and to the Bin Buttis by the NMC Group and which had issued trust receipts
         relating to the “Sham Suppliers Scheme”. However, it was not put to Mr Fleming that the
         JA Claimants or the ADGM Companies had failed to mitigate their losses by not bringing
         proceedings, and no such argument was advanced by Dr Shetty or Mr Manghat. Again, I
         can see no reason to conclude that the JA Claimants acted unreasonably in not bringing
         proceedings against EIB.

Mr Hani Buttikhi and Mr Abdulrahman Basaddiq

175. In his witness statement, Mr Fleming said: “I am aware that the Joint Administrators’ team
         has identified amounts paid to Mr [Hani] Buttikhi and Mr [Abdulrahman] Basaddiq which
         are traceable to NMC funds and which were not disclosed in the audited annual reports”.

176. Mr Buttikhi was a director of NMC plc from 28 June 2017 to 16 February 2020 and acted
         as the Chief Investment Officer of the NMC Group during this period. He was also
         associated with Centurion. Mr Fleming explained in his evidence that the JAs knew that
         he had “a relationship with Sayacorp”, and the JA Claimants entered into a settlement
         agreement with Mr Buttikhi under which the amount payable by Mr Buttikhi would be
         reduced if a settlement was reached with Sayacorp by 29 February 2024. In the event, as
         I have said, a settlement with Sayacorp was agreed only after that date, and, in the event,
         Mr Buttikhi paid US$ 7.5 million in settlement of the claims against him.

177. Mr Basaddiq was a director of NMC plc from 24 February 2014 to 16 February 2020: he
         replaced SAQ on the board and it was said by Mr Manghat that he “represented the
         interests of the [B]in Buttis”. He is a Chartered Accountant: he had held senior positions
         with EY and was involved in the audit of the NMC Group. In March 2026, the JA Claimants,
         NMC plc and its administrators entered into an agreement with Mr Basaddiq, whereby he
         agreed to pay £ 800,000 in settlement of a dispute in which it was alleged that he had
         received AED 23,762,240 from the NMC Group, Dr Shetty, GGCM and others.

178. It was not put to Mr Fleming that the agreements with Mr Buttikhi and Mr Basaddiq were
         improper settlements for the JA Claimants to make or that they failed to mitigate their
         loss by entering into them. I would reject any such contention.

Other directors of NMC plc

179. Finally, I mention for completeness, that Ms Windle asked Mr Fleming about settlements
         with three former directors of NMC plc, including Lord Clanwilliam, under which no sums
         were paid by them, and about the JA Claimants not bringing claims against other directors
         of NMC plc, including Mr Binay Shetty. Mr Fleming explained that the JAs had limited
         resources, and it was not possible to pursue every possible avenue with limited funds. I
         see no proper basis for criticising those decisions.

180. Mr Manghat’s Written Submissions referred to payments made to Ms Nandini Tandon and
         Mr Keyur Nagori, both of whom were appointed non-executive directors in 2014. It does
         not appear to be contended that, therefore, proceedings should have been brought
         against them, and I do not understand what point is being made by Mr Manghat. If and in
         so far as it is suggested that they should have been sued, I would reject the allegation in
         view of Mr Fleming’s explanation about the limited resources available to explore all
         possible claims.

The Claimants’ case about the fraud

Before the IPO

181. Mr Manghat gave evidence, and I accept, that, before the IPO, when Dr Shetty and HE Al
         Mazrouei were the shareholders of NMCH, they had used the company’s money to make
         private investments in real estate and other personal purchases. Mr Manghat explained
         that this was not unusual in the UAE where businesses were commonly sole
         proprietorships or partnerships, although he said that, in the case of NMCH, the practice
         caused cashflow difficulties. When he was cross-examined, Dr Shetty did not dispute
         that before the IPO he “had been extracting money from the NMC Group”.

182. Mr Manghat also said that the “mindset” did not change when the business was
         transferred to a listed company. Here, Dr Shetty disagreed: he said that he had not
         extracted money “after listing. It was not my inten[tion] at all. I know what the meaning of
         listing. I can’t touch one dirham without board’s approval”.

                                                                                                                                                                      53

183. Mr Manghat was appointed to be NMCH’s CFO in February 2011 in place of Mr Joseph
         Pinto. Mr Pinto moved to work in Dr Shetty’s private office, and he did so for many years.
         As Dr Shetty testified, he was wholly trusted by Dr Shetty. However, for a while, Mr Pinto
         continued to deal with the NMC Group’s day-to-day activities, allowing Mr Manghat to
         concentrate on the IPO. He died in 2023.

184. When Mr Manghat became the CFO, Mr Krishnamoorthy reported to him, and in turn Mr
         Suresh Kumar reported to Mr Krishnamoorthy. The three men had worked together since
         2003.

185. In his written evidence, Mr Manghat said that his roles and responsibilities included
         operational matters within the financial department, liaising with banks, working to raise
         equity and restructuring the balance sheet. He was also tasked with bringing the IPO to
         the market.

186. Mr Manghat acknowledged that, in order to manage the IPO, he “had to be fully informed
         about [NMCH’s] true financial position”. He was informed of fund transfers made from
         the NMC Group to persons and companies outside the NMC Group. For example, by an
         email of 4 June 2011 headed “FW: Fund Transfers” and sent to Mr Manghat, Mr Suresh
         Kumar sent a list of transfers made in the period 2 April 2011 to 1 June 2011 to and from
         (among others) Dr Shetty, HE Al Mazrouei, Nexgen, Neopharma and DHE. Mr Manghat
         described this as “routine reporting” to him.

187. It is also apparent from the evidence that, even before the IPO, the NMC Group was
         involved in manipulating its records to disguise the true state of its finances from EY. As
         Mr Jones explained, throughout the Relevant Period, NMCH used suspense or control
         accounts, including a so-called “Advance Account”, the purpose of which was to record
         transactions that it wished to disguise. (It kept the account secret from EY, apparently,
         by reducing the balance in it to zero at each year end. According to the evidence before
         me, EY required trial balances only at each year end and did not require a moving trial
         balance for any time during the accounting period.)

188. Although in his written evidence Mr Manghat said that he had learned of the Advance
         Account “[s]ince leaving the company”, he accepted in cross-examination that around
         the time of the IPO he was aware of it (although he disputed, or at least said that he could
         not remember, whether he knew of it being used afterwards).

189. On 7 July 2011, Mr Manghat and Mr Krishnamoorthy had this email exchange: under the
         heading “Jan-June numbers – Urgent”, Mr Krishnamoorthy wrote that “E&Y has already
         taken the trail [sic] balance as well as the patient numbers as of 31st May, 2011 …
         Changing the figures for the period Jan-May will be suicidal. Better to make adjustments
         for June figures alone”. Mr Manghat replied, “No probs do that way”. (Mr Manghat
         confirmed that “trail” should read “trial” and that a “trial balance is a financial report that
         lists at all account balances from a company’s general ledger at a specific point in time”.)

         I cannot accept the explanation that Mr Manghat gave for this exchange: that it would
         have been “suicidal” to amend the figures for January to May 2011 because it would
         involve too much work and time. I conclude that Mr Krishnamoorthy sent his email
         because he was concerned that EY would be suspicious if the figures were changed, and
         that Mr Manghat so understood it.

The allegations of misappropriation

190. The JA Claimants’ case about the misappropriation of assets, and the fraudulent scheme
         more generally, is confined to the Relevant Period. Baroda observed that the pattern and
         types of payments made through accounts with it did not change materially in the three
         months before and after 2 April 2012, and the JA Claimants include transactions before 2
         April 2012 in a schedule to their Particulars of Claim that lists the transfers of funds
         between the NMC Group and Dr Shetty’s accounts that they allege. However, if there
         were earlier misappropriations or other acts of wrongdoing, there is no claim in respect
         of them, and the evidence of the JA Action Accountant Experts that is relied on in support
         of the claims in the JA Action excludes earlier dealings.

191. The JA Claimants divide the alleged misappropriations to Dr Shetty into two categories,
         the “Direct Shetty Payments” and the “Indirect Shetty Payments”. The Direct Shetty
         Payments comprise the transfer of funds to bank accounts in Dr Shetty’s name at Baroda
         and other banks, for which it is said there was no legitimate reason. They were made from
         companies in the NMC Group and New Medical Centre, which was not incorporated. The
         transfers were frequent and for large amounts: in a schedule to their Particulars of Claim
         the JA Claimants list over 500 transfers, the majority being from NMC Group accounts at
         Baroda and to three accounts at Baroda in Dr Shetty’s name, being (i) an account whose
         number ended 2359 (the “2359 account”), (ii) an account with the number ending 0090
         (the “0090 account”) and (iii) an account with a number ending 7013 (the “7013
         account”).

192. The evidence of the JA Action Accountant Experts, which was not challenged and which I
         accept, was that the total amount so transferred during the Relevant Period was AED
         4,183 million. Large amounts were also transferred from Dr Shetty’s accounts to the NMC
         Group by way of what were called at the trial “counter-payments”. Taking these into
         account, the net value of the Direct Shetty Payments was some AED 2.6 billion.

193. In their Particulars of Claim, the JA Claimants describe the Indirect Shetty Payments as
         “payments into the Shetty Private Baroda Accounts from external companies that had
         received those funds from or at the expense of the NMC Group”. They identify the
         “external companies” involved in these payments as Nexgen, GGCM and FFT, and they
         are said to have amounted in aggregate in the Relevant Period to “at least” AED 426.4
         million. More specifically, it is alleged that:

         a. Between 2013 and 2015, some AED 273 million was transferred from two accounts

Undisclosed Securities

200. The JA Claimants allege that the NMC Group provided for the benefit of Dr Shetty and the
         Bin Buttis what they call “Undisclosed Collateral Benefits”: that, between 2012 and 2020,
         the NMC Group provided “guarantees or some other form of security” (the “Undisclosed
         Securities”) for facilities with banks for Dr Shetty, KBBAM and entities associated with
         them, including Neopharma, GGCM, RCS, Modular Concepts and Centurion. Although
         these were benefits for “related parties” for the purposes of the relevant accounting
         standards and therefore should have been disclosed in the NMC Group’s consolidated
         financial statements, they were not so disclosed. The JA Claimants’ case is that “many of
         the guarantees provided were not called upon in the proof of debt process”. They do not
         say whether the other forms of security (e.g. post-dated cheques) were enforced by the
         banks, but there is no evidence that they were.

The insolvency of the NMC Group

201. The JA Claimants plead that: “The Unlawful Payments caused substantial damage to the
         balance sheet and cashflow of the NMC Group. As a result, over time the wrongdoing
         shareholders and/or managers were required to arrange substantial additional debt to
         shore up the Group’s finances. However, they hid that additional debt from the [NMC plc]
         Board and it was not disclosed in the NMC Group’s accounts, so as to conceal the
         Unlawful Payments themselves and the damage caused by them”.

202. Thus, the JA Claimants’ case is that, in order to disguise the (direct and indirect) impact
         of the misappropriations on the balance sheet and cashflow of the NMC Group, those
         involved in the fraud arranged additional debt and facilities (the “Undisclosed Debt”),
         which were not revealed to EY or the board of NMC plc and were not disclosed in their
         accounts.

203. The expert evidence of the JA Action Accountant Experts demonstrates, and it was not
         disputed by Dr Shetty or Mr Manghat, that, as a result of the Undisclosed Debt and the
         Undisclosed Securities, at all times from 31 December 2013 NMCH and the other
         companies in the NMC Group were “balance sheet insolvent”: that is to say, in the case
         of each company, the value of the company's current assets was less than the amount
         of its current liabilities, taking into account its contingent and prospective liabilities: see
         section 123(2) of the Insolvency Act 1986 (UK) and section 200(2) of the IR 2022. (The
         ADGM Companies ceased to be balance sheet insolvent as of 25 March 2022, as a result
         of the restructuring under their DOCAs.)

204. Mr Jones accepted, and the JA Claimants acknowledge, that the immediate cause of the
         insolvency was not the misappropriations themselves, but the NMC Group’s borrowings,
         including the Undisclosed Debt: that is to say, from 2013, NMCH and the other
         companies would have been balance sheet insolvent even if the net amount of the
         misappropriations were notionally restored to them. The “technical insolvency” (as Mr
         Jones put it) was the scale of the debt and the need to fund it. New Undisclosed Debt not
         only allowed misappropriations to continue, but covered interest and finance costs of
         existing debt and provided cash to cover the inflated revenue.

205. Further, it was the expert evidence of Mr Bezant that, on the insolvency of NMCH, Holding
         and the 34 ADGM Companies would have had no real prospect of avoiding insolvent
         liquidation or administration in the period from 31 December 2013 to 31 December 2018.
         This allegation is the basis of the JAs’ claim against Dr Shetty and Mr Manghat for wrongful
         trading. No claim for wrongful trading was made against Baroda, and accordingly this
         question was not considered by Mr Grantham. Mr Bezant’s evidence about this was not
         challenged by Dr Shetty or Mr Manghat, and I accept it.

The Undisclosed Debt

206. It was not significantly disputed, and I find, that, throughout the Relevant Period, the
         consolidated financial statements of the NMC Group did not properly account for all of
         its borrowings. Nor was it significantly disputed, and I find, that the amount of both the
         total borrowing of the NMC Group and the Undisclosed Debt increased annually from
         2012 until 30 June 2019 (the date of the interim accounts of NMC plc). Thus:

         a. As of 31 December 2012, the total reported debt was AED 1,115.3 million (or some
                  US$ 303 million), and the total unreported debt that has been identified was AED
                  893.1 million (or some US$ 243 million), the total debt therefore being AED 2,008.4
                  million (or some US$ 546 million).

         b. As of 31 December 2013, the total reported debt was AED 1,221 million (or some
                  US$ 332 million), and the total identified unreported debt was AED 1,640.1 million
                  (or some US$ 446 million), the total debt therefore being AED 2,861.1 million (or
                  some US$ 779 million).

         c. As of 31 December 2014, the total reported debt was AED 1,381.5 million (or some
                  US$ 376 million), and the total identified unreported debt was AED 3,251.2 million
                  (or some US$ 885 million), the total debt therefore being AED 4,632.7 million (or
                  US$ 1,261 million).

         d. As of 31 December 2015, the total reported debt was AED 2,682.4 million (or some
                  US$ 730 million), and the total identified unreported debt was AED 5,454.5 million
                  (or some US$ 1,485 million), the total debt therefore being AED 8,136.9 million (or
                  some US$ 2,215 million).

         e. As of 31 December 2016, the total reported debt was AED 3,853.5 million (or some
                  US$ 1,049 million), and the total identified unreported debt was AED 7,530.1
                  million (or some US$ 2,050 million), the total debt therefore being AED 11,383.6
                  million (or some US$ 3,099 million).

         f. As of 31 December 2017, the total reported debt was AED 5,138.6 million (or some
                  US$ 1,399 million), and the total identified unreported debt was AED 9,922.5
                  million (or US$ 2,701 million), the total debt therefore being AED 15,061.1 million
                  (or some US$ 4,100 million).

         g. As of 31 December 2018, the total reported debt was AED 7,319.2 million (or some
                  US$ 1,993 million), the total identified unreported debt was AED 14,283.5 million
                  (or some US$ 3,888 million), the total debt (excluding bonds and sukuk, which I
                  can disregard for present purposes) being AED 21,602.7 million (or some US$
                  5,881 million).

         h. As of 30 June 2019, the total reported debt in the interim accounts was AED 7,695.7

                  million (or some US$ 2,095 million), and the total reported debt was AED 15,025.4

                  million (or some US$ 6,168 million).

207. These increasing levels of Undisclosed Debt reflect both new loans and other facilities
         arranged during the financial year, and the interest and other servicing charges on
         Undisclosed Debt from previous years.

208. The JA Claimants divide the Undisclosed Debt into three categories: (i) term loan
         financing (including syndicated loans); (ii) short-term borrowing in the form of working
         capital loans and/or supplier advances, which involved the arrangements with the so-
         called “Sham Suppliers” that I explain at paras. 216 – 220 below; and (iii) other forms of
         short-term debt financing by way of overdrafts, sales invoice factoring facilities, cheque
         discounting facilities and salary loans.

209. The Undisclosed Debt was generally recorded in internal ledger records of the NMC
         Group (in particular, in the Advance Account or in a specific ledger account pending
         transfer to the Advance Account at the end of accounting periods). It was thus disguised
         through manipulation of the underlying accounting ledgers, and thereby it was concealed
         from EY.

210. Those involved with the fraud were able to conceal the Undisclosed Debt in this way
         because the NMC Group did not have an adequate accounting system. Mr Manghat said
         in his witness statement and confirmed in cross-examination that its “balance sheet was
         essentially kept as a very complicated Excel spreadsheet” and it did not have a “proper
         IT environment”. EY, he said, “never reviewed the company’s general ledger and
         computer accounting systems to complete the auditing process”. Towards the end of the
         Relevant Period, after some years of pressure from EY, the NMC Group began to use a
         consolidated software system, apparently from about mid-2019, but the fraudsters
         continued to conceal the fraud by maintaining two sets of books.

211. Obviously, a sophisticated fraudulent scheme of this kind required those involved to
         track carefully the NMC Group’s true financial position. The evidence shows that they did
         so, and three spreadsheet documents illustrate this.

         a. First, on 20 August 2013, Mr Praveen Kumar sent to Mr Suresh Kumar, with copies
                   to Mr Manghat and Mr Krishnamoorthy, a spreadsheet that set out the NMC
                   Group’s banking position as at three dates: December 2012, March 2013 and
                   June 2013. The spreadsheet included both (i) facilities that were disclosed to the
                   NMC plc board and were to be disclosed to its auditors, and (ii) undisclosed
                   facilities: thus, the position as at March 2013 included an overdraft facility and a
                   trust receipt facility with Al Masraf Bank which was not included in an otherwise
                   similar spreadsheet used in a presentation by Mr Manghat to the NMC plc board.

         b. Secondly, on 25 November 2015, Mr Udayakumar Nair, who worked in the
                   Finance Department, sent Mr Krishnamoorthy and Mr Suresh Kumar a
                   spreadsheet headed “EXPOSURE – 25-11-15”, which listed 50 banking facilities
                   and recorded that the NMC Group’s exposure on them was some AED 8.24

         c. Similarly, on 8 August 2016, Mr Suresh Kumar forwarded to Mr Manghat an email
                   and spreadsheet that listed 69 bank facilities, showing outstanding bank lending
                   of some AED 10.4 billion as at 31 July 2016. The reported figures for 30 June 2016
                   showed outstanding bank lending of some US$ 790 million (or some AED 2.9
                   billion).

212. It is clear from the nature of the fraud, and in particular from the accounting systems
         devised to disguise it from the board and the auditors, that it required all the relatively
         senior executives in NMCH’s Treasury or Finance Department to participate in it. This is
         well illustrated by documents relating to the arrangements made for maintaining two sets
         of books when the NMC Group was finally succumbed to EY’s pressure to upgrade its
         electronic systems. On 11 March 2019, Mr Sreekanth Nair, an executive in the Treasury
         Department, sent Mr Udayakumar Nair an email with the minutes of a meeting of seven
         executives held that day on the topic of “[p]ractical difficulties in the maintaining of two
         sets of books”. They recorded that the group was to meet again on 18 March 2019 “when
         Mr Vyshak [Harindranath, an Associate Vice President for Corporate Accounting and
         Reporting] and Mr Praveen [Kumar] is back to office”.

213. The operation of the scheme was therefore threatened when, on 17 March 2012, Mr
         Tompkins on behalf of the independent directors of NMC plc sent an email to Dr Shetty
         and KBBAM, making a series of governance recommendations, including the
         appointment of a Deputy CFO in Abu Dhabi. Mr Tompkins explained that the
         recommendation came from EY, who had proposed that NMC plc “strengthen the finance
         team under the leadership of [Mr Manghat] through the appointment of an experienced
         Footsie 250 Plc deputy Chief Financial Officer”. Mr Manghat opposed the proposal: he
         wrote to Dr Shetty, KBBAM and others that “I would like to reiterate that self is qualified
         Chartered Accountant from India, England and Wales. Also certified in international
         accounting from UK for last 9 years. For support I welcome, but for this my suggestion is
         we should recruit someone from our region and the best from existing and new recruits
         should be groomed for succession. This to me is the best solution to our organisation
         considering cultural challenges and other issues which we know”.

214. This response does not fit well with Mr Manghat’s evidence at the trial, when he
         minimised his qualifications and experience when he became NMC plc’s CFO: he said
         that he was an Indian Chartered Accountant, and “I don’t have a competent knowledge
         of IFRS [International Financial Reporting Standards] and UK reporting standards”. He

         maintained that position although, as the CFO, he was responsible for reviewing the

215. Mr Manghat was not able satisfactorily to explain why, in these circumstances, he was
         unwilling to accept the suggestion that a suitably experienced accountant be appointed
         to be his deputy CFO. I reject his answer that he was too optimistic about his own
         abilities. I accept Mr Pillai’s submission that Mr Manghat did not want someone recruited
         from outside to discover how the accounting systems were arranged so that the NMC
         Group’s true financial position could be kept disguised.

The Sham Suppliers transactions

216. I have referred to the undisclosed short-term borrowing raised from arrangements with
         Sham Suppliers. This scheme generated increasing amounts by way of short-term
         borrowings throughout the Relevant Period. They amounted to US$ 985,455 million as of
         30 June 2019. I heard detailed evidence about the operation of the scheme, and
         examined individual transactions relating to it, but its main relevance, as I see it, went to
         the JA Claimants’ case against Baroda. I do not need to examine much of it to determine
         the claims against Dr Shetty and Mr Manghat.

217. In summary, the scheme operated in this way:

         a. A third-party company (a Sham Supplier) would issue an invoice to a company in
                   the NMC Group for the supply of goods and services. It was a sham in that it was
                   never intended that there should be any genuine supply.

         b. The NMC Group would arrange for a third-party financial institution (often referred
                   to as the trust receipt bank) to pay the invoice to the Sham Suppliers pursuant to
                   a short-term lending arrangement, typically for 90, 120 or 180 days.

         c. The Sham Suppliers would transfer the funds received to the NMC Group.

         d. When repayment of the short-term lending fell due, the NMC Group would
                   discharge the Sham Supplier’s obligation, together with any interest or other
                   charges.

218. The JA Action Accountant Experts agreed that the Sham Suppliers scheme assisted in
         concealing the NMC Group’s true debt position. The extent of Sham Suppliers activity and
         the funding it provided to the NMC Group was, as Mr Bezant put it, “significant”. Mr
         Bezant also explained that the Sham Suppliers scheme enabled the NMC Group to obtain
         injections of funds and to strengthen its short-to-medium cashflow, without entering into
         formal facility agreements. It thus enabled the NMC Group to operate without taking on
         additional debt for accounting purposes and without disclosing its true financial position.

219. The Sham Suppliers transactions operated and were accounted for differently from
         genuine purchases by the NMC Group in that, when the NMC Group bought genuine
         supplies, (i) the initial supply of goods or services and the corresponding invoice were
         accounted for in the general ledger of the relevant NMC entity, and (ii) the supplier
         retained the funds received for them; and, on the other hand, in the case of the Sham
         Suppliers arrangements, (i) the relevant NMC Group company did not enter the initial
         supply of goods or services in its general ledger to record the purchase as an expense or
         the invoice as a liability, and (ii) the funds received by the Sham Suppliers from the
         financial institution against their invoices were remitted to the NMC Group. The Sham
         Suppliers involved in this scheme included companies in which Dr Shetty had an interest,
         such as Neopharma and Nexgen, which Mr Jones described as “consistently the largest
         supplier, by total value, used for sham supplies in each of those years” from 2012 to 2019,
         and companies that were independent of Dr Shetty and Mr Manghat.

220. Between 2012 and 2019, the use of Sham Suppliers arrangements increased both in
         terms of the number of transactions and the amounts involved, and from 2016 or 2017 it
         became more complex in that the funds from the financial institutions were remitted
         back to the NMC Group through intermediate companies. Mr Jones’ evidence, which was
         not disputed, showed that in 2012 a total of AED 0.2 billion was advanced to the NMC
         Group, using four Sham Suppliers who could be identified; in 2013, a total of AED 1.1
         billion was advanced, using eight identified Sham Suppliers; in 2014, a total of AED 3.1
         billion was advanced, using eight identified Sham Suppliers; in 2015, a total of AED 4.7
         billion was advanced, using fourteen identified Sham Suppliers; in 2016, a total of AED
         6.7 billion was advanced, using 26 identified Sham Suppliers; in 2017, a total of AED 8.8
         billion was advanced, using 30 identified Sham Suppliers; in 2018, a total of AED 8.5
         billion was advanced, using 26 identified Sham Suppliers; and in 2019, a total of AED 10.1
         billion was advanced, using 26 identified Sham Suppliers.

Related Party Transactions

221. The last aspect of the fraud that is pleaded against Dr Shetty is that, between 2012 and
         2018, there were transactions with related parties for which there was “no obvious
         commercial basis”. It is alleged that there were such transactions between the NMC
         Group and Neopharma to the value of at least AED 7.1 billion; between the NMC Group
         and UAE Exchange to the value of at least AED 2.5 billion; between the NMC Group and
         Nexgen to the value of at least AED 18.9 billion; and a “substantial volume” of
         transactions between the NMC Group and each of GGCM, Modular Concepts, DAN and
         DHE.

222. The JA Claimants’ case is that these transactions greatly exceeded the related party
         transactions reported in the NMC Group’s annual accounts. In particular, the related
         party transactions processed through the NMC Group’s Baroda accounts alone greatly
         exceeded the reported related party transactions. Their effect was to give an exaggerated
         picture of the NMC Group’s commercial activity and cash flow.

Offset deposits

223. I also mention for completeness the NMC Group’s “Offset Deposits” arrangements
         which were used to disguise its true finances and to misrepresent them in its financial
         statements. They were pleaded in support of the JA Claimants’ case against Baroda, and
         not against Dr Shetty or Mr Manghat.

224. From 2012, the NMC Group would place term deposits with Baroda, and Baroda would
         grant it overdraft facilities of an equivalent, or almost equivalent, amount. The rate of
         interest paid on the deposits was lower than that paid for use of the overdraft facilities.

225. It is clear, and was confirmed by Mr Saul and Mr Tatham, that, as they put it in their joint
         report, “Offset Deposits were a legitimate and recognised banking product in the UAE
         during the Relevant Period and there were circumstances in which a corporate customer
         might use such a product for legitimate commercial purposes”. For example, such
         facilities can assist a business to manage erratic or unpredicted cashflow demands and
         to ensure that cheques will be honoured.

226. However, as the JA Claimants allege, and I accept, the NMC Group used the offset
         deposits in a manner that could have had no proper commercial purpose in that (as it is
         pleaded) “the NMC Group was depositing money with Baroda for a fixed period (via the
         term deposits) for a relatively low rate of interest, then immediately borrowing that money
         back from Baroda (via the overdrafts) at a relatively high rate of interest and for the entire
         duration of the fixed term deposit, and then renewing these arrangements”. These
         allegations are confirmed by the evidence of the JA Action Accountant Experts.

227. The JA Action Accountant Experts also agree that, for the most part (and with minor
         exceptions), the term deposits associated with the offset deposits were reflected in the
         NMC Group’s annual financial statements between 31 December 2012 and 31 December
         2018, but the corresponding overdrafts were not reported, being either not recorded in
         the corporate general ledger; or being recorded as a credit entry to a suspense account
         and so helping to conceal or suppress the balance on the account; or being recorded as
         a credit entry to an appropriate overdraft liability.

228. Thus, the JA Claimants allege, and I accept, that: “The functional purpose of the Offset
         Deposits was to allow the NMC Group to declare the term deposits as assets in its
         consolidated financial statements, whilst not declaring the offsetting overdrafts. In this
         way, the value of the NMC Group’s apparently available assets was inflated, and the
         extent of its borrowing was concealed”.

ADCB’s “key features” of the fraud

229. In his closing submissions, Mr Pillai emphasised three features of the fraud. They were, I
         think, uncontroversial and might appear obvious, but they are worth identifying and
         emphasising.

230. First, the fraud, which lasted from at least 2012 to 2019, was carried out on a massive
         scale. The Undisclosed Debt amounted to over AED 22.721 billion by June 2019. The
         concealed transactions in the Advance Account included some 20,000 postings with a
         debit value of over AED 100 billion and a credit value of over AED 87 billion.

231. Secondly, the fraud required discipline, organisation and meticulous planning. In order
         to conceal the true state of the NMC Group’s finances from EY, the fraudsters went to
         great trouble manually to reconcile the fake accounts to the ledger data provided to EY.

232. Thirdly, disciplined and time-consuming work was also required to keep track of the NMC
         Group’s true cash position over the many accounts, including the Sham Suppliers
         accounts, and the true debt position across its many short-term facilities.

Assignments

The DOCAs

233. The JA Claimants plead that, by the Related Party DOCAs and the associated

                   assigned to NMCH the Insolvency Claims of the ADGM Companies.

234. Clause 9 of the Related Party DOCAs provided as follows:

                “(a) In accordance with and at the time specified in the Restructuring
                Implementation Deed, the Deed Company absolutely and unconditionally

                Litigation Recoveries) of the [NMCH] DOCA (including the Litigation

                 “all actual and prospective litigation and/or arbitration claims (whether
                 commenced or contingent) of the Deed Company, including civil law claims
                 and statutory insolvency claims assigned pursuant to section 253A of the
                 Regulations and any information or evidence held by the Deed Company in
                 relation to the same, but does not include:

The governing laws

239. The general rule about the law that governs the validity of the assignments is stated in
         Dicey, Morris & Collins on the Conflict of Laws (16th Ed, 2022) (“Dicey”) at para. 25R-057:

              “RULE 143 - (1) As a general rule:

              (a) the mutual obligations of assignor and assignee under a voluntary
              assignment of a right against another person (‘the debtor’) are governed by the
              law which applies to the contract between the assignor and assignee; and

              (b) the law governing the right to which the assignment relates determines its
              assignability, the relationship between the assignee and the debtor, the
              conditions under which the assignment can be invoked against the debtor and
              any question whether the debtor’s obligations have been discharged.

               (2) But in other cases (semble), the validity and effect of an assignment of an
              intangible may be governed by the law with which the right assigned has its most
              significant connection”.

240. I observed during the hearing that Dicey’s commentary on Rule 143 says (loc. cit. at para.
         25-072) that there are cases in which it might be inappropriate to apply the general rule
         and that: “The validity of the assignment of a bare right of action may be governed by the
         lex fori”, citing Trendtex Trading Corp v Credit Suisse [1982] AC 679. Nevertheless, neither
         the JA Claimants nor any other party disputed that the general proposition in Rule 143(1)
         applies in this case. In these circumstances, I shall assume (without deciding) that it
         does, and therefore I shall assume that (as Baroda contended and Dr Shetty, who
         adopted Baroda’s argument, contends) the question whether the Civil Claims are
         assignable is governed by UAE law.

The Assignments of the Insolvency Claims

241. Mr Manghat admitted the assignments in his defence. Dr Shetty’s defence and Baroda’s
         defence denied the assignment of both the Insolvency Claims and the Civil Claims.

242. However, at the trial, no party advanced an argument that the Insolvency Claims were
         validly not assigned to NMCH, and I hold that they were. At the relevant time, the IR 2015
         (as amended from 1 April 2021 by the Insolvency (Amendment No. 1) Regulations 2021)
         empowered an “office-holder” (defined to include an administrator) to assign a right of
         action for fraudulent trading or wrongful trading. Further, the ADGM Real Property
         Regulations 2015 permit the legal assignment of a “thing in action”, with the assignment
         taking effect from the time that written notice is given to the debtor or trustee or other
         person from whom the assignor would have been entitled to claim: see section 152.
         Notice was given to the Defendants by service of the Claim Form and Particulars of Claim

         on 20 December 2022 (if not before then). Even without notice, ADGM law would
         recognise an equitable assignment of the Insolvency Claims.

The Assignments of the Civil Claims

243. The issue at trial between the JA Claimants and Dr Shetty (and between the JA Claimants
         and Baroda before the settlement) was whether the Civil Claims were validly assigned.
         The Civil Claims are governed by UAE law, and therefore I determine their assignability by
         reference to UAE law. The JA Claimants contended that the Civil Claims were assignable
         under UAE law, and so that they were validly assigned. I was much assisted by Mr Al
         Hashimi’s arguments in support of this contention in the JA Claimants’ closing
         submissions. Dr Shetty advanced no submissions of his own about UAE law (either on
         the assignment issue or on any other matter), but Mr Wandowicz said in his closing
         submissions that he adopted Baroda’s argument. In its written opening submissions,
         Baroda advanced an argument that the Civil Claims were not assignable under UAE law,
         and therefore they were not validly assigned. It did not develop this submission in its
         written closing submissions, and, because of the settlement, Baroda did not make oral
         closing submissions.

244. The JA Claimants’ submission is that, while the Civil Code does not make express
         provision for the assignments of rights, the UAE Courts, including the Federal Supreme
         Court, have consistently recognised assignments of rights, and have not required any
         specific formalities for a valid assignment of them. Indeed, it was common ground
         between the JA Claimants and Baroda that the school of Imam Malek (the Maliki school)
         recognises the assignment of rights. The JA Claimants cited an article of Mr Nicholas H D
         Foster, “An Unstoppable Force Meets a Moveable Object? Assignment of Rights in the
         UAE” in Arab Law Quarterly, 2004, Vol. 19 at p.179, in which the author observes that the
         authoritative Egyptian jurist, Dr Abdul Razzak Ahmed El-Sanhouri “writes that it is wrong
         to say that Islamic law did not allow the transfer of a right, basing his argument on the fact
         that the Malikis allowed it”.

245. Baroda accepted that, under UAE law, in principle rights are assignable, “unless
         otherwise provided by law, agreement, or the nature of the obligation”, but it submitted
         that the Civil Claims, being bare rights to litigate, are not assignable. Baroda did not cite
         authority for its distinction between rights to litigate and the underlying substantive right.
         There are exceptions to the general rule that rights are assignable, but rights to litigate are
         not within the recognised excepted categories: Dr El-Sanhouri explains in his Al Waseet
         that these exceptions are rights closely tied to the person of the creditor, such as a right
         to alimony or compensation for moral harm that has not been determined by agreement
         or claimed before the courts; and he provides other examples, including the right of a
         partner under a partnership agreement or the right of a tenant in a lease where the
         personality of the creditor is to be taken into account. Baroda argued that the Hanafi
         school prohibits “the sale or transfer of abstract rights, including lawsuits”, but this
         proposition was disputed by Mr Al Hashimi. Moreover, the Civil Code requires that regard

         be had to the Maliki and Hanbali schools in preference to the Hanafi and Shafi’i schools:
         see article 1 of the Civil Code.

246. I am persuaded by Mr Al Hashimi’s submission that the distinction that Baroda sought to
         draw between rights to litigate and other rights is not supported by, and indeed
         inconsistent with, judgments of the UAE Courts. However that might be, the JA Claimants
         contend, and I accept, that the ADGM Companies validly assigned the underlying rights
         to NMCH, along with the right to enforce them.

247. I need refer to only two of the authorities cited by Mr Al Hashimi to justify his view that the
         Civil Claims were assignable under UAE law. Baroda’s argument was that the UAE
         authorities that regard rights as assignable are concerned only with assignment of debts
         or contractual obligations. A decision of the Federal Supreme Court of 10 October 1999
         in case 127/1999 refutes this. In this case, the claimant bank had sought a ruling “to
         confirm the validity and enforceability of the assignment of rights issued by the Company
         in favor of the Bank, which included its assignment to the Bank of all payments and
         compensation due to it from [a] Ministry for the nursing school and dorms project,
         amounting to 7,124,685 dirhams”. That claim was upheld. It is clear from the judgment
         that the Court was not concerned only with the assignment of debts: it consistently refers
         to the assignment of rights (“rights to future payments and compensation from the
         Ministry for the project”, “transfer of compensation and payments”).

248. The second case to which I refer is the decision of the Dubai Court of Cassation of 26
         March 2006 in case 330/2005. It was cited by Mr Al Hashimi to refute a suggestion by
         Baroda that authorities upon which he relied were concerned with subrogation, rather
         than with assignment. The claimant was the insurer of the Dubai Electricity and Water
         Authority, and the defendants were a company engaged to transport machinery of the
         Authority and an employee of the company. The machinery was damaged in transit, and
         the insurers paid for repairs. The insurers brought proceedings against the Defendants,
         but the claim was dismissed on the basis that the insurers lacked standing to bring it.
         Their appeal to the Court of Cassation succeeded. Referring to the insurers’ complaint
         about the judgment against them, the Court said: “This objection is valid, as the transfer
         of rights allows a creditor to transfer their rights against the debtor to a third party, who
         then becomes the creditor in place of the original one. This principle applies to all rights
         unless otherwise stipulated in by law, agreement or the nature of the obligation. It is
         established by this Court that the transfer of a right is permissible, provided that no legal
         prohibitions or obstacles prevent it. The transfer of rights is formed through mutual
         consent between the transferor and the transferee, independent of the need for the
         debtor’s consent or acceptance. However, the fact that the transfer of rights is formed is
         inadequate for enforcing it against the debtor or in favor of any third party against whom
         the transferee acquired the transferred right from the transferor, except from the date that
         such creditor or third party become aware of the transfer, considering it occurred without
         their consent, and it is plausible that they are unaware of this transfer, leading them to
         treat their original creditor as their sole creditor. Therefore, the debtor should know about

249. I conclude that the UAE Courts would consider the ADGM Companies’ rights relating to
         the Civil Claims to be assignable, and that they would uphold the validity of the
         assignments. I therefore conclude that the rights of the ADGM Companies in the Civil
         Claims and of their Joint Administrators in the Insolvency Claims were validly assigned to

The JA Claimants’ Insolvency Claims: the law

The legislation

250. As I have said, the Insolvency Claims against Dr Shetty and Mr Manghat are claims under
         the IR 2022 for fraudulent trading and wrongful trading. The Claimants allege that Dr
         Shetty and Mr Manghat are liable in respect of fraudulent trading from April 2012, and for
         wrongful trading from “at least” 31 December 2013.

251. The ADGM provisions about fraudulent trading at section 251 of IR 2022 are similar to
         those in the Insolvency Act 1986 (UK) (at section 213 about fraudulent trading in the
         context of winding up, and at section 246ZA about fraudulent trading in the context of
         administration), and those about wrongful trading at section 252 of IR 2022 are also
         similar to those in the Insolvency Act 1986 (UK) (at section 214 about wrongful trading in
         the context of winding up, and at section 246ZB about wrongful trading in the context of
         administration).The English authorities about fraudulent and wrongful trading are
         therefore relevant to the Insolvency Claims under ADGM law.

The fraudulent trading claims

252. Section 251 of the IR 2022 provides as follows:

                “(1) If in the course of the winding-up of a Company or while it is in
                administration it appears that any business of the Company has been carried
                on with intent to defraud creditors of the Company or creditors of any other
                person, or for any fraudulent purpose, subsection (2) applies.

                (2) The Court, on the application of the liquidator or the administrator, as the
                case may be, may declare that any persons who were knowingly parties to the
                carrying on of the business in the manner mentioned are liable to make such
                contributions (if any) to the Company’s assets as the Court thinks proper”.

253. Accordingly, in order to establish a claim for fraudulent trading against a defendant, an
         administrator of a company must prove that:

         a. The business of the company was carried on fraudulently, in the sense that it was

COURT OF FIRST INSTANCE JUDGMENT
ADGMCFI-2022-111 – ABU DHABI COMMERCIAL BANK PJSC V PRASANTH MANGHAT
ADGMCFI-2020-020 - IN THE MATTER OF NMC HEALTHCARE LIMITED (IN ADMINISTRATION) (SUBJECT TO A DEED OF COMPANY ARRANGEMENT) AND THE COMPANIES
LISTED IN SCHEDULE 1 TO THE ADMINISTRATION APPLICATION
ADGMCFI-2022-299 – NMC HEALTHCARE LIMITED (IN ADMINISTRATION) (SUBJECT TO A DEED OF COMPANY ARRANGEMENT) AND OTHERS V BAVAGUTHU RAGHURAM
SHETTY AND OTHERS

                                                                                                                                                                      70

                                                                                       05 November 2026 04:43 PM

                   carried on with the intent to defraud its creditors or those of another person or
                   was carried on for a fraudulent purpose.

         b. The defendant was party to the business being carried on fraudulently (in this
                   sense).

         c. The defendant so acted knowingly.

         d. The defendant acted dishonestly.

         e. The Court should exercise its (discretionary) power to hold the defendant liable
                   to make a contribution to the company’s assets.

         I shall consider the fifth matter later in my Judgment when I come to questions about the
         appropriate remedy if the case in fraudulent trading is established. It is convenient to say
         something about the other requirements here.

The carrying on of a business

254. The Courts take a wide view about what constitutes carrying on a business fraudulently.
         As Miles J said in London Capital & Finance Plc v Thomson [2024] EWHC 2894 (Ch), “the
         case-law does not seek to define or delimit the concept of fraud” (at para. 1537). He
         illustrated this by reference to the well-known dictum of Maugham J in Re William C
         Leitch Bros Ltd [1932] 2 Ch 71: “[I]f a company continues to carry on business and to incur
         debts at a time when there is to the knowledge of the directors no reasonable prospect of
         the creditors ever receiving payment of those debts, it is, in general, a proper inference
         that the company is carrying on business with intent to defraud” (at para. 1539). Miles J
         also cited English criminal cases, which show that it need not be intended that third
         parties should suffer loss, saying this (at para. 1540):

                “In R v Grantham [1984] QB 675, the Court of Appeal dismissed an appeal
                against a direction given to a jury that there could be an intent to defraud if
                someone ‘obtains or helps to obtain credit or further credit when he knows
                there is no good reason for thinking funds will become available to pay the debt
                when it becomes due or shortly thereafter’. At p. 683, Lord Lane CJ said: ‘If
                further authority is required that such actions may amount to an intent to
                defraud, it is to be found in Reg. v. Sinclair [1968] 1 WLR 1246. In that case the
                trial judge had directed the jury, at p. 1249: ‘It is fraud if it is proved that there
                was the taking of a risk which there was no right to take which would cause
                detriment or prejudice to another ... you have to be sure that it was deliberate
                dishonesty ...’. The Court of Appeal (Criminal Division) approved that direction.
                In Reg. v. Allsop (1976) 64 Cr.App.R. 29, the Court of Appeal (Criminal Division)
                held that a person is guilty of fraud if he intends by deceit to induce a course of
                conduct in another which puts that other's economic interests in jeopardy,

                even though he does not intend that actual loss should ultimately be suffered
                by that other’”.

255. However, notwithstanding this generous application of the fraudulent trading legislation,
         the business must have been ‘carried on’ fraudulently: it is not enough that there was
         some incidental dishonesty on the part of the company while conducting its business. In
         Re Murray-Watson Ltd (unreported), 6 April 1977, Oliver J illustrated this point by an
         example of a director of a second-hand car dealership lying to a customer about the age
         of a car, which he considered insufficient to establish that the business was being carried
         on fraudulently. But this does not mean that a company cannot be trading fraudulently
         simply because it is conducting a legitimate business. As Lewison LJ put it in Tradition
         Financial Services Ltd v Bilta (UK) Ltd [2023] EWCA Civ 112: “What must be established
         is that the company’s business (or a discrete part of that business), taken as a whole, had
         been carried on fraudulently”. He gave this example (at para. 93): “Suppose that a
         manufacturer regularly supplies counterfeit designer clothes to a retailing company,
         knowing that the retailer will pass them off as genuine. It is, in my judgment, no misuse of
         language to describe the manufacturer as ‘party to the carrying on’ of a fraudulent
         business, even though he exercises no managerial or controlling role within the retailing
         company; and the manufacturer may have other business activities that are not
         fraudulent”. This was endorsed by the UK Supreme Court: see [2025] UKSC 18 at para.
         36: see para. 258 below.

256. This leads to the question what business was being carried on by the relevant companies:
         by NMCH, by Holding and by the ADGM Companies (who assigned their Insolvency
         Claims to NMCH). Before and during the trial, the parties did not distinguish between the
         business carried on by different companies, but they referred to the fraudulent conduct
         of the business of the “NMC Group” or the “NMC Group companies”. The JA Claimants
         pleaded the fraudulent trading claims against Dr Shetty and Mr Manghat in these terms:
         “… in the period from April 2012 to February 2020, the business of the NMC Group, and
         specifically that of [NMCH, Holding] and the ADGM Companies, was carried on with the
         intent to defraud creditors of the NMC Group, and specifically of [NMCH, Holding] and
         the ADGM Companies, and/or for the fraudulent purposes set out above …”. The claim
         against Baroda was in similar terms, except that it referred to the “Baroda Customers”,
         (sc. to those ADGM Companies that had an account with Baroda) rather than to all the
         ADGM Companies. None of the Defendants suggested that the business of the different
         companies should be distinguished for the purpose of assessing the fraudulent trading
         claims, and all (including Baroda in its written closing submissions) defended the
         fraudulent trading claims on the basis that they were not knowingly party to any
         fraudulent conduct on the part of any company.

257. When they were presenting their closing oral submissions, I asked Counsel for the JA
         Claimants whether the proper approach to the fraudulent trading claims was to consider
         the business that each company was carrying on, or whether it was proper to consider
         the position of the activity of the NMC Group, regarding each company (as I put it) as “a

                                                                                                                                                                      72

                                                                                       05 November 2026 04:43 PM

         cog in the Group”. Specifically, I asked about whether it could be said that an
         intermediate holding company (such as Holding) could be said to be “carrying on” a
         business, and if so what business. The JA Claimants responded that what matters is the
         business carried on by the individual company, and I agree that that is the proper
         approach. It is supported by the decision of Hoffmann J in Re Augustus Barnett & Son
         Ltd (1986) 2 BCC 98904.

258. In that case, the company, which was a subsidiary of Rumasa SA (“Rumasa”), was able
         to continue to trade as a going concern only because Rumasa had given letters of comfort
         which were noted in the company's accounts and had made statements of continued
         support. After the company went into liquidation, the liquidators commenced
         proceedings against Rumasa under section 332 of the Companies Act 1948 (UK) (the
         materially similar predecessor of section 213 of the Insolvency Act 1986 (UK)) on the
         basis that Rumasa had induced the board of the company to continue to trade. Rumasa
         successfully applied for the claim to be struck out on the ground that it disclosed no
         reasonable cause of action. Hoffmann J held that the claim failed because (i) there was
         no allegation of fraud against those who actually carried on the company’s business; and
         (ii) in any event there was no basis for inferring fraud on Rumasa's part. In relation to the
         first ground, he stated (at pp.173-174): “The only point with which I am concerned is
         whether section 332 can form the basis for imposing liability on a parent company
         otherwise than as accessory to fraudulent trading by the persons who actually carried on
         the business of the subsidiary. In my judgment it cannot. The language of the section is
         clear and unambiguous”. In Bilta (UK) Ltd (in liquidation) v Tradition Financial Services Ltd
         [2025] UKSC 18 (at para. 45), the Supreme Court endorsed this, subject to a linguistic
         observation that “we would not speak of accessory liability but rather of liability arising
         from participation in the fraudulent acts of those carrying on the business of the
         company”.

259. This is consistent with the case of Tendcare Medical Group Holdings Pte & Ors Ltd v Gong
         Ruizhong & Ors [2021] SGHC 80, to which the JA Claimants drew my attention. In that
         case, a successful fraudulent trading claim was brought against a holding company in
         the Courts of Singapore under legislation in materially similar (but not identical) terms to
         the English and ADGM provisions. However, there the holding company received funds
         from investors and the claim concerned misappropriation of the funds. The holding
         company had taken an active role in raising funds, and so “carried on” a business. As
         Kannan Ramesh J put it (at para. 52), the “relevant fraudulent business was raising …
         funds”.

260. In this case, NMCH was not merely an intermediate holding company, holding shares in
         the ADGM Companies and other operating companies in the NMC Group. Throughout the
         Relevant Period, it had an active role managing the finances of the whole of the NMC
         Group, including those of the operating companies, and it is not really in dispute that it
         managed them fraudulently. As far as NMCH is concerned, that is the “relevant
         fraudulent business”.

261. The JAs’ case about Holding is less clear. The only pleaded contention against Dr Shetty
         and Mr Manghat is that payments were made from Holding’s account with Baroda to Dr
         Shetty in July 2019 to February 2020 and to Mr Manghat in October 2019. There is no
         pleaded basis for a fraudulent trading claim by Holding before July 2019. As against Mr
         Manghat, the JA Claimants submitted in their submissions in reply that “Holding’s
         relevant business was its holding and group-finance support function”, but it is quite
         unclear to me what its “group-finance support” function is said to have been.

262. None of this, it appears, is of practical importance: the JA Claimants acknowledged that
         Holding’s Insolvency Claims add nothing to those of NMCH. Further, the JA Claimants’
         arguments about this did not emerge until the JA Claimants’ closing reply submissions.
         In these circumstances, I do not propose to determine these claims of Holding in this
         Judgment. I shall allow the parties to make further submissions about it, if so advised,
         when I have issued this Judgment.

263. What of the ADGM Companies, whose claims were assigned to NMCH? Although the
         case about each of the 34 ADGM Companies was not pleaded or argued separately, it is
         clear from the evidence that, while they were carrying on their various businesses as
         operating subsidiaries of the NMC Group, these companies used NMCH to manage and
         conduct their financial affairs. I also conclude, and neither Dr Shetty nor Mr Manghat
         disputed, that: (i) NMCH managed and conducted their financial affairs as part of, and in
         the course of, the fraud that affected the management of the whole of the NMC Group;
         and (ii) the operations of all 34 ADGM Companies were financed by the fraud. I conclude
         that their business, as well as that of NMCH, was carried on fraudulently.

Being “party” to the carrying on of a business

264. In its decision in the Bilta (UK) Ltd case, the UK Supreme Court decided that, on the proper
         interpretation of section 213 of the Insolvency Act 1986 (UK), it is not only those who
         control or manage a business who may be required to make contributions to a company's
         assets because they were knowingly parties to the company's fraudulent trading. The
         Supreme Court accepted, however, that there are some limitations on the circumstances
         in which liability might be incurred, as Lord Hodge DPSC and Lord Briggs explained at
         paras. 25 and 26 of their judgment: “We note certain features of the language which limit
         the circumstances in which liability may be incurred under section 213. First, the person
         to incur liability must be a party to the carrying on by the company of a fraudulent business
         and not merely involved in a one-off fraudulent transaction, unless the fraud is sufficient
         evidence on its own of the carrying on of a fraudulent business: In re Murray-Watson
         Ltd (unreported) 6 April 1977, Oliver J; Morphitis v Bernasconi [2003] Ch 552, para. 46,
         Chadwick LJ. Secondly, being party to the carrying on by the company of a fraudulent
         business does not extend to a mere failure to advise: see In re Maidstone Buildings
         Provisions Ltd [1971] 1 WLR 1085, in which Pennycuick V-C held that the failure of a
         company secretary to give advice to the company's directors did not bring him within the
         ambit of section 332 of the Companies Act 1948, a predecessor of section 213 the IA

                                                                                                                                                                      74
