         1986: p 1092. Thirdly, the person liable must have had an active involvement in the
         carrying on of the fraudulent business by the company: In re Maidstone (above); R v
         Miles [1992] Crim LR 657. Subject to those limitations, there is nothing in the language of
         section 213(2) which restricts the scope of the provision to directors and other ‘insiders’
         who were directing or managing the business of the company”.

265. I also cite para. 36 of the judgment of Lords Hodge and Briggs:

              “Here the provision creates civil liability where a person is knowingly involved in
              fraud when he or she knowingly becomes a party to the carrying on of business
              by a company with intent to defraud creditors or for any fraudulent purpose.
              Liability under section 213(2) depends upon dishonest participation and it exists
              to discourage such participation. The purpose of discouraging fraud is not a
              reason for taking a maximalist interpretation of the relevant words so as to
              extend the ambit of the section if there are indications which might limit that
              ambit. The requirement of knowing participation in the fraudulent carrying on of
              the company's business is such a limitation; but it does not militate against the
              inclusion of a counterparty to a fraudulent transaction or transactions by the
              company within the ambit of the section where the counterparty has the
              requisite knowledge of, including wilful blindness to, the fraudulent activity in
              the conduct of the company's business. We agree with Lewison LJ in para 93 of
              his judgment in this case in which he stated: ‘The extent to which a counterparty
              must be involved in the carrying on of the fraudulent business may depend upon
              the facts. Suppose that a manufacturer regularly supplies counterfeit designer
              clothes to a retailing company, knowing that the retailer will pass them off as
              genuine. It is, in my judgment, no misuse of language to describe the
              manufacturer as ‘party to the carrying on’ of a fraudulent business, even though
              he exercises no managerial or controlling role within the retailing company; and
              the manufacturer may have other business activities that are not fraudulent. The
              manufacturer knows about the retailer's fraudulent business and is actively
              participating in it in the sense of furthering and facilitating it’. In In re Gerald
              Cooper Chemicals Ltd [1978] Ch 262, 268 Templeman J famously stated that ‘a
              man who warms himself with the fire of fraud cannot complain if he is singed.’
              We agree”.

Knowledge

266. The requirement of knowledge raises three questions: (i) when does the participant have
         to know; (ii) knowledge of what; and (iii) what constitutes knowledge.

267. The answer to the first question is clear. The relevant knowledge “has to be
         contemporaneous with the assistance that was given at the time by entering into the
         various transactions. Subsequent knowledge based on hindsight is not enough”: Morris
         v Bank of India [2004] EWHC 528 (Ch) at para. 13.

                                                                                       05 November 2026 04:43 PM

268. On the second question, I need only refer again to the London Capital judgment (cit.
         sup.): “It is enough that the participant had relevant knowledge that their conduct was
         connected with a fraud. Where a participant assists in a fraud, such as by making
         arrangements which are known to conceal what is happening from a third party, the
         participant takes the risk that he or she is part of a fraud, the details of which they do not
         know. Lewison J explained this in the context of participation in VAT fraud in HMRC v
         Livewire Telecom Limited [2009] EWHC 15 (Ch) at [103]. The same principles have been
         applied to dishonest assistance in a breach of fiduciary duty, see Agip (Africa) Ltd v
         Jackson [1990] Ch. 265, at p. 295: (at para 1552): ‘In my judgment, however, it is no
         answer for a man charged with having knowingly assisted in a fraudulent and dishonest
         scheme to say that he thought that it was ‘only’ a breach of exchange control or ‘only’ a
         case of tax evasion. It is not necessary that he should have been aware of the precise
         nature of the fraud or even of the identity of its victim. A man who consciously assists
         others by making arrangements which he knows are calculated to conceal what is
         happening from a third party, takes the risk that they are part of a fraud practised on that
         party” (at para. 1552).

269. Although a person is not liable for a contribution as a party in fraudulent trading unless
         he participated “knowingly”, he does not have to have actual knowledge of the fraud. It is
         sufficient that he has what the English cases often call “blind-eye knowledge” or (as I
         would prefer) “shut-eye knowledge”: see London Capital judgment (cit. sup.) at para.
         1551ff. Thus, it is sufficient that the participant deliberately shuts his eyes to what is
         obvious because he does not want to confirm a suspicion of fraud. In Manifest Shipping
         Co Ltd v Uni-Polaris Insurance Co Ltd [2001] UKHL 1, Lord Scott said this (at para. 116):
         “In summary, blind-eye knowledge requires, in my opinion, a suspicion that the relevant
         facts do exist and a deliberate decision to avoid confirming that they exist. But a warning
         should be sounded. Suspicion is a word that can be used to describe a state-of-mind that
         may, at one extreme, be no more than a vague feeling of unease and, at the other extreme,
         reflect a firm belief in the existence of the relevant facts. In my opinion, in order for there
         to be blind-eye knowledge, the suspicion must be firmly grounded and targeted on
         specific facts. The deliberate decision must be a decision to avoid obtaining confirmation
         of facts in whose existence the individual has good reason to believe. To allow blind-eye
         knowledge to be constituted by a decision not to enquire into an untargeted or
         speculative suspicion would be to allow negligence, albeit gross, to be the basis of a
         finding of privity”.

270. The question in the Manifest Shipping case was about the mental state required for a
         person to have “privity” to a matter within the meaning of section 39(5) of the Marine
         Insurance Act 1906 (UK), but Lord Scott’s observations have been applied elsewhere,
         including with regard to what constitutes fraudulent trading. For example, in In Re Bank
         of Credit and Commerce International SA (In Liquidation) (No.14) [2003] EWHC 1868 (Ch),
         Patten J cited Lord Scott’s speech in support of this statement of the position in a
         fraudulent trading case: “Knowledge includes deliberately shutting one’s eyes to the

         obvious, provided that the fraudulent nature of the transactions did in fact appear obvious
         to those who dealt with these matters at [State Bank of India] at the relevant time. It is
         well established that it is no defence to say that one declined to ask questions, when the
         only reason for not doing so was an actual appreciation that the answers to those
         questions would be likely to disclose the existence of a fraud. But liability in such cases
         depends upon that stage of consciousness having been reached. [State Bank of India’s]
         submission, which I accept, is that one needs to be careful to draw a distinction between
         a conscious appreciation of the true nature of the business being carried on and a failure,
         however negligent, to appreciate that fraud was being perpetrated. The case for [State
         Bank of India] is that at no time during the course of these transactions did it in fact
         suspect that anything untoward was going on” (at para. 11).

Dishonesty

271. As was said in Bilta (UK) Ltd (in liquidation) v Tradition Financial Services Ltd (cit. sup.) at
         para. 36, liability for fraudulent trading “depends upon dishonest participation”. In Ivey v
         Genting Casinos (UK) Ltd [2017] UKSC 67, Lord Hughes explained how the English Courts
         decide whether a person has been dishonest: “When dishonesty is in question the fact-
         finding tribunal must first ascertain (subjectively) the actual state of the individual’s
         knowledge or belief as to the facts. The reasonableness or otherwise of his belief is a
         matter of evidence (often in practice determinative) going to whether he held the belief,
         but it is not an additional requirement that his belief must be reasonable; the question is
         whether it is genuinely held. When once his actual state of mind as to knowledge or belief
         as to facts is established, the question whether his conduct was honest or dishonest is to
         be determined by the fact-finder by applying the (objective) standards of ordinary decent
         people. There is no requirement that the defendant must appreciate that what he has
         done is, by those standards, dishonest” (at para. 74).

272. Before Lord Hughes’ judgment, there had been a prevailing school of thought, following
         the decision of the UK Court of Appeal in R v Ghosh [1982] QB 1053, that an issue of
         dishonesty was to be decided by a two-stage test: (i) a question whether the conduct
         complained of was dishonest by the lay objective standards of ordinary reasonable and
         honest people; and (ii) if so, a question whether the defendant must have realised that
         ordinary honest people would so regard his behaviour. The UK Supreme Court rejected
         the second stage of the test, and it held that issues of dishonesty are to be decided by the
         first question alone.

273. As Lord Hughes explained (loc. cit. at para. 60), this does not mean that beliefs that
         contribute to a person’s understanding that his conduct is justified (in the sense that it
         meets the standards of ordinary reasonable and honest people) are to be disregarded.
         The test of dishonesty is objective: it is clearly stated by Lord Hoffmann in Barlow Clowes
         International Ltd (In Liquidation) v Eurotrust International Ltd [2005] UKPC 37: “Although
         a dishonest state of mind is a subjective mental state, the standard by which the law
         determines whether it is dishonest is objective. If by ordinary standards a defendant’s

SHETTY AND OTHERS

                                                                                                                                                                      77

                                                                                       05 November 2026 04:43 PM

         mental state would be characterised as dishonest, it is irrelevant that the defendant
         judges by different standards” (at para. 10). Further, the objective test is one of universal
         application, and it does not depend upon the views about what is honest in a particular
         business or in a particular country or locality: thus, in Ivey, when explaining the objective
         test (at para. 62), Lord Hughes cited Royal Brunei Airlines Sdn Bhd v Tan [1995] 2 AC 378,
         in which the Privy Council considered what constituted dishonesty in Brunei. However,
         the objective test is to be applied taking into account any relevant knowledge or beliefs
         of the person against whom dishonesty is alleged. The objective test takes that
         knowledge and those beliefs into account. This was explained by Lord Hughes in Ivey (at
         para. 60) by reference to this vivid and helpful example, which derives from the Ghosh
         case:

                “It is plain that in R v Ghosh [1982] QB 1053 the court concluded that its
                compromise second leg test was necessary in order to preserve the principle
                that criminal responsibility for dishonesty must depend on the actual state of
                mind of the defendant. It asked the question whether ‘dishonestly’, where that
                word appears in the Theft Act, was intended to characterise a course of conduct
                or to describe a state of mind. The court gave the following example, at p 1063,
                which was clearly central to its reasoning:

                ‘Take for example a man who comes from a country where public transport is
                free. On his first day here he travels on a bus. He gets off without paying. He never
                had any intention of paying. His mind is clearly honest; but his conduct, judged
                objectively by what he has done, is dishonest. It seems to us that in using the
                word ‘dishonestly’ in the Theft Act 1968, Parliament cannot have intended to
                catch dishonest conduct in that sense, that is to say conduct to which no moral
                obloquy could possibly attach’.

                But the man in this example would inevitably escape conviction by the
                application of the (objective) first leg of the Ghosh test. That is because, in order
                to determine the honesty or otherwise of a person’s conduct, one must ask what
                he knew or believed about the facts affecting the area of activity in which he was
                engaging. In order to decide whether this visitor was dishonest by the standards
                of ordinary people, it would be necessary to establish his own actual state of
                knowledge of how public transport works. Because he genuinely believes that
                public transport is free, there is nothing objectively dishonest about his not
                paying on the bus. The same would be true of a child who did not know the rules,
                or of a person who had innocently misread the bus pass sent to him and did not
                realise that it did not operate until after 10.00 in the morning. The answer to the
                court’s question is that ‘dishonestly’, where it appears, is indeed intended to
                characterise what the defendant did, but in characterising it one must first
                ascertain his actual state of mind as to the facts in which he did it. It was not
                correct to postulate that the conventional objective test of dishonesty involves
                judging only the actions and not the state of knowledge or belief as to the facts

                in which they were performed. What is objectively judged is the standard of
                behaviour, given any known actual state of mind of the actor as to the facts”.

274. Therefore, before coming to apply the objective test to the conduct of a person said to be
         dishonest, it is necessary to consider what he knew or believed that was relevant to that
         conduct. What matters here is the person’s actual state of mind: as far as the legal
         position is concerned, it is beside the point whether his knowledge and beliefs were
         reasonable or unreasonable.

275. I need hardly add that the reasonableness of a belief might be considered, together with
         other relevant evidence, when making findings of fact about the person’s state of mind:
         the Court might conclude that a belief would have been so unreasonable that it does not
         accept the person truly held it, or contrarywise consider it quite understandable that a
         person came to have a belief, albeit a mistaken belief.

The wrongful trading claims: the law

276. Section 252 of the IR 2022 provides as follows:

              “(1) Subject to subsection (3) below, if in the course of the winding-up of a
              Company or while it is in administration it appears that subsection (2) applies in
              relation to any person being a past or present Director of the Company, the
              Court, on the application of the relevant Office-holder, may declare that person
              is to be liable to make such contribution (if any) to the Company's assets as the
              Court thinks fit.

              (2) This subsection (2) applies if—

              (a) the Company has gone into an insolvent liquidation or has entered insolvent
              administration;

              (b) at some time before the commencement of the winding-up of the Company
              or before the Company entered administration, as the case may be, the person
              knew or ought to have concluded that there was no reasonable prospect of the
              Company avoiding going into insolvent liquidation or entering insolvent
              administration; and

              (c) the person was a Director of the Company at that time.

              (3) Subsection (1) shall not apply to any person if the Court is satisfied that after
              the Director first knew or ought to have concluded that there was no reasonable
              prospect of the Company avoiding going into insolvent liquidation, he took every
              step with a view to minimising the potential loss to the Company's creditors as
              (on the assumption that the person had knowledge of the matter mentioned in
              subsection (2)(b)) he ought to have taken.

              (4) For the purposes of this Section, the facts which a Director of the Company
              ought to know, the conclusions which he ought to reach and the steps which he
              ought to take are those which would be known, or reached or taken, by a
              reasonably diligent person having both—

              (a) the general knowledge, skill and experience that may reasonably be
              expected of a person carrying out the same functions as are carried out by that
              Director in relation to the Company (including functions which he does not carry
              out but which have been entrusted to him); and

              (b) the general knowledge, skill and experience that Director has”.

277. The principles applicable to wrongful trading claims under the Insolvency Act 1986 (UK)
         were summarised by Leech J in Wright v Chappell [2024] EWHC 1417 (Ch). They apply
         equally to claims for wrongful trading under IR 2022. At para. 463, Leech J identified three
         conditions in section 214(1) of the Insolvency Act 1986 (UK) about which the liquidator or
         administrator must satisfy the Court:

         a. that the company has gone into insolvent liquidation or, as the case might be,
                   insolvent administration;

         b. that the defendants were directors when the third condition was met; and

         c. that, at some time before the commencement of the insolvent liquidation or
                   insolvent administration, the defendants knew or ought to have concluded that
                   there was no reasonable prospect that the company would avoid going into
                   insolvent liquidation or insolvent administration, the so-called “Knowledge
                   Condition”.

278. If these conditions are satisfied, then the Court has a discretion to order such
         contribution to the assets of the company as it thinks fit, provided there is some causal
         link between the continuation of trading and the amount of the payment: In Re Ralls
         Builders Ltd (In Liquidation) [2016] EWHC 243 (Ch).

279. As with fraudulent trading, the “Knowledge Condition” raises questions about what is to
         be known and what constitutes knowledge. It is not satisfied simply because the director
         knew or ought to have concluded that the company was or would become insolvent: see
         In Re C S Holidays Ltd [1997] BCC 172, 178 and In Re Hawkes Hill Publishing Co Ltd (In
         Liquidation) [2007] BCC 937 at para. 28. It requires that, at the relevant time, the director
         either knew or ought to have known that there was no reasonable prospect of the
         company avoiding going into insolvent liquidation or going into insolvent administration.

280. As to what constitutes knowledge, I shall cite this submission made by the JA Claimants
         in their written opening on the basis of paras. 465, 466 and 479 of the Wright v Chappell
         judgment, which Mr Wandowicz accepted and which Mr Hennessey did not dispute:

SHETTY AND OTHERS

                                                                                                                                                                      80

                                                                                       05 November 2026 04:43 PM

                 “The ‘Knowledge Condition’ may be satisfied by proving that the defendants
                 had actual knowledge (i.e. they ‘subjectively concluded that the Companies
                 had no real prospect of avoiding insolvent liquidation or insolvent
                 administration’) or that they ‘should have concluded that this was the case
                 after an objective evaluation of the facts which they knew or the information
                 which was provided to them at each [relevant date]’. As to the latter, the Court
                 must apply ‘the standard of the reasonably diligent person having both the
                 general knowledge, skill and experience expected of a person carrying out the
                 same functions but also the general knowledge, skill and experience of [the
                 specific defendants] themselves’, which Mr Justice Leech described as the
                 standard of the ‘Notional Director’. If a director’s general knowledge, skill and
                 experience are higher than that of a reasonably diligent person, they will be
                 held to that higher standard; but if a director’s general knowledge, skill and
                 experience is lower than that of a reasonably diligent person discharging the
                 same functions, they will be held to the objective standard (i.e. it is no defence
                 for a director to say that they did not have that general knowledge, skill and
                 experience)”.

281. A further question about the “Knowledge Condition” arises because NMCH, Holding and
         the ADGM Companies were not incorporated in the ADGM until September 2020. In my
         Judgment on the preliminary issues, [2024] ADGMCFI 0007 at paras. 45 to 52, I rejected
         an argument advanced on behalf of Dr Shetty that the “Knowledge Condition” requires
         (actual or constructive) knowledge of an insolvency procedure in the ADGM Court,
         observing that it would be a most unusual case where a director knew or ought to have
         known that a non-ADGM company would become subject to an ADGM insolvency
         process. I held that, although the terms “insolvent liquidation” and “insolvent
         administration” in section 252 of IR 2022 refer to ADGM insolvency processes, the
         question is whether a director of an ADGM company in the position of the defendant
         ought to have concluded that there was no reasonable prosect of avoiding an ADGM
         insolvent liquidation or insolvent administration. The ADGM Court of Appeal upheld my
         decision, and held that, in the circumstances of this case, the question as to whether a
         director ought to have had the requisite knowledge (which it referred to as the “second
         limb” of the “Knowledge Condition”), as follows: “In a case such as the present (where it
         is alleged that there was wrongful trading before the company concerned was continued
         within ADGM) the second limb is to be applied by reference to the factual inquiry which
         would underpin the making of an order by the ADGM Court for an insolvent liquidation or
         insolvent administration. That is, at the risk of undue abbreviation the inquiry would be
         whether the person ought to have concluded that there was no reasonable prospect of
         the Company being and remaining unable [sic] to pay its debts as they fall due (as that
         standard is understood and applied under ADGM law). In a particular case this may be a
         factually difficult inquiry but the standard which is to be applied is clear. Because the
         standard to be applied is clear it presents no legal issue that precludes the section's

                                                                                       05 November 2026 04:43 PM

         application in respect of a continued company’s wrongful trading before continuance”:
         [2024] ADGMCA 0001 at para. 50. (I think that “unable” is an error for “able”.)

282. This answers the plea in Dr Shetty’s defence that “when Dr Shetty was a director of any of
         the relevant companies, none of them was likely to enter into insolvent liquidation or
         insolvent administration within the meaning of the Insolvency Regulations, which
         concern specific insolvency processes within the ADGM”.

283. The ADGM Court of Appeal also rejected an argument of Dr Shetty that section 252 of IR
         2022 does not apply in these circumstances because Leech J’s second condition
         requires that the defendant be a director of a company within the meaning of IR 2022. It
         said this: “As for the point about the application of sub-sec(2)(c)'s requirement that ‘the
         person was a Director of the Company’ at the time of the wrongful trading, there are four
         related observations to be made. First, s 252(1) applies in relation to ‘any person being
         a past or present Director of the Company’ (emphasis added). Second, as noted earlier,
         ‘Company’ is defined as ‘a company formed or registered under [the Companies
         Regulations 2020] (whether or not it was incorporated under these Regulations)’
         (emphasis added). Third, s 252(6) provides that in s 252, ‘Director includes a shadow
         director’. Fourth, s 146 of the Companies Regulations provides that in those regulations
         ‘director’ includes ‘any person occupying the position of a director, by whatever name
         called’ and s 147 defines ‘shadow director’ as ‘a person in accordance with whose
         directions or instructions the directors of the company are accustomed to act’. Together
         these observations require the conclusion that a person who was shown to have been a
         director or shadow director of the body corporate at the time of the alleged wrongful
         trading (before continuance within ADGM) is a person in respect of whom a wrongful
         trading order can be made under s 252. That person was a director of a Company that was
         not incorporated under ADGM law but by continuance became a Company formed or
         registered under ADGM law” (loc. cit. at paras. 51 and 52).

284. I return to the question as to the standard by which the Court will decide whether a
         director “ought to have concluded”. Here, as Leech J said, the Court applies “the
         standard of the reasonably diligent person having both the general knowledge, skill and
         experience expected of a person carrying out the same functions but also the general
         knowledge, skill and experience of [the specific defendants] themselves”. If a director’s
         general knowledge, skill and experience are higher than that of a reasonably diligent
         person, he will be held to that higher standard. On the other hand, if a director’s general
         knowledge, skill and experience is lower than that of a reasonably diligent person
         discharging the same functions, he will be held to the objective standard: it is no defence
         for a director to say that they did not have that general knowledge, skill and experience.
         Further, account should be taken of the size and type of the company: the director of a
         small and unsophisticated company will not be required to have the skill of a director of
         a large and sophisticated entity, such as NMCH.

285. A board of directors may properly delegate management functions and areas of
         responsibility to identified directors or to employees who are not on the board, but
         director’s duties are personal and it remains the duty of each director to monitor and
         supervise the discharge of those functions: see Brumder v Motornet Service and Repairs
         Ltd [2013] EWCA Civ 195 at para. 55 per Beatson LJ. As Popplewell J said in Madoff
         Securities International Ltd (In Liquidation) v Raven [2013] EWHC 3147 (Comm), a
         director “owes duties to the company to inform himself of the company's affairs and join
         with his fellow directors in supervising them” (at para. 191).

286. Finally, a defence to a claim in wrongful trading provided in section 252(3) of IR 2022, is
         not readily available. In the Wright v Chappell case (loc. cit. at para. 488), Leech J said: “It
         is not enough for the directors to prove that they continued trading with the intention of
         reducing the net deficit of the company. They must also show that it was designed to
         minimise the risk of loss to individual creditors”. However, I need say no more about
         section 252(3) of IR 2022: I do not understand either Dr Shetty or Mr Manghat to rely upon
         this defence.

The JA Claimants’ Civil Claims: UAE law

The claims

287. I next introduce the law about the JA Claimants’ Civil Claims, which are governed by UAE
         law. The JA Claimants allege that Dr Shetty and Mr Manghat both acted fraudulently with
         regard to the business of the NMC Group, or in the alternative that each of them failed to
         act with the care reasonably to be expected of a person in their position at the time. On
         the basis of these allegations, they make claims under:

         a. Article 282 of the Civil Code, which provides as follows: “Any harm done to
                   another shall render the doer thereof, even though not a person of discretion,
                   liable to make good the harm”.

         b. Article 285 of the Civil Code, which provides as follows: “If a person deceives
                   another he shall be liable to make good the harm resulting from that
                   deception”.

288. The JA Claimants also make claims against Dr Shetty and Mr Manghat, on the basis of
         essentially the same allegations, under UAE Federal Law No. 8 of 1984 on Commercial
         Companies (“CCL 1984”) and CCL 2015 for breach of their duties or liabilities in their
         capacities as their directors and managers.

289. Further, the JA Claimants rely on article 304 of the Civil Code, which is concerned with
         extortion, and articles 318 and 319, which are concerned with unjust enrichment. These
         articles provide as follows:

                 Article 304:

                 “(1) The hand that has taken remains bound until it gives back.

                 (2) Whoever misappropriates property belonging to another must restore it to
                 that other in the condition it was in when, and at the same place where, the
                 misappropriation took place.

                 (3) If (the tortfeasor) has consumed or damaged the property or if it has
                 diminished or been damaged either with or without a wrongful act on his part,
                 he must make good in kind or in value as at the day and place of
                 misappropriation.

                 (4) He must indemnify for any benefits or increase of the property”.

                 Article 318:

                 “No person may take the property of another without lawful cause, and if he
                 takes it he must return it”.

                 Article 319:

                 “(1) Any person who acquires the property of other person without any
                 disposition vesting ownership must return it if that property still exists, or its
                 like or the value thereof if it no longer exists, unless the law otherwise
                 provides.

                 (2) If the property of any person leaves his possession without his so intending
                 and by unavoidable event becomes joined with the property of another person
                 in such a way that it cannot be separated therefrom without causing harm to
                 one of the owners, the property of the lesser value shall be added as part of
                 the property of the greater value after payment of the value thereof, and if they
                 are of equal value then the property shall be sold and the proceeds distributed
                 unless there is an agreement or a provision of law to the contrary”.

Article 282

290. The Official Commentary on the UAE Civil Code of the Ministry of Justice (the
         “Commentary”) explains the principle that underlies article 282: “In this Article, the
         legislator prescribes the general principle that each act causing harm to another requires
         to be compensated. This principle is based on what is prescribed under the Islamic Sharia
         that: ‘no harm shall be done, nor harm done in return’ (Article (19) of Al-Majalla); ‘harm
         shall be removed’ (Article (20) of Al-Majalla); or ‘if the principal becomes void, the
         substitute shall subsist’ (Article (53) of Al-Majalla)”. (The principles in articles 19 and 20

ADGMCFI-2022-111 – ABU DHABI COMMERCIAL BANK PJSC V PRASANTH MANGHAT
ADGMCFI-2020-020 - IN THE MATTER OF NMC HEALTHCARE LIMITED (IN ADMINISTRATION) (SUBJECT TO A DEED OF COMPANY ARRANGEMENT) AND THE COMPANIES
LISTED IN SCHEDULE 1 TO THE ADMINISTRATION APPLICATION
ADGMCFI-2022-299 – NMC HEALTHCARE LIMITED (IN ADMINISTRATION) (SUBJECT TO A DEED OF COMPANY ARRANGEMENT) AND OTHERS V BAVAGUTHU RAGHURAM
SHETTY AND OTHERS

                                                                                                                                                                      84

                                                                                       05 November 2026 04:43 PM

         of Al-Majalla are embodied in Article 42 of the Civil Code.) Thus, the root of the principle
         is not fault, but an obligation on persons to undo harm that they cause.

291. Accordingly, there are three elements to liability under article 282: (i) a harmful act; (ii)
         harm suffered; and (iii) a causal link between the act and the harm suffered.

292. The harmful act may, as the Commentary explains, be “either positive or negative”, or, in
         language more familiar to the common law, an act or an omission. However, the concept
         of a harmful act has been restricted in UAE law: the Courts have recognised that some
         limits must be placed on what is so regarded. For example, the Dubai Court of Cassation
         said this in a judgment of 27 March 2006, DCC 28/2006: “if a person competes with
         others, whether in work or in trade, he is not liable to pay indemnity for the harm that this
         may cause to others, as long as the competition is within lawful limits. If the person
         exceeds such lawful limits, then the competition is a harmful act that implies the liability
         of the person who carried it out to indemnify others for the damage that may occur to
         them. Exceeding the limits of lawful competition takes place when committing acts that
         contravene the Law or Norms, or using ways or means contrary to the principles of honour
         and honesty in transactions, if they are meant to cause harm to others”. Accordingly,
         when considering a claim under article 282, the Court will evaluate the act, taking into
         consideration a wide range of factors. The Commentary explains: “It is self-evident that if
         the provisions of the legislation were to enumerate the acts out of which harm in this
         sense might be caused, that would be no more than to give examples, and it could not
         result in the creation of a comprehensive and exhaustive statement. The determination
         of what amounts to harm is left to the discretion of the judge, who will be guided therein
         by the general guiding elements to be deduced from the nature of the prohibition in the
         law against causing harm. There is an obligation imposed upon everybody not to cause
         harm to others. A breach of that prohibition is one which involves harm”.

293. As is reflected in the Dubai judgment, the circumstances in which an act will be
         considered to be one which is “harmful” and attract liability under article 282 are
         sometimes expressed in terms of whether the actor has gone “beyond the limit”. The
         Commentary puts it in this way: “The meaning of harm here is an attempt ‘to go beyond
         the limit’ at which one should stop, or ‘to fail to reach that limit’ to which one should go in
         acting or in refraining to act, in such a way as to cause harm”. The Abu Dhabi Court of
         Cassation said in a judgment of 24 January 2024 (1196/2023): “Articles (282) and (283) of
         the Civil Code indicate that the basis of civil liability is the unlawful act or causing harm.
         This means either exceeding the limit at which one must stop or failing to reach the
         required standard, whether by an act or omission, and the occurrence of harm as a
         result”.

294. I therefore accept, at least as far as is material for the purpose of these proceedings, the
         JA Claimants’ contention pleaded as follows:

                 “(b) A claim may be brought under Article 282 by reference to, inter alia:

                 (i) Any violation of a duty imposed by law that causes harm.

                 (ii) Any breach of the societal standards or norms reasonably to be expected
                 of a given person in a given context so as to avoid harm to others (the ‘UAE
                 Tortious Standards’), and which deﬁne what amounts to negligence, taqseer
                 or khataa jaseem (gross negligence) or fraud by a given person in a given
                 context. In this regard:

                 a. Under UAE law, a claim for taqseer or khataa jaseem (the terms being
                      interchangeable for this purpose) will arise where there has been a serious
                      default, such as where a professional person (such as a banker) or a
                      person in a position of particular responsibility (such as a company
                      director or a person who is a signatory on a company’s bank accounts)
                      fails to take reasonable care in the performance of his duties.

                 b. Whether a default is taqseer / khataa jaseem will depend on whether the
                      default is to be considered serious in all the circumstances (for example,
                      by reference to the identity of the malfeasor, his relationship with the
                      claimant, the default in question and the length of time it spanned; the
                      consequences of the default are also relevant)”.

         The pleading explains that: “The translation of taqseer or khataa jaseem as ‘gross
         negligence’ is adopted herein without thereby denoting that concept as understood
         under ADGM law”.

295. In so far as Dr Shetty and Mr Manghat took issue with this in their defences, the points
         were not developed by Mr Wandowicz or Mr Hennessey, and they do not appear to me to
         materially bear upon the questions that I have to decide.

296. I can deal more briefly with the second element of liability under article 282 (harm
         suffered), but it is convenient to mention here (although it is more relevant to ADCB’s
         claim against Mr Manghat than to the JA Claimants’ claims) that, as is expressly stated in
         article 292, the concept of harm includes loss of profit that is the natural result of the
         harmful act. (The word translated as “profit” is “kasb”, which has a wider connotation
         than profit might have, and might be better translated as “gain” or “earnings”.) The
         Commentary illustrates this by reference to a case of deceit: “Article 189 of the Iraqi civil
         code provides that if a person deceives another then he shall make good the damage, and
         if a person says to traders in the market: ‘such and such a young person is my son, sell
         him goods as I have permitted him to trade’ and it appears thereafter that the youth is
         somebody else’s son, the market traders may make a claim against him for the cost of
         the goods sold by them to the youth, and compensation for other damage”. As I
         understand it, Mr Manghat did not challenge this.

297. I come to the requirement that there be a causal link (or, as it is put in the Commentary,
         a “causal relationship”) between the harmful act and the harm suffered. I make two
         observations. First, the JA Claimants submitted that, so far as is material for present
         purposes, it is not necessary for a claimant to show what would have happened if the
         defendant had not done the harmful act. The English law concept of ‘but for’ causation is
         not part of the UAE law with which this case is concerned. I accept the JA Claimants’
         submission, which was not, as I understand it, disputed.

Article 283

298. Secondly, article 283 of the Civil Code must be mentioned. In Whelan’s translation, it
         provides:

                “(1) Harm may be direct or by causation.

                (2) If harm is direct, it must unconditionally be made good, and if it is
                consequential there must be a wrongdoing or a deliberate act or the act must
                have led to harm”.

299. The distinction between direct harm and harm by causation is fact-sensitive and a matter
         for assessment by the trial judge. The Dubai Court of Cassation said in a judgment of 7
         March 2004, 302/2003: “It is also settled that concluding the harmful act and whether it
         occurred by way of causation or directly, as well as the subordination relationship
         between the party that caused the harm and its superior falls under the sole power of the
         trial court without any supervision, so long as its decision in this respect is reasonable
         and substantiated in the case papers”. Similarly, the Abu Dhabi Court of Cassation said
         in a judgment of 19 July 2022, 495/2022: “It is also decided that establishing or denying
         the harmed party’s contribution to the occurrence of the harm through his act is a matter
         of fact which the trial judge shall assess without being subject to the supervision of the
         Court of Cassation in this respect. However, his conclusion in that respect must be
         reasonable and substantiated in the case papers, under which the same court shall have
         the power to determine the establishment of this fault, its extent, whether it occurred
         directly or by causation, and its effect on the occurrence of the harm”.

300. However this might be, it does not seem to me to matter in this case whether the harm
         that the JA Claimants and ADCB say that they have suffered is direct or by causation. In
         either case, harm must be made good if it results from “wrongdoing” or a deliberate act
         or “led to the harm”. “Wrongdoing” occurs when a person has no lawful right to do the
         harmful act: the Abu Dhabi Court of Cassation said in a judgment of 24 January 2024,
         1196/2023: “However, civil liability by way of causation arises when a person commits an
         act whose consequences result in the harm rather than the act itself. In this case, civil
         liability shall be conditional upon the existence of a deliberate act or wrongdoing. A
         deliberate act is when the act intends to cause harm to another, and wrongdoing refers
         to unlawfully committing an act or committing an act that is impermissible, meaning that

ADGMCFI-2022-299 – NMC HEALTHCARE LIMITED (IN ADMINISTRATION) (SUBJECT TO A DEED OF COMPANY ARRANGEMENT) AND OTHERS V BAVAGUTHU RAGHURAM
SHETTY AND OTHERS

                                                                                                                                                                      87

                                                                                       05 November 2026 04:43 PM

         the doer did not have the right to commit the act that caused the harm, whether that act
         is positive or negative”. A harmful act is regarded as deliberate when the harm was
         intended, and it is not sufficient that the act was intended: the Commentary states: “The
         meaning of deliberate here is the deliberate causing of harm, and not the deliberate doing
         of the act”. The third limb of article 283(2) covers cases where the act was by its nature
         harmful: the concept is illustrated by the decision of the Dubai Court of Cassation of 12
         October 2003, 254/2003, in which a contractor, who had used a flame to remove paint
         caused a fire, and damage was caused by the water used by the firefighters. The Court
         said: “Given that the civil defence were forced and compelled by the nature of their duties
         to intervene in order to eliminate the sources of the smoke, the indirect actor (the
         Company - the Appellant in Cassation) shall be therefore liable to make good the harm
         for forcing the direct act that resulted in the occurrence of the harm”.

301. If the allegations of the JA Claimants and ADCB are proved, if and in so far as the harm
         resulting to them was not direct but by causation, liability to make it good is within article
         283(2).

Article 285

302. The elements of a claim in deceit under article 285 are: (i) a deception; (ii) harm suffered;
         and (iii) causation.

303. The principles governing the elements of harm suffered and causation are similar here to
         those applicable to article 282.

304. With regards to the element of deception, the Arabic text uses the terms “taghrir”, which
         is translated as “deceives”, and “ghurur”, which is translated as “deception”. The
         concepts are similar: see, for example, judgment of the Dubai Court of Cassation of 21
         March 2017, 20/2017, in which the terms are used interchangeably. They connote
         intentional wrongdoing: the Commentary states: “The word ‘ghurur’ as to the subject
         matter is used by the jurists in the meaning of making someone accept what is no good
         by lying and misleading means, inducing him to proceed, in a circumstance where if he
         had known the truth he would not have proceeded. Thus, there is no ghurur save where it
         is exercised in respect of the subject matter, and is used as a means of inducement by
         the deceiver by false and lying means designed to make facts appear otherwise than as
         they are, practised deliberately by the deceiver, and in bad faith”.

305. Silence, or a failure to speak when there is a duty to do so, can constitute deceit. Article
         185 of the Civil Code, which is about deceit in the context of contractual relief by way of
         recission uses the same word “taghrir”, and here article 186 expressly states that,
         “Deliberate silence concerning a fact or circumstance shall be treated as a
         misrepresentation if it is proved that the person misled thereby would not have made the
         contract had he been aware of that fact or circumstance”. Although there is no
         corresponding provision in the Civil Code relating to article 285, the case law shows the

COURT OF FIRST INSTANCE JUDGMENT
ADGMCFI-2022-111 – ABU DHABI COMMERCIAL BANK PJSC V PRASANTH MANGHAT
ADGMCFI-2020-020 - IN THE MATTER OF NMC HEALTHCARE LIMITED (IN ADMINISTRATION) (SUBJECT TO A DEED OF COMPANY ARRANGEMENT) AND THE COMPANIES
LISTED IN SCHEDULE 1 TO THE ADMINISTRATION APPLICATION
ADGMCFI-2022-299 – NMC HEALTHCARE LIMITED (IN ADMINISTRATION) (SUBJECT TO A DEED OF COMPANY ARRANGEMENT) AND OTHERS V BAVAGUTHU RAGHURAM
SHETTY AND OTHERS

                                                                                                                                                                      88

                                                                                       05 November 2026 04:43 PM

         position is similar, provided that the Court concludes that, in the circumstances of the
         case, the defendant had a duty to speak. For example, in its judgment of 10 November
         2015, 636/2015, the Dubai Court of Cassation held that the defendants were liable to pay
         compensation “due to the misrepresentations they committed by deliberately remaining
         silent” and by “hiding” them. In another case (183/2011), the UAE Federal Supreme Court
         on 1 June 2011 held that, where an investor had given cheques to reserve an apartment
         and parking space in a development project that had been cancelled, the manager of the
         development company, who knew the project had been cancelled but said nothing, was
         held liable on the basis that: “It is sufficient, in some circumstances, for one party to
         withhold information that he was obliged to disclose to the other, for fraud to be
         established - provided that the concealment was intended to mislead”.

306. Mr Manghat pleads in his defence to ADCB’s claim that, “Avoiding or refraining from
         action will only be actionable where the defendant was legally obliged to act”. In his
         submissions on UAE law, however, Mr Manghat did not maintain this contention: he said
         that he did not take issue with the submissions of the JA Claimants and ADCB that silence
         can trigger liability under article 285.

307. I conclude that liability under article 285 can arise from silence without the defendant
         being under a legal duty to speak. This is illustrated by these two cases, to which the JA
         Claimants and ADCB referred:

         a. In a judgment of 22 April 2019 in case 48/2019, the Abu Dhabi Court of Cassation
                   held that there was deceit when an employee agreed to reduced entitlements
                   from his employer where the business was being restructured. Shortly
                   afterwards, the employer terminated the employment, citing the restructuring to
                   justify it, and declined to pay more than the reduced entitlements.

         b. In a judgment of 24 July 2014 in case 95/2014, the Dubai Court of Cassation held
                   that there was deceit when a professional footballer was retained for a marketing
                   exercise without the value of advertising revenue being disclosed.

308. Baroda submitted that silence in itself cannot give rise to liability under article 285, and
         it observed that the Commentary does not refer to deceit by silence or concealment. It
         argued that silence can only give rise to liability when it is accompanied by conduct: it
         gave as an example the case where the seller of a sheep delayed her milking and
         concealed this in order to trick a buyer into believing she was high-yielding.

309. As I see it, there is no real issue between the parties here. I find it difficult to imagine a
         case in which it would be held that a person is under a duty to speak in the absence of
         any conduct that puts him under such a (legal, commercial or social) obligation.

Article 304

310. As I understand the JA Claimants’ case, they do not rely on article 304 to make distinct
         claims in extortion. Their pleaded contention is that the conduct of Dr Shetty and Mr
         Manghat constituted extortion in breach of article 304 and this supports the claims
         against them under article 282: it shows that their conduct was “beyond the limit”.

311. Article 304 applies where:

         a. a person takes possession of the property of another;

         b. possession is taken without the owner’s permission and without legal
                   justification; and

         c. the taking of possession results in harm to the owner.

312. UAE law takes a wide view about what constitutes property in this context. The
         Commentary says that: “Property [mal] is anything or right having a material value in
         dealing”, and Whelan explains his translation of the Commentary as follows: “Mal,
         translated as property, is a wider word and includes ‘thing’, money, and also non-tangible
         rights”.

313. It is not necessary in order for article 304 to apply that the person taking possession was
         at fault. Professor Mohamed Hussein in Al Motawwal: In Explaining the Civil Code of the
         United Arab Emirates (2015) gives these examples of the application of article 304: “if a
         person pays money thinking that he is indebted, then it thereafter becomes evident to him
         that he was wrong, the taker must return it”; and “if a person withholds lost property to
         himself, he is considered a misappropriator who is under an obligation to indemnify until
         he gives it back to its owner”. He also said that the rule applies “whether or not the
         misappropriator knew that it was the property of a third party”.

Articles 318 and 319

314. As the Commentary explains, the three elements of a claim for unjust enrichment under
         articles 318 and 319 of the Civil Code are these: (i) the defendant has been enriched, or
         he has had a gain; (ii) the enrichment corresponds to the impoverishment of the claimant
         due to a property being transferred or value being given; and (ii) there was no lawful cause
         justifying the enrichment and corresponding impoverishment.

315. The first element is met when, in the words of the Commentary, “the value of what was
         enriched enters into the patrimony of the debtor”. The obligation arises from the receipt:
         it does not depend upon whether the recipient has done anything to effect the transfer,
         nor upon whether he is at fault, nor necessarily upon his knowledge of the receipt,
         although it is less clear whether knowledge might be relevant to whether the defendant
         has truly been enriched or has had a gain or value come into his “patrimony”. Mr

COURT OF FIRST INSTANCE JUDGMENT
ADGMCFI-2022-111 – ABU DHABI COMMERCIAL BANK PJSC V PRASANTH MANGHAT
ADGMCFI-2020-020 - IN THE MATTER OF NMC HEALTHCARE LIMITED (IN ADMINISTRATION) (SUBJECT TO A DEED OF COMPANY ARRANGEMENT) AND THE COMPANIES
LISTED IN SCHEDULE 1 TO THE ADMINISTRATION APPLICATION
ADGMCFI-2022-299 – NMC HEALTHCARE LIMITED (IN ADMINISTRATION) (SUBJECT TO A DEED OF COMPANY ARRANGEMENT) AND OTHERS V BAVAGUTHU RAGHURAM
SHETTY AND OTHERS

                                                                                                                                                                      90

                                                                                       05 November 2026 04:43 PM

         Wandowicz argued that Dr Shetty would not be liable under article 318 if, without his
         knowledge, moneys were transferred into his bank account with Baroda or another bank
         and then transferred on out of the account before Dr Shetty discovered that his account
         was being so used. His argument was that, in those circumstances, it cannot be said that
         Dr Shetty had been enriched: see para. 868 below.

316. The Commentary also explains that the enrichment “may be direct or indirect, material
         or moral”, provided that it is quantifiable in money. The Dubai Court of Cassation said in
         case 409/2022 (22 September 2022), that there is enrichment when “the value of what is
         gained … enters into the debtor’s financial assets”. Although the enrichment must
         correspond to the impoverishment of the claimant, there is no requirement that the gain
         to the recipient and the impoverishment of the loser should be of the same value, as the
         Commentary makes clear in this passage: “the law requires the enriched party to
         compensate the creditor for the loss sustained by the latter, but only to the extent of his
         enrichment. That is, the enriched party shall be required to return the lesser of the two
         values: the value that the enriched party has gained, and the value by which the creditor
         has been impoverished”.

317. It makes no difference to the liability of the person enriched that the money or other
         enrichment is no longer enjoyed by the recipient. The obligation to restore the value of the
         enrichment arises upon receipt and the recipient remains liable for it notwithstanding he
         does not retain what he received.

318. Before leaving articles 318 and 319, there is a pleading matter that I should mention
         concerning the case against Mr Manghat. In the section of the Particulars of Claim where
         the JA Claimants set out their Civil Claims against Mr Manghat, no mention is made of a
         claim in unjust enrichment or of article 318 and 319. However, in the passage directed to
         “Remedies”, it is pleaded that the corporate claimants are entitled to restitution as
         against Mr Manghat: it is pleaded that Mr Manghat was unjustly enriched “in respect of
         funds amounting to at least AED 39,050,000, and other benefits”, but I consider what
         payments he received at paras. 632ff below.

319. Although the omission of the restitution claim from the earlier passages of the Particulars
         of Claim is curious, Mr Manghat did not argue that the claim is not properly pleaded
         against him. In the event, it makes no difference to the outcome whether or not the JA
         Claimants have properly pleaded for restitution on the grounds of unjust enrichment, but
         to my mind they have done so.

CCL 1984 and CCL 2015

320. CCL 2015 came into force on 1 July 2015, and it applied to companies incorporated in the
         UAE, including NMCH, Holding and the ADGM Companies. The JA Claimants plead
         claims by reference to these articles: article 22, article 84, article 150, article 153 and
         article 162. Before 1 July 2015, the CCL 1984 was in force, and the JA Claimants rely on

ADGMCFI-2022-111 – ABU DHABI COMMERCIAL BANK PJSC V PRASANTH MANGHAT

LISTED IN SCHEDULE 1 TO THE ADMINISTRATION APPLICATION
ADGMCFI-2022-299 – NMC HEALTHCARE LIMITED (IN ADMINISTRATION) (SUBJECT TO A DEED OF COMPANY ARRANGEMENT) AND OTHERS V BAVAGUTHU RAGHURAM
SHETTY AND OTHERS

                                                                                                                                                                      91

                                                                                       05 November 2026 04:43 PM

         its corresponding provisions where applicable.

321. Article 22 of CCL 2015 provides as follows: “A person authorised to manage the company
         shall preserve its rights and extend such care as a diligent person. Such person shall do
         all such acts in agreement with the objective of the company and the powers granted to
         such person by virtue of an authorisation issued by the company in this respect”. Article
         1 of the CCL 2015 defines a “diligent person” as: “The person having sufficient experience
         and commitment required in the performance of his work”. There was no corresponding
         duty under the CCL 1984.

322. Article 84 of CCL 2015 provides that a director or manager should be liable to the
         company, partners and third parties for any fraudulent acts, for any losses or expenses
         incurred due to improper use of power, contravention of the provisions of any applicable
         law, the memorandum of association or contract of appointment, or for gross error. A
         broadly corresponding provision was at article 111 of the CCL 1984.

323. Article 150 of CCL 2015 provides that a director or manager should be required to notify
         the board of directors of any common interests or conflicts of interest. A corresponding
         provision was at article 109 of the CCL 1984.

324. Article 153 of CCL 2015 prohibits a company from providing loans to any member of its
         board or execute suretyships or provide guarantees in connection with any loans granted
         to them, and that any such contract should be invalid. A corresponding provision was at
         article 197 of the CCL 1984.

325. Article 162 of CCL 2015 provides that a director or manager should be liable to the
         company, partners or any third party for any fraudulent acts and for any losses or
         expenses incurred due to improper use of power in contravention of any applicable law,
         the memorandum of association or contract of appointment, and any errors of
         management. Corresponding provisions were at articles 111 and 112 of the CCL 1984.

326. As far as is material, Dr Shetty and Mr Manghat did not dispute these provisions of UAE
         law. In his defence, Dr Shetty admitted that the standard of care required by article 22
         was that of a diligent person, but he denied that the required standard was that of a
         person “having sufficient experience and commitment required in the performance of his
         work”, but I infer that the definition in article 1 was overlooked: Mr Wandowicz did not
         pursue the point. Other matters about the provisions of the CCL 2015 and the CCL 1984
         that are pleaded in Dr Shetty’s defence and Mr Manghat’s defence do not affect what I
         have to decide.

Mr Manghat’s defences

327. Before leaving questions of UAE law relating to the JA Action, I mention that Mr Manghat
         pleads in his defence these arguments:

         a. “Article 287 [of the Civil Code] provides that a perpetrator will be relieved of
                   liability if the loss was as a result of, inter alia, an act of a third party, or the act of
                   the person who sustained the loss or damage”; and that any losses of NMCH or
                   Holding were the result of the acts of Dr Shetty or others.

         b. “Article 290 [of the Civil Code] provides that the Judge may reduce the level of
                   indemnity (including to zero), if the person suffering harm participated by his own
                   act in bringing about or aggravating the harm”; and that the JA Claimants cannot
                   claim for loss resulting from its own employees’ acts.

328. As the Commentary makes clear, article 287 of the Civil Code is about causation, and
         whether the causal link between the harmful act and the harm is broken. It operates
         where a defendant “played no part” in the intervening event.

329. In his Post-Hearing Submissions, Mr Manghat agreed that article 287 is about causation,
         and he said that he relies on it “as part of his causation case”. His argument is that the
         conduct of a third party or another intervening event can mean that the losses claimed
         cannot “truly … be treated as the natural and legal consequence of his own conduct”.
         Provided that this submission is not intended to dispute that, under article 283(2),
         consequential loss as well as direct loss may be recoverable, this proposition is not
         controversial. However, Mr Manghat does not specifically identify what intervening
         conduct or events he relies on.

330. Mr Manghat did not develop his argument about article 290 of the Civil Code. If and in so
         far as Dr Shetty or others under his direction acted in fraud of NMCH or its associated
         companies, it was not explained how, under UAE law, their acts would be treated as acts
         of NMCH. I do not consider that they would be, and in any case, I would not consider this
         a proper case to reduce Mr Manghat’s liability under article 290.

ADCB’s claims

UAE law

331. ADCB’s claims are made under articles 282 and 285 of the Civil Code and article 84 of
         the CCL 2015. I need add nothing to what I have already said about articles 282 and 285
         of the Civil Code. I should say something more about ADCB’s claim based on the plea
         that “Mr Manghat was a Manager within the meaning of Article 84 of the 2015 CCL in
         respect of the Manghat Relevant LLCs”.

332. The definition of the “Manghat Relevant LLCs” covered ten companies, but at trial
         ADCB’s case was presented only on the basis that Mr Manghat was a manager of NMCH
         and Al Zahra. It says (i) that Mr Manghat was formally appointed as a manager of NMCH
         on 20 June 2018; (ii) that he was de facto a manager of NMCH from 2011; and (iii) that he
         was formally appointed as a manager of Al Zahra on 1 March 2017, Al Zhara having been
         acquired by the NMC Group in February 2017. In fact, as Mr Pillai acknowledged, it is

         sufficient for ADCB’s claim that Mr Manghat was a manager of NMCH at the material
         times.

333. Mr Manghat admits that he was appointed as “Manager/Board Member” of NMCH on 20
         June 2018, and as a “Manager” of Al Zahra on 1 March 2017. I find it more difficult to
         understand Mr Manghat’s pleaded position about whether he was a de facto manager of
         NMCH before 20 June 2018, and Mr Hennessey did not develop or explain this part of Mr
         Manghat’s case during the trial or in the Post-Hearing Submissions. Mr Manghat’s
         defence denies that “ADCB is entitled to claim compensation from Mr Manghat under
         Article 84 as he was not a ‘manager’ in the relevant sense”. He also pleads, somewhat
         obliquely, that: “The use of the term director/manager is unknown to UAE company law
         and wrongly equivocates the differing roles, corporate governance structures, and in
         many cases differing systems of law”, and that the concept of a “de facto
         director/manager” is “unknown to UAE law”. Further, he avers that: “Article 84 governs
         the conduct of formally appointed managers (as defined in in [sic] the CCL) of LLCs who
         are recorded on the company trade licence”.

334. Whatever the intended effect of this pleading, I have reached these conclusions:

         a. Mr Manghat acted as a de facto manager of NMCH at all times relevant to ADCB’s
                   claim.

         b. A person may be liable as a “Manager” under article 84 of CCL 2015 and the
                   corresponding provisions of its predecessor, CCL 1984, if he in fact acted as a
                   company’s manager.

         c. It is not necessary for a person to be liable under article 84 of CCL 2015 and under
                   the corresponding provisions of CCL 1984 that he be recorded as a manager on
                   the trade licence.

335. As for the first conclusion, I accept ADCB’s submission that Mr Manghat clearly fulfilled
         the role of a de facto manager of NMCH from 2011. As I have said, from the time of his
         appointment as CFO of NMCH in February 2011, Mr Krishnamoorthy reported to him
         directly and Mr Suresh Kumar reported to Mr Kristnamoorthy; and in his witness
         statement, Mr Manghat described his role as including “operational matters within the
         finance department such as ownership of budgets and risk assessments, tracking cash
         flow and keeping track of NMC’s financial health”.

336. I heard submissions from Mr Al Hashimi that a de facto manager may be liable under
         article 84 of CCL 2015, notwithstanding he has not formally been appointed as a
         manager. He cited three cases in support of this proposition, but I need refer only to a
         decision of the Ras Al-Khaimah Court of Cassation of 27 August 2024, 54/2024. In that
         case, the first appellant was employed by the second appellant company, and he
         deceived the second respondent into paying a deposit. At first instance, the claim against

         the employee failed on the basis that he was only the manager and his employer
         company alone was liable. That decision was reversed in the Ras Al-Khaimah Court of
         Appeal, whose decision was upheld by the Court of Cassation. The Court of Cassation
         said this (referring to the employee as “the partner”): “[I]f the partner undertakes
         management activities in the company and serves as the responsible manager, then he
         shall be liable towards the company, its partners and third parties for compensating the
         damages resulting from his fault or negligence in management, and for all acts of fraud,
         abuse of power, and any violation of the law or the company’s articles of association –
         particularly in dealings with the company’s creditors or contractors in a manner that
         leads to the misappropriation of their funds and causes them to lose their rights”. The
         case turned upon what activities the employee undertook, and not upon any formal
         appointment.

337. As for the pleaded case that a person should be named in the company’s trade licence,

         effectively abandoned any reliance on this point in his submissions on UAE law. He
         accepted that this is not a statutory requirement. His case is that: “As a matter of
         practice, a person’s name will not be listed as Manager on the trade licence unless they
         were formally appointed via one of the methods under Article 83 and have provide
         evidence of the same to the relevant corporate authority”.

                   of US$ 1 billion (the “B1 Facility”), and a revolving credit facility of US$ 400 million

342. Mr Manghat pleads that “the limitation period for an action under Article 84 of the 2015

343. The relevant provision of UAE law is article 298 of the Civil Code: “No claim for indemnity
         arising out of a harmful act shall be heard after the expiration of three years from the day
         on which the victim became aware of the occurrence of the harm and of the identity of
         the person responsible for it”. However, this is subject to article 484 of the Civil Code:
         “The prescription period shall be interrupted upon a judicial claim being made or by any
         judicial proceeding being taken by an obligee to enforce his right”: see the judgment of
         the Dubai Court of Cassation of 24 July 2024 (2/2024). Thus, as ADCB submits, the
         prescription period under UAE law was interrupted by the proceedings brought by ADCB
         in England against Mr Manghat and others on 2 December 2020. (UAE law does not, as I
         understand it, consider that the prescription period is interrupted by foreign proceedings
         before a court or tribunal that lacks jurisdiction, but ADCB’s English proceedings were
         not stayed on the basis that the English Court had no jurisdiction over them but because
         it declined to exercise its jurisdiction.)

344. It appears from Mr Manghat’s UAE law submissions that there is no issue between the
         parties about the relevant UAE law. As I understand his position, he maintains that the
         claim under article 84 of CCL 2015 is barred because it was introduced by amendment
         pursuant to permission granted at a hearing on 31 August 2023, and it was based upon
         materially different facts from what ADCB had previously alleged.

345. I cannot accept that. At the hearing on 31 August 2023, counsel then representing Mr
         Manghat argued that the amendment to introduce the claim under article 84 should not
         be permitted because it was not based on “substantially the same” facts as claims that
         were already pleaded. This argument was based upon the Limitation Act 1980 (UK).
         Under section 35 of that Act, a cause of action introduced by amendment is deemed to
         have been made when the action was originally brought, but amendments to allow this
         are permitted only if they arise out of the same or substantially the same facts as are
         already in issue in the proceedings. These rules governed the amendments to introduce
         the article 84 claim because, although, under the Foreign Limitation Periods Act 1984
         (UK), the question of limitation is generally subject to UAE law, being the law governing
         the claim, nevertheless the English doctrine of relation back applies and so for limitation
         purposes it is deemed to have been brought when the proceedings were brought: see
         sections 1 and 35 of the Limitation Act 1980 (UK). (Both the Foreign Limitation Periods Act
         1984 (UK) and the Limitation Act 1980 (UK) have force in the ADGM under the ADGM
         Application of English Law Regulations 2015.)

346. For reasons that I explained in my ruling on 31 August 2023, I rejected the argument on
         behalf of Mr Manghat. I considered that the claim under article 84 was made on
         substantially the same factual basis as claims already made. For the same reasons, I

         reject Mr Manghat’s argument that the claim is barred under UAE law because it is based

347. I therefore reject Mr Manghat’s limitation defence.

The 2019 allegations and subsequent matters

Introduction

348. I come to consider the evidence upon which the JA Claimants and ADCB rely in support
         of their contention that Dr Shetty and Mr Manghat were parties to the fraud. Perhaps
         somewhat counter-instinctively, it is convenient first to consider the JA Claimants’
         allegations about the involvement in early 2019 of Dr Shetty and Mr Manghat in matters
         arising from EY’s audit of the NMC Group for the year ended 31 December 2019, and in
         what happened thereafter, before coming to earlier events.

349. Some of the evidence about the audit was most directly concerned with the case against
         Baroda, but it is necessary to set out the history at some length. The JA Claimants not only
         contend that these matters, in themselves, give rise to claims against Dr Shetty and Mr
         Manghat, but also that they support their case that Dr Shetty and Mr Manghat were party
         to earlier wrongdoing, not least because their evidence about the later events shows that
         they were dishonest witnesses.

The first balance confirmations

350. The financial years for NMC plc ended on 31 December. On 27 December 2018, Mr
         Sameeh Ullah of EY wrote to Baroda’s Abu Dhabi branch asking for information for its
         audit about the balances on the accounts with it. The letter was countersigned on behalf
         of NMCH so as to authorise Baroda to provide the information. There were similar letters
         about other companies in the NMC Group.

351. By letters of the same date, NMCH also wrote to Baroda’s Abu Dhabi branch, asking it to
         provide “confirmation letters” addressed to EY’s Abu Dhabi office in respect of balances
         as of 31 December 2018 for identified accounts “along with other facility accounts
         maintained with you such as Term Deposits & Marginal Deposits, if any”. It said that a
         NMC employee, Mr Sajeem Rashid, was authorised to collect the confirmation from the
         branch “physically”.

352. On 7 January 2019, Ms Jissa Mary, who worked in the Advances Department of Baroda’s
         Abu Dhabi branch, sent to Mr Praveen Kumar draft balance confirmation letters in
         respect of NMCH’s accounts (and also accounts of NMC Specialty Hospital LLC and New
         Marketing and Trading Co LLC). Mr Praveen Kumar amended the draft letters and
         returned them to Ms Jissa Mary under cover of an email with the instruction “please
         proceed”. Mr Kumar’s amendments were significant: whereas Ms Mary’s drafts had
