         included a confirmation showing debit balances of some AED 369 million on a term loan
         (the AED 367 Million Facility) and of AED 170 million on an overdraft in the same letter as
         set out substantial credit balances, Mr Praveen Kumar provided two separate letters, one
         for the credit balances and the other for the debit balances. On 9 January 2019, Mr
         Jaideep Sharma of Baroda signed 15 separate letters, including letters recording
         separately the two large debit balances. Each letter was (as the JA Claimants put it) “self-
         contained, in the sense that it did not make clear that it was one of a set of 15”. Mr Praveen
         Kumar collected them from Baroda’s Abu Dhabi branch.

353. The JA Claimants’ case, which I accept, is that the NMC Group arranged to have these
         separate letters so that it could provide EY with confirmations of NMCH’s credit balances
         with Baroda without revealing the debit balances (on the AED 367 Million Facility and the
         overdraft). It could not use the confirmations drafted by Ms Mary because the debit
         balances were included in the same letter as the credit balances, and because the letter
         said that the overdraft facility with the debit balance of AED 170 million was “fully secured
         by pledge and lien” over term deposits. Those term deposits, which were of AED 300
         million in aggregate, were themselves confirmed in a separate letter.

354. Messages sent on WhatsApp by Mr Praveen Kumar to Mr Suresh Kumar on 9 January 2019
         show the purpose of the NMC Group executives. He wrote that initially the letters that he
         went to collect had mentioned “lien details under deposit confirmation”. He managed to
         have the reference to the lien removed.

The Usman letter

355. On 13 February 2013, Mr Jayakumar received an email that was signed in the name of
         Mohammed Usman. (It is unclear whether that was his real name.) It was headed
         “Confidential Information – Borrower in UAE”, and told Baroda that “one of the major
         groups based out of UAE to which your bank has a huge exposure, is in deep financial
         distress. The group has raised multiple loans across the various entities within the group.
         Its financials are not reliable at all as it is fully manipulated. The aggregate of the
         borrowings of the Group are more than 6x of that disclosed in its financials. Majority of its
         borrowings, including many of the facilities availed from your bank, are not disclosed in
         its financials. Unless you take immediate action, this will turn out to be another big NPA
         [sc. Non-Performing Asset] for your bank. You may write to the auditors of your major
         customers (at least the top 5 in UAE) directly and confirm the year end balances. You will
         be surprised with the outcome”.

356. In response, Baroda instructed officers of branches in the GCC zone, including the Abu
         Dhabi branch, to obtain certificates from the auditors of five identified customer groups,
         “certifying the present [outstanding] bank borrowing of the companies vis-à-vis the Bank
         borrowing as per their latest [balance sheets]”. Three of the five customers, including the
         NMC Group, banked with the Abu Dhabi branch, and Mr Jaideep Sharma contacted their
         auditors. He received replies from the auditors of the two other customers, and, having

         received no reply from EY about the NMC Group, he arranged a meeting. A note made by
         EY (the “EY Note”), which sets out EY’s record of their dealings with the balances on the
         NMC Group’s accounts at Baroda in February and March 2019, records that, on 25
         February 2019, Mr Sharma met Mr Ullah at EY’s offices. According to the EY Note, which
         I accept as accurate, Mr Sharma asked Mr Ullah to confirm that the balances provided by
         Baroda “appear[ed] in the NMC books as they ha[d] a regulatory requirement to ensure
         this”, but Mr Ullah declined to do so because of concerns about client confidentiality.
         Therefore, on 26 February 2019, Baroda sent EY a letter, signed by Mr Mahaveer Gupta,
         asking that EY provide the outstanding bank balances and borrowings as at 31 December
         2017 and 31 December 2018 for NMCH, Neopharma, UAE Exchange and NMC Specialty
         Hospital LLC.

Early March 2019

357. As it appears from the EY Note, EY decided to send Baroda copies of the confirmations
         that it had received from Baroda in January 2019, considering that this would give Mr
         Sharma “indirect confirmation” of what appeared in the NMC Group’s books.
         Accordingly, on 2 March 2019, Mr Ullah sent Baroda by email copies of 24 confirmation
         letters from Baroda. They did not include letters relating to four accounts with debit
         balances amounting to some AED 980 million, which, as I infer, had not been provided to
         EY by the NMC Group. In response, Mr Sharma asked whether EY had more confirmation
         letters from Baroda or whether they had sent the “full set”. Mr Ullah replied with an email
         attaching requests for balance confirmations that EY had sent for Baroda on 27
         December 2018, and he wrote “Kindly send us balance confirmations”.

358. Accordingly, at 4.15 pm on 2 March 2019, Mr Sharma sent an email setting out the
         balances on 31 December 2018 on 11 accounts that NMCH had with Baroda showing
         credit balances, and six accounts with debit balances, together with details of a bank
         guarantee. They included the four debit balances for which Mr Ullah had not sent
         confirmation letters earlier that day. Mr Sharma attached to his email the relevant
         confirmation letters, and he wrote: “We request you to advise if there is any difference or
         discrepancy”.

359. It appears that Mr Ullah spoke to the NMC Group about the information in Mr Sharma’s
         email. At 8.56 pm, he forwarded to Mr Deepak Ghosh a copy of Mr Sharma’s email and its
         attachments, and Mr Ghosh sent it on to Mr Manghat and other NMC executives.

360. On 3 March 2019, Mr Praveen Kumar sent to Baroda’s Credit Department, apparently for
         Mr Sharma, an email attaching four balance confirmation letters: they did not cover the
         four debit balances. Shortly afterwards, Mr Sharma sent Mr Ullah an email in which he
         wrote: “As per our telephonic talk and various discussions held, we request you to
         provide the confirmation of Bank balances of NMC Healthcare as on 31.12.18”.

361. These developments clearly caused a major problem for the NMC Group executives who

         were trying to finalise, and have EY certify, accounts for the year ended 31 December
         2018 without revealing the true liabilities to Baroda. The problem was the more urgent
         because, on 5 March 2019, NMC plc’s Audit Committee was due to meet with EY in order
         to finalise the financial statements for the year ended 31 December 2018.

362. On 3 March 2019, Lord Clanwilliam, as the Chairman of the Audit Committee, had an
         informal meeting (by way of what he termed a “dress rehearsal” for the formal meeting)
         with Mr Victor Veger, EY’s lead audit partner, and probably with others. Lord Clanwilliam
         recalled, and I accept, that he was told that Baroda had caused some confusion in the
         course of the audit by mixing up NMC Group accounts and personal accounts of Dr
         Shetty. Although Lord Clanwilliam suspected that this was nothing other than a
         confusion, his memory is corroborated by his contemporaneous note: “BANK OF
         BARODA. ADDITIONAL LOANS. 1bn AED. £200m!! Shenoy What is this Baroda Bank.
         Story??”.

363. Mr Veger’s mention to Lord Clanwilliam of confusion between accounts of the NMC
         Group and Dr Shetty’s personal accounts reflects how the NMC Group executives were
         going about dealing with their problem, and, as I infer, discussions that they had already
         had with EY. The EY Note records that EY considered that they required a letter from
         Baroda’s Abu Dhabi branch explaining the conflicting information that they had been
         given. EY provided a draft letter for the NMC Group to pass on to Baroda under cover of
         an email to Mr Ghosh dated 3 March 2019. The EY Note also states that it was agreed that
         EY would collect the letter “directly” from Baroda, and that “[o]n 4 March 2019, EY
         directly picked the letter from bank which confirmed that additional balances of AED 1
         billion doesn’t belong to NMC”.

364. A letter (the “First Resolution Letter”, as it was referred to during the trial) was indeed
         provided to EY. The First Resolution Letter was dated 4 March 2019 and appeared to be
         on Baroda’s letter paper, to be signed by Mr Jaideep Sharma and to bear Baroda’s stamp.
         It purported to confirm a list of balances on NMCH’s accounts with Baroda as of 31
         December 2018 which did not include the four debit balances. It went on to state that:
         “[F]ollowing facilities/balances communicated on email dated 2 March 2019 to Mr.
         Sameeh Ullah of Ernst & Young in his capacity as independent external auditors of
         [NMCH] for the audit as per December 31, 2018 do not belong to [NMCH]”; and it listed
         accounts including the four debit balances. The letter concluded: “These have been
         reported by an error”.

365. However, the First Resolution Letter was not signed by Mr Sharma, and did not come from
         Baroda: it was a forgery by NMC Group’s executives. I find that it was created with Mr
         Manghat’s knowledge and assistance. The WhatsApp and email exchanges between
         NMC Group executives on 3 March 2019 demonstrate this. At 11.47 am Mr Suresh Kumar
         sent Mr Manghat a draft of a proposed letter that was intended to deceive EY about the
         true liabilities. At about 3.45 pm Mr Praveen Kumar sent (i) an email to Mr Udayakumar
         Nair and (ii) a WhatsApp message to Mr Suresh Kumar to which he attached a draft letter

         to EY that listed balances with Baroda other than the four debit balances, and Mr Suresh

         letter that he had received from EY. In all material respects, the First Resolution Letter
         was in the same terms.

366. Their WhatsApp exchanges of 4 March 2019 also reflect how the NMC executives went
         about satisfying EY’s requirement that they collect the letter from the branch. As I
         interpret exchanges between Mr Suresh Kumar and Mr Ghosh, they were aware that Mr
         Sikkander Ali of EY was to collect it, and they arranged for Mr Praveen Kumar to meet him,
         presumably at or near the branch, and to hand over the First Resolution Letter. At 12.34
         pm, Mr Suresh Kumar reported to Mr Ghosh that Mr Praveen Kumar would be “going with
         Sikander any time now”; at 12.49 pm he reported to Mr Ghosh that Mr Ali had “collected
         and gone”; and at 12.51 pm he reported similarly to Mr Manghat. At 1.06 pm, Mr Praveen
         Kumar reported to Mr Suresh Kumar that Mr Ali had sent an “image” of the First
         Resolution Letter to Mr Ullah. Somehow, apparently through impersonation of Baroda
         staff, Mr Ali was tricked into believing that he had collected the forged letter from Baroda
         directly.

367. This trick, however, did not fully resolve the difficulties for those seeking to deceive EY
         because Mr Ullah wanted to speak to Mr Sharma after receiving the letter from Mr Ali. At
         11.29 am Mr Ghosh had sent a message to Mr Suresh Kumar: “After collecting Sameeh
         [sc. Mr Ullah] will talk to Jaydeep [sc. Mr Jaideep Sharma]”.

368. Apparently, they did speak. At 4.47 pm, Mr Ullah sent Mr Sharma an email in which he
         wrote: “Further to our call, as you requested to be specific about my query. Our query is
         noted below: Bank initially confirmed accounts (listed below) belong to NMC. However,
         in revised confirmation it was confirmed to us that these accounts doesn’t belong to
         NMC. We just want to understand the process flaw which resulted the revision of
         confirmation”. It listed accounts, including those with the four debit balances.

369. Mr Sharma was, of course, puzzled by this email, and did not understand the reference
         to a “revised confirmation”. He must have spoken to Mr Ullah about it: he does not, he
         said, recall the conversation, but Mr Ullah sent him another email at 5.51 pm, which was
         introduced with the words “[f]urther to our discussion”. He attached to it balance
         confirmation letters from Baroda, which did not include letters relating to the four debit
         balances. He also attached a copy of the First Resolution Letter. Mr Sharma, of course,
         realised that the confirmation letters were incomplete, and that, although the First
         Resolution Letter appeared to bear his signature, he had not signed it. However, having
         spoken to Mr Mahaveer Gupta, he responded to Mr Ullah without telling him that he had
         not signed the First Resolution Letter. He simply told EY by email that Baroda was “not
         confirming the balance of trailing mail [sic. Mr Ullah’s email of 4.47 pm] right now. We are
         examining the things internally. We will revert to you very shortly”, and later at 7.28 pm on
         4 March 2019 that “the Balances as on 31.12.2018 of [NMCH] which was informed vide
         our mail dated 02.03.2019 along with the Balance Certificates”, and repeated the

         balances set out in the email of 2 March 2019.

370. Late on 4 March 2019, at 6.55 pm, Mr Suresh Kumar set up a WhatsApp group called
         “Financial 2018” for Mr Manghat, Mr Shenoy, Mr Ghosh, Mr Udayakumar Nair and
         himself. Its purpose was to coordinate a way of handling the situation, and they
         exchanged their ideas that evening. Mr Suresh Kumar told Mr Ghosh that he had
         cancelled a trip to Dubai that was planned for 5 March 2019 because Baroda “need[ed]
         to be managed”.

371. The plan that was developed is apparent from a WhatsApp exchange at 4.37 pm on 5
         March 2019 between Mr Suresh Kumar and Mr Shenoy in which Mr Kumar outlines a
         conversation that might be held between EY and Baroda. He suggested that Baroda might
         explain that: “NMC is one of the oldest accounts we are having in our branch and our
         relationship started even before their listing, we are having their promotors accounts also
         and its legal obligation is with the share holders and not with N[MC]. It is just tagging the
         names that caused the error while preparing the confirmations manually”. I shall explain
         shortly why Mr Kumar was constructing such a conversation.

Dr Shetty becomes involved

372. Mr Manghat and Dr Shetty were due in London on 5 March 2019 for the meeting of NMC
         plc’s Audit Committee with EY to finalise the accounts. At 00.25 am on 5 March 2019, Mr
         Manghat sent Dr Shetty a WhatsApp message in which he wrote that there “is a big issue”
         and another in which he repeated “[i]t’s a big big problem”. He said that the problem was
         about “bank”: he was certainly referring to Baroda because he provided Dr Shetty with Mr
         Mahaveer Gupta’s telephone number. Mr Manghat was on his way to London, and he told
         Dr Shetty that Mr Suresh Kumar would speak to him. At 08.34 am on 5 March 2019, Dr
         Shetty sent Mr Manghat this WhatsApp message: “I talked to Mr Mahaveer [Gupta].
         Nothing to worry. He is proud of us. Any cooperation. He is ready” (with added
         punctuation).

373. The meeting of the Audit Committee took place in London at 2.00 pm on 5 March 2019. It
         was preceded by an informal buffet lunch in the board room. Lord Clanwilliam gave
         evidence that, in the course of it, a partner of EY, either Mr Veger or Mr Mobin Khan,
         mentioned that an error had occurred in connection with Baroda in that the Bank had
         apparently mixed up the accounts of the NMC Group and personal accounts of Dr Shetty,
         and sent EY statements about Dr Shetty’s personal accounts. Lord Clanwilliam said that
         he had a “very acute memory” of Dr Shetty saying that the accounts in question related
         to him and not to NMC. He accepted that Dr Shetty did not claim to have investigated the
         matter, but he recalled that Dr Shetty corroborated what EY had said about the accounts
         being his personal ones. He remembered thinking: “What is Dr Shetty doing with a $ 200
         million loan?”

374. In his second witness statement, Dr Shetty said that he had no recollection of saying this

         or of the buffet lunch. When cross-examined, he said that he recalled that there was a
         buffet lunch and when asked about Lord Clanwilliam’s account, he responded: “His
         recollection is correct, but the subject is wrong. 200 million, never, never, never”.

375. The minutes of the Audit Committee record that the meeting was attended by Lord
         Clanwilliam, Dr A Abdullah and Mr J Bomford as members of the Committee, and eleven
         invitees, including Dr Shetty, Mr Manghat, Mr Shenoy, Mr Ghosh, Mr Veger and Mr Khan.
         The Committee agreed that the draft annual report for 2018 should be presented to the
         board for approval.

376. The minutes themselves do not refer to the issue about the Baroda balances. However,
         an internal note of EY states that there was reference at the meeting to the “outstanding
         issue with respect to Bank of Baroda confirmation”, and that Dr Shetty explained that
         “this bank serves him across many investments and territories and he would not be
         surprised they have coupled too many of his accounts to NMC as this is his primary
         business venture”. Although Lord Clanwilliam had no recollection of this exchange, he
         did not dispute the accuracy of EY’s records, and I accept their accuracy.

377. It is the JA Claimants’ pleaded allegation that “Dr Shetty dishonestly corroborated the
         view expressed by EY that the statements by Baroda showing loans of around USD 200
         million were a mix-up and the loan was personal to him and not connected with NMC: Dr
         Shetty knew that that was not the case”. Dr Shetty’s defence pleads that he said that “the
         explanation provided by EY seemed plausible to him, because he and his businesses had
         extensive dealings with Baroda, making administrative errors not unlikely. This was an
         honest statement”. Mr Manghat pleads that at the meeting of the Audit Committee on 5
         March 2019, “Dr Shetty assured the Audit Committee and EY that the discrepancy
         between the earlier and the later confirmation letters was a personal debt and not a
         liability of the NMC Group. It was agreed at the meeting that this was a simple accounting
         mistake”.

378. As I have said, the JA Claimants contend that Dr Shetty acted dishonestly in supporting
         the explanation to EY that his accounts had been confused with NMCH accounts. They
         invite me to infer that, after Mr Manghat had contacted him about the “big, big problem”,
         he spoke to Mr Suresh Kumar, who asked him to play his part in the scheme by telling EY
         and the Audit Committee that the debts to Baroda were his personal liabilities, and he
         agreed to do so; and that, having spoken to Mr Gupta at Mr Suresh Kumar’s request to ask
         him that Baroda cease to communicate directly with EY, he was confident that the
         scheme would succeed.

379. Mr Wandowicz disputed this. He submitted that the far simpler, and more probable,
         explanation is that “Dr Shetty was told the same lie as EY” by the NMC executives. I
         cannot accept that submission, and I accept the JA Claimants’ contention.

380. First, Mr Wandowicz’s submission is not in line with what Dr Shetty pleads and is not

         supported by his evidence. His pleaded position is that he “does not recall the
         conversations with Mr Kumar or Mr Gupta”.

381. Secondly, if Dr Shetty had been told by Mr Suresh Kumar or Mr Manghat that the problem
         resulted from Baroda confusing his accounts and NMCH’s accounts, I would find it
         strange that he did not, as it appears from the evidence, say so either at the buffet lunch
         or at the meeting: his natural response when the matter arose would surely have been to
         say that he had been given the same explanation by the executives.

382. Thirdly, Mr Wandowicz’s submission does not fit with Mr Manghat’s WhatsApp
         messages: a mix-up of this kind between accounts would not have been a “big, big
         problem”. More importantly perhaps, if the strategy had been to convince Dr Shetty that
         the problem was simply the result of administrative confusion within Baroda, Mr Manghat
         would have minimised it when communicating with Dr Shetty about it, and he would not
         have described it as he did.

383. I should add that, although it was put to Dr Shetty that he knew of the forged First
         Resolution Letter, it is not an essential part of the JA Claimants’ case that he was aware
         of it by 5 March 2019. I need not, and do not, make any finding about that.

The fake telephone conversation and the Second Resolution Letter

384. EY did not regard the position about the Baroda balances as fully resolved by the time of
         the meeting of the Audit Committee. The EY Note records that “EY … requested NMC
         management to … confirm with the bank directly what is correct vs incorrect and in doing
         so NMC arranged a call between EY and the bank on 5 March 2019 afternoon. Call was
         attended by [EY representatives in London and Abu Dhabi]. From Bank side it was
         attended by [Mr Jaideep Sharma and Mr Mahaveer Gupta]. [Mr Mahaveer Gupta] explains
         that they have various facilities with Dr. B R Shetty and these are tagged manually. Due to
         manual tagging some of [the] personal facilities of Dr. Shetty were included under NMC
         account[s] by mistake. [Mr Jaideep Sharma and Mr Mahaveer Gupta] also confirmed that
         [the] letter issued on 4 March 2019 remains correct and confirmed that they understand
         the balances of AED 1 Billion will not appear in NMC books. EY requested [the] bank that
         due to all these confusions and multiple confirmations, we need a fresh letter from [the]
         bank and also requested [that] it would be good if [the] same can be signed by some body
         independent of [Mr Jaideep Sharma and Mr Mahaveer Gupta] in the bank”. (EY made a
         more detailed note of the telephone call, but this suffices for present purpose.)

385. In fact, there was no call between EY and Baroda. By a brazen trick, Mr Jaideep Sharma
         and Mr Mahaveer Gupta were impersonated on the call by, respectively, Mr Udayakumar
         Nair and Mr Suresh Kumar (or possibly another NMCH Executive). They conducted a
         conversation along the lines that Mr Suresh Kumar had outlined to Mr Shenoy in their
         WhatsApp exchanges on 5 March 2019 between 5.42pm and 7.14pm UAE time, after the
         meeting of the Audit Committee had started.

ADGMCFI-2022-299 – NMC HEALTHCARE LIMITED (IN ADMINISTRATION) (SUBJECT TO A DEED OF COMPANY ARRANGEMENT) AND OTHERS V BAVAGUTHU RAGHURAM
SHETTY AND OTHERS

                                                                                       05 November 2026 04:43 PM

386. On 5 March 2019, Mr Ullah sent to Mr Shenoy and Mr Ghosh an email attaching the draft
         letter that they required from Baroda, stating that they would “directly pick this from
         Bank”. It stated balances on the NMCH account, omitting the four accounts with debit
         balances. It was to have two signatories, and it was said to supersede “all other
         communications and confirmations sent earlier to Ernst & Young”. The NMC executives
         therefore had to complete their subterfuge by forging signatures on the letter (which was
         referred to at trial as the “Second Resolution Letter”) and by impersonating Baroda
         personnel when EY went to collect it from the branch the next day. The messages in the
         “Financial 2018” WhatsApp group reflect their arrangements and show that Mr Manghat,
         a member of the group, was privy to them.

387. The NMC executives again succeeded in tricking EY: the EY Note records that “[o]n 6
         March 2019, EY directedly picked the letter from [the] bank. EY noted that [the] letter is
         signed by the same persons ([Mr Jaideep Sharma and Mr Mahaveer Gupta]) however, EY’s
         request that it also need to have an independent signature was not accommodated. EY
         then sent back the letter to [the] bank and [the] bank reissued the letter which was
         counter signed by Easwar [Nagarajan], Chief Manager – Corporate banking. On receipt of
         [the] letter, EY agreed it with NMC books and noted that it is now tying to NMC books”.

388. Mr Manghat continued to be party to the dealings with EY. When the 2018 financial
         statements were finally approved and signed by EY late on 6 March 2026, Mr Manghat
         sent WhatsApp messages on the “Financial 2018” group saying that: “God is great” and
         “[t]hat power helped us do this”.

Mr Devarakonda Ananda Kumar’s meeting with Mr Manghat and Mr Shenoy

389. However, the problems with EY were not finally resolved on 5 March 2019. On 11 March
         2019, Mr Jaideep Sharma sent an email to Mr Ullah, explaining that Baroda was merging
         with two other banks, and requesting that EY “provide us the confirmation of trailing mail
         (which was sent on 04.03.2019 at 7.28 PM) that the exposure mentioned in trailing mail is
         considered in Bank Borrowings and Bank Balances”. EY did not reply to Baroda, but Mr
         Ullah contacted the NMC Group about it: on 14 March 2019, Mr Ghosh wrote to Mr Suresh
         Kumar that Mr Ullah, was “here asking about the bank issue”, and on 17 March 2019, Mr
         Ullah forwarded the email to Mr Prasanth Shenoy, with a copy to Mr Manghat, and asked
         to discuss it.

390. On 17 March 2019, Mr Manghat and Mr Shenoy met Mr Devarakonda Ananda Kumar in
         Oman. The JA Claimants plead that Mr D A Kumar, Mr Manghat and Mr Shenoy discussed
         the “audit issue” and the repayment of outstanding debt owed to Baroda by the NMC
         Group. They do not accept that it was a chance meeting: their contention is that it was
         one of two meetings arranged by the NMC Group executives that led to an agreement that
         the NMC Group would repay virtually all its liabilities to Baroda in exchange for Baroda
         not revealing the true position about its accounts with the NMC Group, the second
         meeting being between Dr Shetty and Mr Jayakumar in India.

ADGMCFI-2022-299 – NMC HEALTHCARE LIMITED (IN ADMINISTRATION) (SUBJECT TO A DEED OF COMPANY ARRANGEMENT) AND OTHERS V BAVAGUTHU RAGHURAM
SHETTY AND OTHERS

                                                                                       05 November 2026 04:43 PM

391. Mr D A Kumar’s account of the meeting on 17 March 2019 is pleaded in Baroda’s defence.
         It is that he was in Muscat for reasons unconnected with the NMC Group and “Mr
         Manghat and Mr Shenoy were also in Oman on business, staying at the same hotel as Mr
         D A Kumar and the three of them had an informal chat in the hotel lobby. Insofar as it is
         alleged that this constituted a formal meeting, that is denied: it was a short, chance
         encounter. Mr Shenoy mentioned the discrepancy in the information that had been
         provided by EY MENA. He told Mr D A Kumar that: (a) the Bank had provide the correct
         balances to EY MENA; and (b) this had resulted in EY MENA signing off the balance sheets
         for the NMC Group. Mr D A Kumar therefore believed that the issue had been resolved
         upon the Bank providing correct information to EY MENA. The parties also briefly
         discussed the repayment of the debt, where Mr D A Kumar explained that the [Central
         Bank of the UAE] believed the Bank to be in breach of regulatory restrictions in UAE
         relating to banks’ exposure as a proportion of their capital base”.

392. According to Mr D A Kumar, the background to the exchange about the NMC Group
         repaying its debts to Baroda was this. The regulatory regime of the Central Bank of the
         UAE (“CBUAE”), to which Baroda’s Abu Dhabi branch was subject, placed a limit on the
         permissible exposure of any individual customer by reference the bank’s total balance
         sheet. Mr Kumar said that in late 2018, the CBUAE expressed concern about Baroda’s
         exposure to the NMC Group in view of the AED 367 Million Facility; that his understanding
         was that the CBUAE would allow a “grace period” until the end of March 2019 to remedy
         the position; and that the NMC Group had been informed that they would have to repay
         Baroda by the end of March 2019. (This evidence was strongly disputed by the JA
         Claimants, but I do not need to resolve that issue.)

393. Mr Manghat pleads in his defence that he “has no knowledge” of the meeting. However,
         in his witness statement in reply to Mr D A Kumar’s account, he said that he was in Oman
         on other matters, and by chance he met Mr D A Kumar. He said that he had no recollection
         of Mr D A Kumar mentioning the AED 367 Million Facility, but that “if he had done so I
         would have told him that Dr Shetty was meeting P S Jayakumar to discuss the loan”. Mr D
         A Kumar’s evidence was that Mr Manghat and Mr Shenoy had said that Dr Shetty would
         be meeting Mr Jayakumar “soon” to speak about repayment of the loan.

394. I am unable to accept that Mr Manghat and Mr Shenoy met Mr D A Kumar by chance,
         having gone to Muscat for other reasons. The evidence show that their flights were not
         booked until late on 16 March 2019, and they flew to Oman in the early hours of 17 March
         2019, arriving at their hotel at 3.00 am. They left the hotel at 9.28 am to return to Abu
         Dhabi. Shortly after they arrived at the hotel, and apparently before meeting Mr D A
         Kumar, Mr Manghat sent these WhatsApp messages to Dr Shetty, who had flown to
         Assam, India on 16 March 2019: “Urgently need to speak to you. It’s manageable but your
         presence is needed today. Will speak. Thanks”; “Regarding Bank of Baroda”; and “Sir it’s
         better if you can reach [M]umbai today. Will let you know in an hour”. It appears that Dr
         Shetty did not reply by WhatsApp to these messages: about two and a half hours after the
         last message, Mr Manghat wrote again, “Ignore above messages. We spoke after this”. I

         conclude that Mr Manghat and Mr Shenoy had gone to Oman in order to discuss with Mr
         D A Kumar the continuing difficulties about the audit. This does not necessarily mean that
         they had made an arrangement with Mr D A Kumar to meet him: the meeting might have
         come as a surprise to him: I need not decide that question.

Dr Shetty’s meeting with Mr Jayakumar

395. There was a meeting between Dr Shetty and Mr Jayakumar on 18 March 2019 at Baroda’s
         offices in Mumbai, Dr Shetty having flown there from Assam.

396. Mr Manghat’s pleaded case about this meeting is that the Abu Dhabi branch of Baroda
         was unable to explain why contradictory letters were sent to EY, and that they suggested
         that he discuss the matter with “senior management”, and that he and Mr Shenoy
         “accompanied Dr Shetty … on a trip to India, where Dr Shetty met alone met with [Mr
         Jayakumar]”. He continued that, while it “appeared from the outcome of that meeting
         that Dr Shetty once again agreed to settle the liability”, Mr Manghat denied that he (Mr
         Manghat) had promised Baroda that “the NMC Group would repay all its outstanding debt
         to the bank by the end of March 2019”.

397. Dr Shetty’s pleaded case about the meeting is this. He admits that he had a meeting with
         Mr Jayakumar “in or about March 2019”. He then pleads that “Dr Shetty was asked to
         attend that meeting (at very short notice) by Mr Manghat. … When Dr Shetty arrived at the
         meeting, he was met by Mr Shenoy (alone), and Mr Shenoy informed him that Mr Manghat
         and he were meeting Mr Jayakumar, and that in light of Mr Jayakumar’s seniority within
         Baroda, Mr Manghat considered it desirable for Dr Shetty to meet with Mr Jayakumar first.
         Mr Manghat himself had not yet arrived, and Dr Shetty did not see him that day”. He said
         that he met Mr Jayakumar “briefly” in Mr Jayakumar’s office. They were alone, and “to the
         best of Dr Shetty’s recollection, [they discussed] the plan to list UAE Exchange”. He said
         that he did not know what was discussed at any subsequent meeting that Mr Jayakumar
         had with Mr Manghat and Mr Shenoy.

398. When Dr Shetty first pleaded his defence, he averred that Mr Manghat provided him with
         no explanation for asking him to go to Mumbai to meet Mr Jayakumar. It seems to me
         incredible that he would go to Mumbai at very short notice without being given a reason
         to do so. After WhatsApp exchanges were disclosed, Dr Shetty accepted that he was
         given an explanation, although he has no recollection of it. He also pleads that he “would
         have acted according to Mr Manghat’s counsel”.

399. In his second witness statement, Dr Shetty said that he spoke with Mr Jayakumar “briefly
         about the plan to list UAE Exchange” and that he did not discuss with him “NMC Group’s
         debt obligations”. When he was cross-examined, Dr Shetty confirmed his written
         evidence and his (amended) pleaded case about what was discussed at his meeting with
         Mr Jayakumar.

400. Mr Jayakumar gave evidence about a meeting with Dr Shetty alone on, as he inferred from
         the documents, 18 March 2019. He said that he had been told before the meeting by Mr
         D A Kumar that the NMC Group was going to repay the AED 367 Million Facility because
         of the regulatory issue. Mr Jayakumar’s account of the meeting was that Dr Shetty told
         him of the proposal to repay the facility, and that he asked whether “he could obtain some
         additional limit for one of the other companies in the group”. When Mr Jayakumar replied
         that he did not think that Baroda could lend more without further repayment, Dr Shetty
         offered to repay the remainder of the NMC Group’s overdraft and short-term borrowing.
         Mr Jayakumar responded that he should discuss that with the branch. There was also
         some discussion of Dr Shetty’s plans to list the UAE Exchange business. He denied that
         he reached any agreement about repayment of the AED 367 Million Facility “in exchange
         for the Bank turning a blind eye to the incorrect information that had been provided to EY
         MENA”. His evidence was that he did not recall discussing the balance confirmations
         with Dr Shetty or any other representative of the NMC Group.

401. The WhatsApp messaging between Dr Shetty and Mr Manghat shows that Dr Shetty asked
         Mr Manghat to brief him before his meeting, and Mr Manghat’s response. Dr Shetty wrote
         early on 18 March 2019: “Good morning what was the discussion with [Mr Jayakumar]?
         What exactly happened! Please explain so that I speak on the same page let me prepare
         myself by noontime I will arrive Mumbai”.

402. Mr Manghat gave this account in reply:

                    “1. [Mr D A Kumar] briefed [Mr Jayakumar] 4 days back all what happened
                   including documents.

                   2. [Mr Jayakumar] was worried whether we have issues but for which [Mr D A
                   Kumar] comforted.

                   3. [Mr Jayakumar] used his [EY] contact in India to help them to get information
                   from [UAE] EY. This is not legal so they can't pursue.

                   4. This Saturday [16 March 2019] I got a call from EY [UAE] that they received a
                   new mail from Bank stating all issues.

                   5. [Mr] Shenoy spoke to [Abu Dhabi] BOB [sc. Abu Dhabi branch of Baroda] who
                   suggested to speak to [Mr D A Kumar]. [Abu Dhabi] team was helpful this time.

                   6. [Mr] Shenoy and me met [Mr D A Kumar] today morning in [M]uscat. He told us
                   the entire issue and showed his willingness to help.

                   7. He spoke to EY [I]ndia and stopped them in contacting EY [UAE]. This is very
                   good[.]

                   8. He also spoke to [Mr Jayakumar] explainin[g] that these are not in accounts and
                   client ready to pay off without affecting credibility[.]

                   9. [Mr Jayakumar] was very upset but concluded that client close the account[.]

                   10. This settlement to be done this week. No option. This will also stop EY issue[.]

                  Sir, the only way is somehow convince [Mr Jayakumar] they [accept] our funds and
                  close but without probing.

                  We have done good analysis and ha[ve] clear story for all his probable questions
                  which I can send maybe after you read this. Also we can speak before we go to
                  [Baroda]”.

403. Dr Shetty’s responses indicated no surprise about this briefing. He thanked Mr Manghat
         for it, and wrote “No issues take it easy he is our wellwisher”.

404. The WhatsApp messaging shows clearly that Dr Shetty was aware of the problems that
         Mr Manghat and others were trying to resolve by the time that he met Mr Jayakumar. Dr
         Shetty said that he could not remember the messages, and Mr Wandowicz submitted that
         “Dr Shetty cannot explain a message he does not recall”. There is no explanation for the
         messages that is consistent with Dr Shetty’s case. The messages speak for themselves
         unambiguously.

405. Before I determine what was discussed between Dr Shetty and Mr Jayakumar, I refer to
         two issues about the arrangements for the meeting between the JA Claimants and Dr
         Shetty:

         a. Whether Dr Shetty went to Mumbai specifically in order to meet Mr Jayakumar, as
                   the JA Claimants contend. Dr Shetty’s case is, as Mr Wandowicz put it in his
                   closing submissions, that “it is tolerably clear that … he was already going to be
                   in Mumbai for a meeting, and this [sc. the meeting with Mr Jayakumar] was just
                   slotted in at short notice when Mr Manghat asked”; and

         b. Whether Dr Shetty met with Mr Manghat before the meeting to be briefed further.
                   Dr Shetty’s evidence was that he did not meet Mr Manghat at any time on this visit
                   to Mumbai. He said that he saw Mr Shenoy when he went to Baroda’s offices and
                   he recalled that Mr Shenoy touched his feet.

406. On the first issue, I conclude that Dr Shetty went to Mumbai specifically for the meeting.
         He had flown from Abu Dhabi to Assam in his private jet on 16 March 2019. He left Assam
         on 18 March 2019, and, flying via Kishangarh, Rajasthan, he arrived in Mumbai at 2.15 pm.
         He left for Abu Dhabi at 5.00 pm after 2 hours 45 minutes. Even if he did not meet Mr
         Manghat before the meeting with Mr Jayakumar, he would not, on the face of it, have had
         time for a separate meeting with an (unidentified) third party.

407. The JA Claimants submitted that this is significant. Whereas, as Mr Jayakumar testified
         “[o]n occasion, Dr Shetty … visited Baroda’s corporate office in Mumbai when he was in
         Bombay” and they had “high level” conversations, the meeting on 18 March 2019, as the
         JA Claimants argued, “was not an ordinary commercial relationship meeting. Dr Shetty
         made a substantial detour, for a very short visit to Mumbai, at a critical moment in the
         March 2019 events specifically to see Mr Jayakumar”. Mr Wandowicz observed that the
         detour would be less burdensome when travelling by private jet, but I still see force in the
         JA Claimants’ point.

408. Mr Wandowicz made three points in support of Dr Shetty’s case about this. First, and
         most importantly, he relied on a schedule for Dr Shetty’s travels between 16 and 18
         March 2019 that he had disclosed. It includes the visit to Mumbai and an entry “Meeting
         in Trident BKC [a hotel in the Bandra Kurla Complex] on Monday 18 March at 3.15pm”. Mr
         Wandowicz argued that this timing would allow Dr Shetty, having landed in Mumbai at
         2.15 pm, to go to Baroda’s offices for a short meeting before 3.15 pm. Furthermore, Mr
         Wandowicz submitted that it shows that the 3.15 pm meeting was recorded on the
         schedule for the trip when Dr Shetty set off on his travels on 16 March 2019.

409. Mr Wandowicz’s second argument was based on the terminology in Mr Manghat’s
         WhatsApp message on 16 March 2019: “Sir it’s better if you can reach [M]umbai today”.
         It was argued that this wording indicates that Mr Manghat knew that Dr Shetty would be
         going to Mumbai and his request concerned when it was “better” that he should arrive,
         rather than that he should go at all.

410. Thirdly, he pointed to the evidence that Mr D A Kumar had been told at the meeting in
         Oman that Dr Shetty would be meeting Mr Jayakumar “soon”, and he argued that this
         indicated that the meeting had already been arranged.

411. Mr Thanki convincingly answered Mr Wandowicz’s first argument. He pointed out that the
         schedule, and comparable documents, are in the nature of programmes that would likely
         have been updated from time to time as plans for the trip developed. There is no good
         reason to think that the entries on which Mr Wandowicz relies were in a document
         completed before Dr Shetty left Abu Dhabi on 16 March 2019.

412. I can place little weight on Mr Wandowicz’s second argument: the general style of Mr
         Manghat’s WhatsApp messages does not bear such exact analysis.

413. Mr D A Kumar’s evidence does not suggest that a meeting had been arranged, but only
         that one was expected.

414. Further, there is no support from either Dr Shetty’s evidence or his disclosure for the
         contention that Dr Shetty went to Mumbai for another meeting that had been planned in
         advance. Accordingly, there is no information about whom Dr Shetty might have been
         meeting or his purpose in doing so. In any case, I do not find it credible that, if there were

         such a meeting with a third party, no documentary trace of it remains.

415. I conclude that, if Dr Shetty did meet Mr Manghat before seeing Mr Jayakumar, there
         would simply have been no time during Dr Shetty’s stop in Mumbai for even the briefest
         of meetings with Mr Jayakumar before a 3.15 pm meeting with a third party. This leads to
         the second issue. Again, the WhatsApp exchanges are the best evidence about this. At
         1.38 pm, Mr Manghat sent Dr Shetty a message: “We are in lobby of hotel”. Shortly
         afterwards, Dr Shetty sent a message: “On my way shall we” (sic.). Dr Shetty had no
         convincing explanation for this exchange. I conclude that Dr Shetty met Mr Manghat and
         Mr Shenoy before he went to Baroda’s offices, and the purpose can only have been to
         discuss with them how he should conduct the meeting with Mr Jayakumar. This is likely
         the meeting referred to in the schedule.

416. Whatever was discussed at the meeting between Dr Shetty and Mr Jayakumar, Mr
         Manghat was clearly relieved at the outcome, and grateful to Dr Shetty. He sent him a
         WhatsApp message that evening: “Thanks a lot for the way you led us. God is kind to us”.
         The language of this message to my mind confirms my conclusion that Mr Manghat had
         met Dr Shetty. So too does an email that Dr Shetty sent to Mr Jayakumar, which refers to
         his “colleagues”, and which is most naturally understood as referring to Mr Manghat and
         Mr Shenoy.

417. This email was sent to Mr Jayakumar by Dr Shetty on 19 March 2019. In it, he thanked Mr
         Jayakumar in effusive terms for “the time and courtesies extended to my colleagues and
         me as we came over to see you”; he wrote that he “wish[ed] to thank from the bottom of
         my heart at the display of your understanding of an entrepreneur’s pain”; and also for Mr
         Jayakumar’s “support and guidance on NMC”. It is apparent from the WhatsApp
         exchanges that Mr Manghat had helped to draft the email. The JA Claimants contend that
         its extravagant expressions of gratitude support their case that Mr Jayakumar had agreed
         to help cover up the problem with the balance confirmations.

418. When Dr Shetty was asked about the letter in cross-examination, he responded that “it’s
         not my letter”. I accept that he did not draft it, but he approved the wording: he responded
         to the draft provided to him: “Thanks Prasanth, excellent”. On 20 March 2019, he
         forwarded the text to his son, Binay, who replied, “Very well written”.

419. In late March 2019, NMCH repaid some AED 1,000 million of its liabilities to Baroda,
         including the facilities referred to in the confirmation letters that the NMC Group had not
         produced to EY. In parallel, the NMC Group also placed offset deposits with Baroda
         totalling AED 875 million.

420. Dr Shetty was interested in ensuring that the NMC Group made these repayments. In his
         defence, Dr Shetty pleaded that he “has no contemporaneous knowledge of the
         repayment of NMC Group’s liabilities to Baroda and, for the avoidance of doubt, was not
         aware of or party to any discussions relating to the same”. I cannot accept that denial

                                                                                                                                                                     112

                                                                                       05 November 2026 04:43 PM

         because it is inconsistent with this exchange by WhatsApp between Dr Shetty and Mr
         Manghat on 31 March 2019: Dr Shetty wrote to Mr Manghat: “Good evening please inform
         [Baroda] we closed”, and Mr Manghat replied, “Yes we [did]”. Shortly afterwards, Dr
         Shetty wrote: “[Completely] paid? As on which date?”. Mr Manghat’s reply is not in
         evidence, but Dr Shetty was satisfied and concluded the exchange with the message:
         “Good”.

421. When asked about these exchanges in cross-examination, Dr Shetty said, “We don’t
         know who sent this message”, and he insisted that he did not want to check that Baroda’s
         facilities were repaid. However, the WhatsApp exchange was disclosed by Dr Shetty, and,
         when Mr Manghat was cross-examined, it was not suggested to him that the exchange
         was not a genuine one.

422. In his closing submissions, Mr Wandowicz did not seek to maintain the pleaded case. He
         acknowledged that, in their WhatsApp messages, “Mr Manghat plainly did advise Dr
         Shetty that it was vital for NMC to repay Baroda’s liabilities”, and “it definitely would have
         got through to Dr Shetty”. He submitted that, in this context, it “is entirely to be expected
         of Dr Shetty to ask: how are you doing? Have we done what you said was so vital?”. He
         submitted on this basis that the exchanges were “consistent with both parties’ cases”.

423. I disagree. First, the exchanges are not consistent with Dr Shetty’s (original or amended)
         pleaded case. Secondly, the submission is inconsistent with Dr Shetty’s evidence, which
         itself is not credible. Thirdly, Dr Shetty’s enquiries about repayment reflect Dr Shetty’s
         real concern that the facilities were repaid, and more than merely an interest about
         whether Mr Manghat had carried out his plans. They support the JA Claimants’ case that
         Dr Shetty had himself given a commitment to Mr Jayakumar.

424. In an internal Baroda letter of 13 May 2019, Mr D A Kumar explained why Baroda had not
         charged NMCH for repaying the AED 367 Million Facility early: that he had instructed the
         Abu Dhabi branch “on the instructions of our higher management on 18.03.2019, to settle
         the outstanding under [NMCH]”; that the AED 367 Million Facility had been repaid on 25
         March 2019; and that the working capital facilities reduced to nothing on 28 March 2019.
         He said that no prepayment penalty charge had been levied since the facilities, including
         the AED 367 Million Facility, had been “settled at the instance of our bank”.

425. I return to what was discussed at the meeting between Dr Shetty and Mr Jayakumar. I
         reject Dr Shetty’s evidence that the discussion was only about a plan to list UAE
         Exchange, and that there was no discussion of the NMC Group’s liabilities to Baroda. I
         am prepared to accept that there might have been some mention of the listing of UAE
         Exchange, but I find that there was discussion about the NMC Group’s liabilities and
         settlement of them. Dr Shetty’s evidence about that is inconsistent with Mr Jayakumar’s
         evidence. Furthermore, it is inconsistent with the briefing for the meetings that he had
         requested and received from Manghat. Nor does it explain why the NMC Group’s
         borrowings were repaid shortly after the meeting.

ADGMCFI-2022-299 – NMC HEALTHCARE LIMITED (IN ADMINISTRATION) (SUBJECT TO A DEED OF COMPANY ARRANGEMENT) AND OTHERS V BAVAGUTHU RAGHURAM
SHETTY AND OTHERS

                                                                                                                                                                     113

                                                                                       05 November 2026 04:43 PM

426. The JA Claimants advanced persuasive arguments in their closing submissions in support
         of their case that Dr Shetty and Mr Jayakumar agreed that the NMC Group would repay
         virtually all their liabilities to Baroda in exchange for Baroda not revealing the true position
         about their accounts with Baroda. However, I did not hear Baroda’s response to the JA
         Claimants’ submissions. It would be unfair to Baroda and to Mr Jayakumar personally to
         make a finding about that, and it is unnecessary to do so in order to deal with the claims
         before me.

427. I therefore reach these conclusions about what Dr Shetty said at the meeting to deal with
         what Mr Manghat called the “EY issue”. The objective of Mr Manghat and the NMC Group,
         as Mr Manghat had briefed Dr Shetty, was that Baroda should be content to have their
         exposure to the NMC Group repaid and should not probe further into the EY issue. It was
         hoped that this could be achieved, although Mr Jayakumar was “worried”, and, as Mr
         Manghat understood (whether rightly or not) and as he told Dr Shetty, Mr D A Kumar had
         explained to Mr Jayakumar that Baroda’s loans “are not in accounts and client [sc. the
         NMC Group] ready to pay off without affecting credibility”. As Dr Shetty pleaded, he acted
         according to Mr Manghat’s counsel, and he spoke to Mr Jayakumar with a view to securing
         the result that Mr Manghat sought. Accordingly, I conclude that he assured Mr Jayakumar
         that the NMC Group would repay facilities that Baroda had extended to it so that Baroda
         was no longer exposed; and that he did so because he hoped that, in these
         circumstances, Baroda would not reveal to EY the true position on the Baroda accounts
         as of 31 December 2018.

428. I reach no conclusion as to what Mr D A Kumar had in fact told Mr Jayakumar about the
         EY issue before the meeting. Nor do I reach any conclusion about whether, in response
         to Dr Shetty, Mr Jayakumar said that Baroda would be content to be repaid without doing
         anything about the EY issue. It might be that Dr Shetty judged at the meeting that, having
         been promised that the borrowings from Baroda would be repaid, Mr Jayakumar and
         Baroda were satisfied, and he decided not to press for any express commitment about
         the EY issue, perhaps judging that it would be impolitic to do so.

The response to the Muddy Waters Reports

429. I go next to the response of Mr Manghat and others at the NMC Group after the First Muddy
         Waters Report was published on 17 December 2019. As I have said, Mr Zo’Mot and Mr El
         Khaldi at ADCB were seriously concerned about it, but at a meeting on 18 December
         2019, Mr Manghat told them that the Report was “short seller noise”. He repeated such
         assurances over the coming weeks. He also promised that a substantial part of NMC’s
         debts to ADCB would be repaid. In fact, US$ 400 million was repaid on the C1 Facility, but
         otherwise promised repayments were not made.

430. ADCB contended that Mr Manghat was making promises that were unrealistic and giving
         assurances that he knew were untrue. Mr Manghat denied that: he said that the NMC
         Group was “holding discussions” and that the “principal shareholders are also giving me

         belief that the funds are coming in”. I accept ADCB’s contention, and I do not accept Mr
         Manghat’s evidence. His WhatsApp exchanges show that the NMC Group was facing
         severe cash deficiencies: for example, he wrote on 20 January 2020 that Noor Capital
         PSC should not be paid on a cheque that fell due, writing “[t]hey want some money but
         we don’t have”, and on 29 January 2020, Mr Suresh Kumar wrote to Mr Manghat, “[w]e
         need cash urgently. We have only sufficient cash to pay off the cheques for the next 2-3
         days”.

431. After NMC plc announced that the Freeh Group had been engaged to conduct an
         investigation, Mr Shenoy and Mr Suresh Kumar were concerned about how documents
         might be concealed from the investigators. In a WhatsApp message of 18 January 2020,
         Mr Suresh Kumar told Mr Shenoy: “We want to shift the non showing bank files to some
         other place. Can we ask ravi rai for a vacant flat got”, to which Mr Shenoy replied “Ok”.
         (The term “non showing bank files” refers, of course, to bank facilities that had not been
         disclosed to NMC plc’s board or disclosed in its financial statements.)

432. Mr Manghat too had exchanges with Mr Suresh Kumar on 29 and 30 January 2020 about
         obstructing the investigation and planning what information should be revealed: his
         responses about the exchanges in cross-examination were not convincing, but he did
         accept that he “messed up”. On 8 February 2020, Mr Suresh Kumar sent Mr Manghat
         messages about how to respond to the questions of Freeh Group’s investigators: “In
         tomorrow’s meeting with [Freeh Group] I can talk on the lines agreed the other day”,
         “Particularly with regard to balance confirmation”. In cross-examination, Mr Manghat
         agreed that “it could be” that he had agreed with Mr Suresh Kumar how he should
         respond to the investigators’ questions, but he sought to excuse this on the basis that his
         mind was not “on any of the investigation”.

433. Mr Manghat continued to obstruct the investigation. On 21 February 2020, he sent an
         email to Mr Dan Luther of Channel Capital, whose lending to the NMC Group was
         undisclosed, giving instructions that it was not to engage with the Freeh Group in any way,
         an instruction that he was forced to withdraw on 24 February 2020.

434. Mr Manghat denied in his defence in the ADCB Action that he sent the email of 21
         February 2020, pleading that it is not in language that he “would use”, and that he had left
         for Saudi Arabia earlier on 21 February 2020 and was not accessing his emails. When
         asked about this in cross-examination, Mr Manghat initially sought to maintain his denial
         about sending the email of 21 February 2020, but then he only said that he did not
         remember doing so, saying: “I might have done a lot of mistakes”. I conclude that, even
         if Mr Manghat did not himself send the email, he was aware of it and authorised it. It is
         incredible that, in the circumstances, an email of this kind would otherwise have been
         sent in his name.

435. Further, Mr Jones identified two emails dated 24 February 2020, with Excel attachments,
         giving instructions to delete electronic documents by reference to key words and specific

         email addresses, including those of Mr Manghat and Ms Sabina D’Mello. (Ms D’Mello had
         been Mr Manghat’s personal assistant from 2011 to 2017, and she became an
         Administration Manager in the CEO’s office at about the time when he became the CEO.)
         I cannot believe that this was done without Mr Manghat’s knowledge and involvement. Mr
         Wandowicz observed, and I accept, that the specified email addresses did not include
         those of Dr Shetty, and that this purge did not affect emails to or from Dr Shetty’s family
         office.

436. I should refer to one other happening on 24 February 2020, after the Freeh investigators
         discovered evidence that the NMC Group had been falsifying financial information. A
         board meeting of NMC plc was held at 3.00pm that afternoon. The minutes record this:
         “Judge Freeh advised that his team had encountered a number of problems progressing
         some areas of review relating to the Treasury function. Following some obstructions and
         delays in the process relating to both cash reconciliation and Supply Chain Financing
         reviews, Mr Suresh Kumar was being interviewed earlier in the day and the interview had
         been terminated forcibly by Mr Manghat just as progress was being made. The specific
         questioning of Mr Kumar related to bank statement documentation. Suresh Kumar … had
         subsequently been suspended from his employment by Mr Manghat”.

437. Mr Manghat accepted that he had interrupted an interview of Mr Suresh Kumar by the
         Freeh investigators. In his defence and his evidence, he said that he intervened because
         Mr Kumar’s wife telephoned him and told him that Mr Kumar was “locked in a room”. Mr
         Manghat was concerned because Mr Kumar had “cardiac issues” at the time. When he
         was denied entry to the office where the interview was being conducted, Mr Manghat
         called Mr Mark Tompkins, who, with Mr Bomford, came and gave instructions that the
         interview of Mr Suresh Kumar should be discontinued.

438. This account cannot reasonably be reconciled with the minutes of the Board meeting,
         which was chaired by Mr Tompkins and attended by Mr Bomford. However, I do not need
         to determine quite how Mr Suresh Kumar’s interview came to be curtailed. It is not
         disputed that Mr Suresh Kumar was suspended from his employment on 24 February
         2026, and that on 26 February 2026, when NMC plc made an announcement about the
         Undisclosed Debt, Mr Manghat was “removed” from his post as CEO “with immediate
         effect”.

439. In the ADCB proceedings, Mr Manghat pleads that “it was [his] recommendation to
         appoint the Freeh Group and was fully supportive of the Independent Review Committee
         appointing Glasser Weil LLP and [the Freeh Group] to investigate issues in the company
         and was fully supportive of their investigation. Mr Manghat encouraged NMC’s treasury
         team to give full-cooperation to the investigators …”. I am not able to say whether Mr
         Manghat suggested that the Freeh Group should conduct an investigation, but it is clear
         from Lord Clanwilliam’s evidence that, if he did so, this was after the Independent Review
         Committee had decided that there should be a professional investigation and it only
         remained to choose who should conduct it. (According to Mr Manghat, Lord Clanwilliam

         and Mr Basaddiq were in favour of KPMG conducting it.) I cannot accept that he
         supported the investigation or encouraged others to do so. He tried to thwart it.

Dr Shetty’s WhatsApp Exchange with Mr Krishnamoorthy

440. It appears that, in February or March 2020, Mr Krishnamoorthy returned to help the NMC
         Group to deal with the crisis that it faced. On 16 February 2020, Dr Shetty resigned as a
         director and Joint Non-Executive Chairman of NMC plc. On 27 February 2020, NMC’s
         shares were suspended from trading on the London Stock Exchange and the Financial
         Conduct Authority had announced an investigation in its affairs.

441. Against this background, on 5 and 6 March 2020, Dr Shetty and Mr Krishnamoorthy
         exchanged WhatsApp messages. On 5 March 2020, Mr Krishnamoorthy wrote that
         “Situation here is very bad. We are bankrupt”, and he asked whether Dr Shetty could
         provide funds by “trading off” one of his assets. Dr Shetty declined to do so, but he
         provided Mr Krishnamoorthy with contact details for a Mr Sunil Kaushal at Standard
         Chartered Bank. Mr Krishnamoorthy replied that Standard Chartered would not help:
         “NMC owes billions to them”, “Noone will help in the crisis hour”, “All banks are asking
         where the money went”, “We cannot tell them that we used unrelated business
         purposes”.

442. Early on 6 March 2020, Dr Shetty asked, “Billions, where gone my dear?”. Mr
         Krishnamoorthy replied, “SUT Hosp 200 crores, SUT land 200 crores, Neopharma month
         after month, Oman cement factory and port, Egypt hosp, UAE Exchange, loan of Burj
         Khalifa, Assam tea estate, Travelex acquisition, your villa in Abu Dhabi etc”. Dr Shetty
         asked, “My God! Can you give proofs?”.

443. Shortly after that, it seemed that their exchanges were finished for the night, and Dr Shetty
         and Mr Krishnamoorthy wished each the good night. However, Dr Shetty was clearly
         anxious for further information, and shortly afterwards he resumed their
         communications, asking about “Centurion proceeds” and an allegation of Muddy Waters
         about siphoning money by overvaluing assets. Then he asked “if any binami companies
         in my name please inform me”. Dr Shetty explained in cross-examination that “binami”
         (or “benami”) is a Bengali term and here it means sham. Mr Krishnamoorthy responded
         to this last request, “Binami cos I am not aware. Maybe Modular Concepts in Pradeep
         Rai’s name is what they are referring”. Mr Rai is Dr Shetty’s brother-in-law.

444. The exchanges ended with Mr Krishnamoorthy emphasising the NMC Group’s dire
         financial position: “Right now, the priority is to keep NMC up and running. If we can have
         some funds in the system, we can pull on for some more time. We are literally cash
         starved. If you can support on that, it will be the best thing that NMC can have now”.

445. I set out this exchange in some detail because Mr Wandowicz submitted in his opening
         submissions that it demonstrates that Dr Shetty was shocked by the expenditure that Mr

         Krishnamoorthy listed. To my mind, this outs too much weight on Dr Shetty’s responses.

Departure of Dr Shetty and Mr Manghat to India

446. The JA Claimants plead that “Between February and April 2020, each of Dr Shetty, Mr
         Manghat, Mr Shenoy, Mr [Suresh] Kumar and Mr Ghosh left the UAE and departed to India
         without warning. …. In addition, towards the end of February 2020 around 30 members of
         the NMC Group’s Corporate Accounts and Reporting team also departed for India without
         warning”. They also plead of Dr Shetty that the lack of a legitimate basis for the Direct
         Shetty Payments is to be inferred from, inter alia, his “departure to India shortly after the
         suspension of trading in NMC plc’s shares, and his prolonged failure to return to and
         resume his residence in the UAE …”.

447. There was no evidence supporting the allegation that Dr Shetty or Mr Manghat or other
         persons left for India “without warning”. Both Dr Shetty and Mr Manghat gave evidence
         explaining why they went to India in or about February 2020. There is no proper basis to
         reject their evidence. Nor is there any mystery about why Dr Shetty did not return to the
         UAE for some time: he was, as Mr Wandowicz put it, “forcibly kept in India at the
         instigation of Baroda” and “spent four years fighting in the Indian courts for the right to
         come back home to the UAE”.

448. I assume that the supposed relevance of the JA Claimants’ plea is that in some way the
         departures to, and Dr Shetty’s stay in, India are some evidence in support of the case that
         Dr Shetty, Mr Manghat and others were party to the fraud. If so, I reject the point, and the
         allegation does not influence the conclusions that I have reached.

Dr Shetty’s investigation and proceedings

449. Shortly after the fraud was uncovered by the Muddy Waters reports, Dr Shetty
         commissioned Mr Mohammed Soliman Omar of WiseHouse Consultancy, an accounting
         expert with the UAE Federal Court and the UAE Ministry of Justice, to conduct an
         investigation and to produce a report. His brief was this: “to review the financial
         statements of both NMC Healthcare LLC and Finablr LLC and to identify Dr Shetty’s
         responsibilities for those financial statements and the validity of the financial statements
         contained therein. Work towards fact-finding to the extent possible about the financial
         crisis announced by NMC, exposing facts about the discovered fraud and embezzlement
         attributed to both the management of NMC and UAE Exchange”. WiseHouse produced a
         report dated 30 April 2020. In summary, it exonerated Dr Shetty in respect of the matters
         investigated, and made findings adverse to Mr Manghat and others. Dr Shetty deployed
         the report in proceedings that he brought in India against Mr Manghat and sixteen others:
         indeed, I infer that this was its purpose, since the report describes Dr Shetty as the
         “Plaintiff” and lists seventeen “Respondents”. Certainly, Dr Shetty brought criminal
         proceedings in both India and the UAE against Mr Manghat and others.

450. I do not know in any detail what material was available to Mr Omar, and Mr Wandowicz
         rightly accepted that his findings are not probative in these proceedings. However, he
         relied on the fact that Dr Shetty commissioned the report and brought criminal
         proceedings against others to argue that this response to the disclosure of the fraud
         “contrasts sharply with the theory that he was the mastermind behind it”.

451. I shall take this argument into account, but it seems to me to have limited force. Perhaps
         surprisingly in view of the argument advanced on his behalf, Dr Shetty gave no evidence
         about why he obtained the report and brought the proceedings. I am left to conjecture
         whether his response to the disclosure of the fraud was that of an innocent victim of it, or
         whether he considered that attack was the best form of defence.

Was Dr Shetty party to the fraud?

Introduction to JA Claimants’ case

452. The JA Claimants summarised their case against Dr Shetty as being that, after the IPO, Dr
         Shetty continued to treat the NMC Group’s property and credit as if they were his own,
         and that he had no regard for the corporate boundary between the NMC Group and his
         private interests. This, they say, manifests itself in the Direct Shetty payments and the
         use of the funds of the NMC Group for Dr Shetty’s private benefit more generally, and the
         same disregard for proper corporate boundaries and governance manifests itself in other
         aspects of the fraud, including the Undisclosed Debt, the Related Party Transactions and
         the Undisclosed Securities.

Assam Tea Company Ltd

453. In support of this broad contention, Mr Thanki referred to a Revised Resolution Plan
         (“RRP”) dated 5 August 2018, which set out an offer by BRS Ventures to buy an insolvent
         Indian company called Assam Company India Ltd (“ACI”) under the provisions of the
         Indian Insolvency and Bankruptcy Code 2016. In support of the offer, the RRP identified
         an available “surplus liquid investible fund”: it stated that, as of 3 July 2018, BRS Ventures
         had available to it INR 2,030 crores, comprising funds in Dr Shetty’s accounts of 235.83
         crores and “Balance in various current accounts of M/s UAE Exchange LLC, & UAE NMC
         Healthcare LLC” of 1,794.17 crores. The funds of NMCH and UAE Exchange, it was
         submitted, were being treated as available to BRS Ventures if Dr Shetty so chose.

454. Although in his email of 6 March 2020 Mr Krishnamoorthy included the “Assam tea
         estate” as one of the purchases on which the NMC Group’s “billions” were spent, it is not
         alleged by the JA Claimants that ACI was bought with its funds. Dr Shetty’s evidence that
         he bought it with his own money was not challenged. They rely upon the evidence given
         by Dr Shetty in cross-examination as illustrating how he regarded, and was willing to treat,
         the NMC Group’s corporate assets. He readily agreed that, in the RRP, he represented
         that the 2,030 crores were his “own surplus investment fund”. When it was put to him

         that he was using “a publicly traded company’s treasury as your own private assets to
         secure” a personal purchase, he responded “Not private asset. It’s my company.
         Publicly listed, and that will be the net worth of the company. It will add to my net worth”.
         Although the answer is slightly obscure and Dr Shetty might not have appreciated quite
         what he was being asked, Dr Shetty clearly thought that the assets of the NMC Group, in
         which he was a minority shareholder, were part of his own “net worth”.

455. In response to this argument, Mr Wandowicz pointed out (i) that the JA Claimants do not
         plead any allegation about the purchase of ACI; (ii) that, in this part of his cross-
         examination about the RRP, Dr Shetty was being asked a lengthy legal document written
         in 2018; and (iii) that there is no evidence about his involvement in putting together or
         approving the RRP.

456. I do not criticise the JA Claimants for not pleading the point: they do not allege that it
         involved dishonesty on Dr Shetty’s part or that the purchase of ACI was part of the fraud
         on the NMC Group. The purchase was introduced into the case when Dr Shetty relied in
         his opening submissions on his response to Mr Krishnamoorthy’s email of 6 March 2020
         as evidence of his innocence, and it was proper in those circumstances for the JA
         Claimants to explore the matters that Mr Krishnamoorthy listed.

457. I decline to place weight upon how the RRP presented the “surplus liquid investible fund”
         of 2,030 crores: there is no sufficient reason to think that Dr Shetty was party to drafting
         that part of the document or even that he was aware of its exact wording. However, that
         is not a complete answer to Mr Thanki’s point. I accept that Dr Shetty’s answers in cross-
         examination reflected an indifference on his part to corporate boundaries, and a
         readiness to regard corporate funds as his to deploy as he saw fit. This was not because
         he was unaware of the principles governing corporate funds: Mr Wandowicz did not
         suggest that he was, and I have already referred to Dr Shetty’s evidence in cross-
         examination that “I know what the meaning of listing. I can’t touch one dirham without
         board’s approval”.

Introduction to Dr Shetty’s case

458. Dr Shetty denies that he was party to the fraud. In his Defence, he pleads this by way of a
         summary, “Dr Shetty had no knowledge of the fraud at the NMC Group. The Claimants’
         case against Dr Shetty is wholly inferential, but the indirect evidence upon which it rests,
         when properly considered, does not support the inferences drawn by the Claimants as to
         Dr Shetty’s alleged deliberate wrongdoing. The true position is that Dr Shetty was himself
         misled, and a victim of fraud committed by others. At all material times, Dr Shetty
         honestly believed: (i) the NMC Group to be a successful and robust business; (2) that he
         was a wealthy individual, largely but not exclusively owing to (what he thought was) the
         success of the NMC Group; and (3) that the NMC Group would continue to be successful
         long after his retirement, representing his legacy to the UAE”. He goes on to plead that the
         fraud commenced when financial control over the NMC Group was concentrated into the

SHETTY AND OTHERS

                                                                                                                                                                     120

                                                                                       05 November 2026 04:43 PM

         hands of “Mr Manghat and his trusted associates”; that even before 2102 Dr Shetty was
         not directly involved in financial matters, and relied on others; that Dr Shetty reposed full
         trust and confidence in Mr Manghat; and that only in retrospect he now realises that Mr
         Manghat and his associates “orchestrated and carried out the fraud at the NMC Group”.

Initial observations on Dr Shetty’s argument

459. I make four initial observations about the argument that Dr Shetty presented. First, Mr
         Wandovicz suggested in his closing submissions that the “logical route” is to determine
         whether Dr Shetty was party to the fraud is first to decide whether Dr Shetty was an honest
         witness, and, in light of the answer to that, to decide such questions as whether he
         “dishonestly procured the Direct and Indirect Payments”; whether he knew that NMC
         funds were being used for transactions relating to Travelex, RCS, the aircraft, the Dubai
         Hills Villas and investments through NNMC; and whether “Dr Shetty knew of the
         accounting irregularities within NMC”. This does not seem to me logical or a sensible
         approach: the question whether Dr Shetty’s evidence was honest should not be decided
         in the abstract or as a matter of general impression, but by testing it against the
         documentary and other evidence about the Direct and Indirect Payments, the other
         acquisitions and the accounting records.

460. Secondly, Mr Wandowicz engaged with the JA Claimants’ case on this basis that “What is
         alleged is that [Dr Shetty] was the protagonist of this fraud, the man who directed [that]
         some 3 billion dirhams is stolen for him by Mr Manghat and his team. So no narrow or
         lesser basis has been advanced”. I cannot accept that this fairly represents how the case
         was presented by the JA Claimants. I acknowledge that, in the Particulars of Claim, they
         plead that the frauds were “perpetrated by certain of the former management of [NMCH]
         (who were also the principal shareholders of [NMC plc])”, but I cannot accept that the
         case advanced against Dr Shetty was as narrow as Mr Wandowicz contended. The case
         was that the fraudulent schemes were operated by Mr Manghat and others in NMCH’s
         financial operation. The essential case against Dr Shetty was that he received enormous
         benefits from the fraud, knowing that he was not entitled to them and dishonestly.

461. Thirdly, Mr Wandowicz submitted that, while dishonesty in civil proceedings is proved on
         the balance of probabilities, “it is not open to the Court to find that the defendant acted
         dishonestly if the evidence is equally consistent with the defendant acting honestly”. He
         cited in support of this contention the speech of Lord Millett in the Three Rivers DC case
         (cit. sup., at para. 186): “It is not open to the court to inter dishonesty from fact which
         have not been pleaded, or from facts which have been pleaded but are consistent with
         honesty. There must be some fact which tilts the balance and justifies an inference of
         dishonesty, and this fact must be both pleaded and proved”.

462. In view of the volume of transactions that were alleged by the JA Claimants to be unlawful,
         necessarily the JA Claimants’ approach to the trial in general and the cross-examination
         of Dr Shetty in particular was to examine in detail particular transactions that they
