ADGMCFI-2022-111 – ABU DHABI COMMERCIAL BANK PJSC V PRASANTH MANGHAT
ADGMCFI-2020-020 - IN THE MATTER OF NMC HEALTHCARE LIMITED (IN ADMINISTRATION) (SUBJECT TO A DEED OF COMPANY ARRANGEMENT) AND THE COMPANIES
LISTED IN SCHEDULE 1 TO THE ADMINISTRATION APPLICATION
ADGMCFI-2022-299 – NMC HEALTHCARE LIMITED (IN ADMINISTRATION) (SUBJECT TO A DEED OF COMPANY ARRANGEMENT) AND OTHERS V BAVAGUTHU RAGHURAM
SHETTY AND OTHERS

                                                                                                                                                                     121

                                                                                       05 November 2026 04:43 PM

         submitted prove Dr Shetty’s participation in the fraud. As he developed his argument
         orally, Mr Wandwicz submitted that, therefore, in respect of these exemplar transactions,
         it was incumbent on the JA Claimants to demonstrate that “[t]hose examples must be
         definitely inconsistent with honesty”.

463. I agree with Mr Thanki that this overstates the position. The proper approach is that
         explained by Flaux J in JSC Bank of Moscow v Kekhman [2015] EWHC 3073 (Comm) at
         paras. 19 and 20: there too, counsel for the defendants cited Lord Millett’s speech and
         submitted that the test derived from it is this: "…the primary facts must necessarily lead
         to the inference that [the first defendant] is guilty of fraud because otherwise and ex
         hypothesi the primary facts can be consistent with innocence … You don't get to
         arguability until you've established that there is a proper fraud plea. You don't establish
         that there is a proper fraud plea before particulars are pleaded which are only consistent
         with [the first defendant] being dishonest and which cannot be consistent with [the first
         defendant] being honest". Rejecting that submission and holding that the submission
         exaggerates what is required for a valid plea of fraud, Flaux J said, “The claimant does not
         have to plead primary facts which are only consistent with dishonesty. The correct test is
         whether or not, on the basis of the primary facts pleaded, an inference of dishonesty is
         more likely than one of innocence or negligence. As Lord Millett put it, there must be some
         fact ‘which tilts the balance and justifies an inference of dishonesty’. At the interlocutory
         stage, when the court is considering whether the plea of fraud is a proper one or whether
         to strike it out, the court is not concerned with whether the evidence at trial will or will not
         establish fraud but only with whether facts are pleaded which would justify the plea of
         fraud. If the plea is justified, then the case must go forward to trial and assessment of
         whether the evidence justifies the inference is a matter for the trial judge”. In support of
         this, Flaux J cited the speech of Lord Hope in the Three Rivers case (loc. cit. at paras. 55,
         56).

464. I add that, in assessing whether the inference of dishonesty is more likely than one of
         innocence or negligence, the Court is not required in a case such as this (as Mr
         Wandowicz appeared to argue) to examine each allegation of dishonesty in relation to a
         transaction in isolation. The proper approach is to examine the totality of the evidence
         relied upon by the parties and to assess it cumulatively.

465. Finally, Mr Wandwicz understandably argued that Mr Manghat was responsible for
         operating the fraudulent scheme. That is not, of course, enough to exculpate Dr Shetty.
         The JA Claimants’ case is that Dr Shetty and Mr Manghat acted together, not only in early
         2019 but from 2012 and throughout the Relevant Period, in that, for example, they co-
         signed transfer requests, and Dr Shetty rewarded Mr Manghat for what he did (for
         example, as I shall explain, in respect of his assistance on the Travelex acquisition).

The Direct Shetty payments

466. The JA Claimants contend that between 2 April 2012, the date of the IPO, and 16 February
         2020, when he resigned as a director of NMC plc, Dr Shetty received a total of at least
         AED 4.1 billion of payments from the NMC Group into his personal accounts at Baroda
         and other banks, some AED 3.9 billion of payments being received from NMCH, Holding
         and the ADGM Companies; and that, taking into account payments from Dr Shetty’s
         personal accounts back to the NMC Group over that period, he received a net sum of at
         least AED 2.6 billion from the NMC Group.

467. As I have said, most of the Direct Shetty payments were made to three accounts in Dr
         Shetty’s name at Baroda. Dr Shetty accepted that two of the accounts, the 2359 account
         and the 0090 account, were his.

468. He also accepted that he used the 2359 account for his personal banking: it appears to
         have been his principal personal account. This is shown by an analysis by the JA
         Claimants of cheques that Dr Shetty accepted were signed by him and drawn on the 2359
         account for his personal benefit. I need not list them all: they include payments for
         property acquisitions, including a payment of AED 90.5 million for land on Al Reem Island;
         payments for service fees and other expenses relating to his properties in Burj Khalifa and
         elsewhere; and payments connected with his interests related to education.

469. The 2359 account was also used to make the payments by post-dated cheques under his
         settlement agreement with HE Al Mazrouei: see para. 100 above. The JA Claimants
         referred in particular to a payment of an instalment of AED 60 million: when the cheque
         was first presented on 20 August 2015, it was returned because there were insufficient
         funds in the account. On 1 September 2015, AED 60 million was transferred from NMCH
         to the 2359 account, and the cheque was paid when it was re-presented.

470. Dr Shetty’s evidence was that he did not recall the 7013 account, but he said that it might
         have been his. I conclude it was. This is shown by emails sent to Dr Shetty on 27
         November 2018 and 30 January 2019 relating to payments into that account for his sale
         of shares to a Dr Nandini Tandon.

471. The accounts at other banks into which the JA Claimants says that Direct Shetty
         Payments were made are:

         a. an account at Falcon Private Bank Limited (with a number ending in 1520);

         b. an account at National Bank of Fujairah (with a number ending 0966);

         c. an account at UNB (with a number ending 1638);

         d. an account at First Gulf Bank (with a number ending 8014); and

         e. an account at First Gulf Bank (with a number ending 8025).

472. Dr Shetty accepted in his evidence that he “was aware of” the account at Falcon Private
         Bank Limited.

473. He said that he did not recall opening or using an account at National Bank of Fujairah,
         but the account opening form is in evidence: on its face, it was signed by Dr Shetty and
         the signature verified by a Mr Ravinder Ochani. There is no reason to think that the
         signature and the verification are not genuine, and I conclude that the account was Dr
         Shetty’s.

474. Dr Shetty also said that he was also unaware of the accounts at UNB and First Gulf Bank.
         Although the JA Claimants said that the evidence shows that Dr Shetty used these
         accounts, they did not identify specific evidence to show this, and I have been unable to
         find it. On the other hand, however, no argument about this was developed by Mr
         Wandovicz. In these circumstances, I shall allow the JA Claimants, if so advised, to
         identify the relevant evidence after I have issued this judgment and allow further
         argument about these payments into these accounts.

475. Dr Shetty did not admit in his Defence that the transfers to his accounts were made, but
         they are proved by the evidence of Mr Jones and the JA Action Accountant Expert, which
         was not challenged. The JA Action Accountant Experts also agreed that the Direct Shetty
         payments were accounted for by recording a debit in the Advance Account or some other
         suspense account; and a credit was recorded to a bank or cash ledger account.

476. Dr Shetty contended that he did not himself “operate” the 2359 account, the 0090
         account and other accounts. However, he accepted in cross-examination that he
         maintained control over his accounts, and he was the sole signatory on them. This is clear
         from Baroda’s mandates for accounts 2359 and 7013, and, although the documentation
         does not include the mandate for account 0090, I accept the JA Claimants’ submission
         that the operation and control of account 0090 would not have differed from that of his
         other accounts.

477. Further, Dr Shetty pleaded that his accounts with Baroda were used “to make payments
         on behalf of the NMC Group in the course of legitimate NMC business”, and that therefore
         “no inference can be drawn from the fact that money was paid into one of [his Baroda
         accounts] as to the purpose or intended recipient of the money …”. Dr Shetty exemplified
         this contention only with small payments from his account made on 19 November 2012,
         9 March 2013 and 1 September 2013, the total of these payments being less than AED 5
         million. They are said to have been made in relation to the lease of a plot for a clinic. I
         cannot accept that, as Dr Shetty said, the inference to be drawn is that these payments
         are “part of the overall established practice of using the Shetty Private Baroda Accounts
         for business purpose for the group”. There was no evidence that I can accept that

         supported that contention and Mr Wandowicz did not develop a convincing argument in
         support of the pleaded inference.

478. The JA Claimants say that their contention that the Direct Shetty Payments were not
         properly made is supported by their treatment in NMC’s internal records. Large payments
         were recorded in the NMC Group’s internal records and on NMC’s “SCORE” database
         with no real statement as to their purpose, and often with such uninformative
         explanations as “remittance” or “transfer to Chairman” or “Own Account Transfer”. Dr
         Shetty did not assert or explain in his evidence that there was any other legitimate basis
         for him receiving the Direct Shetty payments. They were not salary or other remuneration.
         No NMC Group company other than NMC plc could properly pay him dividends. It was
         not plausibly suggested that the payments were by way of legitimate loans to Dr Shetty.
         None of the Direct Shetty Payments were recorded as expenses in the NMC Group’s
         income statements.

479. In his oral opening, Mr Wandowicz identified that a transfer which the JA Claimants list as
         a Direct Shetty Payment and which is described in the NMC Group’s records as a
         “transfer to Chairman” is shown by the documentary evidence (in particular by an email
         from one of Dr Shetty’s own companies, BRS Capital, to Mr Suresh Kumar and Mr Pinto
         dated 7 March 2018) to be by way of repayment to the company of a short term loan that
         it had extended to the NMC Group. This explanation was not supported by Dr Shetty’s
         evidence, but I accept it. However, this isolated case does not change the pattern for the
         vast majority of the Direct Shetty Payments.

Dr Shetty’s knowledge of the Direct Shetty Payments

480. Mr Thanki suggested in his closing submissions that there was a change of emphasis in
         the presentation of Dr Shetty’s argument about the Direct Shetty Payments between his
         opening submissions, in which it was emphasised that the JA Claimants had not
         investigated the destiny of misappropriated funds (“No-one knows where the
         misappropriated funds ultimately went, because [the JA Claimants] did not carry out any
         meaningful asset tracing exercise and so do not come to Court with evidence of the
         onward chain, or who, at the end, was left with the stolen money”, and the closing
         submissions, in which emphasis was placed on Dr Shetty’s state of knowledge. I see
         nothing in that point: it has always been a central plank of Dr Shetty’s defence that he
         knew nothing of the fraud, and the argument that there is no or limited information about
         what happened to the misappropriated funds was properly advanced in support of that.

481. In response to Dr Shetty’s denial that he knew of the Direct Shetty Payments, the JA
         Claimants say that ordinarily payments into a person’s personal bank account are
         received for the account holder’s benefit and, at least where he is the sole signatory on
         the account, it can generally be inferred that they were made for his benefit. In this case,
         they contend, it simply is not credible that such large sums should be paid into his
         accounts over so long a period without his knowledge. They illustrated the point by

         observing that 83 transfers to Dr Shetty each exceeded in amount the total of his
         legitimate remuneration during the Relevant Period. (The evidence about his legitimate
         remuneration was not disputed. When he was the CEO of NMC plc, Dr Shetty’s monthly
         salary rose from AED 125,000 in 2012 to AED 390,000 in 2016. He also received annual
         payments under a Short-Term Incentive plan, which rose from AED 421,875 in 2013 to
         AED 1,950,000 in 2016. He received shares, and no cash, under a Long-Term Incentive
         Plan. Upon becoming the Non-Executive Chairman in March 2017, he became entitled to
         fees of £ 210,000 per annum.)

482. The JA Claimants also argued that the inference that the Direct Shetty Payments were
         made with Dr Shetty’s knowledge and for his benefit is corroborated in that:

         a. As far as the evidence goes, the Direct Shetty Payments were made by transfers
                   that, on the face of it, bear his signature. The JA Claimants submitted that all the
                   transfers in evidence were made under what appears to be his (wet or electronic)
                   signature, and Mr Wandowicz identified no exception.

         b. The fraudulent scheme could have been operated secretly from Dr Shetty only if
                   not only Mr Manghat and other senior executives of NMCH but also numerous
                   more junior personnel were party to concealing it from him.

         c. Dr Shetty’s personal banking was handled in his own office, in particular by Mr
                   Pinto, whom Dr Shetty said was honest and loyal to him. For example, when
                   asked about a document signed by him in which he authorised persons including
                   Mr Pinto, to collect bank statements for his personal accounts from Baroda, Dr
                   Shetty acknowledged the document, saying “Of course, Joseph Pinto’s signature
                   is there, it must be okay”.

483. As for the first point, Dr Shetty responded that his apparent signature on documents
         relating to the transfer of funds is not cogent evidence against him, even assuming (rightly
         or wrongly) that his signatures were not forged. First, there is no dispute, as I understand
         it, that Mr Manghat and those who worked with him had access to Dr Shetty’s electronic
         signature and Mr Wandowicz persuasively submitted that it appears that electronic
         signatures were used to make many of the transfers that the JA Claimants say were
         unlawful. Further, even where a document bears a wet signature, it does not follow that
         Dr Shetty understood the nature or purpose of the document when he signed it. He
         pleaded that he was asked to sign large numbers of documents on a day-to-day basis,
         and, even when he was given some explanation about the document, it was only a brief
         one.

484. I accept that Dr Shetty signed documents without reading them properly and trusted the
         explanations that he was given about why they were required. That said, I find it difficult
         to accept that, over the years of the Relevant Period, he signed so many obviously
         important documents, such as agreements for banking facilities and documents relating

         for such large transfers, without taking any interest in at least their general nature and
         purpose.

485. I also see force in the JA Claimants’ other two points: first, Dr Shetty’s case is that his
         accounts were misused without his knowledge not only by Mr Manghat but also (to list
         only those whom would Dr Shetty claims in his first witness statement were involved) Ms
         D’Mello, who, according to Dr Shetty’s evidence, “operated the 2359 bank account”; Ms
         Rachel dela Torre, who had been an account executive since 2006; Mr Udayakumar Nair;
         Mr Praveen Kumar; Mr Prajeesh Veerachan, an account executive since at least 2013;
         and Mr Suresh Nandiraju, the CEO of Nexgen. Mr Sreekanth Nair’s email of 11 March 2019
         (see para. 212 above) also illustrates how many were involved in maintaining the fraud.
         It is inherently most improbable that a secret on such a scale might have been maintained
         successfully for so many years. No explanation was suggested as to why so many
         persons would have been willing to deceive Dr Shetty, or how Mr Manghat and others
         principally responsible for the fraud could have supposed that, with so many involved,
         the scheme could have been kept secret from him.

486. In his witness statement, Dr Shetty said that “Bank statements would never come to me.
         Bank of Baroda never sent me intimation about transactions or bank statements for my
         accounts”. It is clear from the evidence that Mr Pinto had access to statements and other
         documents relating to Dr Shetty’s personal accounts. Mr Mahaveer Gupta said that
         sometimes he would go to Baroda to collect them. Dr Shetty explained that he dealt with
         banking matters without visiting the offices of Baroda (or, as I infer, of other banks), but
         through his private office staff and in particular through Mr Pinto, and in cross-
         examination he accepted that his office staff, including Mr Pinto, had access to the
         statements and would review them. Thus, on Dr Shetty’s own case, Mr Pinto and others
         were in a position readily to identify large and suspicious movements in the accounts,
         such as many of the Direct Shetty Payments. This is inconsistent with Dr Shetty’s case
         that the Direct Shetty Payments were part of a scheme on the part of the executives of
         the NMC Group of which he was unaware and of which he was the victim.

487. I conclude that, throughout the Relevant Period, Dr Shetty was aware that he was
         receiving into his bank accounts payments from the NMC Group which were not properly
         justified.

The Indirect Shetty Payments

488. In support of their case against Dr Shetty about the Direct Shetty Payments, the JA
         Claimants also rely upon particular benefits that, they say, he received. Before I come to
         those, I shall consider the Indirect Shetty Payments. The JA Claimants’ case is that,
         during the Relevant Period, Dr Shetty also received into his personal accounts at least
         AED 426.4 million, which derived from the NMC Group and was channelled to him, much
         of it through Nexgen, and also through GGCM and FFT, all companies that, they say, were
         in Dr Shetty’s control.

489. As with the Direct Shetty Payments, the evidence of Mr Jones and the JA Action
         Accountant Experts that the payments were made was not significantly challenged. It
         shows that:

         a. There were 53 Indirect Shetty Payments made through two accounts that Nexgen
                   had with Baroda;

         b. There were three transfers made from GGCM’s 9228 account; and

         c. There was one payment from FFT to Dr Shetty’s 2359 account.

         All were funded by the NMC Group.

490. Their treatment in NMC’s internal records was similar to that of the Direct Shetty
         Payments: in so far as they were recorded in the general ledger, they were entered as
         debits in the Advance Account; and in so far as they are recorded on SCORE, generally no
         purpose for the payment was given.

491. Dr Shetty, however, contends that he did not control Nexgen, GGCM or FFT, and that the
         Indirect Shetty Payments were made into his bank accounts without his knowledge. He
         pleads that:

         a. He had an indirect ownership interest of 45% in Nexgen, but that Mr Manghat was
                   a director of it from 15 June 2010, and Dr Shetty delegated to him “full powers and
                   authority to undertake all acts on behalf of Nexgen …, including in respect of
                   financing”;

         b. He did not control FFT. He pleads that, as he now understands, it was controlled
                   by Mr Manghat and/or Mr Praveen Kumar; and

         c. He had an interest in GGCM, but he pleads that its “legitimate business … was
                   operated on a day-to-day basis by Anil Kumar Hegde”.

492. As for Dr Shetty’s interest in Nexgen, although he said in his witness statement that “I
         now understand that I hold an indirect ownership interest of 45%”, this was misleading.
         In cross-examination he accepted that he understood “right from the day one” that he
         had an interest in the company.

493. The JA Claimants accept that Dr Shetty did not have “the day-to-day operational levers”
         over Nexgen, and that he might not have known of all its transactions. The evidence
         shows that, at least with regard to the operation of the Baroda accounts, NMC personnel
         gave banking instructions on Nexgen’s behalf, apparently without reference to Nexgen.
         The JA Claimants argue that nevertheless Dr Shetty had control of Nexgen, in as much as
         he could have rescinded the management powers that he conferred on Mr Manghat, and
         he signed Nexgen’s financial statements. Whether Dr Shetty should be described

         therefore as having “control” of Nexgen seems to me an arid semantic question. The
         more important question is whether he knew that Nexgen was an instrument for making
         Indirect Shetty Payments from the NMC Group to him.

494. As with the Direct Shetty Payments, the JA Claimants’ first point is that the volume and
         value of the payments into Dr Shetty’s personal accounts was such that it is simply
         incredible that he was, as he contends, wholly unaware of them. As I have said, there
         were 53 such payments in the Relevant Period, and their total value was AED 273 million.
         The evidence of the JA Action Accountant Experts, which was not challenged and which I
         accept, was that, of the payments from Nexgen’s 7928 Baroda account, AED 111.6
         million was funded by transfers at the expense of the NMC Group, and a further AED 131.6
         million of payments from Nexgen’s 5596 account was so funded. Payments from Nexgen
         were used to purchase Travelex, RCS and the Dubai Hills properties, and, as I infer, were
         made in order to do so. There was no legitimate reason for the NMC Group to fund the
         payments to Dr Shetty and for his benefit, and there was no proper explanation as to why,
         if these payments were to be made by the NMC Group, they should be routed via Nexgen.

495. The JA Claimants conclude their submission about the Nexgen Indirect Payments as
         follows: “[Dr Shetty’s] contention that Mr Manghat controlled Nexgen, taken to its logical
         conclusion, asks the Court to accept that Mr Manghat decided, 53 times, to transfer funds
         totalling more than AED 270 million into Dr Shetty’s personal accounts without Dr
         Shetty’s knowledge or approval. That is not credible”. I certainly accept that Dr Shetty’s
         case about this matter is highly improbable and that this is further powerful evidence that
         he was party to the fraud.

496. I come to the Indirect Shetty Payments via GGCM. Dr Shetty accepted in cross-
         examination that GGCM was “under his control”.

497. There was no dispute that the three payments for a total of AED 84.535 million were made
         to Dr Shetty’s 2359 account from the 9228 account; nor was there any challenge to the
         evidence of the JA Action Accountant Experts that the payments were funded by the NMC
         Group. It was not suggested by Dr Shetty that there was any legitimate reason for the
         payments.

498. There is documentary evidence about how only one of the three transfers was effected:
         Baroda was given instructions dated 1 June 2017 to transfer AED 28 million from NMCH’s
         0050 account to GGCM’s 9228 account, and from GGCM’s 9228 account to Dr Shetty’s
         2359 account, and both instructions appear to be signed by Dr Shetty. In his evidence, Dr
         Shetty denied that he ever authorised any payment from GGCM, and he asserted that the
         transfer instruction of 1 June 2017 from the GGCM account was “fabricated by them”.

499. I did not find it easy to follow Dr Shetty’s evidence about payments from the GGCM
         account, and my impression is that, at this stage in his cross-examination, Dr Shetty
         became rather confused. In the end, I am not persuaded of the JA Claimants’

         submissions that the “natural inference” from the evidence “is that Dr Shetty knew about
         and authorised these payments”. However, this conclusion does not seem to me much
         to assist Dr Shetty: I am driven to conclude that he knew of NMC funds being directed
         directly and indirectly into his own accounts and for his benefit, and he was not
         concerned about the route whereby the funds were being transferred.

500. The single Indirect Shetty Payment that the JA Claimants say was made via FFT was a
         transfer of AED 70 million into Dr Shetty’s 2359 account on 22 May 2014. As Mr Bezant
         explained, it was funded by NMCH and New Medical Centre Trading Ltd, who had
         transferred a total of AED 73.7 million to FFT shortly before the payment to Dr Shetty.

501. The purpose of the FFT Indirect Shetty Payment was to contribute to his acquisition of
         Travelex: see paras. 526ff below. As I explain, I conclude that Dr Shetty knew that funds
         from the NMC Group were being used for the acquisition. It is likely that he knew that
         some of the funds reached his account through FFT, but even if he was not aware of that,
         it suffices for present purposes that (as with the GGCM Indirect Shetty Payments) he
         knew of NMC funds being directed directly and indirectly into his own accounts and for
         his benefit, and he was not concerned about the route whereby the funds were being
         transferred.

The Sobha & BR aircraft

502. In his Defence, Dr Shetty denied that he asked for any of the Direct Shetty Payments to
         be made to him. In cross-examination, he qualified this position, saying “I never asked
         them to transfer any money except these [2 or 3 items] … It is true that I must ask them to
         give the aircraft money and other money”. No other – I never asked any money”. As I shall
         explain, it appears that the “other money” related to money used to invest in the Oman
         Cement Company. First, I consider the money spent on an aircraft for Dr Shetty’s use.

503. By a memorandum of understanding dated 6 July 2014, Dr Shetty and Mr Puthan N C
         Menon entered into an arrangement whereby they acquired a Gulfstream G450 aircraft
         “for their private use … with equal partnership”: this is recorded in a settlement
         agreement dated 15 January 2017 ending the arrangement, after Dr Shetty had told Mr
         Menon by an email dated 18 August 2016 that he wished to do so. The aircraft was
         operated by Sobha & B R Aviation Ltd (“Sobha & BR”), a BVI company.

504. The JA Claimants allege that NMC funds were used to meet the costs of the
         arrangements. Dr Shetty did not dispute this, and I so find. In particular, the evidence of
         the JA Action Accountant Experts shows that:

         a. On 17 September 2014, US$ 4,206,834.84 was paid to Sobha & BR; and

         b. On 12 August 2015, AED 5 million was paid to Mr Menon.

         Both payments were made from one of NMCH’s Baroda accounts.

505. In his Defence, Dr Shetty accepts that, at Mr Menon’s invitation, he shared in the
         acquisition of the aircraft. (He pleads that he “resigned his stake in the aircraft on 1
         October 2015”. It is not clear quite what he meant by that: it is clear that he had some
         residual interest in it after October 2015, but I do not consider this in itself discredits Dr
         Shetty’s evidence about the aircraft, the JA Claimants’ submissions notwithstanding.)

506. Dr Shetty also pleads that he “was clear with Mr Manghat that the aircraft was a personal
         investment but that it was subsequently to be used by Dr Shetty for business purposes.
         Accordingly, Dr Shetty understood that following the acquisition, the costs of maintaining
         the aircraft going forward would be split between the various businesses for whose
         purposes Dr Shetty would be travelling (including the NMC Group, UAE Exchange,
         Neopharma etc)”, and that payments were then arranged by Mr Manghat and Ms D’Mello.
         He denies having any “contemporaneous knowledge that the aircraft had not been, as he
         had instructed and understood, acquired with his personal funds”.

507. I cannot accept the pleaded case that Dr Shetty believed that he had instructed that his
         personal money be used to funds the acquisition of the aircraft: it is inconsistent with the
         evidence that Dr Shetty gave when he was cross-examined. The JA Claimants relied here
         on his answer agreeing that that he had signed the transfer request for payment on 17
         September 2014, but that answer in isolation does not seem to me very significant. More
         telling to my mind was his answer, when refuting that GGCM funds were used, that he
         had “authorised Manghat and his brother to pay from UAE Exchange about the Sobha
         airline, Sobha BR. And how they manage it, we don’t know. But GGCM, no way”. This is
         not, of course, evidence that NMC Group money was used, but it is not consistent with
         the case that Dr Shetty thought that he was using his personal money.

508. There is also evidence that Dr Shetty again looked to the NMC Group to fund the aircraft
         in January 2016 (after Dr Shetty pleads that he had resigned his share in it). On 30
         December 2015, Mr Binay Shetty sent Mr Manghat a WhatsApp message that Dr Shetty
         had agreed to “continue with the plane” and that there were “some payments due”. On
         3 January 2016, he requested “Payment to Menon of [AED] 5 m if possible immediately or
         total of [AED] 16.43 m by 10th Jan” and he asked if that was possible. No payment was
         made, and Mr Binay Shetty asked again on 13 January 2016. Mr Manghat replied that “Dr
         Shetty took 15 mio kept for payments to Oman” but that he would “work for funds”.
         Although Dr Shetty denied that the funds were used for payments “to Oman”, but I reject
         that evidence: by a transfer request dated 14 January 2016, NMCH requested Baroda to
         make a transfer of AED 15 million to Dr Shetty’s 2359 account.

509. Three further payments relating to the aircraft were made in 2016 from Dr Shetty’s 2359
         account, and each was funded by a Direct Shetty Payment by the NMC Group:

          a. On 21 January 2016, NMCH transferred AED 41,375,000 to the 2359 account, and on
               the same day AED 5 million was paid by Dr Shetty to PNC Investments LLC. (Mr Jones’

               evidence that the payment by Dr Shetty was made “in relation to the private aircraft”

          b. On 14 March 2016, NMCH transferred AED 2,200,000 to the 2359 account. On the
               same day, Mr Udayakumar Nair emailed a request that this transfer be made and that
               then AED 2,161,520 be transferred to Mr Menon.

          c. On 11 July 2016, NMCH transferred AED 3,817,139 to the 2359 account, and the
               same amount was transferred on the same day from the 2359 account to Mr Menon.

         Dr Shetty offered no answer to the JA Claimants’ complaints about these payments.

510. I should also mention that in 2015 GGCM made two payments relating to the aircraft: a
         payment of 5 million to Sobha & BR on 8 January 2015, and a payment to Mr Menon of
         AED 5 million on 1 June 2015. There is no evidence that these payments were made from
         funds provided by the NMC Group. The JA Claimants abandoned any reliance upon them
         in their closing submissions.

511. The JA Claimants contend that Dr Shetty knew that NMC Group funds were being used to
         make payments for the private aircraft, and that he allowed and approved of this. I accept
         their contention.

Oman Cement City Company

512. It is not in dispute that in 2016 Dr Shetty made an investment in an Oman Cement City
         Project. It was an investment unconnected with the NMC Group.

513. On 16 January 2016, Mr Pinto sent to Mr Krishnamoorthy Suresh an email which read, “As
         advised by Dr Shetty, we have prepared the transfer letter for remitting Omani Rial 1.5
         Million as the validation fee (5%) for the Oman City Project”. The attached request was
         for the transfer to 1.5 million rials from the 2359 account to an account at the National
         Bank of Oman. On the same day, NMCH’s treasury department sent Baroda a request to
         transfer AED 15 million from NMCH’s 0242 account to Dr Shetty’s 2359 account.

514. When he was asked about this matter in cross-examination, Dr Shetty accepted the
         propositions that “that was how things worked … when you wanted money”, and that he
         “would simply ask for money to be deposited in his personal account and the NMC
         treasury team would make that happen”. I consider that it would place too much weight
         on these answers to take Dr Shetty to have accepted that, as a general rule, this was how
         the Direct Shetty Payments and the Indirect Shetty Payments came to be made. His
         answers are to be understood in the context of questioning about the investment in the
         Oman City Cement Project. I do accept that his answers are an admission that he asked
         to have money transferred from the NMC Group for his investment in the Oman City
         Cement Project.

515. Further, on 9 May 2016, Baroda transferred AED 47,750,000 to Dr Shetty’s 2359 account,
         and on the same day Dr Shetty gave instructions to remit OMR 5 million (the approximate
         equivalent of AED 47,750,000) to HE Al Sayyid in respect of the Oman investment. When
         asked about the requests for these transfers, Dr Shetty suggested that his signature on
         the transfer request to Baroda might be electronic, and there is no reason to doubt that.
         Mr Wandowicz submitted that, on the other hand, the signature of the request for the
         transfer from his own account to HE Al Sayyid was Dr Shetty’s wet signature, and I am
         prepared to accept that.

516. There is further evidence that Dr Shetty was willing to use NMC Group funds for his Oman
         City Cement Project investment. On 14 March 2017, Dr Shetty sent Mr Krishnamoorthy a
         WhatsApp message: “Since process takes time for the proceeds of NMC, I need to pay
         tomorrow 6 million Omani royal to port project and 25 million to Dr Sabha Brindavan
         College”. He then sent another message, “Please arrange and oblige either from NMC or
         Dubai Islamic proceeds”. (The reference to “Dubai Islamic proceeds” was to a facility
         that Dr Shetty was arranging with Dubai Islamic Bank. Mr Krishnamoorthy explained that
         the facility would not have been available since it was earmarked for another purpose.)

517. In re-examination, Dr Shetty said that the “proceeds of NMC” referred to a dividend
         payment expected from NMC plc, but I cannot accept that explanation. No dividend was
         due from NMC plc: its dividend was not paid until June 2017, and when it was paid, it was
         only about US$ 6.5 million, whereas Dr Shetty was looking for a payment of 6 million
         Omani rials, the equivalent of over US$ 15 million, in March 2017. While Dr Shetty might
         not have known quite what dividend he would be receiving from NMC plc, he was too
         acute a businessman to have expected a sufficient dividend to cover the payments that
         he required.

518. Mr Wandowicz pointed out that the JA Claimants’ pleading does not specifically refer to
         this investment in Oman, although transfers related to it are included in the Schedule of
         Direct Shetty Payments. I am not impressed by that point. This matter is of particular
         relevance because they explain the qualification that Dr Shetty himself introduced when
         he was cross-examined to his own pleaded case, where he denied requesting any of the
         transfers into his accounts.

519. Mr Wandowicz also observed that the use of an electronic signature on the transfer
         request to Baroda of 9 May 2016 is consistent with Dr Shetty signing the request for the
         transfer of OMR 5 million in the belief that he was investing his own funds. That might
         explain the May 2016 instalment of the investment if taken in isolation, but the other
         evidence about the Oman investment undermines Mr Wandowicz’s contention.

The 2017 aircraft purchase

520. The JA Claimants also cross-examined Dr Shetty about the purchase of an aircraft by Dr
         Shetty in 2017. As with the Oman investment, Mr Wandowicz observed in his closing

         submissions that their pleadings do not refer to this purchase, but this line of inquiry too
         arose from Dr Shetty’s evidence when he was cross-examined that he asked for money
         for the aircraft.

521. In WhatsApp messages to Dr Shetty on 30 May 2017, Mr Krishnamoorthy wrote “Urgently
         need USD 7.5 m for the plane. The last day for payment is Monday”; “NMC’s fund position
         is very tight”; “Can [you] ask Pramod [Manghat] to spare this amount. We can pay it back
         in a week’s time”. I understand that the request that Mr Promoth Manghat might “spare
         the money” was that UAE Exchange might provide the funds.

522. Dr Shetty agreed to the request. The exchange went on to refer to a dividend that Dr Shetty
         was to receive, but, as I have said, the dividend that NMC plc paid him in 2017 was of only
         US$6.5 million and it would not have covered the full amount needed.

523. There is no pleaded allegation, and there is no satisfactory evidence, about what funds
         were in fact used to buy the aircraft. The JA Claimants submit that the WhatsApp
         exchange, together with Dr Shetty’s acceptance in cross-examination that he was
         unconcerned whether money from the NMC Group or UAE Exchange was used to fund
         the purchase, is another example of Dr Shety’s readiness to regard corporate assets of
         the NMC Group as his own. I accept that this matter provides some further evidence to
         corroborate the JA Claimants’ contention.

Travelex

524. Travelex was a foreign exchange business. Dr Shetty acquired it in 2014. It was never part
         of the NMC Group.

525. Payment was made for Travelex in two instalments: a payment of AED 366,360,000 on 22
         May 2014, and a payment of AED 360,313,000 on 1 September 2014. The May instalment
         was paid to Linklaters LLC’s Client Account. (Linklaters LLC acted for Dr Shetty on the
         acquisition.) The September payment was made to an account in the name of “Project
         Squadron”.

526. The May instalment was paid by way of a transfer from Dr Shetty’s 2359 account, which
         received funds to make the payment into the account by way of:

         a. Transfers totalling AED196,390,000 made by New Medical Centre Trading LLC on
                   20 May 2022;

         b. A transfer of AED 70 million made by UAE Exchange on 21 May 2014; and

         c. Transfers totalling AED 100 million made by Nexgen and FTT on 22 May 2014,
                   Nexgen and FFT having received the funds to make these payments from New
                   Medical Trading LLC, NMCH and NMC Specialty Hospital LLC.

SHETTY AND OTHERS

                                                                                                                                                                     134

527. Payment of the September instalment was also made by a transfer from Dr Shetty’s 2359
         account, which had received the funds from NMCH by a series of twenty payments,
         nineteen for AED 18 million each and one for AED 18,313,000. (The funds were transferred
         in this way because Baroda’s mandate for the operation of the accounts of NMCH and its
         subsidiaries allowed transfers of these amounts on the signature of Dr Shetty and Mr
         Manghat. It would have required the signatures of Dr Shetty and KBBAM for the full
         amount to have been made by way of a single transfer. The JA Claimants relied upon this
         point in support of their case against Baroda, but it does not appear to me important to
         the cases against Dr Shetty and Mr Manghat.)

528. As far as the evidence goes, Dr Shetty’s signature was on the transfers from the NMC
         Group accounts (as well as those from his personal accounts). However, they appear to
         have been electronic signatures, and Dr Shetty gave evidence that they were “fake”, and
         “scanned” signatures used without his knowledge. Moreover, as Mr Wandowicz
         submitted, the evidence shows that the transaction to acquire Travelex was handled for
         Dr Shetty by Mr Manghat and his brother, Promoth: the advisers and others corresponded
         with them, and it was the finance department at NMCH, and not Dr Shetty’s office, that
         arranged the payments for the acquisition. I do not consider that, in itself, the fact that Dr
         Shetty’s signatures were on the transfers provides any real support for the JA Claimants’
         contentions about Travelex.

529. Dr Shetty does not dispute that the payments that I have described were made. His case
         is that, as far as he was aware, no funding for the Travelex purchase came from the NMC
         Group. He pleads that he “understood that he was making a personal investment funded
         by initial lending of USD c750m, and – or so he thought- investment by UAE Exchange”.
         In his first witness statement, he said that he did not know that NMC funds were being
         used, and that he “believed that the funds of UAE Exchange were deployed for the
         acquisition of Travelex”. In his second witness statement, he said that he “was given to
         understand that the initial funding for the Travelex acquisition came from a USD $750
         million facility arranged by Goldman Sachs and later refinanced through a separate $490
         million loan”; and that Mr Manghat and his brother, Promoth, informed him that the
         remaining funds “were intended to be provided by UAE Exchange”.

530. The documentary evidence does not support the contention that UAE Exchange was
         making an investment in Travelex, or that it was otherwise involved in the purchase,
         beyond providing AED 70 million of the funding that Dr Shetty used for the May
         Instalment. Nor did Dr Shetty unequivocally support this case when he was cross-
         examined. He was shown a report in “The National” of an interview that he gave on 1 July
         2014, in which he is recorded as saying this about the acquisition: “Travelex was my own
         acquisition. I have known them a long time and saw the opportunity. They approached
         me a year or so back as a potential buyer”. In cross-examination, he confirmed that it was
         his own acquisition. At the same time, he said that it was “supposed to be UAE money to
         be put”, and that “We had surplus money there in the Exchange”.

LISTED IN SCHEDULE 1 TO THE ADMINISTRATION APPLICATION
ADGMCFI-2022-299 – NMC HEALTHCARE LIMITED (IN ADMINISTRATION) (SUBJECT TO A DEED OF COMPANY ARRANGEMENT) AND OTHERS V BAVAGUTHU RAGHURAM
SHETTY AND OTHERS

                                                                                                                                                                     135

                                                                                       05 November 2026 04:43 PM

531. I am persuaded by the JA Claimants that Dr Shetty’s case about Travelex is so improbable
         as to be incredible. I find it impossible to think that, if UAE Exchange was making an
         investment as Dr Shetty pleaded, there would not be documents reflecting this. Nor can
         I accept that Dr Shetty made such large transfers from his personal account without
         understanding them and the source of the funds. I also find it impossible to accept that
         Mr Manghat was (as Dr Shetty said when he was cross-examined) the “architect” for the
         acquisition, while keeping the arrangements for it secret from Dr Shetty but without
         obtaining any benefit to himself.

532. I reject Dr Shetty’s evidence about the Travelex acquisition, and I conclude that he knew
         that his personal acquisition was largely funded with money from the NMC Group, and
         that the funding was unlawful.

533. I reach this conclusion without relying on Mr Manghat’s evidence. As I have said and shall
         explain further, generally I do not consider that I can rely upon his evidence. However,
         with regard to Travelex, in my judgment it provides some additional support for the
         conclusions that I have reached. He said that, as he had understood it, Dr Shetty was
         concerned that he would lose his shares in UAE Exchange because of his dispute with HE
         Al Mazrouei, and he wanted to acquire Travelex “to break UAE Exchange’s hold on the
         market, as Travelex was a bigger brand”.

Acquisitions though NNMC: introduction

534. The JA Claimants further contend that Dr Shetty used NMC Group funds to make other
         personal acquisitions, using NNMC as a vehicle to do so. Those acquisitions included
         RCS, the Dubai Hills Villas and an oil investment in Wyoming.

535. As I have said, although Dr Shetty was the sole shareholder of NNMC and he and Mr
         Manghat were its directors, his case is that he was never aware of its existence during the
         Relevant Period. He pleads that it was incorporated on or around 17 April 2014, and that
         he infers that its incorporation was “arranged by (or on the instructions of) Mr Manghat”.
         He also pleads that, shortly thereafter, on 6 May 2014, the NNMC board purportedly
         passed a written resolution which authorised either Dr Shetty or Mr Manghat to open and
         operate bank accounts for it; and that on 10 September 2014 a request was made for
         Baroda to open a bank account in NNMC’s name. Dr Shetty disputes his signatures on
         the documents putting these arrangements in place, and says that, if they were genuine,
         they were obtained by deception. Thus, Dr Shetty’s case is this: “The incorporation and
         operation of NNMC ostensibly controlled by Dr Shetty, under a name designed to suggest
         it was a part of the NMC Group, but where it was in reality controlled by Mr Manghat,
         appears to [have] been intended to disguise this entity as part of legitimate business
         structures, so as to (1) minimise the risk of detection, (2) create plausible deniability,
         and/or (3) enable Mr Manghat and his associates to represent externally that NNMC was
         legitimate”.

ADGMCFI-2022-299 – NMC HEALTHCARE LIMITED (IN ADMINISTRATION) (SUBJECT TO A DEED OF COMPANY ARRANGEMENT) AND OTHERS V BAVAGUTHU RAGHURAM
SHETTY AND OTHERS

                                                                                                                                                                     136

                                                                                       05 November 2026 04:43 PM

536. I shall return to this contention after considering the particular acquisitions that the JA
         Claimants say were made by Dr Shetty through NNMC.

Royal Catering Services

537. In May 2015, Dr Shetty, with Mr Manghat’s help, acquired RCS from Captain Abdi
         Simreem and HE Al Mazrouei for AED 410 million. It was purchased in the name of NNMC.
         The JA Claimants say that Dr Shetty used NNMC to divert NMC funds for this private
         acquisition.

538. Dr Shetty does not dispute that RSC was his private acquisition, and that it was never
         part, and it was never intended that it should be part, of the NMC Group. He also agrees
         that Mr Manghat helped him make the acquisition. However, his case is that it was
         “hidden” from him that NNMC was involved: in cross-examination, he said “I’m not aware
         of it at all”.

539. The acquisition was made following a Memorandum of Understanding in the form of a
         letter dated 20 December 2014 from NNMC to Captain Simreem and HE Al Mazrouei. It
         was, or purported to be, signed by Dr Shetty and countersigned by Captain Simreem and
         HE Al Mazrouei, the countersignatures being dated 21 December 2014. It was on what
         appears to be NNMC letterhead. On 21 December 2014, a Mr Sharad Bhandari of Ardent
         Advisory, who were apparently financial advisers based in Abu Dhabi, sent an email to Dr
         Shetty at his NMC email address, which was copied to Mr Manghat and reads as follows:
         “Dear Mr Shetty, Just to let you know MOU has been signed with Royal Catering. We will
         now move forward with Due Diligence etc. I am meeting Prasanth [Manghat] today at
         4.30pm to finalise the time table and due diligence requirements. Prasanth [Manghat]
         has been a tremendous help to expedite the process”.

540. When the Sale and Purchase Agreement (the “SPA”) was concluded in May 2015, Ardent
         Advisory circulated it on 21 May 2015, the email being copied to two of Dr Shetty’s emails
         addresses. The email said that “we already have Dr Shetty’s signature”, but, as it appears
         from an email sent by Ardent Advisory to the sellers on the same day, he had signed only
         the last page of the SPA. Further signatures were required from him. Ardent Advisory
         proposed a meeting on 23 May 2015 for the signatures to be finalised, but that plan did
         not work smoothly because Dr Shetty had to travel to India for a meeting and presentation
         in the Prime Minister’s Office, and then on to Doha and Morocco. Ardent Advisory asked
         that he should speak directly to Captain Simreem about the delay in signing to
         documentation.

541. It is not disputed that Dr Shetty was in Morocco on 28 May 2015, and he signed the
         documentation for the acquisition when he was there. The SPA named NNMC as the
         purchaser, and it described NNMC as “a limited liability company established in the [BVI]
         … represented by Dr B R Shetty …”. It ran to 47 pages, and each page was initialled by Dr
         Shetty.

542. Two addenda to the SPA were executed, dated 5 August 2015 and 22 October 2015. Dr
         Shetty signed the addenda on behalf of both NNMC and Royal Gourmet Investment LLC
         (“RGI”). On 21 October 2015, a final execution version of the second addendum was
         circulated to, among others, Dr Shetty. It said that Dr Shetty represented the purchaser,
         and that NNMC had nominated RGI, also represented by Dr Shetty, as the “Purchaser’s
         Nominee”. It provided at clause 4 for payment of consideration of AED 405 million in
         accordance with a schedule.

543. I find it impossible to accept Dr Shetty’s contention that it was hidden from him that
         NNMC was involved in his acquisition of RSC. It was clearly identified on the documents
         that he signed, and I cannot believe that he signed them without knowing for whom he
         was signing. Moreover, Ardent Advisory and others communicated with him without
         making any attempt to disguise the arrangements and asked him to engage with Captain
         Simroon direct. Obviously, Mr Bhandari had not been told to keep NNMC hidden from Dr
         Shetty.

544. In his closing submissions, Dr Shetty made no admission about the funding of payments
         for RCS, explaining that he has no knowledge about how the acquisition was funded.
         However, Dr Shetty did not challenge the evidence of Mr Jones and the JA Action
         Accountant Experts that the acquisition of RCS was funded by five Direct Shetty Payment
         and one Indirect Shetty Payment, and I accept their conclusions:

a. AED 134 million from NMCH to the 2359 account pursuant to a transfer request
         to Baroda dated 21 May 2015;

b. AED 68.444 million from NMC Specialty Hospital LLC to the 2359 account
         pursuant to a transfer request to Baroda dated 9 November 2015;

c. AED 8.5 million from NMCH to the 2359 account pursuant to a transfer request to
         Baroda dated 9 November 2015;

d. AED 70 million from Beiersdorf Cosmetic Trading Co LLC (“Beiersdorf”) to the
         2359 account pursuant to a transfer request to Baroda dated 10 November 2015;

e. AED 62.5 million from NMC Specialty Hospital LLC to the 2359 account pursuant
         to a transfer request to Baroda dated 10 November 2015; and

f.  AED 62 million from Nexgen to the 2359 account pursuant to a request to Baroda

    dated 10 November 2015, the payment by Nexgen being funded by NMCH and

    NMC Specialty Hospital LLC.

545. I reject Dr Shetty’s answer to the JA Claimants’ case about RSC. I conclude that Dr Shetty
         knew that the investment was funded with funds from the NMC Group.

546. I add that, while Dr Shetty pleaded, and Mr Wandowicz argued, that, after the acquisition,
         RCS was refinanced, and AED 275 million was returned to NMCH, this does not seem to
         me to answer the JA Claimants’ complaint in any way.

Dubai Hills Villas

547. The JA Claimants plead that Dr Shetty bought real property, specifically two plots for
        development in the Dubai Hills Grove Community, using funds derived from the NMC
        Group, and that between May 2014 and May 2018 a total of some AED 70.4 million was
        spent in acquiring and developing the plots. They contend that the payments to Dr Shetty
        for this purpose were fraudulent.

548. Their case is that the costs of the development and its funding was this:

a. On 8 May 2014, a payment of AED 7,935,914 was made out of the 2359 account,
         and it was funded by a Direct Shetty Payment into the 2359 account from NMCH
         of the same amount that day;

b. On 14 May 2014, a payment of AED 5,964,924 was made out of the 2359 account,
         and it was funded by a Direct Shetty Payment into the 2359 account from NMCH
         of the same amount that day;

c. On 19 May 2014, payments of AED 1,327,676 were made out of the 2359 account,
         and they were funded by a Direct Shetty Payment into the 2359 account from
         NMCH of the same amount that day;

d. On 18 August 2014, payments of AED 3,056,850 were made out of the 2359
         account, and they were funded by a Direct Shetty Payment into the 2359 account
         from NMCH of the same amount that day;

e. On 25 September 2014, payments of AED 9,200,956 were made out of the 2359
         account, and they were funded by a Direct Shetty Payment into the 2359 account
         from NMCH of the same amount that day;

f.  On 20 December payments of AED 3,056,850 were made out of the 2359 account,

    and they were funded by a Direct Shetty Payment into the 2359 account from

    NNMC’s account at Baroda on 18 December 2014;

h. On 25 April 2015, payments of AED 2,037,900 were made out of the 2359 account,
         and they were funded in part by a Direct Shetty Payment into the 2359 account
         from NMCH of the AED 1.710,000;

i.  On 1 August 2015, payments of AED 9,200,996 1,327 were made out of the 2359

    account, and they were funded by a Direct Shetty Payment into the 2359 account

    from NMCH of approximately the same amount that day;

j.  On 27 September 2016, a total of AED 2 million was transferred by NMCH to the

    property developer, Dubai Hills Estate LLC; and

k. On 7 March 2018 and 28 March 2018, a total of AED 17.4 million was paid by
         Nexgen to Dubai Hills Estate LLC.

549. Dr Shetty does not dispute that funds from the NMC Group were so expended, nor that
         the plots were his own personal investment and their purchase and development were
         unconnected with the NMC Group. He pleads that one villa was for his own use and the
         other for Dr Reddy of Hetero FZCO. He continues, “the opportunity to acquire the villas
         was presented to Dr Shetty by Mr Manghat and Mr Manghat informed Dr Shetty that they
         would be acquired with the benefit of a dividend due to Dr Shetty from Nexgen (and that
         Dr Reddy was also interested in purchasing a villa using the dividend due to him)”. He said
         that he did not “handle the acquisition of the villas himself”, but the transaction was
         handled for him by Mr Joseph Antony of BRS Ventures and any payments made by Ms
         D’Mello, on the instructions, as he inferred, of Mr Manghat. Thus, Dr Shetty’s answer to
         this part of the JA Claimants’ case is (in the words of his second witness statement) that
         he denies “having any contemporaneous knowledge that NMC (or indeed NNMC, of
         which he was unaware) funds were used to purchase the villas”.

550. I accept the JA Claimants’ submission that the purchase was not in fact funded by a
         dividend from Nexgen: the annual accounts of Nexgen do not record the payment of any
         dividend for the year ended 31 December 2015. The issue is whether Dr Shetty believed
         that it was so financed. The JA Claimants say that Dr Shetty’s contention simply beggars
         belief.

551. I agree: the JA Claimants’ arguments are wholly convincing. The two plots cost some AED
         46 million in total. Nexgen’s audited accounts for the year ended 31 December 2015,
         signed by Dr Shetty and Mr Manghat, record total assets of AED 16 million in 2014 and
         AED 59 million in 2015, and incomes of AED 1.3 million in 2014 and AED 13.7 million in
         2015. AED 19.8 million and AED 26.2 million. I find it incredible that Dr Shetty believed
         that a dividend from Nexgen might significantly contribute to the purchase. He disavows
         accounting ability, but he is an acute and experienced businessman. I conclude that Dr
         Shetty knew that the acquisition as wholly or largely funded by the NMC Group.

552. This conclusion is corroborated by the evidence of Mr Manghat, who pointed out that it
         was Dr Shetty, and not he, who benefitted from the villas being acquired with NMC
         Group’s funds. No credible reason was suggested as to why Mr Manghat would want to
         trick Dr Shetty into thinking that the acquisition was being financed by a dividend (or
         dividends) from Nexgen.

Wyoming oil field

553. In July 2014, NNMC invested some US$ 3.9 million in leases for oil exploration in
         Wyoming, United States of America. As I understand it, it is not disputed, and in any case
         I find, that the investment was funded by a transfer of AED 14.33 million (the equivalent
         of some US$ 3.9 million) from one of NMCH’s accounts with Baroda. On its face, the
         transfer request bears Dr Shetty’s signature, but he denies signing it.

554. The JA Claimants submitted that I should not accept Dr Shetty’s denial. They argued that
         he could not possibly know whether or not he signed this particular request, given the
         many documents that he used to sign, sometimes in batches. To my mind, this issue is
         not important. I am prepared to consider the complaint about the Wyoming investment
         on the basis that Dr Shetty did not sign the instructions for the transfer of funds himself.
         After all, his electronic signature might have been used.

555. On 3 July 2014 and 5 July 2014, Mr Michael Lansing, a Wyoming lawyer, sent emails to a
         Mr Tariq Basheer about investing in a lease and the procedure for doing so. It is clear from
         the documents that Mr Basheer was in contact with Mr Manghat.

556. On 7 July 2014, Ms D’Mello sent an email to Mr Basheer, copied to Mr Manghat. She
         attached to it “lease transfer documents” said to have been signed by Dr Shetty. When
         he was cross-examined, Dr Shetty said that he was not aware of the documents.

557. However, Mr Lansing required the documents to be notarised so as to comply with
         Wyoming law. On 13 July 2014, Ms D’Mello circulated scanned copies of the documents
         which had been notarised and stamped. One stamp affirmed that the documents were
         “Signed before me by Dr B R Shetty”; another is the stamp of the Indian Embassy in Abu
         Dhabi; a third stamp bears the date of 13 July 2014; a fourth provides details of the
         consular official, Mr S P Rawat, who notarised the documents, his signature appearing
         above the stamp.

558. Dr Shetty denied attending the Embassy to sign the documents, but I find that his
         signatures on the documents are genuine, and the notarisation is truthful. It is highly
         improbable that a consular official would improperly notarise a document of this kind (or
         any kind), and the more improbable that an official would improperly notarise a
         document as being signed by a person of the prominence and standing that Dr Shetty had
         in the UAE.

559. I reject Dr Shetty’s denial that he knew of NNMC’s investment in Wyoming oil exploration.
         I also conclude that he knew that the investment was funded by the NMC Group.

Other NNMC payments

560. The JA Action Accountant Experts, whose evidence was not disputed, agreed that a total
         of AED 17.5 million was transferred by NMCH to NNMC. As well as the payment relating

         to the Wyoming investment, there were seven other transfers between July 2014 and
         March 2015. The NMC Group’s records provide no explanation for them.

561. The JA Claimants plead that the money was put into various investments, and that it is to
         be inferred that “these investments were made as part of the establishment of a personal
         investment portfolio on behalf of Dr Shetty and that they were made at his direction, for
         his personal benefit and at the expense of the NMC Group”.

562. I therefore return to Dr Shetty’s contention that he was unaware of the existence of
         NNMC, and that it was incorporated and operated by Mr Manghat without his knowledge.
         I find it impossible to accept Dr Shetty’s contention in view of NNMC’s part in the
         acquisition of RSC and the investment in Wyoming. The payments from NNMC for the
         Dubai Hills Villas, though less powerful evidence by themselves, provide some further
         support for my conclusion.

563. Mr Wandowicz, however, argued that there is other evidence that supports Dr Shetty’s
         case. He advanced five points, but I am not persuaded by them.

564. First, he submitted that some documents relating to NNMC’s affairs are “fabrications”,
         citing minutes of a board meeting said to have taken place in the UK on 3 February 2015
         and to have been attended by Mr Manghat and Dr Shetty. In fact, Dr Shetty was in the UAE
         on 3 February 2015. This does not, to my mind, show that Dr Shetty did not know of
         NNMC’s existence.

565. Next, Mr Wandowicz placed reliance on Mr Manghat’s response to Dr Shetty’s contention
         that Mr Manghat arranged an investment by NNMC in the La Sara oil field in Texas. Emails
         relating to it were found on Mr Manghat’s email address with BRS Holdings. Mr Manghat
         denied that he had access to that account. Mr Wandowicz submitted that Mr Manghat’s
         denial was untrue, and that his false denial vindicates Dr Shetty’s case that Mr Manghat
         controlled the investments through NNMC. However, this would not mean that Dr Shetty
         was unaware of the La Sara investment, still less that he was unaware of the existence of
         NNMC.

566. Thirdly and fourthly, Mr Wandowicz relied on (i) Mr Manghat’s denial that NNMC was
         incorporated by him or on his instructions; and (ii) his denial that he exercised any control
         or transacted any business for NNMC. These denials, it was said, were clearly untrue.
         Again, even if Mr Manghat did arrange the incorporation of NNMC and even if he did
         exercise control over NNMC and transact business for it, these matters do not provide
         support for Dr Shetty’s denial that he knew of its existence.

567. Finally, Mr Wandowicz referred to an investment made by NNMC in an entity called Kelvin
         Gulf (apparently its original name) or Lattice (as it was apparently renamed). It was the
         subject of a WhatsApp exchange between Dr Shetty and Mr Manghat on 28 October 2019.
         Dr Shetty sent Mr Manghat an image of the trade licence, asking what the company was

         and explaining that his daughter, Reema, had told him that he owned it. Mr Manghat
         responded that Dr Shetty had helped a Mr Tariq Basheer, a banker with Dubai Islamic
         Bank, in relation to it, and saying that they could “skip it to him” since it was of no great

568. When asked about this in cross-examination, Mr Manghat accepted that it was most
         likely that, at some time in 2014, he had told Dr Shetty that Mr Basheer needed help, Dr
         Shetty had agreed to provide help and to invest in the Kelvin Gulf entity, and Mr Manghat
         had arranged it. Dr Shetty submitted that this illustrates Mr Manghat’s control of his
         investments, and that, since Dr Shetty was apparently unaware of the acquisition of
         Kelvin Gulf or Lattice, which Mr Wandowicz described as “a substantive acquisition”, it
         would not be reasonable to assume without solid evidence that he knew of NNMC, a
         “mere investment vehicle”.

569. I am not persuaded by this argument. It is understandable that Dr Shetty might not have
         recognised the name of the company that his daughter mentioned as being the object of
         some help afforded to Mr Basheer some five years earlier, especially if the company had
         changed its name and was then of little value. I cannot accept that this supports the
         contention that Dr Shetty might not have been aware of the company through which he
         acquired RSC and the Wyoming investment.

570. I conclude that Dr Shetty knew of NNMC, that it was a vehicle for his personal
         investments and that his investments through NNMC were improperly funded by the
         NMC Group.

The misappropriations for Mr Manghat’s benefit

571. I shall consider the allegations that funds were misappropriated from the NMC Group for
         the benefit of Mr Manghat later in my judgment. Here, I only say that these allegations are
         peripheral to the case that Dr Shetty was party to the fraud.

The misappropriations for the benefit of the Bin Buttis

572. The JA Claimants plead that it is to be inferred that Dr Shetty was party to the fraud and
         participated in fraudulent trading from, inter alia, “his knowledge of the Unlawful
         Payments (alternatively, his knowledge of some part thereof)”, and in their pleading the
         term “Unlawful Payments” is used to cover payments to the Bin Buttis and their related
         companies, as well as payments to or for the benefit of Dr Shetty and Mr Manghat. They
         did not develop their case about Dr Shetty’s knowledge of payments to the Bin Buttis and
         their associated companies in their cross-examination of Dr Shetty or their submissions.
         I do not need to make any findings about this part of the case against Dr Shetty, and I
         decline to do so.

Dr Shetty and other aspects of fraudulent scheme

573. I uphold the JA Claimants’ case that Dr Shetty knew of and benefitted from the Direct
         Shetty Payments and the Indirect Shetty Payments, and that, as Dr Shetty knew, there
         was no lawful basis for them. I also uphold, in substance, their case about Dr Shetty’s
         involvement in the events of March 2019. Therefore, these core elements of the case
         against him are established. The JA Claimants also say that their allegations about other
         elements of the fraudulent scheme are not “makeweights” in that they “demonstrate the
         same disregard for corporate boundaries, the use of NMC resources for Dr Shetty’s
         benefit and the concealment of the resulting financial position”. Nevertheless, I can deal
         with these further allegations against Dr Shetty relatively briefly.

Dr Shetty: the Undisclosed Securities

574. I have introduced the Undisclosed Securities at paras. 200ff above. The JA Claimants
         plead that, during the Relevant Period, “on at least 16 occasions loans or facilities were
         entered into by related parties where the NMC Group provided guarantees or some other
         form of security, which should have been, but were not, disclosed in the NMC Group’s
         consolidated financial statements”. The other forms of security included post-dated
         cheques, undated security cheques and other collateral. (In their closing submissions,
         the JA Claimants said that there were seventeen undeclared financial benefits: I believe
         that they overlooked that the case about one of the benefits originally pleaded, a facility
         for Dr Shetty provided by AHB, had been abandoned.) The JA Action Accounting Experts
         agree that:

         a. Such arrangements should have been disclosed in NMC plc’s financial
                   statements, being financial guarantee contracts within the definition in the IFRS
                   9: A contract that requires the issuer to make specified payments to reimburse
                   the holder for a loss it incurs because a specified debtor fails to make payment
                   when due in accordance with the original or modified terms of a debt instruction”.
                   (The term “debt instrument” is not defined.)

         b. None of these arrangements alleged by the JA Claimants was disclosed in the
                   financial statements.

575. Mr Manghat admits the Undisclosed Securities, and Baroda did not dispute them in its
         expert accounting evidence. Dr Shetty did not admit them in his Defence. However, he
         did not challenge that evidence of Mr Jones or of the JA Action Accountant Experts about
         them, and I find that the Undisclosed Securities were proved by the JA Claimants.

576. Dr Shetty also pleads that “to the extent that the alleged Undisclosed Collateral Benefits
         were provided in respect of Dr Shetty and/or entities said to be connected with him”, then
         (i) he denies knowledge of them; (ii) he does not admit that were valid or enforceable

ADGMCFI-2022-299 – NMC HEALTHCARE LIMITED (IN ADMINISTRATION) (SUBJECT TO A DEED OF COMPANY ARRANGEMENT) AND OTHERS V BAVAGUTHU RAGHURAM
SHETTY AND OTHERS

                                                                                                                                                                     144

                                                                                       05 November 2026 04:43 PM

         against the NMC Group companies; and (iii) he does not admit that granting them was
         unlawful or improper and, if and to the extent that it was, Dr Shetty denies knowing this.

577. Mr Wandowicz advanced no argument that the banks had accepted invalid or
         unenforceable security, and I reject that suggestion. Nor would it be an answer to the
         complaint of non-disclosure if the securities were unlawful or otherwise improper. Nor
         was it argued that Dr Shetty was unaware of his disclosure obligations. The real question
         is whether Dr Shetty knew of the Undisclosed Securities.

578. Of the sixteen Undisclosed Securities, seven supported by bank facilities granted to Dr
         Shetty personally, in one case jointly with KBBAM. Of the other nine, seven were granted
         to entities associated with Dr Shetty (three to Neopharma, two to GGCM and one each to
         RCS and Modular Concepts). The other two were granted to KBBAM and Centurion.

579. The six facilities were granted to Dr Shetty by First Gulf Bank, Baroda and Dubai Islamic
         Bank, and they were, in total, to a value of over AED 900 million. The joint facility for Dr
         Shetty and KBBAM was a facility of AED 523 million granted by AHB and ADCB.

580. On the face of it, Dr Shetty signed all the facilities that he was granted, and I find it
         impossible to believe that all these banks were deceived into accepting forged
         signatures. I conclude that they were signed by him. Further, I find it impossible to
         believe that Dr Shetty was, in some way, tricked into signing the facilities agreement
         without knowing that he was doing so. After all, in the case of the joint facility, it was not
         suggested that KBBAM was tricked into entering into a facility without knowing of it, and I
         find it impossible to suppose that, in these circumstances, Dr Shetty was so deceived.

581. This conclusion is corroborated in the case of the facility granted to him by Dubai Islamic
         Bank: I have already referred (at para. 516) to Dr Shetty’s WhatsApp message on 14 March
         2017 in which he told Mr Krishnamoorthy to use either NMC funds of “Dubai Islamic
         proceeds” to the Oman Cement City investment and make other payments.

582. I accept that JA Claimants’ submission that Dr Shetty knew that facilities that he had
         entered into personally were supported by security from the NMC Group, and that the
         facilities were not disclosed as they should have been in NMC plc’s financial statements.

583. I also conclude that Dr Shetty was aware of the other Undisclosed Securities. For
         example, in May 2017, Modular Concepts obtained a facility from Commercial Bank of
         Dubai, secured by a personal guarantee of Dr Shetty, a personal guarantee of Mr Rai and
         a corporate guarantee of NMCH: it is one of the Undisclosed Securities. Dr Shetty
         acknowledged in cross-examination that it was “wrong” for NMCH to give a corporate
         guarantee, but he denied that he had signed his personal guarantee. Given his
         relationship with Mr Rai, I cannot accept that.
