Dr Shetty: the Undisclosed Debts

584. I have explained that debts incurred by the NMC Group were not disclosed in its financial
         statements throughout the Relevant Period. Dr Shetty does not dispute the JA Claimants’
         allegations about the debt that was hidden. His case about this is pleaded as follows:
         “[Dr Shetty] was one of the persons from whom the fraudulent conduct, and the
         additional debt taken on to prop up NMC’s finances, was hidden. In particular, at all
         material times, Dr Shetty’s understanding of the financial position of NMC was as
         presented to him at the board meetings of NMC plc”.

585. The JA Claimants submit that this position is incredible. The Undisclosed Debt was
         incurred in significant part in order to cover up the vast payments to Dr Shetty and for his
         benefit. They contend that, given Dr Shetty’s knowledge of the scale of these payments,
         he cannot have believed that the finances of NMC, as a listed group, were being properly
         presented to the market.

586. Further, Dr Shetty was aware of the lending facilities that the NMC Group entered into.
         The JA Claimants cited these examples of facilities that he signed: a facility with Dubai
         Islamic Bank dated 25 March 2012 for AED 200 million; a facility with Abu Dhabi Islamic
         Bank dated 8 February 2016 for AED 275 million; and a facility with Dubai Islamic Bank
         dated 25 March 2018 for facilities of US$ 350 million.

587. When he was cross-examined, Dr Shetty denied that he was closely involved in the
         borrowing arrangements for the NMC Group, but the question is not whether he was
         involved in entering into the facility arrangements, but whether he knew of them. His
         more important answers in cross-examination are therefore these: answer, “but they
         don’t give unless I sign, that’s for sure”; question, “… I suggest you would have had at
         least a rough idea of the true scale of NMC’s debts”; answer, “Rough idea I had, yes”. I
         would reject any suggestion that, in giving these answers, Dr Shetty was confused or did
         not mean what he said.

588. I conclude that, given Dr Shetty’s knowledge of the vast funds that he was receiving from
         the NMC Group and given that he had a rough idea of the NMC Group’s borrowings, he
         must have known that the borrowings were not fully reported in its consolidated financial
         statements.

Dr Shetty: the Sham Suppliers

589. As I have explained, the Sham Suppliers Scheme was a crucial part of the machinery
         whereby the NMC Group executives managed to present a false picture of the financial
         position in the audited financial statements. The JA Claimants submit that I should infer
         that Dr Shetty was aware of it.

590. Here, they rely upon two points: the first is the WhatsApp exchange between Dr Shetty
         and Mr Krishnamoorthy on 6 March 2020 (see para. 441 above), and in particular Dr

LISTED IN SCHEDULE 1 TO THE ADMINISTRATION APPLICATION
ADGMCFI-2022-299 – NMC HEALTHCARE LIMITED (IN ADMINISTRATION) (SUBJECT TO A DEED OF COMPANY ARRANGEMENT) AND OTHERS V BAVAGUTHU RAGHURAM
SHETTY AND OTHERS

                                                                                                                                                                     146

                                                                                       05 November 2026 04:43 PM

         Shetty’s inquiry whether there were any “binami companies in my name”. Here, the JA
         Claimants’ point that Dr Shetty did not ask whether sham (binami) companies were being
         used, but whether they were in his name. They submit that the formulation of the
         question suggests that Dr Shetty was aware that sham companies were being used as
         part of the fraudulent scheme.

591. Thus far, I see some force in the JA Claimants’ point. However, it is not surprising that by
         early March 2020 Dr Shetty knew that sham companies had been used: there must have
         been a good deal of discussion after the revelations of the Muddy Waters reports about
         how the fraud was carried out. It does not follow that Dr Shetty was aware of the Sham
         Suppliers Scheme before those reports and while the scheme was being operated.

592. The JA Claimants’ second point was about Nexgen. I have already, when considering the
         acquisition of the Dubai Hills villas, referred to Nexgen’s financial statements, which Dr
         Shetty signed, and to Dr Shetty’s case that he believed that a dividend from Nexgen would
         finance it: see para. 549 above. The 2015 statements record cash at bank balances of
         AED 349,296, and total shareholders’ equity of AED 4,625,617. The JA Claimants’ point,
         as I understand it, is that Dr Shetty must have realised that Nexgen had no significant
         legitimate commercial business and its activity was largely by way of Sham Suppliers
         transactions.

593. Again, I am not convinced by the JA Claimants’ argument that this shows that Dr Shetty
         was aware of the Sham Suppliers Scheme. There is no sufficient reason to think that Dr
         Shetty would be concerned to turn his mind to the nature of Nexgen’s activities.

594. I am therefore not persuaded that the JA Claimants have shown that Dr Shetty was aware
         of the Sham Suppliers Scheme while the fraud was being perpetrated. He must have
         known that improper and dishonest devices were being used to disguise the fraud in the
         internal accounts, but he might well have been content to leave it to others to deal with
         these matters – either out of indifference or because he decided that it was best that he
         did not know.

Dr Shetty: the Related Party Transactions

595. In their Particulars of Claim, the JA Claimants plead their case about related party
         transactions as follows: “Based on investigations, between 2012 and 2018, there were
         related party transactions (i) between the NMC Group and Neopharma totalling at least
         AED 7.1 billion, (ii) between the NMC Group and UAE Exchange totalling at least AED 2.5
         billion, and (iii) between the NMC Group and Nexgen totalling at least 18.9 billion. There
         was also a substantial volume of related party transactions between GGCM, Modular
         Concepts, [DAG] and [DHE]. These related party transactions greatly exceeded the
         related party transactions reported in the NMC Groups annual accounts. … Based on
         these investigations, there was no obvious commercial basis for these transactions”.

ADGMCFI-2022-111 – ABU DHABI COMMERCIAL BANK PJSC V PRASANTH MANGHAT
ADGMCFI-2020-020 - IN THE MATTER OF NMC HEALTHCARE LIMITED (IN ADMINISTRATION) (SUBJECT TO A DEED OF COMPANY ARRANGEMENT) AND THE COMPANIES
LISTED IN SCHEDULE 1 TO THE ADMINISTRATION APPLICATION
ADGMCFI-2022-299 – NMC HEALTHCARE LIMITED (IN ADMINISTRATION) (SUBJECT TO A DEED OF COMPANY ARRANGEMENT) AND OTHERS V BAVAGUTHU RAGHURAM
SHETTY AND OTHERS

                                                                                                                                                                     147

                                                                                       05 November 2026 04:43 PM

596. Dr Shetty did not plead in response to the substance of these allegations, declining to do
         so until they were “properly pleaded”. His complaint was that he could not understand
         what volume of unreported transactions is alleged, who was alleged to have given
         instructions for them, who was responsible for reporting them, and the relevance of
         whether or not there was a commercial basis for them. If a party does not understand the
         case against him, there are proper and familiar procedures that he can use to deal with
         the problem. It is not open to him simply to decline to engage with it, as Dr Shetty did. In
         any case, the case that the JA Claimants advanced was clear enough, particularly after
         their Reply. I reject Dr Shetty’s pleading complaint.

597. International Accounting Standard 24 (“IAS 24”), which was in force throughout the
         Relevant Period, provides as follows: “If an entity has had related party transactions
         during the periods covered by the financial statements, it shall disclose the nature of the
         related party relationship as well as information about those transactions and
         outstanding balances, including commitments, necessary for users to understand the
         potential effect of the relationship on the financial statements”. Further, the notes to the
         accounts of NMC plc included a note about related party transactions: for example, the
         statements for the year ended 31 December 2015 said, “These represent transactions
         with related parties, including major shareholders and senior management of the Group,
         and entities controlled, jointly controlled or significantly influenced by such parties. or
         where such parties are members of the key management personnel of the entities”.

598. There is no dispute that Neopharma, Nexgen, GGCM, DHE, DAN, UAE Exchange and
         Modular Concepts were all related parties for the purposes of IAS 24 and the notes to the
         accounts. Dr Shetty admits 90% ownership of Neopharma, a 54% indirect interest in
         Nexgen, 100% ownership of GGCM and DHE, ownership of DAN and a 40% interest in
         UAE Exchange. He also knew that Mr Rai, who had a 30% shareholding in Modular
         Concepts, was the NMC Group’s production manager.

599. Further, the evidence of Mr Jones and the JA Action Accountant Experts establishes that
         there were substantial transactions between the NMC Group and the each of these
         companies, which should have been, but was not, disclosed in the financial statements.
         (The only difference between the experts was whether the volume of transactions should
         be described as “vast”, Mr Grantham declining to endorse this description for the
         understandable reason that it involved a subjective assessment.) The scale of the
         omission from the accounts was illustrated by the JA Claimants in their final submissions
         by reference to (i) the year ended 31 December 2014, when the related party transactions
         exceeded AED 2 billion but the financial statements reported related party purchases of
         US$32.34 million (or AED 118.77 million) and related party sales of US$9.78 million (or
         AED 35.92 million); and (ii) the year ended 31 December 2018, when the related party
         transactions exceeded AED 4 billion but the financial statements reported related party
         purchases of US$130.11 million (or AED 477.83 million) and related party sales of
         US$0.15 million (or AED 0.55 million).

ADGMCFI-2022-299 – NMC HEALTHCARE LIMITED (IN ADMINISTRATION) (SUBJECT TO A DEED OF COMPANY ARRANGEMENT) AND OTHERS V BAVAGUTHU RAGHURAM
SHETTY AND OTHERS

                                                                                                                                                                     148

                                                                                       05 November 2026 04:43 PM

600. The real issue here is that the JA Claimants plead that Dr Shetty knew of the related party
         transactions (or some of them) and allege “his knowing participation, during the relevant
         period, in the preparation and publication of false and misleading consolidated financial
         statements of NMC plc and the wider NMC Group”; and Dr Shetty denies knowing
         participation.

601. The JA Claimants made detailed submissions that, to my mind, demonstrate that the
         dealings with each of the related parties differed so much from what was disclosed that
         Dr Shetty must have realised that proper disclosure was not being made. I do not propose
         to set out those submissions in detail. I accept them, and shall confine myself to four
         illustrations of why I do so:

         a. NMC plc’s statements for 2015 and 2016 did not disclose any related party
                   dealings with Neopharma, but Mr Jones’ reconstruction of the Neopharma ledger
                   account in NMCH’s records (which was not challenged) proves debts of AED 400
                   million in 2015 and AED 477 million in 2016.

         b. The related party note in NMC plc’s 2016 statement records a single sum of US$
                   14,000 for its sales to related parties, but by a transfer request to Baroda dated 9
                   October 2016 and apparently signed by Dr Shetty, DHE paid AED 48,846,000 (or
                   US$ 13.3 million) to NMCH. As Dr Shetty accepted, this alone shows that sales
                   to related parties were massively understated in 2016.

         c. Dr Shetty pleads that “GGCM was a substantial supplier of services to the NMC
                   Group”, but no dealings with GGCM were disclosed in any of NMC plc’s accounts
                   during the Relevant Period. Dr Shetty was its 100% owner and its sole authorised
                   signatory, and he received Indirect Shetty Payments from it. As I have said, I
                   cannot accept that Dr Shetty was unaware of GGCM.

         d. No dealings with Modular Concepts were disclosed in any of NMC plc’s accounts
                   during the Relevant Period. I accept the JA Claimants’ submission that, given Mr
                   Rai’s role as the NMC Group’s Head of Procurement, and his relationship with Mr
                   Rai, Dr Shetty cannot have been unaware that there were dealings between the
                   NMC Group and Modular Concepts.

602. When he was asked about the failure to declare related party transactions, Dr Shetty
         disavowed that it was his responsibility. He said, “This is CFO’s job. Auditors’ job”; and
         he said that he had no responsibility because “my job is to make the companies and
         progress it”. I accept that Dr Shetty’s responsibilities did not extend to calculating for
         himself the exact level of transactions with the various related entities. I do not accept
         that he had no responsibility in respect of what was reported in the financial statements
         nor that he believed that he had no responsibility in respect of what was reported. I note
         that Dr Shetty signed NMC plc’s financial statements for the years 2012 to 2016. Further,
         at a meeting of NMC plc’s board on 26 August 2012, which Dr Shetty attended, the

                                                                                                                                                                     149

                                                                                       05 November 2026 04:43 PM

         company secretary, Mr Simon Watkins, had spelt out the importance of disclosure of
         related party transactions, and the directors confirmed that their interests were fully
         disclosed. I cannot believe that Dr Shetty was unaware of his duties with regard to
         disclosure.

603. I conclude that, as a director of NMC plc and the signatory of the financial statements
         between 2012 and 2016, Dr Shetty was aware that related party transactions were not
         properly disclosed in them.

The Neopharma Promissory Note Scheme

604. In their closing submissions, the JA Claimants contended that what they called the
         “Neopharma Promissory Note Scheme” is a further illustration of Dr Shetty’s knowledge
         that “artificial financing arrangements” were being used by the NMC Group. In a
         WhatsApp exchange on 25 May 2017, Mr Krishmanoorthy put forward the idea that
         Neopharma might issue a promissory note for AED 500 million payable to a NMC
         company, and that the NMC Group might “monetise” it: that is to say, that Neopharma
         should produce a promissory note that the NMC Group might discount. Mr Suresh Kumar
         observed that Neopharma was not in a position to make such a large payment. In
         subsequent messages, Dr Krishnamoorthy said that he had spoken to Dr Shetty about
         the idea, apparently on 23 May 2017 when he was in London for a NMC plc board meeting.
         Further, Mr Krishnamoorthy said that, since he and Mr Suresh Kumar thought differently
         about the proposal, “Monday, we will meet with Dr Shetty together with [Mr Manghat]” to
         resolve the question.

605. The scheme would not have been a legitimate device to raise money: there is no evidence
         as to whether or not it was implemented. However, the JA Claimants submitted that the
         exchanges show that “the NMC finance teams had no qualms bringing illegitimate
         schemes to him for consideration“ and that “[t]his exchange … supports the inference
         that Dr Shetty was aware of, and engaged with, the deliberate generation of artificial
         funding arrangements”.

606. When the JA Claimants presented this argument, I observed that they had not pleaded
         anything about the Neopharma Promissory Note Scheme, and I questioned whether they
         could properly rely upon in it in support of an allegation of dishonesty or improper
         conduct on the part of Dr Shetty. Mr Thanki accepted that they could not pursue an
         allegation that the Neopharma Promissory Note Scheme was dishonest, and he was right
         to do so.

607. However, the JA Claimants still sought to argue that this exchange illustrates that Dr
         Shetty took important decisions about, or at least was involved in important decisions
         about, the finances of the NMC Group, that the NMC finance executives consulted him
         about such questions, and that Dr Shetty’s entities outside the NMC Group were treated
         as “instruments freely available for NMC’s financing purposes”. These arguments were,

         in reality, advanced to support the contention that Dr Shetty was party to the fraud, and I
         cannot accept that they can properly be advanced without being pleaded. I shall
         disregard the JA Claimants’ contentions about the Neopharma Promissory Note Scheme.

Dr Shetty: the Offset Deposits

608. The JA Claimants invited me to find that the Offset Deposit scheme “formed part of the
         machinery by which the extraction of NMC Group resources was funded and concealed”,
         and that it was not hidden from Dr Shetty but he either knew of it or deliberately shut his
         eyes to it. As I have said, the complaint about Offset Deposits is pleaded in support of
         the JA Claimants’ case against Baroda, and not against Dr Shetty (or Mr Manghat). I
         decline to make the findings that the JA Claimants invite about Dr Shetty knowing of the
         scheme or deliberately shutting his eyes to it.

Dr Shetty’s arguments

609. When I assess whether I should conclude from this evidence and these considerations,
         taken together, that Dr Shetty knew of and was party to the fraud on the NMC Group, I
         must, as Mr Wandowicz powerfully argued, give full consideration to Dr Shetty’s position
         and achievements, his personality and qualities, his modus operandi, and the nature of
         his dealings with others and in particular with Mr Manghat.

610. By the time of the IPO, Dr Shetty was 70 years of age. He was, as Lord Clanwilliam
         testified, looking to step back from a leadership role and to have his son, Mr Binay Shetty,
         succeed him. He had achieved great financial success and was immensely wealthy. He
         also had won public recognition, both in India and in the UAE. Mr Wandowicz argued that
         there was absolutely no reason for him to turn to dishonesty and fraud, and thereby risk
         both ignominy and severe punishment. It is even more improbable, it was said, that Dr
         Shetty would have entered into a fraudulent scheme to misappropriate corporate assets
         without making any attempt to disguise his association with it, but the scheme alleged by
         the JA Claimants involved funds being transferred to Dr Shetty’s personal accounts in the
         UAE under instructions to banks that referred, for example, to “transfer to Chairman” or
         “transfer to Dr Shetty” and with corresponding entries in the NMC Group’s internal
         accounts.

611. As I have already said, it is right in cases of this kind to consider whether the conduct
         alleged against a defendant is explicable in terms of what might plausibly have motivated
         him or might account for his conduct. Mr Thanki suggested that the explanation for Dr
         Shetty’s conduct might be pure greed, but I am not wholly comfortable with that
         explanation. However, to my mind, it is not difficult to suppose that, after conducting his
         business affairs with great success and over many years without the discipline that
         comes with operating through a listed company, Dr Shetty was not prepared or willing to
         accept the constraints that are the price of a public listing. He knew the rules, as he

         acknowledged in cross-examination, but apparently he was not willing to change his
         ways and to comply with them.

612. This view of Dr Shetty’s state of mind is, to my mind, more consistent with the fact that,
         through the counter-payments, monies were returned to the NMC Group from Dr Shetty’s
         personal accounts, albeit in a distinctly lesser amount than the payments to them, than
         the view that Dr Shetty might have been motivated by simple greed.

613. What of Dr Shetty’s qualities and personality? I accept Mr Wandowicz’s submission that
         Dr Shetty’s success in business came not from expertise in accounting matters but, as it
         seems to me, hard work, an acute eye for business opportunities and, as it was put, “his
         ability with people”. I accept his evidence that he was “not directly involved or qualified
         in financial functions of the companies”. As Lord Clanwilliam put it, “Dr Shetty was a man
         of words, not a man of detail”.

614. I do not consider that this answers the JA Claimants’ case. The allegations against him
         are not about detailed financial arrangements, and do not depend on him having much
         knowledge of accounting principles or familiarity with record keeping. They are based on
         the movements of large sums of money over an extended period, and how Dr Shetty made
         major acquisitions.

615. It was not in dispute between the parties that Dr Shetty was not involved in the routine
         conduct of the business of the NMC Group. His involvement in its financial affairs was,
         as Mr Wandowicz submitted, very limited. I accept Dr Shetty’s evidence that his
         “leadership style was always consultative and built on trust and delegation”. Mr
         Manghat’s evidence suggested that Dr Shetty could be demanding and at times a bully,
         and the evidence of Mrs Manghat provided some support for this, but this is not reflected
         in the documentary evidence, and I do not give weight to that evidence when assessing
         the case against Dr Shetty.

616. Consistent with this style of leadership, the evidence illustrates gaps in Dr Shetty’s
         knowledge about the NMC Group’s and his own business interests. One example that Mr
         Wandowicz referred to concerns the acquisition of the Kelvin Gulf or Lattice business
         (see para. 567 above). I mention two other illustrations:

         a. On 28 October 2019, Dr Shetty was visiting Saudi Arabia. He sent WhatsApp
                   messages to Mr Manghat in which he inquired how many hospitals the NMC
                   Group had in Saudi Arabia and asked for their names and locations; and also how
                   many hospitals it had worldwide. Mr Manghat provided the information, and Dr
                   Shetty replied “Congratulations”.

         b. On 18 March 2015, a Mr M Padmanabha Rao of Larsen & Toubro Limited, who
                   were contractors engaged in constructing the Khalifa City hospital for the NMC
                   Group, sent Dr Shetty an email asking him to expedite payments said to be

                   overdue. Dr Shetty apparently wrote on the printed copy of the email an
                   instruction for payment to be expedited, which was sent to Mr Manghat and Ms
                   de Mello for the matter to be dealt with. Mr Wandowicz persuasively referred to
                   this as showing Dr Shetty involving himself at “a high level” and leaving it to others
                   carry out his wishes.

617. Also consistently with this style of leadership, Dr Shetty had few dealings with Baroda,
         and, as I would infer, with the NMC Group’s other banks. Unsurprisingly, their dealings
         were mostly with the NMCH employees who operated the NMC Group’s treasury
         function. The picture given by the Baroda witnesses was, as Mr Wandowicz described it,
         that their engagement with Dr Shetty was “either … in the nature of courtesy contact with
         a VIP client only touching on business at a high level if at all, or seeing him at organised
         business or cultural gatherings of the Indian community in Abu Dhabi or similar formal
         functions”. (As Mr Wandowicz acknowledged and as is reflected in the events of March
         2019, his relationship with Mr Jayakumar was of a different nature, and they discussed
         business, albeit typically in general terms.)

618. It is also clear that persons within the NMC Group had access to Dr Shetty’s personal
         bank accounts. For example, it seems from a WhatsApp exchange on 18 July 2017 that
         Mr Kumar was able to check Dr Shetty’s balance, and asked Mr Manghat (and not Dr
         Shetty) whether he might use the funds there. As I have said, those in Dr Shetty’s personal
         office, in particular Mr Pinto, received his bank statements and handled his personal
         banking.

619. Mr Wandowicz was also able to argue that not only were the NMC Group executives in a
         position to make payments and conduct transactions without involving Dr Shetty but on
         occasions they would trick or manipulate him. Thus:

         a. In a WhatsApp message on 25 December 2016, Mr Krishnamoorthy reported to
                   Mr Suresh Kumar that he had “[m]anaged to get the letter signed” by telling Dr
                   Shetty the “[s]ame story”. There is no evidence about what letter is referred to or
                   what “story” was told, but the natural interpretation is that Mr Krishnamoorthy
                   had misled Dr Shetty.

         b. In a WhatsApp message on 30 June 2016, Mr Krishnamoorthy reported to Mr
                   Suresh Kumar that he had secured Dr Shetty’s signature on an instruction for
                   funds to be transferred from Canara Bank to Baroda because Dr Shetty “was
                   excited with the new plane + Canara Bank official was with me while signing so
                   didn’t realise”. Again, the purpose of the transfer is unclear, but Dr Shetty was
                   purposely being kept uninformed about it, if not actively deceived.

         c. On 1 October 2018, Mr Manghat sent a WhatsApp message to Mr Suresh Kumar
                   about a drawdown of AED 50 million from a facility with AHB: “I will tell you how
                   to use others. Don’t tell anyone now including Dr Shetty on withdrawal”. On 4

                   October 2018, he sent another message, “Hope next week can withdraw. Don’t
                   tell Dr Shetty for what this funds are”, and “If he ask tell it’s for Tower”.

620. Mr Manghat was asked about the messages of October 2018 when he was cross-
         examined. Although he was reluctant to admit it, I conclude that he was telling Mr Suresh
         Kumar to mislead Dr Shetty about the purpose of the facility, and to give Dr Shetty the
         false impression that it was to do with the acquisition of the Al Maryah Tower (see para.
         757 below).

621. Mr Manghat explained that he did not wish Dr Shetty to know about the drawdown of the
         AHB facility because “there was pressure on the finance” (that is to say, on the NMC
         Group’s cash flow) and that, if Dr Shetty knew of the drawdown, he would have “definitely
         put pressure on them to make payments to Neopharma” but there was “pressure from
         everyone” and the NMC Group needed to be “very careful on what we do”. There is other
         evidence that Dr Shetty would make demands for funds without recognising that they
         were required for other purposes.

622. I accept that on occasion Dr Shetty was misled into signing documents. However, the
         significance of this must be kept in proportion. Dr Shetty gave no evidence to suggest that
         he was misled in this way as to the purpose of any of the acquisitions that were pleaded
         against him by the JA Claimants, nor do these examples provide any proper basis for
         thinking that Dr Shetty was misled with regard to any of the payments made to him
         directly or indirectly by the NMC Group.

Conclusion about whether Dr Shetty was party to the fraud

623. I conclude that throughout the Relevant Period Dr Shetty participated in the fraud, and in
         particular that:

         a. He knowingly received from the NMC Group substantial sums that were
                   improperly transferred to him and for his benefit both directly and indirectly;

         b. He either used or allowed his signature to be used to make such transfers;

         c. He knowingly acquired with funds so transferred personal assets and
                   investments, including Travelex, his share with Mr Menon in the aircraft, RCS, the
                   Dubai Hills Villas and the Wyoming oil field and other investments through
                   NNMC;

         d. He opened and used personal bank facilities knowing that they were supported
                   by Undisclosed Securities, which were not properly disclosed in the NMC Group’s
                   financial statements;

e. As a director of NMC plc, he allowed the NMC Group to publish financial
         statements that concealed its true financial position, including by way of its
         borrowings and related party transactions; and

f.            In March 2019, Dr Shetty knew of and assisted in hiding the NMC Group’s

              borrowings from EY.

624. The JA Claimants have not proved that Dr Shetty knew about the Sham Suppliers Scheme.
         I make no findings about Dr Shetty’s knowledge of the payments to or for the benefit of
         Mr Manghat and the Bin Buttis. It is not open to the JA Claimants to pursue allegations
         against Dr Shetty in relation to the Neopharma Promissory Note Scheme or the Offset
         Deposits.

Was Mr Manghat party to the fraud?

Introduction

625. As it is put in the JA Claimants’ closing submissions, their case against Mr Manghat is, in
         summary, this: “Mr Manghat was the principal operational architect of the fraud. He was
         not its primary beneficiary, although his unlawful extractions were far from modest. He
         built and maintained the finance function through which the fraud was carried out. He
         facilitated the extraction of NMC Group funds for the benefit of the principal
         shareholders. He established and orchestrated the Sham Supplier scheme. He
         determined what the auditors were told. He received unlawful payments totalling AED
         64.1 million. When the fraud threatened to unravel in March 2019, he acted to conceal it.
         The evidence shows a close relationship with Dr Shetty. Dr Shetty ensured that Mr
         Manghat was rewarded for his participation in the fraud, authorising substantial unlawful
         payments to him”.

626. For his part, Mr Manghat said in his Post-Hearing Submissions that the “kernel” of his
         defence is that “Dr Shetty and the principal shareholders were the true architects and
         beneficiaries of the fraud”. (I should state that there is no persuasive evidence that the
         Bin Buttis devised the fraud.)

627. Mr Manghat’s fundamental defence is identified by ADCB as being that “despite his
         position, he was ignorant of, and had no involvement in, the Loan Recycling Scheme”.
         His defence in the JA Action is similar. Further, (i) Mr Manghat denied receiving any money
         from the NMC Group to which he was not entitled, and (ii) in his closing submissions, Mr
         Hennessey submitted that the JA’s claim against Mr Manghat “rests on a reconstruction
         exercise, and one which Mr Jones accepted was underlined with assumptions,
         assumptions applied to incomplete and difficult-to-parse records”. (Mr Hennessey also
         submitted that ADCB’s case is fatally flawed because there is no credible evidence that
         it relied on any representation of Mr Manghat, but I defer consideration of the point

                                                                                       05 November 2026 04:43 PM

         because it does not go directly to whether Mr Manghat was party to the fraud: see para.
         774 below.)

628. The case that Mr Manghat was party to the fraud throughout the Relevant Period, as
         pleaded by the JA Claimants in paragraph 119 of its Particulars of Claim (and essentially
         adopted by ADCB), has five limbs:

         a. Knowledge of the unlawful payments for the benefit of the Principal Shareholders;

         b. Knowledge of the unlawful payments for Mr Manghat’s own benefit;

         c. Facilitation of the unlawful payments;

         d. Knowledge of the related party transactions, the Undisclosed Debt and the
                   Undisclosed Securities, concealment of the Undisclosed Debt and Undisclosed
                   Securities and knowing participation in the preparation and publication of false
                   and misleading financial statements; and

         e. “[K]nowledge of and involvement in the events of March 2019 …”.

629. I need not return to the events of March 2019. For reasons that I have explained, I
         conclude that Mr Manghat was deeply involved both in misleading EY through the forged
         First Resolution Letter and Second Resolution Letter, and in manoeuvres later in March
         2019 to ensure that Baroda did not contact EY and reveal the extent of the borrowing at
         31 December 2018.

Mr Manghat’s qualifications and expertise

630. In assessing the other four limbs of the case against Mr Manghat, I keep in mind that, as I
         have said, Mr Manghat was a qualified chartered accountant. Nevertheless, in cross-
         examination, when he was asked about periodic meetings that, according to the FRP
         Report, he was to attend with the “Corporate Finance team” to review the “completeness
         and accuracy of consolidated IFRS management accounts” and of “consolidated IFRS
         statements”, Mr Manghat described himself as an “ignorant person” with regard to IFRS.
         I cannot accept the picture that Mr Manghat gave of his abilities, and it is inconsistent
         with his contemporaneous claims, such as his exchange with Mr Tompkins in March 2012
         about appointing a deputy CFO: see para. 213 above.

631. I have also already said that it was evident from his cross-examination that Mr Manghat
         is a very intelligent man, with an alert mind and an impressive memory for detail. Mr
         Zo’Mot formed a similar opinion from his dealings with Mr Manghat: “he was financially
         astute, highly intelligent, deeply engaged with the details of any deals (both past and
         present) and was someone who knew at all times exactly what was going on at NMC,
         particularly in respect of its financial position, borrowings and relationships”.

Payments to Mr Manghat

632. Coming to the remaining four limbs of the case that Mr Manghat was party to the fraud, I
         take first his knowledge and facilitation of the payments for his own benefit. In the JA
         Claimants’ pleading, the alleged benefits fall into three categories: the Direct Manghat
         Payments, the payments by GGCM (the “Manghat GGCM Payments”) and benefits from
         payments made to NNMC.

633. I can dispose immediately of the third category. It is not pleaded unequivocally that Mr
         Manghat himself received benefits from the payments to NNMC: the pleaded case is that
         “it is inferred that he either benefited from those investments [sc investments from
         payments by the NMC Group to NNMC] directly, or allowed and facilitated the dissipation
         of the NMC Group’s assets in this way in order to benefit his co-conspirator, Dr Shetty, at
         the expense of the NMC Group”. At trial, neither the JA Claimants nor ADCB contended
         that I should infer that Mr Manghat himself benefited from the NNMC payments.

634. When considering the Direct Manghat Payments and the GGCM payments, I have in mind
         the level of Mr Manghat’s legitimate earnings from NMC, which were set out in Mr Jones’
         unchallenged evidence. They comprised his salary, benefits and a cash element of a
         Short-Term Incentive Plan (“STIP”). (Further bonuses by way of shares in NMC plc might
         be awarded under the STIP and a Long-Term Incentive Plan.) His remuneration is
         sufficiently illustrated by referring to Mr Jones’ undisputed evidence that in 2012 his
         remuneration was just under US$ 300,000; in 2015 it was just over US$ 600,000; in 2016
         (when he had an unusually high pension benefit) it was US$1.345 million; and in 2019 it
         was US$ 1.746 million.

The Direct Manghat Payments

635. The JA Claimants and ADCB plead that Mr Manghat received Direct Manghat Payments
         totalling AED 9.3 million for which there was no legitimate basis. ADCB abandoned the
         claim in respect of one pleaded payment, for AED 50,000, which was said to have been
         withdrawn from Baroda on 13 August 2014. The JA Claimants still asserted in their closing
         submissions that Mr Manghat received “a total of at least AED 9.3 million”, but they did
         not explain how they might justify pursue the claim about the AED 50,000 payment, and
         (if and in so far as they pursue it) I reject it.

636. Of the remaining Direct Manghat Payments, the first was by way of a transfer from
         NMCH’s 0242 account on 9 October 2012. Mr Manghat admits the transfer and pleads
         that he recalls that the payment was made “to reimburse certain legal fees incurred by
         him” in respect of the legal proceedings brought by HE Al Mazrouei (see para. 100 above),
         which Dr Shetty had promised to reimburse to him. There is no documentary evidence
         that supports this explanation, and NMCH’s internal ledgers do not record any purpose
         for the payments. As Mr Pillai observed, it seems strange that Mr Manghat, given his level
         of remuneration in 2012, should have been expected to incur the fees and that they were

         not directly borne by Dr Shetty or NMCH. It is also remarkable that the expenses
         amounted, apparently, to exactly AED 100,000. Nevertheless, if I were considering this
         payment in isolation, I would not consider that there is sufficient basis to reject Mr
         Manghat’s explanation. However, given my conclusion that the other Direct Manghat
         Payments were unlawful, I am unable to accept Mr Manghat’s evidence and conclude
         that there was no proper reason for the payment.

637. Secondly, on 28 January 2013, AED 400,000 was withdrawn in cash from NMCH’s 0242
         account by two cheques, both apparently signed by Mr Manghat. On the same day, Mr
         Praveen Kumar sent an email to “Rajesh.v” at an NMC email address, which was copied
         to Mr Suresh Kumar and Mr Veerachan: it was headed “Cash Withdrawal” and read
         “Please find attached the receipt & cheque copy pertaining to cash withdrawal of Dhs
         400,000. The proceeds is being handed over to Mr Prasanth Manghat by Mr Prajeesh V.V
         [sc. Mr Veerchan]”. Mr Manghat said in evidence that he could not remember receiving
         this money and denied taking any cash from NMCH. I reject that. I cannot accept that
         those working in the finance department under Mr Manghat would have sent and copied
         emails falsely recording that a significant sum was being given to him in cash.

638. Next, on 14 April 2014, Mr Veerachan sent an email to Ms Meena Thomas of NMCH’s
         accounts department and asked her to issue a cash cheque for AED 300,000 “as
         instructed by Mr Suresh Kumar”. Later that day, he sent a further email to her in which he
         said that he had withdrawn AED 300,000 from Baroda and handed over AED 150,000 to
         Mr Suresh Kumar and “to Ms Sabina [D’Mello] AED 150,000 (for Mr Prasant Manghat)”.
         He added “This is only for our records”. The second email was copied to both Mr Suresh
         Kumar and Mr Manghat. Mr Manghat denied that he had received this money from Ms
         D’Mello, and he said in evidence that he did not need “this much cash, physical cash”. I
         reject his denial: I cannot accept that Mr Veerachan would have copied his email to Mr
         Manghat if he was not to receive the money.

639. Fourthly, on 21 April 2014, Mr Praveen Kumar sent an email to Ms Thomas asking her to
         prepare to cash a cheque for AED 1.35 million, “the proceeds of the same to be handed
         over to Mr Prasanth Manghat”, and later that day he sent her another email, which was
         copied to Ms D’Mello and others, and in which he referred to four cheques and confirmed
         that the “proceeds of the same is being handed over to Ms Sabina [D’Mello] for Mr
         Prasanth Manghat”. Mr Manghat denied receiving this money, and when shown Ms
         D’Mello signature acknowledging receipt of it, he pointed out that she had not specifically
         confirmed that she had handed it over to Mr Manghat or would do so. I find it impossible
         to accept that Ms D’Mello did not hand it over, especially given how many of her fellow
         employees knew that she was receiving the cash for Mr Manghat.

640. On 22 October 2017, NMCH’s accounts department sent Mr Praveen Kumar an email
         headed “Cash cheque for Mr Prasanth Manghat” and attaching a cheque drawn on
         NMCH for AED 250,000, which bore a receipt signed by Ms D’Mello, “Received on behalf
         of Mr Prasanth Manghat”. In his Defence to the ADCB claim, Mr Manghat denied receiving

         any cash payments, but in cross-examination he said that he might have given NMCH a
         cheque and received the cash “to make certain payments, maybe some of the family,
         charity events, taking care of people and all”. In view of my conclusions about other
         Direct Manghat Payments find this (tentative) explanation entirely unconvincing. There is
         no documentary support for the sudden suggestion that Mr Manghat might have made a
         corresponding payment for the NMC Group.

641. The sixth Direct Manghat Payment was made by way of a bank transfer by NMCH of AED
         2 million on 4 December 2018. It was described in the NMC Group’s internal records as
         “Allowances paid to Prasanth Manghat”. In cross-examination, Mr Manghat said that this
         was a payment made to him by the NMCH board, and that there should be documentary
         support for this “in the payroll, because it was paid though the payroll, because in 2018
         they were not paying through payroll”. I did not find his account evidence coherent, but I
         also find it incredible. It was not pleaded or otherwise foreshadowed before Mr Manghat
         gave evidence, and it is not consistent with Mr Jones’ unchallenged evidence about Mr
         Manghat’s legitimate remuneration. Mr Manghat did not explain why the board of NMCH
         might have decided that he should receive this substantial payment. I conclude that the
         payment was not legitimate.

642. Next, on 28 October 2019, a Direct Manghat Payment was made by Holding (whereas all
         the others were made by NMCH). It was a bank transfer from Baroda for AED 1 million. It
         was described in Baroda’s bank statement as “allowance paid”. Mr Manghat said that he
         could not remember the payment. However, it is clear from a WhatsApp exchange
         between Mr Manghat and Mr Suresh Kumar on 28 October 2018 that Mr Manghat
         requested AED 1 million urgently, and Mr Suresh Kumar arranged the payment. In cross-
         examination, Mr Manghat said that Mr Suresh Kumar would have had authorisation from
         “payroll” to make the payment, but this is again inconsistent with Mr Jones’ evidence and
         there is no explanation for why a payment should be made by Holding.

643. Finally, by a letter apparently dated 6 November 2019, Mr Manghat wrote to NMCH’s
         Chief Compensation Officer that the “management is pleased with the performance of
         the following employees and in recognition of their efforts, have decided to give them a
         special incentive as follows: 1. Prasanth Manghat, AED 4 m, 2. Prasanth Shenoy, AED 4
         m, 3. Suresh Kumar, AED 2 m, 4. Hani Buttikhi, AED 2 m. Please do the needful”. The
         letter was signed by Mr Manghat (alone). On 7 and 13 November 2019, Mr Manghat
         received two payments, each of AED 2 million.

644. Mr Manghat’s pleaded defence (in both the JA Action and the ADCB Action) is that he
         “does not recall signing the letter to NMC’s Chief Compensation Officer, and would not
         have been authorised to do so for a payment to himself”, but that he “understood that in
         2018, the Board at [NMCH] level (at the time, [SAQ], [KBBAM] and Dr Shetty) discussed
         and approved management incentive payments. It is to be inferred that they (and not Mr
         Manghat) authorised and/or instructed the payment, which came into effect in 2019. It
         was common in the NMC Group for payments to be delayed”. When he was cross-

         examined, Mr Manghat did not put forward this contention merely as a matter of

645. Again, I am unable to accept Mr Manghat’s explanation. First, it contradicts Mr Jones’
         unchallenged evidence. Secondly, no documentary evidence supports Mr Manghat’s
         account. Thirdly, it is inconsistent with a letter of 21 March 2019, sent to Mr Manghat by
         Mr Bomford in his capacity of Chairman of NMC plc’s Remuneration Committee, in which
         he set out the position as to the bonuses or incentive payments that Mr Manghat was to
         receive, in particular the cash element of the STIP, a payment of AED 2,528,277 that he
         was to receive in March 2019. (This third point was not put to Mr Manghat when he was
         cross-examined, but it had been specifically pleaded against him by the JA Claimants,
         and Mr Manghat did not answer it.)

646. I conclude that Mr Manghat was entitled to none of the Direct Manghat payments, and
         there was no legitimate reason for them to be made. I also conclude that Mr Manghat
         knew that.

The GGCM Manghat payments

647. The JA Claimants and ADCB contend that between March 2015 and December 2019 Mr
         Manghat received payments from GGCM of AED 54,836,500 by way of monies transferred
         from GGCM’s 9228 account to his account at Baroda.

648. They also plead that the source of the funds for the Manghat GGCM Payments was “the
         NMC Group and/or Dr Shetty”. However, the unchallenged evidence of the JA action
         Accounting Experts was that AED 24.7 million of the AED 54,836,500 could be shown to
         have been funded by the NMC Group, and I accept their evidence.

649. Mr Manghat pleads that he did not “hold any role at GGCM”, but he “accepts that GGCM
         was a vehicle used by Dr Shetty to make payments, and he (Mr Manghat) and Ms D’Mello
         assisted relevant individuals in facilitating payments on his behalf”. ADCB allege that he
         apparently “controlled and/or operated GGCM’s 9228 Account, including via Ms
         D’Mello”, and identified WhatsApp messages from Mr Manghat in which he instructed Ms
         D’Mello to make payments from it. He also directed that payments be made to GGCM’s
         account for Dr Shetty’s use (or “for boss”).

650. The JA Claimants and ADCB contend that Mr Manghat received the payments as a reward
         for his help with the fraudulent scheme. Mr Manghat admits in his pleading that he
         received AED 45 million, and at trial he did not dispute that he received the full amount of
         the GGCM Manghat payments. He said that the payments were, or that he believed them
         to be, legitimate payments from Dr Shetty. He pleads that he understood that the GGCM
         Manghat payments “were effectively payments from Dr Shetty personally, and for

         purposes unconnected with NMC (including, for example, payments to his daughter
         Reema Shetty and her businesses)”. More specifically, he pleads that “[t]hroughout the
         IPO, the Board and Dr BR Shetty promised to reward Mr Manghat with equity and/or a
         bonus for his work”; that in 2014, Dr Shetty agreed to pay him a bonus personally (i) for
         his work on the IPO, (ii) because his remuneration had been below market rates, (iii) for
         work in relation to the dispute with HE Al Mazrouei, and (iv) for “assisting Dr Shetty
         personally with aspects of his Travelex acquisition, including to arrange a pledge of his
         shares to raise personal funds”; and that in early 2017, Dr Shetty signed a commitment
         (the “Commitment”) to award Mr Manghat and his brother, Mr Promoth Manghat, a
         bonus of US$ 40 million “contingent upon the sale of Dr B R Shetty’s interests in NMC,
         UAE Exchange and Travelex”.

651. The Commitment is headed “Bonus Payment”, and it reads as follows: “In connection
         with the sale of the interest of Dr B R Shetty in NMC, UAE Exchange and Travelex it is
         hereby agreed to award a bonus of USD 40 Million each to Mr Prasanth Manghat and Mr
         Promoth Manghat in the following manner:

                   “On transfer of shares in NMC and SPA execution of UAE Exchange and
                   Travelex: USD 30 Million each

                   On closing of UAE Exchange and Travelex sale transaction: USD 10 Million
                   each”.

         As it appears on its face, Dr Shetty had signed it and written on it a note saying “good
         luck”. According to Mr Manghat, he asked Dr Shetty to write this in order to prevent him
         from later denying his signature. The Commitment also had a space marked for Mr Binay
         Shetty to sign, but his signature is not on the copy that is in evidence.

652. Dr Shetty disputed that he ever owed Mr Manghat or his brother any bonus under any such
         agreement or otherwise, and he gave evidence that “no such ‘bonus’ payment was ever
         approved by me from Guide’s accounts or otherwise”.

653. There are twelve GGCM Manghat payments. The first was by way of a transfer of AED 5
         million made to him on 25 March 2015. He had initially told Ms D’Mello to transfer AED 3
         million, but on learning thar GGCM had available a balance of AED 10 million, he told Ms
         D’Mello to arrange transfers of 5 million each to himself and his brother, Promoth. An
         internal spreadsheet of NMCH, headed “Guide Transactions”, described these payments
         as “Incentives – AED 5 mio Travelex Deal”.

654. In his written evidence, Mr Manghat said that Mr Promoth Manghat and he worked to
         arrange a facility agreement to refinance borrowing entered into by BRS Ventures &
         Holdings in respect of the acquisition of Travelex, which involved reducing the borrowings
         to AED 490 million by transferring AED 250 million of the funding to UAE Exchange. He
         said that Dr Shetty promised that they would receive in return a 10% equity interest in

ADGMCFI-2022-299 – NMC HEALTHCARE LIMITED (IN ADMINISTRATION) (SUBJECT TO A DEED OF COMPANY ARRANGEMENT) AND OTHERS V BAVAGUTHU RAGHURAM
SHETTY AND OTHERS

                                                                                                                                                                     161

                                                                                       05 November 2026 04:43 PM

         Travelex, but that in the event they did not receive “any equity interest or remuneration for
         this work”. He continued, “Instead the promise was later amended that we would be paid
         a substantial amount between us, which was essentially a cumulative bonus for this and
         years of other work we had done for him. This was never paid in full”.

655. I find this evidence incredible. A 10% equity interest in Travelex would have been worth
         some US$90 million (as Mr Manghat accepted in cross-examination), and I cannot accept
         that Dr Shetty would have agreed to such a reward for arranging a facility of AED 490
         million, or for arranging a pledge of shares, or for both. When it was put to Mr Manghat in
         cross-examination that a bonus of 10% of the equity in Travelex would have been
         absurdly generous, he said that there was no connection between the promise of 10% of
         the equity and the arrangement of the facility, but that, to my mind, contradicts his
         witness statement. In his oral evidence, he described how he had complained to Dr
         Shetty that he was under-remunerated compared with other executives, and Dr Shetty
         had promised him money and invited him to “take the payment from my other
         companies”. In respect of the transfer on 25 March 2015 specifically, Mr Manghat said
         that “Dr Shetty would have told: okey, take it from Guide. Send this transfer letter”.

656. I find Mr Manghat’s explanation in cross-examination no more convincing that his written
         evidence. He was vague about when Dr Shetty made his promises, he did not explain why
         his brother received the same amount as he did on 25 March 2015, and his evidence did
         not explain why the payments were said in NMCH’s internal record to be an incentive in
         relation to Travelex. I consider ADCB’s submission much more probable: that both
         transfers were a reward for facilitating unlawful payments from the NMC Group (and
         possibly from UAE Exchange) to Dr Shetty to fund the Travelex acquisition.

657. The second GGCM Manghat payment was by way of a transfer on 7 September 2015 of
         AED 1,836,500. Mr Manghat explained the payment on the basis of Dr Shetty’s promise.
         However, no documentary record supports this explanation. More importantly it was one
         of four payments made from GGCM on 7 September 2015, the others being a payment of
         AED 2 million to Dr Shetty, of AED 918,250 to Mr Bassadiq, and of AED 1,285,550 to Mr
         Krishnamoorthy. I infer that they were connected with the payment of the second
         instalment of the price for the Travelex acquisition.

658. The third GGCM Manghat payment was by way of a transfer of AED 750,000 on 29 May
         2016, and it was recorded in NMCH’s records as “Transfer to Prasanth Manghat (as per
         mail from Suresh K on 26.05.16) One off Bonus”. In an email of 26 May 2016, Mt
         Krishnamoorthy gave instructions for the transfer of AED 1.75 million by NMCH to GGCM,
         and by a further email later that day, he gave instructions to Ms D’Mello that, upon receipt
         of this money from NMCH, GGCM should make payments of AED 750,000 to Mr Manghat,
         and of AED 500,000 each to Mr Roy Cherry, who worked for the Bin Buttis, and himself. It
         was put to Mr Manghat in cross-examination that the payment was “because of your
         involvement in operating and concealing the loan recycling scheme”, a suggestion that
         Mr Manghat denied. The payment was clearly funded by NMCH. Although I cannot tell

ADGMCFI-2022-299 – NMC HEALTHCARE LIMITED (IN ADMINISTRATION) (SUBJECT TO A DEED OF COMPANY ARRANGEMENT) AND OTHERS V BAVAGUTHU RAGHURAM
SHETTY AND OTHERS

                                                                                                                                                                     162

                                                                                       05 November 2026 04:43 PM

         from the evidence why this payment was made, I am satisfied that there was no proper
         reason for NMCH to make it, and I cannot accept that Mr Manghat was unaware of the
         source of the money. It was not lawful, as Mr Manghat knew.

659. Next, on 3 October 2016 GGCM transferred AED 1.5 million to an account of Mr Manghat
         at Noor Bank and AED 750,000 to his account at Baroda. Mr Bezant gave unchallenged
         evidence that these were funded by a transfer into GGCM’s account on 27 September
         2016 of AED 2,254,133 which was described in the NMC Group’s internal records as
         being by way of a cheque for “Everest Summit General Contracting”, presumably Apex
         Everest Summit General Contracting (“Everest”), one of the Sham Suppliers, received
         from “SK”, presumably Mr Krishnamoorthy.

660. When he was cross-examined, Mr Manghat denied that he was involved in directing that
         these payments be made, and he said that they were made by Dr Shetty. This evidence
         is inconsistent with WhatsApp exchanges between Mr Manghat and Ms D’Mello on 3
         October 2016, which show that Ms D’Mello sought confirmation from Mr Manghat about
         what transfers should be made.

661. The other seven GGCM Manghat payments, totalling AED 45 million, were made between
         3 May 2018 and 11 December 2019, after Mr Manghat claims that Dr Shetty had entered
         into the Commitment. Mr Manghat’s case was that these payments were made by Dr
         Shetty by way of part payment of what was payable under the Commitment.

662. I must therefore return to Mr Manghat’s case that Dr Shetty gave the Commitment to pay
         both Mr Prasanth Manghat and Mr Promoth Manghat US$ 40 million. I cannot tell whether
         the Commitment is genuine, and I should make clear that I have not taken it into account
         when assessing the case against Dr Shetty. However, even if Dr Shetty did give the
         Commitment, it is clear that payment under it was contingent upon the completion of the
         proposed sale by Dr Shetty of all his shares in NMC plc, UAE Exchange and Travelex for
         US$ 2 billion. That proposed sale did not materialise. When he was cross-examined, Mr
         Manghat was reluctant to accept that the condition for the payments was not fulfilled: he
         said “Those things happened actually. … But in a different manner”. I cannot accept that
         anything happened to fulfil the condition stipulated in the Commitment for the payments.

663. There is another reason that I cannot accept either that any payments were made by
         GGCM (or by Dr Shetty through GGCM) under the Commitment, or that Mr Manghat
         believed that any payments had been so made. In 2020, Mr Prasanth Manghat and Mr
         Promoth Manghat brought proceedings against Dr Shetty in the Dubai Courts, in which
         they claimed US$ 80 million under the Commitment. (It appears from the material before
         me that Dr Shetty never received notice of the proceedings because the Court was given
         the wrong address for service, but that does not matter for present purposes.) The
         proceedings were dismissed by a judgment dated 29 September 2021, and an appeal was
         dismissed on 22 August 2022, but Mr Wandowicz rightly made it clear that he did not rely
         upon the decision of the Dubai Court in these proceedings. However, he submitted, and

COURT OF FIRST INSTANCE JUDGMENT
ADGMCFI-2022-111 – ABU DHABI COMMERCIAL BANK PJSC V PRASANTH MANGHAT
ADGMCFI-2020-020 - IN THE MATTER OF NMC HEALTHCARE LIMITED (IN ADMINISTRATION) (SUBJECT TO A DEED OF COMPANY ARRANGEMENT) AND THE COMPANIES
LISTED IN SCHEDULE 1 TO THE ADMINISTRATION APPLICATION
ADGMCFI-2022-299 – NMC HEALTHCARE LIMITED (IN ADMINISTRATION) (SUBJECT TO A DEED OF COMPANY ARRANGEMENT) AND OTHERS V BAVAGUTHU RAGHURAM
SHETTY AND OTHERS

                                                                                                                                                                     163

                                                                                       05 November 2026 04:43 PM

         I accept, that the claim in the Dubai proceedings for the full amount of US$ 80 million,
         including the full amount of US$40 million to which Mr Manghat claims to have been
         entitled, is inconsistent with his argument that payments by GGCM had already been
         made by way of part fulfilment of the Commitment.

664. The case that Mr Manghat now advances is also inconsistent with what he must have told
         Ms D’Mello in September 2018. The background was that Baroda’s Compliance
         Department had queried why GGCM was paying AED 18 million to a Mr Jagannath Rout.
         On 24 September 2018, Ms D’Mello sent an email to Baroda, copied to Mr Manghat,
         attaching a letter on GGCM letterhead dated 17 September 2018, which read “Further to
         the Investment Advisory Agreement and in recognition of achievement towards the
         incessant growth of company, a one-time incentive of AED 40 million each was awarded
         to Mr Prasanth Manghat and Mr Jagannath Rao (sic). This is for your information”. It was
         signed, probably electronically, in the name of Dr Shetty. I accept Mr Wandowicz’s
         submission that the letter was written at the direction of Mr Manghat.

665. The letter is not referred to in Mr Manghat’s Defence or in his witness statements. In
         cross-examination, he said that he had had no recollection of it, but he cannot have
         forgotten about receiving such a large amount. It is inconsistent with Mr Manghat’s case
         that he had no “role” in GGCM that he should receive this incentive. Moreover, it is
         inconsistent with his case that GGCM was making payments to him under the
         Commitment and in order to discharge Dr Shetty’s promise.

666. I should refer specifically to the GGCM Manghat payments of AED 8 million on 21 January
         2019 and of AED 5 million on 29 January 2019. The payment on 21 January 2019 was
         discussed in a WhatsApp exchange with Mr Suresh Kumar. Mr Manghat was clearly
         involved in arranging the payment. At the same time as the payment on 29 January 2019,
         GGCM made similar payments to Mr Shenoy and Mr Suresh Kumar, as is clear from the
         instructions that Ms D’Mello sent Baroda giving instructions for them to be made. Ms
         D’Mello described the payments to Baroda as being “towards Annual Bonus”. There was
         no reason that GGCM should pay any of them an annual bonus. However, the fact the
         payments were being made to Mr Shenoy and Mr Suresh Kumar as well as to Mr Manghat,
         and the description of the payment as being towards an annual bonus, are both
         inconsistent with Mr Manghat’s explanation for the payment of AED 5 million.

667. I reject Mr Manghat’s case about why he was being paid the GGCM Manghat payments.

668. I cannot tell whether Dr Shetty allowed Mr Manghat to receive the payments because he
         was involved facilitating the fraud on the NMC Group or whether Mr Manghat was simply
         arranging the payments for himself. Whatever the explanation, I conclude that, as Mr
         Manghat knew, the NMC Group was providing GGCM with funds to make some of the
         payments, and that, as Mr Manghat knew, there was no legitimate reason for the NMC
         Group to do so.

Payments for benefit of Dr Shetty

669. As I said at paras. 177ff above, before the IPO Dr Shetty and other shareholders received
         benefits and payments from the NMC Group, and Mr Manghat was kept informed about
         what funds were transferred to them. I conclude that he also knew that NMC Group funds
         were being so used after the IPO and until 2020. The net dissipations to Dr Shetty alone
         exceeded AED 2.6 billion, and it is implausible that dissipations on this scale were not
         known to Mr Manghat as the CFO of NMC plc or thereafter, when he was still closely
         involved with the NMC Group’s finances. However, the ADCB and the JA Claimants do
         not rely only on this general proposition but upon more specific evidence, including the
         following.

670. The documentary evidence shows that NMCH’s Finance Department continued to
         monitor funds that were expended outside the group. For example, on 3 November 2012,
         Mr Veerachan sent Mr Suresh Kumar an email headed “Non NMC Healthcare Fund
         transfers” and listed transfers to UAE Exchange, Neopharma and Dr B R Shetty. Mr
         Manghat was kept appraised of information of this kind, as is shown by an email sent on
         28 September 2014 by Mr Praveen Kumar to Ms D’Mello. It was headed “Fund Transfers
         2013-2014 and attached a spreadsheet (the “September 2014 Spreadsheet”), which
         comprised a summary of all transfers, and separately listed (i) “payments to Dr Shetty”
         from the NMC Group and also from Nexgen and FFT; (ii) transfers to “BRS UAE
         companies”, including GGCM and DHE; (iii) “BRS UK companies”, which listed payments
         to BRS Ventures & Holdings and NNMC (both in fact incorporated in the BVI); (iv)
         payments to “BRS other”; (v) payments to KBBO, which included payments to, among
         other companies, Infinite and Centurion; (vi) payments to UAE Exchange; (vii) payments
         to NMC plc, which included, among other things, payments to Mr Basaddiq; and (viii) a
         list of payments to “NMCH others”, which include payments to, among others FFT and
         Modular Concepts. The payments to Dr Shetty in the first list totalled some AED 1,290
         million, and the payments in the “KBBO” list totalled some AED 94 million.

671. I find that the September 2014 spreadsheet was sent to Ms D’Mello because she was Mr
         Manghat’s Personal Assistant: in his cross-examination in another context, he accepted
         that she was “a postbox for getting to me”. However, Mr Manghat was unwilling to accept
         this when he was asked about the September 2014 Spreadsheet. In my judgment, there
         was no other reason that it should be sent to her. In any case, the Finance Department
         would not have sent such a document to Ms D’Mello if they had expected it to be kept
         secret from Mr Manghat.

672. The payments listed on the September 2014 Spreadsheet including payments connected
         with the acquisition of Travelex and the Dubai Hills Villas.

673. Mr Manghat’s pleading denied that he knew about the Travelex acquisition, and his
         written evidence was that he had discovered only later that NMC Group funds were used
         for this purpose. When he was first cross-examined, Mr Manghat maintained his denial

ADGMCFI-2022-299 – NMC HEALTHCARE LIMITED (IN ADMINISTRATION) (SUBJECT TO A DEED OF COMPANY ARRANGEMENT) AND OTHERS V BAVAGUTHU RAGHURAM
SHETTY AND OTHERS

                                                                                                                                                                     165

                                                                                       05 November 2026 04:43 PM

         that he was involved in the transfer of funds from the NMC Group for this purpose (or any
         illegitimate purpose). However, later in his cross-examination, when asked about his
         signature on instructions to Baroda to make transfers of the funds used for the
         acquisition, Mr Manghat changed his evidence: he said that he knew of some of the
         payments from the NMC Group for this purpose, but not all of them. To avoid
         misunderstanding about this change of evidence, I intervened in the cross-examination
         and asked “did you know at the time, that is to say back in 2014, that Dr Shetty was
         arranging some payments for Travelex out of the NMC companies? Mr Manghat replied,
         “If you ask me like a yes or no, Your Honour, yes, I knew. But some of them, I didn’t know”.

674. Mr Manghat sought to justify this use of the NMC Group’s money on the basis that “Dr
         Shetty and the shareholders also used to bring money back into the company also”, and
         a claim that “most of the shareholders, most of the directors knew this situation”. Had he
         believed that the payments from the NMC Group were proper for these or any other
         reasons, he would not have denied any contemporaneous knowledge. I cannot accept
         that Mr Manghat, a chartered accountant, did consider that the payments were legitimate
         or proper.

675. I also conclude that Mr Manghat knew that NMC Group funds were being used to support
         Dr Shetty’s purchase of the Dubai Hills Villas. This is clear from an email exchange on 14
         August 2014 that Mr Manghat had with his secretary, Ms Ruelle D’Silva. She wrote to Mr
         Suresh Kumar asking for funds to be transferred to Dr Shetty’s account to cover cheques
         issued for the purpose, and she copied it to Mr Manghat (and Mr Udayakumar Nair). Mr
         Manghat asked what the cheques were for (“What’s this. For what. Who directed.
         Pl[ea]s[e]”) and she replied that they were for the Dubai Hills Villas. Shortly afterwards,
         Ms D’Silva sent Mr Manghat an email about these payments, writing that she understood
         from Ms D’Mello that “Sir knows about this and we just need to pay instalments”. No
         response is in evidence. I cannot accept Mr Manghat’s suggestion that Ms D’Silvo’s email
         does not refer to him because “Nobody used to call me ’Sir’ in the office to be honest, I’m
         telling you”.

676. In my judgment, Mr Manghat was aware that the purchase of the Dubai Hills Villas was to
         be funded in part by the NMC Group event before these emails. After the exchanges, he
         signed the instructions for the transfer of the required funds from NMCH.

677. Mr Manghat had WhatsApp and email exchanges with Ms D’Mello on 31 May 2015 and 1
         June 2015 about further payments by the NMC Group relating to the Dubai Hills Villas.
         On 17 October 2015, Ms D’Mello told him by WhatsApp that a further payment instalment
         in the sum of some AED 9.2 million was due. When asked about this in cross-
         examination, Mr Manghat said that he did not respond to it because “I don’t want to be
         party to that maybe” and that he did not want “to ask too many questions because this is
         not related to my business”. I cannot accept that explanation, but in any case, it would
         not assist Mr Manghat if he had shut-eye knowledge, rather than actual knowledge.

SHETTY AND OTHERS

                                                                                                                                                                     166

                                                                                       05 November 2026 04:43 PM

         Further, he went on to sign the transfer request for the payment to be funded by the NMC
         Group and, despite Mr Manghat’s evidence, I find that he knew its purpose.

678. Mr Manghat continued to facilitate payments by the NMC Group to Dr Shetty or for his
         benefit after he ceased to be the CFO of NMC plc and became its Deputy CEO and then
         its CEO. I have already referred to the exchange between Mr Manghat and Mr Binay Shetty
         that shows that Dr Shetty was using NMC Group funds for the plane and for his
         investment in the Oman Cement City: see para. 508 above. Other WhatsApp exchanges
         with Mr Binay Shetty further illustrate that after October 2014 Mr Manghat knew that the
         NMC Group was continuing to provide financial support for Dr Shetty. On 4 October 2015,
         Mr Binay Shetty asked Mr Manghat to make payments to BRS Ventures & Holdings for staff
         costs and other expenses of another company of Dr Shetty referred to as BRS Catering,
         and Mr Manghat replied thar he could provide AED 500,000 “tomorrow or so”. There were
         similar requests on 8 November 2015, on 10 April 2015, and on 8 May 2015, when Mr
         Binay Shetty asked for “2 m for real estate funding” and “2.5 for Foodsters”, a food
         company of his sister, Ms Reema Shetty. On 7 July 2017, after Dr Shetty had agreed to
         dispose of some of his shares in NMC plc to the Bin Buttis, Mr Manghat wrote that Dr
         Shetty’s office staff might have to be taken off the NMC Group payroll.

679. In his witness statement, Mr Manghat said that he had learned only afterwards that “Dr
         Shetty’s personal staff in his own office were paid by NMC” and that this should have been
         discovered by EY as auditor. That evidence was misleading. In cross-examination, Mr
         Manghat did not deny that he knew about these payments, but he sought to justify them
         on the basis that the private office sometimes worked for the NMC Group while Dr Shetty
         was the CEO of NMC plc.I might have accepted that explanation if Mr Manghat’s evidence
         about this had been consistent, but in the circumstances, I am unable to do so.

Payments for benefit of Bin Buttis

680. Mr Manghat also knew that the NMC Group was conferring illegitimate benefits on the Bin
         Buttis. They were recorded in the September 2014 spreadsheet. Further, on 14 August
         2019 in a WhatsApp group to which Mr Manghat belonged, there was discussion of
         payments by the NMC Group to Emirates Hospital Group LLC (“Emirates HG”), an
         interest of the Bin Buttis.

681. When he was asked about the Emirates HG payments, Mr Manghat responded that the
         NMC Group was “managing” the company. He also said “It is very difficult for me to say
         that this is a completely unlawful payment”. I am not persuaded by these explanations.

Conclusion about Mr Manghat’s involvement with misappropriations

682. I therefore conclude that, throughout the Relevant Period, Mr Manghat knew of and
         facilitated unlawful misappropriations from the NMC Group for the benefit of Dr Shetty,
         of the Bin Buttis and of himself.
