                                                                                       05 November 2026 04:43 PM

Mr Manghat’s knowledge of the Undisclosed Debt

683. I also conclude that Mr Manghat knew that the NMC Group was disguising the
         misappropriations by producing false financial statements that did not disclose its true
         borrowing obligations and through the Sham Suppliers Scheme.

684. Mr Manghat signed audit representation letters for the years ended 31 December 2015,
         31 December 2016, 31 December 2017 and 31 December 2018, and approved the
         Directors’ Reports and Directors’ Statements in NMC plc’s annual reports and accounts
         for those years. He did not suggest that he was unaware of the debts that they reported.

685. NMCH’s Finance Department monitored the borrowings of the NMC Group and kept Mr
         Manghat informed of them: see para. 211 above, where I described the spreadsheets of
         20 August 2013, 25 November 2015 and 8 August 2016. (ADCB’s pleaded case against Mr
         Manghat refers in particular, to the spreadsheets of November 2015 and August 2016,
         but not that of August 2013.)

686. When he was cross-examined, Mr Manghat accepted that, if he had opened the
         spreadsheet that he was sent in November 2015, he would have known that “this is a
         huge gap”. He suggested that he might not have looked at it because he opened emails
         on his blackberry and so could not open spreadsheets; and that he might not have
         opened them on his laptop. The subject line of Mr Krishnamoorthy’s email said “Total
         Exposure as on date - Pl. have a look it”. I cannot accept that Mr Manghat, who clearly
         liked to engage with financial details, would not have done so. Of the August 2016
         spreadsheet, Mr Manghat said in cross-examination, “… it looks I think I have not done
         proper exercise at that time. I should have looked at it a little more. Because I was more
         a Blackberry guy, and my laptop was in the office …. I should have done a little more care”.
         Again, I find that evidence incredible.

687. I add only that, even if Mr Manghat did not open the spreadsheets, Mr Krishnamoorthy
         and Mr Suresh Kumar clearly were not operating on the basis that the undisclosed
         facilities (those not reported in the financial statements) were to be concealed from Mr
         Manghat. They knew that he was well aware of them.

688. On 6 October 2016, Mr Krishnamoorthy sent Mr Manghat and copied to Mr Suresh Kumar
         an email with the subject line “Bank position”. He wrote that “The situation looks grave
         with the undisclosed bank liabilities standing close to US$ 2 billion mark”, and he
         provided a statement of the position as at 30 September 2016 giving a total for “Liabilities
         disclosed in the books” of AED 4.078 billion and “Undisclosed Liabilities to the banks” of
         AED 6.922 billion. His anxiety is evident from his email. He referred to “having issues in
         meeting the coverage ratio” for the 2015 Syndicated Facilities, and the difficulties in
         obtaining further facilities, and the need for at least US$ 500 million “to manage this
         situation”. He asked for a discussion about the position. Mr Krishnamoorthy was

COURT OF FIRST INSTANCE JUDGMENT
ADGMCFI-2022-111 – ABU DHABI COMMERCIAL BANK PJSC V PRASANTH MANGHAT
ADGMCFI-2020-020 - IN THE MATTER OF NMC HEALTHCARE LIMITED (IN ADMINISTRATION) (SUBJECT TO A DEED OF COMPANY ARRANGEMENT) AND THE COMPANIES
LISTED IN SCHEDULE 1 TO THE ADMINISTRATION APPLICATION
ADGMCFI-2022-299 – NMC HEALTHCARE LIMITED (IN ADMINISTRATION) (SUBJECT TO A DEED OF COMPANY ARRANGEMENT) AND OTHERS V BAVAGUTHU RAGHURAM
SHETTY AND OTHERS

                                                                                                                                                                     168

                                                                                       05 November 2026 04:43 PM

         obviously writing on the understanding that Mr Manghat was already aware that some of
         the bank borrowings were not “disclosed in the books”.

689. In cross-examination Mr Manghat acknowledged that he was likely to have read the email
         “at the time”. He said that, in response to Mr Krishnamoorthy’s concerns, he put in place
         a plan to raise “non-bank borrowing money” and to “bring money from shareholders”.

690. Mr Manghat also said that there was nothing improper about the “undisclosed bank
         liabilities”, claiming that he had consulted Clyde & Co for legal advice and was advised,
         “They call it like off-balance sheet liability or something like that. Something like that. …
         this is not written as unaccounted or something, for someone to get really scared. He
         says that this is the problem. Unaccounted is not undisclosed”. Mr Manghat put forward
         a similar explanation in re-examination (four weeks after he had first given this evidence),
         when he said that this was “off the book accounting” or “off balance sheet liabilities”
         governed by “certain accounting … regulations”; and he referred again to consulting
         Clyde & Co and needing to obtain “a legal opinion which has to be given to the auditors”.

691. Although some of the language was rather confused, the thrust of what Mr Manghat was
         saying is clear enough. I reject his evidence: it was untruthful. It is inconsistent with any
         sensible reading of Mr Krishnamoorthy’s email. Nothing corroborates the evidence about
         raising “non-bank borrowing” or money from shareholders, nor the evidence that he or
         anyone else sought or obtained or provided to EY legal advice about the proper
         accounting treatment of the NMC Group’s bank borrowings. It was not put to any of the
         accounting experts that there were accounting regulations that justified, or might
         conceivably be thought to justify, the NMC Group’s accounting treatment of its
         borrowings, and no relevant regulation was identified. If advice of the kind described had
         been obtained, there would have been no need for the subterfuge in March 2019.

692. I should refer to one further piece of evidence that Mr Manghat was aware that the NMC
         Group was not disclosing all its borrowings to EY or it its financial statements: a
         WhatsApp exchange on12 December 2018 between Mr Ghosh and Mr Suresh Kumar
         about a question raised by EY and a facility with AHB bank. Mr Ghosh asked whether “we
         [are] showing this time”. Mr Suresh Kumar replied “No” and reminded Mr Ghosh “PM
         wasn’t very keen on showing”. “PM”, of course, refers to Mr Manghat.

Mr Manghat’s involvement with the Sham Suppliers Scheme

693. It is alleged in the JA Action that Mr Manghat was involved in the Sham Suppliers Scheme,
         which was used to help disguise the NMC Group’s true financial position. ADCB did not
         rely on this in its pleaded case, although Mr Pillai submitted that his involvement with it
         confirms his central role in the Loan Recycling Scheme. However that might be, in
         assessing ADCB’s claim, I shall not take account of the evidence about his involvement
         with the Sham Suppliers Scheme (or about the Sham Suppliers Scheme itself).

LISTED IN SCHEDULE 1 TO THE ADMINISTRATION APPLICATION
ADGMCFI-2022-299 – NMC HEALTHCARE LIMITED (IN ADMINISTRATION) (SUBJECT TO A DEED OF COMPANY ARRANGEMENT) AND OTHERS V BAVAGUTHU RAGHURAM
SHETTY AND OTHERS

                                                                                                                                                                     169

                                                                                       05 November 2026 04:43 PM

694. I have described the nature and scale of the Sham Suppliers Scheme at paras. 216ff
         above. The JA Claimants argue that Mr Manghat knew about it and facilitated it. In Mr
         Manghat’s Defence to their claims, he admitted that “the NMC Group used supply-chain
         financing from around 2013, as was commonplace in the industry”, but he said that he
         “has no knowledge of any non-genuine supply-chain financing”.

695. When Mr Manghat was first cross-examined, he appeared to maintain his pleaded
         position. However, when he was asked about a request by Mr Praveen Kumar on 16
         March 2016 to EIB to remit funds to the account of Everest at Baroda on the basis of an
         invoice from Everest where there was no genuine sale, Mr Manghat agreed that
         apparently in that case supply-chain financing was being used as a device for obtaining
         finance by misrepresenting Everest as a real supplier.

696. Everest was a company of a Dr Dinesh Acharya, who was a director of several of Sham
         Suppliers. Email exchanges between Dr Acharya and Mr Manghat demonstrate that Mr
         Manghat was closely involved in arranging with Dr Acharya to use his companies in the
         scheme. For example, by an email to Mr Manghat of 2 January 2015, Dr Acharya wrote
         that the Baroda account for Everest was opened and that its “licence, stamp, letterhead
         etc all ready”. He told Mr Manghat to “start using this company” and that he would have
         two more companies ready later in the month. This is not information that would have
         been provided to Mr Manghat by a supplier engaging in genuine, arms-length sales. On 14
         January 2015, Dr Acharya sent Mr Manghat another email in which he said that he had
         four companies “for our purpose” and listed Everest and three other Sham Supplier
         companies. It referred to a company called Apex Capital Commercial Brokerage LLC,
         saying that it “mainly can be used for parking and distribution of commission and
         payments” and that its “documents and stamp are left in your office”. Mr Manghat could
         not explain what “our purpose” refers to or why a genuine suppliers might provide the
         NMC Group with its letterhead or stamp. I conclude that Dr Acharya and Mr Manghat were
         engaged in setting up Sham Suppliers to assist the NMC Group and that Dr Acharya was
         to receive commission for his assistance. I shall not set out in detail subsequent email
         exchanges with Dr Acharya that are in evidence. They corroborate this conclusion.

697. Mr Manghat’s involvement in setting up Sham Supplier arrangements is also illustrated
         by his dealings with FFT and its registered manager, Mr Vijayakumar Madavana
         Viswanathan. FFT was a Sham Supplier. Mr Manghat pleaded that he had “no knowledge
         of FFT or its business”. However, when Mr Manghat was shown in cross-examination an
         email of 11 December 2013 in which he sent Mr Suresh Kumar a copy of FFT’s trade
         licence, he agreed that he knew of the company. Mr Manghat remained involved with FFT,
         and, for example, on 12 October 2014 he sent to Mr Vajayakumar a request from Baroda
         for “know your customer” documentation. He also directed payments for Mr
         Vajayakumar: for example, on 9 March 2016 he signed a transfer request for a payment
         of 80 lakhs (about AED 438 ,000) and he received in copy Ms D’Mello’s email under cover
         of which she sent the request to Baroda. I accept the JA Claimants’ submission that Mr

                                                                                                                                                                     170

                                                                                       05 November 2026 04:43 PM

         Vajayakumar was, like Dr Acharya, being paid for his assistance with the Sham Suppliers
         Scheme and Mr Manghat was arranging the payments.

698. When Mr Manghat’s cross-examination resumed after the break because of his illness,
         Mr Manghat gave a new explanation for the Sham Suppliers Scheme. He said that, after
         the financial difficulties in 2009, 2010 and 2011, banks were reluctant to make large
         overdraft facilities available, but they would provide what was called a “trust receipt
         facility” or a “bill discounting facility”. Before Mr Manghat left Neopharma, he said, the
         NMC Group had arrangements with Neopharma and others for them to provide invoices
         that they might discount. This, he said, was a common market practice in the UAE. He
         specifically confirmed that he meant by this evidence that it became a common practice
         for banks, including international banks, to discount invoices knowing that they were not
         for any genuine supply. He initially said that he did not know whether Baroda followed
         this practice, but then he said that it “definitely” would have done so.

699. In re-examination, Mr Manghat repeated this evidence about bank practices: he said “this
         was something, like, widely accepted, or widely tolerated market practices, the whole
         thing, and there was no intention to defraud, or to do any illegitimate business within the
         company …. We never believed that what we are doing is part of a large scam, or a fraud
         or something, because it was widely used”. He confirmed that the accepted practice that
         he was describing might involve creating an invoice where there was no real transaction.

700. I reject this evidence as dishonest. I cannot accept that there was any such common
         practice in the UAE (or elsewhere), nor that Mr Manghat believed that there was. It was
         not put to any of the banking witnesses, neither to the expert witnesses nor to the bankers
         from ADCB and Baroda who gave evidence of fact, that there had been any such a
         practice on the part of banks. Nor can I accept that, if there had been, it would have been
         acceptable to the banks’ regulators.

Conclusion

701. Having made these findings about Mr Manghat’s involvement in the fraud, it is not
         necessary for me to examine the contention that he was aware of the undisclosed related
         party transactions and the Undisclosed Securities. With that qualification, I conclude
         that all five limbs of the case that he was a party to the fraud are proved: see para. 628
         above. Thus, I accept ADCB’s contention that Mr Manghat was party to a scheme
         whereby money and assets were extracted from the NMC Group, while the false
         appearance of a successful and growing business was maintained, when it was, in
         reality, balance sheet insolvent: see para. 203.

702. It is true, as Mr Hennessey argued, that the amount that Mr Manghat received through
         misappropriations from the NMC Group are dwarfed by those received by Dr Shetty and
         the Bin Buttis. That said, the benefits to him would be regarded as very substantial by
         most businessmen, and he also received substantial reported remuneration as a senior

         executive of an apparently successful listed company. His rewards were enough to
         answer any argument that he had no motive for participating in dishonesty.

ADCB’s claim: liability

703. As I have explained, in the ADCB Action, ADCB claim in respect of monies advanced
         under the so-called Core Facilities. I shall next consider (i) ADCB’s procedures for
         granting facilities, and (ii) the requirements for advancing monies under them.

ADCB’s procedures for credit decisions

704. It was the responsibility of ADCB’s Business Unit to prepare credit proposals and the
         accompanying documentation. Proposals were then analysed and checked by the
         Business Risk Division, and the Investment Banking Group would advise on structuring
         proposed transactions and pricing.

705. The analysis of the Business Risk Division, in particular, would involve consideration of
         the customer’s (or proposed customer’s) financial information. It included a so-called
         Moody Risk Analysis by a dedicated team, whereby the financial data for recent years was
         compared to examine changes over time. ADCB’s software was used to produce a Moody
         Risk Analysis rating (or “MRA Rating”) to measure credit risk.

706. The Investment Banking Group’s remit covered consideration of how the borrowing
         would be repaid, and leverage rations. Their work too necessarily required consideration
         of the customer’s financial data.

707. The analysis and memoranda of the Business Risk Division and the Investment Banking
         Group, to which on the customer’s annual financial statements and other financial
         information was crucial, were (in Mr Zo’Mot’s words) “the information basis of ADCB’s
         decision making further up the chain”.

708. The proposal, including the MRA Rating, would be assessed by the Credit Group, which
         had authority to sanction credit within stipulated limits. In such cases, the Credit Group
         might sanction or refuse the proposal or might seek further information or refer it for
         consideration by a more senior body. If the facility exceeded the authority of the Credit
         Group, the proposal would be considered by a more senior body: the Management Risk
         & Credit Committee (“MRCC”), or for larger proposals the Board Risk & Credit Committee
         (“BRCC”), or, if the proposed exposure to the borrower (or borrower group) would exceed
         10% of ADCB’s capital fund, ADCB’s Board of Directors. Those bodies similarly might
         sanction or refuse the proposal, might seek further information or might refer it
         elsewhere.

Compliance Certificates: 2015 and 2017

709. Before ADCB entered into the Core Facilities in respect of which it claims, ADCB had
         earlier in the Relevant Period entered into syndicated facility agreements with NMCH,
         under which it was both a lender in the syndicate and the agent bank. They included what
         were referred to as:

         a. the “2015 Syndicated Facilities”, whereby under an agreement dated 14 May
                   2015 NMCH was provided with various facilities totalling US$ 425 million and AED
                   1,469.2 million;

         b. the “2017 Intercreditor Agreement”, whereby NMC plc and NMCH entered into
                   an agreement dated 8 March 2017 with ADCB and other banks about the ranking
                   and priorities in respect of a security package for a US$ 575 million bridging
                   facility under an agreement of 13 December 2016; and

         c. the “2017 Syndicated Facilities”, whereby under an agreement dated 8 May
                   2017 NMCH was provided with a US$ 250 million facility.

710. Under these three agreements, in order to demonstrate its compliance with their
         requirements, NMCH was obliged to provide to ADCB, as agent bank, Compliance
         Certificates signed by two senior officers of the NMC Group, namely:

         a. Quarterly Compliance Certificates, that confirmed that NMCH was not in default
                   of its obligations, and provided stipulated financial information, including
                   information relating to its debts. These required a statement of (inter alia) the
                   exact amount of NMCH’s “Borrowings” (sc. the aggregate of the NMC Group’s
                   total outstanding principal, capital or nominal amount of Financial Indebtedness)
                   and its “Total Net Debt” (sc. the aggregate of the NMC Group’s total borrowings,
                   less any obligations to others in the NMC Group and after deducting cash that it
                   held); and

         b. Monthly Coverage Ration Compliance Certificates, confirming that NMCH was
                   not in default of its obligations and information about the value of its receivables
                   and the “coverage ratio”. They were designed to provide information about
                   whether it could meet its debt obligations from its earnings. These certificates
                   required the statement “We confirm that no Default is continuing”, and the term
                   “Default” was defined to include “Insolvency” where “the value of the assets of
                   the Company or any member of the Group is less than its liabilities (taking into
                   account contingent and prospective liabilities)”. There was no challenge to the
                   expert evidence that there was a Default (as defined) at all relevant times when
                   certificates were provided by the NMC Group under the 2015 Syndicated
                   Facilities, the 2017 Intercreditor Agreement or the 2017 Syndicated Facilities.

711. Mr El Khaldi explained the importance of certificates of this kind to ADCB’s lending
         decisions. The following evidence was not challenged, and I accept it: “…the compliance
         and other certificates from NMC entities were a vital part of the way in which we perceived
         the financial condition of our borrower. ADCB relied on these certificates. If they had not
         been provided, or they had been supplied in a form that suggested that NMC was in
         financial difficulty or in default, it would have immediately set alarm bells ringing. I would
         have been notified. It would have called into question ADCB’s decision to lend and it
         would have affected all decision-making about our relationship with NMC”.

The Core Facilities

712. I have listed the Core Facilities at para. 339 above.

713. The 2018 Syndicated Loan: as Mr Zo’Mot explained, the 2018 Syndicated Loan was the
         largest syndication arrangement that ADCB had been involved in, and they replaced and
         increased the NMC Group’s existing borrowing, including the 2015 Syndicated Facilities,
         and the 2017 Syndicated Facilities. Although its origins were in the ADCB’s previous
         syndicated arrangements with the NMC Group, I do not accept Mr Manghar’s description
         of it as “simply a continuation of the credit that ADCB and others extended to NMC”.

714. It appears that NMCH approached ADCB’s Investment Banking Group for the facility. On
         10 February 2018, Mr Avinash Ravikumar of the Investment Banking Group sent an email
         to Mr El Khaldi and Mr Ali Ahmed, a Senior Relationships Manager at ADCB, and copied it
         to Mr Zo’Mot, Mr Nobilli and Mr Le Jeune. He attached a financial model and a
         presentation, which reflected the NMC Group’s published financial statements. Mr El
         Khaldi proceeded to draft a proposal with the assistance of the Business Risk Division.

715. The proposal went to the Credit Group, which adopted the financial assessment of the
         “NMC/KBBO Group” (as ADCB grouped facilities and accounts for regulatory purposes)
         from the Risk Assessment Note (“RAN”) for ADCB’s 2018 annual review, which had
         recently been completed. The RAN had reviewed the financial information of the NMC
         Group and recommended renewal of its facilities. The Credit Group recommended the
         proposal be accepted with ADCB’s participation in the syndicate of US$ 850 million “to
         maintain our relationship with the NMC Group”, but that its participation be sold down to
         US$ 650 million within 90 days.

716. The proposal next was considered at a meeting of the MRCC on 19 February 2018, which
         Mr Zo’Mot attended. The MRCC decided to approve the proposal, waiving the
         requirement that US$ 200 million be sold down within 90 days, but it required the
         Business Unit to “explore potential sell down and report back on the subject within 6
         months”. I accept Mr Zo’Mot’s explanation that the MRCC’s purpose was to allow ADCB,
         if so advised, to sell down its participation “in an orderly fashion … rather than in a rushed
         fashion”. On 22 February 2018, Mr Zo’Mot presented the matter to the BRCC, who
         adopted the proposal as recommended by the MRCC.

718. I should refer to some of the terms of the 2018 Syndicated Facilities Agreement:

         a. Clause 18.11 set out representations that the NMC Group’s financial statements
                   complied with Generally Accepted Accounting Principles and fairly represented
                   NMCH’s financial condition and operations.

         b. Clause 19 provided for NMCH to provide financial information to ADCB including

                   statements, and certificates (the “Compliance Certificates”) to verify that
                   information.

                   interest, tax, depreciation and amortisation).

719. Clause 4 of the 2018 Syndicated Facilities Agreement:

         a. required NMCH to provide a certificate about the NMC Group’s financial position
                   as a condition precedent to utilising the facilities (the “CP Certificates”): clause
                   4.1; and

         b. provided that the syndicated banks were not obliged to respond to a request by
                   NMCH to use the facilities unless, at the time of the request and when it proposed
                   to use the funds, no “Default”, such as insolvency or breach of a financial
                   covenant, was continuing or would result from the proposed loan: clause 4.2.

720. The Sukuk Offering: on 2 May 2018, Mr Manghat opened discussions with Mr Zo’Mot that

         Group recommended its acceptance. It recorded its recommendation as follows: “Given
         that the request is in line with recently approved BRCC prudential limit for the Group of
         USD 850M, We recommend request subject to standby limit to be utilized by obligor –

Mr Manghat’s representations

731. ADCB’s case under article 282 of the Civil Code is that Mr Manghat is liable for his
         “knowing participation in the Loan Recycling Scheme during the Relevant Period”. Its
         case under the CCL 2015 is similarly that he “used his control of NMCH and Al Zahra to
         perpetrate” the scheme. I shall return to the cases under article 282 and the CCL 2015,
         but I need next to explain further ADCB’s claim under article 285 that alleges deception
         on Mr Manghat’s part.

732. ADCB’s case under article 285 of the Civil Code is that “[b]y implied and express
         representations over time Mr Manghat endorsed the honesty, accuracy and legitimacy of

ADGMCFI-2022-111 – ABU DHABI COMMERCIAL BANK PJSC V PRASANTH MANGHAT
ADGMCFI-2020-020 - IN THE MATTER OF NMC HEALTHCARE LIMITED (IN ADMINISTRATION) (SUBJECT TO A DEED OF COMPANY ARRANGEMENT) AND THE COMPANIES
LISTED IN SCHEDULE 1 TO THE ADMINISTRATION APPLICATION
ADGMCFI-2022-299 – NMC HEALTHCARE LIMITED (IN ADMINISTRATION) (SUBJECT TO A DEED OF COMPANY ARRANGEMENT) AND OTHERS V BAVAGUTHU RAGHURAM
SHETTY AND OTHERS

                                                                                                                                                                     177

                                                                                       05 November 2026 04:43 PM

         the NMC Group’s financial statements and other financial information; its true debt
         levels; and the propriety of its operations”. Thus, the representations are said to have
         been made both impliedly and expressly.

733. Before describing ADCB’s case under article 285 further, I sound a note of caution about
         the emphasis on “representations”. The concept is familiar to the common law, not least
         in the context of fraud claims, and, as Mr Pillai observed, ADCB’s case was originally
         formulated by reference to representations when in was proceeding against Mr Manghat
         and others in England. The basic concept of UAE law that underlies article 285 is, as I
         understand it, a more general principle of deception: that, if a person deceives another,
         he should be liable to make good any harm that results. That said, in view of how ADCB
         presented its case, it is convenient to consider what it says about representations.

734. ADCB says that Mr Manghat is liable under article 285 because he failed to inform ADCB
         of the true financial position of the NMC Group when he ought to have done so. As I have
         said, silence, or a failure to speak when there is a duty to do so, can constitute deceit:
         see para. 305. Mr Manghat does not contend that he informed ADCB of the true financial
         position of the NMC Group’s financial position, or that it was mis-stating its indebtedness
         in its financial statements: his case is that he did not know that it was doing so. I also find
         that ADCB was not otherwise aware that the financial statements did not properly
         represent the NMC Group’s financial position. Understandably, it supposed that they did.

735. The argument about whether Mr Manghat’s failure to inform ADCB of the true financial
         position amount to deception under article 285 of the Civil Code is essentially one of UAE
         law, to which I shall return: see para. 771. However, the context of the argument is this.

736. First, as I have concluded, Mr Manghat was, throughout the Relevant Period aware of the
         NMC Group’s true financial position, and knew that it was mis-stated in the financial
         statements.

737. Secondly, throughout the Relevant Period, Mr Manghat actively engaged with ADCB on
         behalf of the NMC Group, in particular in discussing potential new facilities. He engaged
         particularly with Mr Zo’Mot, and, although Mr Manghat challenged Mr Zo’Mot’s evidence
         about how frequently they met and how formal their meetings were, I conclude that Mr
         Manghat played a leading role in negotiations with ADCB about transactions and in its
         relationship with the NMC Group more generally. I accept Mr Zo’Mot’s evidence that,
         “Certainly from my perspective Mr Manghat ran NMC from October 2014 onward;, taking
         the lead on all NMC financing requests to ADCB, managing all key meetings, and
         providing key updates to ADCB in relation to NMC”. In an email to Mr Zo’Mot dated 22
         June 2016, Mr Manghat wrote, “You have been my long and trusted banking partner and
         aware of our Group’s acquisition strategy”. Furthermore, in 2018, Mr Manghat
         participated in “roadshows” for the Sukuk Offering, and on 27 June 2019, Mr Manghat,
         with Mr Shenoy, made a presentation to ADCB’s Board of Directors. At both, he deployed
         the NMC plc’s financial statements to describe the NMC Group’s position and outlook.

738. Coming to ADCB’s case that Mr Manghat endorsed the NMC Group’s financial
         statements and other financial information by express and implied representations,
         ADCB alleges that, through the Compliance Certificates, Mr Manghat made both implied
         representations and, in the case of six certificates, which expressly certified the level of
         “Borrowings” or “Total Net Debt” of the NMC Group, express representations.

739. ADCB introduced the allegations about express representations by an amendment made
         with the Court’s permission early in the trial, the amended pleading being filed on 3 April
         2026. In his Post-Hearing Submissions, Mr Manghat denied that he had had “sufficient
         time to respond to the specific allegations which were introduced after the trial had
         commenced”. I do not accept that: the Post-Hearing Submissions were presented over
         six months after the allegations were introduced.

740. Mr Manghat was a co-signatory on six certificates relating to the use of the 2015
         Syndicated Facilities, eight certificates relating to use of the 2018 Syndicated Loan and
         four certificates under the Club Facility. ADCB contends that he made implied
         representations about the NMC Group’s financial position by signing each of these
         certificates, and also that he made express representations in four certificates under the
         2015 Syndicates Facilities, one under the 2018 Syndicated Loan, and one under the Club
         Facility.

741. ADCB’s case about the express representations is this:

         a. By the four certificates provided to ADCB under the 2015 Syndicated Facilities,
                   Mr Manghat represented that the levels of “Borrowings” (as defined in the
                   agreement of 14 May 2015) stated in the certificates were correct.

         b. By a certificate of 27 March 2018 provided to ADCB under the 2018 Syndicated
                   Loan and by a certificate of 15 May 2019 provided to ADCB under the Club Facility,
                   he represented that the levels of Total Net Debt (as defined) stated in the
                   certificate were correct.

742. The implied representations that ADCB pleads are that Mr Manghat (and his co-signatory,
         whether Mr Krishmnamoothry, Mr Suresh Kumar or Mr Shenoy) represented (i) that NMCH
         was carrying on its business properly and legitimately; and (ii) that the signatory honestly
         believed that the certificate was based on genuine financial records of the NMC Group.

743. I accept that the second representation was implicit in the certificates, but I am not
         persuaded that the certificates involved the signatories in making the first of the
         representations. I confine myself to two problems that I see with ADCB’s allegation here.
         First, the meaning of the alleged representation is vague: why does it refer only to NMCH,
         whereas otherwise certificates related to the position of the NMC Group? What does
         “properly” mean (assuming that it is not tautologous with “legitimately”)? Would the
         representation be belied if any incidental aspect of NMCH’s business was not carried on

“properly”? Further, given that the second implied representation is qualified by
reference to the signatory’s belief, I do not understand why the first implied
representation is not similarly qualified.

744. Mr Manghat did not dispute that, if he made the express representations and the second
         category of implied representations, they were untrue. The issues between ADCB and Mr
         Manghat on this point, as I understand it, are (i) whether Mr Manghat did in fact sign the
         certificates as ADCB alleges, and (ii) whether the certificates were of any real
         importance.

745. In his Defence, Mr Manghat admitted signing six Compliance Certificates. When he was
         cross-examined, at first he appeared to accept that, where his signature appeared on a
         certificate, he had either signed it himself or authorised that it be signed in his name.
         Later in his cross-examination, he appeared to question his signatures: he said that, at
         some times each year, he had been in India and could not have signed them. I am not
         persuaded: even if he was not in the UAE, that does not mean that he did not authorise
         the certificates be signed electronically in his name. There would have been no reason
         for anyone to use his signature otherwise: other certificates were presented that had two
         other signatories.

746. Mr Manghat sought to belittle the importance of Compliance Certificates, and so
         implicitly of any misrepresentations in them. To give the flavour of his evidence about
         this, I cite this answer from his cross-examination: “Compliance certificates, by nature,
         is not considered to be extremely serious by the company or the bank. It was just a
         document …. So in the whole thing, if you look at it, compliance certificate, I’m not saying
         it is not there. And legally, yes. I’m bound that I have signed it. … But in practical – in a
         country like the UAE, culturally, these certificates are all just like a paper that moves, from
         one office to another office. But the entire thing is done in a completely different fashion”.
         If Mr Manghat was seeking to argue that misrepresentations in certificates of this kind,
         particularly deceitful misrepresentations, would normally have been regarded as
         unimportant by ADCB and other banks conducting business in the UAE, I reject that
         evidence. No such suggestion was put to the ADCB witnesses, the Baroda witnesses or
         the expert banking witnesses.

747. Next, ADCB’s argument that Mr Manghat made implied representations in or through
         NMC plc’s financial statements for the years 2015, 2016, 2017 and 2018 (the “Accounts
         Representations”). The Accounts Representations that ADCB alleges are these:

a. Each set of accounts had been honestly prepared;

b.  Each set of accounts was not materially misstated;

c. Mr Manghat was not aware of any matters that prevented each set of accounts
         from giving a true and fair view; and

         d. Mr Manghat was not aware of any reason to suppose that false information had
                   been supplied to EY for their work on the accounts.

748. I accept ADCB’s case with regard to the third and fourth of the Accounts Representations,
         which are confined to Mr Manghat’s knowledge. I need not deal with the first and second
         of the Accounts Representations, and I say no more about them.

749. Mr Manghat pleads that he did not make the Accounts Representations because anything
         that he did in connection with the Core Facilities was done in his capacity as CEO of NMC
         plc, on its behalf and in the course of his employment. This is irrelevant: it is no defence
         in UAE (or ADGM) law for an employee who is guilty of deceit to show that he was acting
         in the course of his employment.

750. Finally, with regard to representations that ADCB alleges were made by Mr Manghat, I
         refer to the CP Certificates in respect of the 2018 Syndicated Loan. By certificates dated
         26 February 2018, Mr Manghat signed certificates about, inter alia, the amount of NMC
         plc’s indebtedness and the amount of its Total Net Debt in relation to its EBITDA. (One of
         the CP Certificates stated that Mr Manghat signed it as Dr Shetty’s attorney. The reason
         for this is obscure, but it cannot affect whether Mr Manghat is liable under article 285 in
         respect of what he certified.)

751. ADCB pleads that, in signing the CP Certificates, Mr Manghat impliedly represented to
         ADCB (i) that NMC plc was carrying on its business properly and legitimately, and (ii) that
         he honestly believed the certificates were based on genuine financial records of the NMC
         Group. For reasons that I have explained (see para. 743 above) I am not persuaded that
         Mr Manghat made the former representation, but I accept that he made the latter.

752. ADCB further alleges that Mr Manghat made representations by signing the 2018
         Syndicated Facilities Agreement and the Club Facilities agreement, and also that he is
         liable in respect of representations made by third parties, including other executives of
         NMCH and solicitors who conducted negotiations relating to the Core Facilities. As I see
         it, these contentions add nothing to ADCB’s case under article 285 of the Civil Code, and
         I say nothing about them.

Mr Manghat’s case about why ADCB provided the Core Facilities

753. Mr Manghat argued that, when deciding to provide the Core Facilities, ADCB did not rely
         on the NMC Group’s financial information or any representations that he made. He
         argued that it practised what he called “name lending”, which I understand to be
         extending credit on the basis of the customer’s reputation and which he describes as a
         says was a common practice. He pleaded that ADCB relied upon the fact that “the
         Principal Shareholders were well-known high net worth individuals within the UAE and
         customers of ADCB in their own right”. He also pleaded that ADCB relied upon
         information available from the CBUAE, reports from the Etihad Credit Bureau, EY, ADCB’s

         “own personal and external advisers”, “market analysis and rating agencies” and “other
         market participants”.

754. These allegations have no support in the documentary or other evidence. I conclude that,
         in so far as ADCB had regard for any of these matters when considering and deciding upon
         proposals for the Core Facilities, they were of pretty peripheral importance. The NMC
         Group’s financial information and the representations about it made by Mr Manghat and
         other NMC executives were clearly central to ADCB’s lending decisions.

755. Further, Mr Manghat denies that “ADCB relied at all or to any material extent on the
         apparent integrity or accuracy of NMC plc’s audited financial statements”, and he pleads
         that “ADCB knew or ought to have known that the Financial Statements were materially
         misstated …”. In support of this contention, Mr Manghat’s relies on these allegations:

         a. That loans were made by AHB to NMCH that were undisclosed in the books of the
                   NMC Group;

         b. That guarantees are said to have been given by the NMC Group to AHB to support
                   personal borrowing of Dr Shetty;

         c. That as of 30 June 2019, the NMC Group had borrowings that had been extended
                   by AHB and UNB of some AED 418 million, which is said to have been
                   “unaccounted for in the accounts”; and

         d. That ADCB made a loan of US$5 million to Dr Shetty on 17 December 2019, after
                   the publication of the First Muddy Waters Report.

756. I make two preliminary observations about this contention. First, many of the points
         relied upon in support of it were not put to ADCB’s witnesses. Secondly, it would be
         beside the point if ADCB ought to have known that financial information was misleading:
         that does not answer the contention that Mr Magnhat’s misrepresentations led ADCB to
         provide the Core Facilities or otherwise answer the claim in deceit.

757. Mr Manghat does not allege that ADCB entered into a loan to NMCH that was not
         disclosed. However, he pleads that an example of such a loan is a facility provided by
         AHB in connection with its sale of the Al Maryah Tower to Dr Shetty and KBBAM (or a
         special purpose vehicle owned by them) for AED 500 million. On 23 July 2018, AHB’s
         Board Credit Investment Committee (“AHB BCIC”) approved a loan of AED 475 million to
         KBBAM and Dr Shetty for this purpose. The security for the loan was to include a
         guarantee provided by NMCH. However, on 26 December 2018, AHB BCIC approved a
         proposal to waive the requirement for security from NMCH because it was said that
         article 153 of CCL 2015 prohibited company guarantees in support of loans to directors.
         Neither with nor without a guarantee from NMCH does this arrangement exemplify a loan
         to NMCH.

                                                                                       05 November 2026 04:43 PM

758. With regard to guarantees given by the NMC Group in support of lending to Dr Shetty, Mr
         El Khaldi explained that, at around the time that ADCB acquired AHB, it was arranged by
         a facility letter dated 26 March 2019 that ADCB would provide a Murabaha facility for AED
         475 million to Dr Shetty and KBBAM. It was supported by a guarantee dated 1 May 2019
         provided by NMCH and signed by Mr Suresh Kumar and by a guarantee dated 1 May 2019
         and provided by UAE Exchange. Mr Manghat’s argument, as it appears, is that, because
         ADCB pleads that the guarantee provided by NMCH was an undeclared collateral benefit,
         therefore it is to be inferred that ADCB knew that it was “improper” for NMC to guarantee
         this facility. I do not accept that: the fact that ADCB now complains about the Undeclared
         Securities is not a reason for inferring that it knew this (or ought to have known this)
         contemporaneously, and there is no evidence that it did so.

759. Mr Manghat also pleads that he “has become aware of a guarantee given in favour of
         personal borrowing to Dr Shetty by [AHB]”. He does not plead, and did not state, who is
         said to have given the guarantee. All that he says about it is that he recalls that it was
         given “in relation to the financing of a hospital” and that “the provision of a guarantee for
         its construction was discussed by the Board”. No witness of ADCB (and no other witness)
         was asked about this allegation, and it was apparently not pursued at trial.

760. No evidence was put forward to support the allegation that ADCB knew or ought to have
         known about AED 418 million of Undisclosed Debt. Again, this allegation was not pursued
         in cross-examination of any witness or otherwise at trial.

761. There is no evidence that ADCB granted Dr Shetty a facility of US$ 5 million in December
         2019 after the publication of the First Muddy Waters Report. However, ADCB accepts that
         it agreed to provide him with a facility of AED 40 million (the equivalent of about US$ 11
         million) in the wake of the First Muddy Waters Report. Mr Zo’Mot gave evidence that Mr
         Nobili and he recommended that this facility be provided, and it was approved on 18
         December 2019. Its purpose was to provide Dr Shetty with short-term personal finance
         to cover margin calls relating to facilities that he had obtained against his shares in NMC
         plc when their value had dropped after the report. Mr Zo’Mot explained that he
         recommended the facility because Dr Shetty was a long-standing customer of ADCB in
         good standing, the funding was relatively modest and was short-term, and Dr Shetty had
         a satisfactory account history. It was granted at a time when ADCB was being assured by
         Mr Manghat that the Muddy Waters Report was “short seller noise”. I accept Mr Zo’Mot’s
         evidence about the decision to grant Dr Shetty the facility, and I do not think that it has
         any relevance to ADCB’s claims.

762. To my mind, neither the decision to provide this facility to Dr Shetty nor the other matters
         that Mr Manghat pleaded support the contention that ADCB knew or ought to have known
         that the NMC Group’s financial information was materially misstated. I reject the
         allegation.

ADCB’s case under article 282 of the Civil Code

763. As I said at para. 291 above, there are three elements to liability under article 282 of the
         Civil Code: (i) a harmful act; (ii) harm suffered; and (iii) a causal link between the harmful
         act and the harm.

764. As I have also explained at para. 293 above, a harmful act occurs when a person does
         something that, as it is put, “goes beyond the limit”. I have concluded that Mr Manghat
         knowingly participated in the fraud, or, as ADCB puts it, the Loan Recycling Scheme. As
         ADCB submits, this conduct constituted a harmful act for the purposes of article 282.

765. I accept ADCB’s submission that it suffered harm, which constitutes not only its loss by
         way of being unable to recover what is owing from NMCH (or from the guarantors of, or
         other security for, the facilities that it provided to NMCH), but also the loss of profit or
         return that it was entitled to receive under the terms of the facilities: see para. 296 above.

766. The causal link: I do not need to decide whether the harm that ADCB suffered is direct
         harm from Mr Manghat’s act, or whether UAE law would regard it as harm by causation.
         If it is not direct harm, Mr Manghat is liable for it under article 283(1) of the Civil Code: his
         conduct clearly constitutes wrongdoing, both because he acted dishonestly in
         perpetrating the fraud, and because, having been appointed a director of NMC plc on 26
         June 2014, he acted in breach of his duties under sections 171 to 175 of the UK
         Companies Act 2006 and his common law duties under English law.

767. In his Defence to the ADCB claim, Mr Manghat refers to article 284 of the Civil Code,
         which provides that “[i]f [the same harm is caused by] a direct actor and an indirect actor,
         judgment shall be against the main actor”. This point was not pursued at trial and, in my
         judgment, article 284 does not assist Mr Manghat. I cannot regard him as a “indirect
         actor”. He was central to the Loan Recycling Scheme.

768. I have explained at paras. 327ff above that Mr Manghat is not assisted by article 287 or
         article 290 of the Civil Code.

ADCB’s case under article 285 of the Civil Code

769. The elements of a claim under article 285 of the Civil Code are (i) a deception; (ii) harm
         suffered; and (iii) a causal link.

770. I have concluded that Mr Manghat made representations to ADCB about NMC plc’s
         financial statements and the NMC Group’s financial position. Those representations
         were false, Mr Manghat knew them to be false when he made them, and they were
         dishonest. That is enough to establish that he was guilty of deception.

771. ADCB also argues that Mr Manghat is liable for deception by his silence. I have explained
         that there may be deception for the purposes of article 285 when a person who is under

ADGMCFI-2022-299 – NMC HEALTHCARE LIMITED (IN ADMINISTRATION) (SUBJECT TO A DEED OF COMPANY ARRANGEMENT) AND OTHERS V BAVAGUTHU RAGHURAM
SHETTY AND OTHERS

                                                                                                                                                                     184

                                                                                       05 November 2026 04:43 PM

         a legal, commercial or moral obligation to speak fails to do so. ADCB presented its case
         that Mr Manghat is so liable as follows: “Despite engaging with ADCB over a number of
         years …, Mr Manghat never revealed the true position to it. UAE law did not permit Mr
         Manghat to remain silent in these circumstances. Mr Manghat’s failure to disclose the
         Loan Recycling Scheme was a betrayal of the good faith on which all commercial
         relationships depend. There can be on doubt that Mr Manghat deliberately refrained from
         revealing what he was doing to ADCB because he was intent on preserving the public
         picture of the NMC Group’s success so as to continue to benefit personally from his
         corrupt conduct”. I accept that submission, and ADCB’s contention that Mr Manghat’s
         silence was a deception under article 285.

772. With regard to harm, the observations that I made in respect of the case under article 282
         apply equally here.

773. There is a clear causal link between Mr Manghat’s deception and the harm that ADCB
         suffered. But for the deceptions, ADCB would not have entered into any of the Core
         Facilities, nor would it have advanced funds under them. Mr Hennessey argued that it is
         fatal to a claim based upon a misrepresentation that there was no evidence from the
         persons in ADCB who made the decisions to enter into the Core Facilities: as it was put
         in his Post-Hearing Submissions, that “[n]one of ADCB’s witnesses was responsible for
         final lending decisions or able to give specific evidence as to the decisions made in
         respect of the Core Facilities relied upon by ADCB”.

774. I am not impressed by that argument. Reliance is not an ingredient of a claim under
         article 285 and further, as in English law (see Credit Suisse Life (Bermuda) Ltd v Ivanishvili
         [2025] UKPC 53), UAE law does not require that anyone in ADCB was consciously aware
         that, or to have consciously thought about whether, Mr Manghat was representing that
         the accounts and certificates were accurate or honest. It is enough, in the circumstances
         of this case, that there was a causative link between the representations and ADCB’s
         understanding about the NMC Group finances and financial information. That
         understanding was flawed because of the misrepresentations, the resulting analysis and
         recommendations were similarly flawed, and the flawed analysis and recommendations
         fed into the decisions to extend Core Facilities. All these matters are proved by the
         documents in evidence, and confirmed by Mr Zo’Mot’s evidence.

ADCB’s case under the CCL 2015

775. Mr Manghat is also liable to ADCB under article 84 of CCL 2015. I have found that, from
         1 July 2015 when CCL 2015 came into force, he was a manager of NMCH, and he operated
         the Loan Recycling Scheme fraudulently (within the meaning of article 84). ADCB also
         argued (i) that before 1 July 2015 Mr Manghat’s conduct similarly makes him liable under
         CCL 1984, and (ii) Mr Manghat was liable under CCL 2015 as a manager of Al Zahra after
         its acquisition in February 2017. However, as I see it, any liability for ADCB’s losses
         relating to the Core Facilities arises from his conduct after I July 2015, and the claim in

ADGMCFI-2022-299 – NMC HEALTHCARE LIMITED (IN ADMINISTRATION) (SUBJECT TO A DEED OF COMPANY ARRANGEMENT) AND OTHERS V BAVAGUTHU RAGHURAM

                                                                                                                                                                     185

                                                                                       05 November 2026 04:43 PM

         respect of Al Zahra adds nothing to the claim in respect of NMCH. I therefore say nothing
         about those arguments in this judgment, but I permit ADCB to make further submissions
         about them, if so advised, when I have handed down this judgment.

Mr Manghat’s defences

776. It remains to refer to other arguments that Mr Manghat pleads in answer to ADCB’s claim.
         First, he relies on article 291 of the Civil Code, which provides as follows: “If a number of
         persons are responsible for a harmful act, each of them shall be responsible in proportion
         to his share in it, and the judge may make an order against them in equal shares or by way
         of joint or several liability as between them”. Mr Manghat did not develop any specific
         argument about how article 291 applies in this case.

777. I shall assume, without deciding, that article 291 is not to be regarded as a procedural
         provision, but a matter of substantive law. However, as I understand it, the purpose of
         article 291 is to enable the Court to do justice by apportioning liability where two or more
         parties to the proceedings have been held to be liable for harm. It has no application
         where, as here, there is only one defendant in the proceedings. Further, even if article 291
         had any application, I would have seen no reason to reduce Mr Manghat’s liability given
         the nature of his wrongdoing and his part in the fraudulent scheme.

778. Other points raised by Mr Manghat concern the DOCA’s. There are three:

         a. First, it is said that the provisions of the DOCA arrangements, and in particular
                   clause 13 of the NMCH DOCA (see para. 135 above), “are characterised as a
                   settlement of the claims (or ‘accord’) under article 722 of the … Civil Code”; and
                   that the effect of this is that “a party loses the right to claim in respect of any of
                   the settled matters and such claims can no longer be heard in Court because they
                   are extinguished by Article 730 and 741 of the … Civil Code”.

         b. It is said to be an abuse of rights under article 106 of the Civil Code to bring a claim
                   that has been extinguished under the DOCAs.

         c. Mr Manghat argues that, because ADCB has sought to recover its debt arising out
                   of the Core Facilities from NMCH, with whom it has settled, and from Dr Shetty,
                   therefore “a UAE Court may consider ADCB’s claim to amount to a plea that they
                   are jointly liable”; and under article 455 of the Civil Code, where a creditor has
                   settled with one person who had a joint liability, he can pursue others jointly liable
                   only for the balance of the claim.

779. Article 722 of the Civil Code provides that, “An accord is a contract whereby a dispute is
         removed and litigation between two opposing parties ceases by mutual consent”.
         Neither the NMCH DOCA nor clause 13 of it is an accord within the meaning of article
         722. Although, as I said in NMC Healthcare Limited v Noor Capital PSC (cit. sup.) at para.
         55, “[i]n some ways, the position of the creditor, whether or not it votes for the DOCA, is

COURT OF FIRST INSTANCE JUDGMENT
ADGMCFI-2022-111 – ABU DHABI COMMERCIAL BANK PJSC V PRASANTH MANGHAT
ADGMCFI-2020-020 - IN THE MATTER OF NMC HEALTHCARE LIMITED (IN ADMINISTRATION) (SUBJECT TO A DEED OF COMPANY ARRANGEMENT) AND THE COMPANIES
LISTED IN SCHEDULE 1 TO THE ADMINISTRATION APPLICATION
ADGMCFI-2022-299 – NMC HEALTHCARE LIMITED (IN ADMINISTRATION) (SUBJECT TO A DEED OF COMPANY ARRANGEMENT) AND OTHERS V BAVAGUTHU RAGHURAM
SHETTY AND OTHERS

                                                                                                                                                                     186

                                                                                       05 November 2026 04:43 PM

         comparable to that of a contracting party…”, the arrangements reached under a DOCA
         are not contractual but come about through a statutory process. Further, a DOCA does
         not take effect by mutual consent: as I said in the Noor Capital case, it is binding upon
         creditors whether or not they consent to the DOCA arrangement. In any case, even if the
         NMCH DOCA arrangement is to be regarded as an “accord”, Mr Manghat was not a party
         to it and it did not extinguish any liabilities that he has.

780. Article 106 of the Civil Code provides that:

                   “(1) A person shall be liable for an unlawful exercise of his rights.

                   (2) The exercise of a right shall be unlawful:

                   (a) if there is an intentional infringement [of another’s rights];

                   (b) if the interests which such exercise of rights is designed to bring about
                   are contrary to the rules of Islamic shari’a, the law, public order, or morals;

                   (c) if the interests desired are disproportionate to the harm that will be
                   suffered by others; or

                   (d) if it exceeds the bounds of custom or practice”.

781. I cannot understand how any question of abuse of rights is engaged by these
         proceedings, and Mr Manghat has not attempted, either in his submissions on UAE law
         or elsewhere, to explain this plea.

782. Nor did Mr Manghat develop or explain that article 455 of the Civil Code applies to the
         claim against him, and I see nothing in this point. Article 455 concerns joint liabilities,
         and it provides that “If the share of one of the jointly liable obligors in an obligation
         becomes extinguished for any reason other than satisfaction, the obligation shall not
         become extinguished with regard to the other obligors save to the extent of the share of
         that obligor”. Mr Manghat’s pleaded case is that NMCH’s debt arising out of the Core
         Facilities “had been settled and extinguished” and its claim against Dr Shetty “was
         dismissed in part due to ADCB’s delay and conduct in bringing the joinder application”.

783. The decision to refuse the joinder application of 3 May 2024 (see para. 22 above) was
         procedural: it did not extinguish any claim against Dr Shetty. ADCB’s claim against NMCH
         in debt could not be said to give rise to a joint liability with Mr Manghat, and Mr Manghat
         did not explain on what basis UAE Court “may consider” it to do so.

ADCB’s claim: quantum

Introduction

784. Article 292 of the Civil Code provides as follows: “In all cases, compensation shall be
         assessed on the basis of the amount of harm suffered by the victim, together with loss of
         profit, provided that that is a natural result of the harmful act”.

785. ADCB pursues claims for these heads of compensation:

         a. Unpaid debts;

         b. Costs incurred in respect of the administration of NMC plc; and

         c. Loss of profit.

786. It says that these heads of damage are all the natural result of Mr Manghat’s wrongdoing.
         In its pleaded case, it also claimed US$ 5 million in respect of loans that it made to the
         Joint Administrators of NMC plc pursuant to a Deed of Funding dated 9 April 2020, but it
         abandoned that claim in the course of its closing submissions.

787. Further, as a result of corporate income tax being introduced in the UAE, ADCB has been
         subject to tax for tax periods commencing on and after 1 June 2023, and it contends that
         it is entitled to have its compensation increased in order for it to cover the full loss after
         tax.

788. ADCB acknowledges that it must give credit in the assessment of its damages in respect
         of:

         a. The net recovery from a pledge of shares in the sum of US$19,521,279;

         b. Recoveries from the administration in the sum of US$5,292,824; and

         c. Receipt of cash from Exit Instruments in the sum of US$5,139,620.

789. Mr Manghat contends that ADCB should also give credit for the value of the Exit
         Instruments that it has received under the DOCA arrangements.

790. Questions as to what heads of damage are recoverable and matters should be brought
         into account to offset the amount of recoverable damages are matters of substantive
         law, and are therefore governed by the lex causae, UAE law. As such, it governs in
         particular (i) any issue between ADCB and Mr Manghat as to whether the heads of
         damage for which ADCB claims are the “natural result of [Mr Manghat’s] harmful act”;
         and (ii) whether the value of Exit Instruments should be deducted from ADCB’s
         compensation. Procedural questions about the assessment of damages under

         recoverable heads and enforcement of a judgment for damages are governed by the lex
         fori, and so by ADGM law.

Exit Instruments

791. The Exit Instruments that ADCB holds fall into three categories:

         a. Exit Instruments that ADCB received in respect of its claims against NMCH and
                   other companies that were put into administration (the “Creditor Exit
                   Instruments”);

         b. Exit Instruments that ADCB received because it chose to convert AFF funding that
                   it provided to the JAs into further Exit Instruments (the “AFF Exit Instruments”);
                   and

         c. Exit Instruments that ADCB bought from third parties (the “Purchased Exit
                   Instruments”).

792. ADCB’s contentions are (i) that the value of none of the Exit Instruments is to be brought
         into account in assessing its compensation, and (ii) in the alternative, that only the value
         of the Creditor Exit Instruments should be brought into account.

793. I consider the second question straightforward. ADCB’s interests in the AFF Exit
         Instruments and the Purchased Exit Instruments are unconnected with the losses that it
         suffered as a result of Mr Manghat’s wrongdoing. It holds them as a result of separate
         commercial decisions that ADCB subsequently made. I see no case for bring them into
         account when assessing ADCB’s compensation.

794. ADCB’s submission on the first question is based on article 42 of the Civil Code:

                   “(1) No harm shall be done, nor harm done in return.

                   (2) Harm shall be removed.

                   (3) Harm may not be averted by causing similar harm (to another)”.

795. ADCB’s argument is that UAE law does not regard harm as having been “removed” by
         receiving a promise or prosect of future payment, and therefore it is not “removed” by
         receipt of an instrument such as the Exit Instruments that bring the prospect of financial
         receipts in the future.

796. ADCB illustrates its argument by reference to the position where a creditor with a claim
         against two debtors obtains judgment against one of them. The claim against the other is
         not reduced by the prospect of recovery under the judgment against the first. These were
         the circumstances considered by the Dubai Court of Cassation’s judgment in case

ADGMCFI-2022-299 – NMC HEALTHCARE LIMITED (IN ADMINISTRATION) (SUBJECT TO A DEED OF COMPANY ARRANGEMENT) AND OTHERS V BAVAGUTHU RAGHURAM
SHETTY AND OTHERS

                                                                                                                                                                     189

                                                                                       05 November 2026 04:43 PM

         445/2008/75, where claimants, having obtained judgment against a corporate entity,
         then made a claim against its shareholders and mangers for mismanagement. The
         defendants contended that judgment against them would risk double recovery. The Court
         of Cassation said this: “This ground for challenge is rejected. This is since it is settled in
         the jurisprudence of this Court that if there are multiple sources for a debt and its object
         remains the same, such that the source of one of the liable party’s obligation is the
         contract and the source of another’s obligation is the harmful act, then each of them shall
         be liable vis-à-vis the harmed party to pay full compensation. The creditor may claim the
         entire debt from any debtor. However, this does not mean the [the creditor] would receive
         compensation twice. This is because the creditor’s right in this respect is limited to
         receiving the compensation once such that if payment s received from one of the debtors,
         the liability of the other debtors shall be discharged in respect of this debt, and the
         creditor shall not be entitled to have recourse enforce it against them”.

797. ADCB contends that the position with regard to the Exit Instruments is analogous. If
         payment is received under the Creditor Exit Instruments, then it constitutes
         compensation that is to be brought into account in assessing damages recoverable from
         Mr Manghat. But unless and until payment is received, ADCB is entitled to judgment to
         compensation assessed without regard to them.

798. I am persuaded by ADCB’s argument. At the hearing, I expressed concern that ADCB
         would be overcompensated if, after it had entered judgment against Mr Manghat and
         enforced it, it were to receive payments under the Creditor Exit Instruments or sell them.
         However, this could, if appropriate, be handled under the ADGM procedural regime by a
         stay of execution in respect of an appropriate amount of ADCB’s judgment. If, after I have
         issued this judgment, Mr Manghat applies for a partial stay of the judgment against him, I
         shall consider whether to exercise my discretion to order one.

The claim for unpaid debts

799. ADCB pleads three alternative cases about the compensation that it is entitled to receive
         in respect of the monies outstanding under the Core Facilities.

          a. Its primary case is that it is entitled to compensation in the total amount of
               US$1,022,293,332, being the sums by way of principal and interest or, in the case of
               the Islamic facilities, profit payments. (I shall refer to interest and profit payments
               together as ADCB’s “return”.)

          b. ADCB’s first alternative case is that it is entitled to compensation in the sum of US$
               965,111,653, being the amount outstanding as of 22 March 2022 by way of principal
               and return that had fallen due.

          c. ADCB’s second alternative case is that it is entitled to compensation in the sum of
               US$ 958,348,763, being the total amount of sums payable by way of return (as in

                                                                                                                                                                     190

                                                                                       05 November 2026 04:43 PM

               ADCB’s primary case) but discounting back to 22 March 2022 payments that were to
               fall due at a later date.

800. The factual basis for these calculations is supported by evidence of Mr Zo’Mot, which I
         accept. The calculations of the loss on the basis of the evidence of fact were reviewed
         by Mr Taylor, and his reasoning in support of them is explained in his reports. I also accept
         his evidence about this and his conclusions.

801. ADCB submits that UAE law does not stipulate exactly how this part of ADCB’s claim is
         to be calculated, that all three methods of assessment are reasonable, but that its
         primary case most closely reflects decisions of the UAE Courts. I agree that ADCB’s
         primary claim is the appropriate way to present the loss. The case law establishes that
         the loss of an opportunity to obtain a future benefit is not mere potential harm, but, if a
         claimant shows a reasonable expectation that it will obtain it, it is regarded as harm
         actually suffered. ADCB cited in support of this proposition judgments of the Dubai Court
         of Cassation in cases 216 & 239/2006 (15/1/2006), 46 & 49/2006 (8/5/06) and 130/1012
         (14/10/12).

802. Further, I see no rational basis for assessing damages by reference to 22 March 2022, as
         is done in the two alternative calculations pleaded by ADCB. This date was used only
         because (as I was told by Mr Ralston, who presented this part of ADCB’s argument) that
         is when ADCB decided that it was not “going to be paid”. This is not relevant for the
         purpose of assessing its compensation.

803. In accepting ADCB’s primary case about the basis for calculating this head of damages,
         I recognise that, in principle, any payments under the facilities that have not yet fallen
         due should be discounted to the date of the judgment. I was told by Mr Ralston, and it
         was not disputed, that this would make negligible difference to the sum to be awarded
         because the obligations under the facilities have fallen due apart from a small sum under
         the Murabaha Facility. However, if Mr Manghat is so advised, I give permission for him to
         make submissions about this when I determine what order to make following this
         judgment.

Costs in relation to the administration of NMC plc

804. ADCB’s claim in respect of costs that it incurred is pleaded as “[t]he advisory and
         profession fees and disbursements incurred in relation to the English administration”. It
         is said that they “would have been avoided had the Loan Recycling Scheme not been
         perpetuated”. In support of this claim, ADCB relies on two invoices of A&M Europe, one
         for £315,000 dated 3 April 2020 in respect of a “retainer” and the other for £380,961.61
         dated 30 April 2020 for “Legal Fees”.

805. ADCB’s pleaded claim is in the sum of US$ 949,291.70, which is said to be the equivalent
         of the total of the two invoices, £695,961.66 converted at an appropriate rate of
         exchange.

806. There is scant evidence about the circumstances in which these costs were expended.
         Mr Zo’Mot gave evidence that they are legal costs incurred by “ADCB Group Finance”,
         that they were for services provided to “ADCB Group Legal”, and that they are
         outstanding.

807. Understandably, in view of the (relatively) small sum involved, the trial did not focus on
         this claim, and I did not engage with Mr Ralston about it when he presented ADCB’s oral
         submissions. I shall therefore permit ADCB to present further submissions about it after
         I have handed down this judgment, if it is advised to pursue it. However, as matters stand,
         it seems to me that I simply do not have enough evidence about the costs incurred in
         respect of the administration of NMC plc and the circumstances in which they were
         incurred to be satisfied that the claim is within the proviso to article 292 and otherwise to
         uphold it.

808. This part of ADCB’s case faces a further difficulty. English (and so ADGM) law places
         restrictions on what legal costs may be recovered as damages: see Musst Holdings Ltd v
         Astra Asset Management UK Ltd [2026] EWHC 1599 (Ch). No reference was made to
         these restrictions at the trial. It seems to me distinctly arguable that the questions
         whether these restrictions apply to ADCB’s claim go to quantification of a head of
         damages, and so are to be regarded as procedural and governed by the lex fori, ADGM
         law. In any case, nothing before me suggests that in this respect UAE law differs from
         ADGM law. If this head of damages is pursued by ADCB, therefore, I should need to be
         satisfied that these legal costs are of a kind that are recoverable and in an amount that is
         recoverable.

Loss of profit

809. ADCB’s claim for loss of profits is made on the basis that, if the NMC Group and in
         particular NMCH had met its obligations in respect of the Core Facilities, it would have
         deployed the money on other lending and investment and earned profits therefrom.
         Evidence of Mr Zo’Mot and Mr Le Jeune, which I accept, supports the contention that the
         moneys would have been so used (and, even in the absence of such evidence, I would
         have inferred this). Understandably, ADCB does not identify a specific alternative
         transaction or specific alternative transactions that it might have entered: the lost profit
         is calculated on the basis of the rate of return calculated by reference to ADCB’s earnings
         of net interest margin. Mr Taylor reviewed the claim made on this basis, by reference to
         the information in ADCB’s financial statements about its performance. Further, he
         confirms the evidence of Mr Zo’Mot and Me Le Jeune that in and after March 2020 there
         was a market for lending in the UAE and the funds that ADCB would have had available
         could have been placed in the market.
