810. Mr Taylor calculated this head of claim, by assessing the lost profits by way of capital
         returns together with fees and commission and reducing the claim to take account of (i)
         saved costs by way of staff expenses, general administrative expenses and an allowance
         for credit loss, and (ii) saved tax. The reduction for tax is required because ADCB has been
         required to pay corporate tax at a rate of 9% for periods since 1 June 2023. (The rate has
         increased from the original rate of 9% to 15%, but ADCB is content for its compensation
         to be assessed on the basis of the 9% rate.)

811. Mr Taylor’s calculations produce an assessment of ADCB’s loss of profit in the sum of
         US$ 168,988,980. This is on the basis that (i) he calculated what he called Historical Loss
         of Profits, meaning losses to 31 December 2025, to be US$ 163,819,911, (ii) his
         assessment of the “Projected Loss of Profits” thereafter at US$ 9,453,775 and (iii) an
         allowance for profit on the recoveries that ADCB has made in the sum of US$ 4,284,706.

812. I do not propose to explain in detail Mr Taylor’s calculations of these figures. It suffices
         to say that they were fully supported by cogent reasoning in his reports, and nothing in his
         cross-examination (or in the evidence more generally) materially challenged them.

Is the compensation claimed by ADCB “a natural result of the harmful act”?

813. I revert to the question whether the compensation that ADCB claims is “a natural result
         of the harmful act”, so that the proviso to article 292 is satisfied. Mr Manghat pleads that
         “Harm is only a natural result of a harmful act if it is directly caused”. In his submissions
         he explained this plea: “The reference to ‘directly caused’ is to the concept that Article
         292 does not permit recovery of ‘consequential loss’”; and that the requirement operates
         “in a similar manner to the principle of remoteness”. However, he did not argue that UAE
         law has a distinction between direct loss (or loss directly caused) and consequential loss
         as it might be understood in English law. He accepts that “losses that would be within
         either limb of the rule in Hadley v Baxendale [(1854) 9 Ex Ch 341] are considered to be
         direct and therefore recoverable”.

814. I do not consider it helpful introduce English concepts of remoteness or causation of
         damages into the interpretation and application of the proviso in article 292. Its origin is,
         as is explained in ADCB’s submissions, in Egyptian law, where the concept is expressed
         in terms of the “normal result” (rather than natural result). The concept is explained by Dr
         El-Sanhouri, who says that the concept covers damage which could not have been
         avoided “by exerting reasonable effort” on the part of the claimant. He illustrates it by an
         example of a buyer of a diseased cow. If the diseased cow infects and kills the rest of the
         buyer’s stock, the question is whether the buyer could have avoided their death by
         making reasonable efforts. If not, he is entitled to compensation. On the other hand, if
         the buyer claims for “the inability to cultivate his land, the inability to repay debts, the
         seizure of his land and its sale at a depreciated price – these are all damages that the
         farmer could have avoided by exerting reasonable effort, as he could have resorted to
         other livestock, whether by purchase or lease, to cultivate his land. In this way, the chain

         of successive damages that befell him die to the death of the livestock would have been
         halted. Consequently, these damages are considered indirect damages, and there is no
         ground for compensation for them”.

815. Applying this guidance, I am satisfied that the claims for unpaid debts and loss of profit
         are covered by the proviso to article 292 and are recoverable.

Conclusion about quantum

816. In my judgment, therefore, ADCB has established its claim to damages by way of:

         a. Monies outstanding in the sum of US$ 1,022,293,322, subject to any adjustment
                   that Mr Manghat persuades me is proper: see para. 803 above.

         b. Loss of profit in the sum of US$ 168,988,980.

817. Against this, ADCB acknowledges that it must give credit for:

         a. US$ 19,521,279, in respect of recovery from security by way of a pledge of shares;

         b. US$ 5,292,824, in respect of recoveries from the ADGM administration; and

         c. US$ 5,139,620 by way of sums received in respect of the Creditor Exit
                   Instruments.

818. I shall determine the claim about costs in relation to NMC plc’s administration at or after
         a future hearing.

The Insolvency Claims

819. In my judgment, the JA Claimants have proved the conditions for declaring that Dr Shetty
         and Mr Manghat are liable to make contributions to the assets of NMCH both under
         section 251 of IR 2022 on the grounds of fraudulent trading and under section 252 of IR
         2022 on the grounds of wrongful trading.

Fraudulent Trading

820. I identified the ingredients of a claim for fraudulent trading at para. 253 above, but I repeat
         them here for convenience. They are:

         a. The business of the company was carried on fraudulently;

         b. The defendant was party to the business being carried on fraudulently;

         c. The defendant so acted knowingly;

                                                                                                                                                                     194

                                                                                       05 November 2026 04:43 PM

         d. The defendant acted dishonestly; and

         e. The Court should exercise its (discretionary) power to hold the defendant liable
                   to make a contribution to the company’s assets.

821. I have concluded that the relevant businesses of NMCH and the ADGM Companies were
         carried on fraudulently throughout the Relevant Period (or for such part of it as the
         particular ADGM Company was in the NMC Group.

822. I have concluded that Dr Shetty and Mr Manghat were both party to the business being
         carried on fraudulently.

823. For the same reasons, I conclude that they both did so knowingly.

824. In my judgment, Dr Shetty was dishonest. It seems likely that, before the listing of NMC
         plc, Dr Shetty and his partners in his various business enterprises had been accustomed
         to treating the funds of the businesses as assets that were available for their use as they
         saw fit. There is no dispute that Dr Shetty knew that this was not proper when NMC plc
         was listed: see para. 182 above. Taking this into account and judging Dr Shetty’s conduct
         by the standards of ordinary decent people, it was dishonest.

825. I also find that Mr Manghat was dishonest. Given his qualifications as an accountant, he
         clearly knew that the five aspects of the fraud that the JA Claimants identified (see para.
         628 above) and the fraud as a whole were unlawful and improper.

826. In view of the scale of Dr Shetty’s participation in the fraudulent scheme throughout the
         Relevant Period and the benefits that he obtained as a result of it, the Court should
         certainly exercise its discretion to hold Dr Shetty liable to make a contribution. Further,
         in view of his central role in operating the fraud, the amount of the misappropriations and
         the distortion of the financial statements, it is equally clear that Mr Manghat should be
         held liable for a contribution.

827. I have explained that I do not determine any claim of Holding for fraudulent trading in this
         judgment: see para. 262 above.

Wrongful trading

828. With regard to the wrongful trading claim. The only controversial issue is about the so-
         called Knowledge Condition: see para. 277 above. As the Court of Appeal has explained
         (see para. 281 above), in this case the Knowledge Condition is to be interpreted as turning
         on whether Dr Shetty or, as the case might be, Mr Manghat knew or ought to have
         concluded that there was no reasonable prosect of NMCH and other companies being
         and remaining able to pay its debts as they fell due.

829. In Dr Shetty’s case, he accepted that, if he is held to have known of the fraud, then the

                                                                                                                                                                     195

         Court may order a contribution on the basis of wrongful trading.

830. I need therefore add only this: Mr Wandowicz submitted that, if I conclude that it had not
         been shown that Dr Shetty was aware of the fraud, then it is not open to the JA Claimants
         to argue that the Knowledge Condition is satisfied by reference to what Dr Shetty ought
         to have known. His argument was that the pleading against him does not sufficiently state
         the basis on which this is averred, and because, when he was cross-examined, the
         allegation was put to him only in general terms. It was said that, in the absence of a more
         specific allegation, Dr Shetty did not have a fair opportunity to engage with the contention
         against him.

831. I cannot accept that Mr Wandowicz’s argument. Dr Shetty was a director of NMC plc, and
         until 2017 its CEO. On the face of it, it is unusual that an individual should be unaware of
         so many large payments into his personal bank account; and unusual that a director
         should be unaware that financial statements should so utterly misstate the true financial
         position of a company; and unusual that a signatory of major bank facilities should claim
         to be unaware of them. Without a credible explanation from Dr Shetty of how he was
         nevertheless unaware of these matters, the JA Claimants could not be expected to do
         more than contend in general terms that any director in Dr Shetty’s position ought to have
         been aware of such matters.

832. In any case, I cannot accept that Dr Shetty did not understand what case in wrongful
         trading was being made against him by the JA Claimants “in sufficient detail to enable
         that party properly to prepare to answer it” (as it was put by Saville LJ in the British Airways
         Pension Trustees case (cit sup): see para. 29). Mr Wandowicz submitted that, had the
         case against Dr Shetty been pleaded with more particularity, other evidence might have
         been adduced. I am not persuaded: the specific example given was that “an expert on
         UAE corporate governance might have been needed to decide the point whether a
         reasonably diligent person in Dr Shetty’s position would have done or not have done
         something”. I cannot accept that expert evidence of this kind would have been permitted,
         especially given that NMC plc is an English company. Nor can I accept that Dr Shetty was
         prevented in this or any other respect from preparing and presenting his defence to the
         case against him because it was not pleaded more specifically.

833. Coming to the case against Mr Manghat, my findings about his involvement in the fraud
         establish that the Knowledge Condition is met. Any director who knows that such large
         borrowings have been concealed from the auditors and that the published accounts so
         utterly misstated the true financial position must be taken to have known that there was
         no reasonable prosect of obligations being satisfied as they fell due; and if he did not
         actually know this, he certainly ought to have known.

834. I therefore consider that it would be proper to declare Dr Shetty and Mr Manghat are liable
         to make a contribution to NMCH’s assets on the basis of wrongful trading. However, in

         view of my conclusions about fraudulent trading, there is no need for me to do so. I shall
         order contributions on the basis of the fraudulent trading claims.

Contributions

835. These points about the proper approach to determining what contribution (if any) should
         be ordered against a person for fraudulent trading or wrongful trading are well-
         established and uncontroversial:

         a. The Court has a very broad discretion as to what contribution should be ordered
                   according to the circumstances of the case. In Re Produce Marketing Consortium
                   Ltd (No 2) [1989] BCLC 520 597G/H Knox J said, “Parliament has indeed chosen
                   very wide words of discretion and it would be undesirable to seek to spell out
                   limits on that discretion…”.

         b. “The nature of the inquiry is as to loss caused by the company’s creditors
                   generally through an insufficiency of assets with which to meet their debts”: see
                   Re Tradestar Ltd [ 2018] EWHC 3595 (Ch) at para. 21 per Fancourt J.

         c. The purpose of an award of compensation is not to punish, and so it should be
                   assessed by way of making a contribution that will prevent or mitigate a shortfall
                   in assets of the company as a result of which it is unable to discharge its liabilities:
                   Morphitis v Bernasconi [2003] EWCA Civ 289 at para. 55 per Chadwick LJ.

         d. The assessment of compensation does not engage questions of causation,
                   contribution or mitigation such as fall to be considered when measuring damages
                   for common law claims: Bouchier v Booth [2023] EWHC 3195 at para. 64.

         e. The Court is not required to make a calculate with precision that amount of any
                   contribution, and some elements of it are typically “broad-brush”, and inevitably
                   so: Re Bank of Credit and Commerce International SA Cit (cit sup) at para. 122.

836. I also accept the JA Claimants’ submission that I should be guided by this observation of
         Miles J in London Capital & Finance plc v Thompson [2024] EWHC 3438 (Ch): “I do not
         accept that the correct approach is to take the person who may be regarded as most
         responsible for the fraud and then apply, as it were, a sliding scale of culpability of the
         relevant defendant and then order anyone who is not at the 100% point to make a lesser
         contribution. The court has to take into account the extent of the culpability of the relevant
         defendant …” (at para. 30).

Diminution in the value of assets

837. A question that arose towards the end of the trial about whether contributions ordered
         on the basis of fraudulent trading and wrongful trading should be capped by the amount
         of any increase in the deficiency in the value of assets available to pay creditors as a

         result of the defendant’s wrongful conduct (referred to as the Increased Net Deficiency
         or “IND”). Before their closing submissions, it appeared that the JA Claimants sought
         contributions in respect of both the fraudulent trading and the wrongful trading claims in
         the amount of the IND (and no greater amount). In particular:

         a. Quinn Emanuel Urquhart & Sullivan UK LLP, the JA Claimants’ solicitors,
                   instructed Mr Bezant that, on the fraudulent trading claim, “the objective is to
                   quantify the diminution in the net assets available to the body of creditors
                   between: (i) the date upon which Baroda acquired knowledge of the fraud
                   (assuming the Court finds this to be the case) and (ii) 27 September 2020 (ie the
                   Administration Date …)”. (This instruction was directed to the claim against
                   Baroda, but the inference was that this approach was to be adopted on the claims
                   against the other defendants, mutatis mutandis.) Mr Bezant was also instructed
                   that “the relief sought in respect of the wrongful trading claim is substantially the
                   same”.

         b. During the trial, on 27 April 2026, I asked the parties to clarify the principles that
                   they contended should govern the assessment of contributions. (I was concerned
                   that, since the opening submissions had not engaged with this question, the
                   closing submissions would lack focus.) In response, I received short notes from
                   the JA Claimants on 6 May 2026, from Dr Shetty on 13 May 2026 and from Baroda
                   on 15 May 2026. In their note, the JA Claimants said that their primary case was
                   that “once liability for fraudulent trading or wrongful trading is established, the
                   defendant is liable for all the creditors’ losses, i.e. the increase in the net
                   deficiency to creditors between the date the knowledge condition is satisfied (the
                   so-called ‘Knowledge Date’) to the date of administration. That is the starting
                   point in wrongful trading cases, so a fortiori is the position in fraudulent trading
                   cases” (emphasis in original).

 838. In their closing submissions, however, the JA Claimants argued that the amount of the
         contribution, at least in respect of fraudulent trading, need not be limited to the IND. (This
         change was apparently prompted by observations that I made in the course of the hearing
         when, on 22 May 2026, I suggested that there might be circumstances in which it would
         be unfair to recent creditors of an insolvent company if contributions were necessarily
         limited to the IND. As I put it, it would be little comfort to creditors at the time of
         liquidation or administration that others would have suffered loss in an earlier insolvency
         process.) The JA Claimants recognised that a line of first instance wrongful trading cases
         in the English Courts, well exemplified by the judgment of Snowden J in Re Ralls Builders
         Ltd [2016] EWHC 243 (Ch), support the proposition that the Court should not order a
         contribution that exceeds the IND, but these authorities were powerfully criticised by Prof
         Gabriel Moss QC in an article entitled “No Compensation for Wrongful Trading – Where
         Did it All Go Wrong?”, 30 Insol Int 49. For my part, I find his criticisms persuasive.
         However, as far as the wrongful trading claims are concerned, to my mind it would have
         been wrong for me to depart from a well-established and apparently consist approach in

                                                                                                                                                                     198

                                                                                       05 November 2026 04:43 PM

         the English cases from Re Produce Marketing Consortium Ltd (No 2) [1989] BCLC 520 to
         Wright v Chappell (cit sup).

 839. The JA Claimants’ more powerful point is that a more flexible approach should be
         adopted in fraudulent trading cases, and it would not be inconsistent with the English
         authorities to do so. I accept that there would be no inconsistency. Indeed, in Morphitis
         v Bernasconi (cit sup) Chadwick LJ suggested (at para. 53) that the appropriate order in a
         fraudulent trading case might be that a director why knowingly part to a misappropriation
         should contribute an amount equal to the assets that were misapplied. He did not
         indicate that this measure would be inappropriate if the amount misapplied exceeded
         the IND, and it is difficult to see why justice would require this. See too Goode, Principles
         of Insolvency Law (5th Ed, 2018) para. 14-56 fn 266.

 840. In Re Ralls Builders Ltd (cit sup), Snowden J said this: “In my judgment, the fact that any
         contribution to be made under section 214(1) must be distributed pari passu among the
         general body of unsecured creditors of the company is a significant pointer to the fact that
         the purpose of section 214 is not to provide differential redress for individual creditors,
         depending upon an assessment of the extent of their loss caused by the period of
         wrongful trading. So, for example, a creditor whose debt was incurred after the relevant
         date cannot claim to recover 100% of his loss in priority to a creditor whose debt was
         incurred prior to that date and who may only have been marginally disadvantaged (or not
         disadvantaged at all) by the continuation of trading” (at para. 236). I recognise the force
         of this point, and that it would apply to claims of fraudulent trading as much as to claims
         of wrongful trading. This might well be a consideration that is relevant to deciding the
         appropriate contribution in any specific case. Equally, the IND might well be a relevant
         consideration when assessing the amount of contribution in a fraudulent trading case.
         But I cannot accept that it would invariably be wrong or contrary to an established
         practice to order that a defendant liable for fraudulent trading pay a contribution that
         exceeds the IND.

 841. If necessary, therefore, I would conclude that the contribution in respect of fraudulent
         trading in a case such as this might properly exceed the IND. However, I need not decide
         the point. As I see it, the JA Claimants’ argument in their closing submissions was
         directed, at least primarily, to their claim in fraudulent trading against Baroda. They say
         that, if Dr Shetty and Mr Manghat were knowingly party to fraudulent trading from April
         2012 (as they plead and as I find), I should order contributions of US$ 5,397 million. Mr
         Bezant and Mr Grantham agreed that this represents the relevant IND after 30 April 2012,
         and their evidence about this was not challenged by Dr Shetty or Mr Manghat.

The Appropriate Contributions

842. As I have said, the JA Claimants submit that the appropriate amount to order as
         contributions from Dr Shetty and Mr Manghat is US$ 5,397 million, being the IND since 30
         April 2026. I agree, subject only to:

         a. The observation that the order might properly be adjusted to reflect the JA
                   Claimants’ success on the Civil Claims;

         b. The question of interest, to which I come at para. 856 below; and

         c. The amount of the contribution ordered might properly be rounded to reflect the
                   broad-brush nature of the exercise.

843. In his Note of 13 May 2026, Dr Shetty submitted that, unless it was established that the
         NMC Group was insolvent as a result of it taking on borrowings to fund the Unlawful
         Payments, it would not be right for the contribution to be measured by the full loss of the
         creditors. His argument was that there would then be no sufficient causal connection
         between the misappropriations and some of the loss. I cannot agree. It seems to me, in
         so far as I understand the submission, that it seeks to import into the assessment of the
         contribution an inappropriate requirement that the loss would not have occurred but for
         the knowing fraudulent trading on the defendant’s part. That approach is inappropriate
         because, while there should be some causal nexus between the loss caused to creditors
         as a result of the fraudulent trading and the contribution ordered so that it is not
         unconnected and disproportionate to the defendant’s responsibility (see Morris v Bank
         of India (cit sup) at para. 122), a generous approach is adopted to what nexus is sufficient,
         which is less restrictive than the approach to causation for common law negligence
         claims or even for common law deceit claims. Here, it is clear that the misappropriations
         themselves, together with the borrowing to finance and disguise them and the associated
         costs of servicing it, were a major reason that the NMC Group, which had a positive equity
         value at the time of the IPO, rapidly became hopelessly insolvent.

844. To my mind, it is right that Dr Shetty should bear the full consequences of the insolvency.
         I see no reason that his contribution should be limited to the amount of the
         misappropriations, still less to the amount of the misappropriations for his own benefit.
         Although Dr Shetty was not, apparently, involved in the day-to-day operation of the fraud,
         that does not mitigate his responsibility for it. It is nothing to the point that, for example,
         Dr Shetty has not been shown to have known about the Sham Suppliers Scheme: as Miles
         J put it in the London Capital case (cit sup), he was taking , “the risk that he … [was] part
         of a fraud, the details of which [he] do not know”: see para. 268 above. Indeed, Dr Shetty
         accepted in his Note of 13 May 2026 that, if I find (as, in effect, I have) that he was one of
         the principals who instigated the fraud and that the NMC Group became insolvent as a
         result of borrowings taken to fund the misappropriations, then “it would be unrealistic to
         resist an order for the entire amount of the insolvency”.

845. In his Post-Hearing Submissions, Mr Manghat submitted that the Court should weigh
         these considerations when assessing his contribution:

         a. “Mr Manghat’s actual (as opposed to nominal) role and decision-making authority
                   (particularly in contrast with the principal shareholders)”;

         b. “Mr Manghat’s alleged … illegitimate financial gain (again, particularly in contrast
                   with the principal shareholders)”; and

         c. “The lack of a causal nexus between allegations specifically against Mr Manghat
                   and the alleged losses”. Here he argued that his conduct “cannot fairly be
                   categorised as equivalent to that of the principal shareholders or indeed although
                   now moot, Baroda”.

846. Although all these considerations adopt the “sliding scale” approach which Miles J
         rejected and which I too reject (see para. 836 above), I shall comment upon each of these
         points.

847. Mr Manghat submits that, notwithstanding his “ostensible job title”, he had no oversight
         over financial transactions, and that he was “subservient” to Dr Shetty and the Bin Buttis.
         This is an unlikely submission from a man who was the CEO of a FTSE 100 company, it is
         inconsistent with the evidence, and I reject it. Undoubtedly Dr Shetty and the Bin Buttis
         had great authority and influence over the NMC Group and more generally in the UAE, but
         Mr Manghat was charged with making major decisions for the NMC Group, and in
         particular ran its financial and treasury arrangements. He had a leading role, indeed the
         leading role, in effecting the misappropriations and operating the fraud to finance them
         and disguise them. To give just two illustrations, I refer to the Sham Supplier Scheme, and
         the tricks devised in early 2019 to deceive EY.

848. Although the misappropriations for the direct benefit of Mr Manghat were relatively
         modest compared with those of Dr Shetty and the Bin Buttis, as I have said, they would
         be regarded as very substantial by most businessmen.

849. I find it difficult to understand Mr Manghat’s submission about the “lack of a causal
         nexus” between his conduct and the losses. The JA Claimants have established the five
         aspects of his involvement in the fraud that they pleaded against him: see para. 628
         above. The causal nexus between each aspect and the losses is also established.

850. I conclude that it is right that Mr Manghat too should bear the full consequences of the
         insolvency.

The argument that the Defendants are not connected with the ADGM

851. Despite the decision made on the preliminary issues that a claim can be brought for
         fraudulent or wrongful trading notwithstanding that the defendant has no connection
         with the ADGM (see para. 16 above), Baroda argued that, when assessing compensation,
         the Court should take into account that, throughout the Relevant Period, it had “had no
         connection with the ADGM and could not have anticipated or predicted liability under [IR
         2022, section 251]”. The point was adopted by Dr Shetty.

852. I do not accept that this answers the claims for fraudulent trading or that the contribution

ADGMCFI-2022-299 – NMC HEALTHCARE LIMITED (IN ADMINISTRATION) (SUBJECT TO A DEED OF COMPANY ARRANGEMENT) AND OTHERS V BAVAGUTHU RAGHURAM
SHETTY AND OTHERS

                                                                                                                                                                     201

                                                                                       05 November 2026 04:43 PM

         to be made by either Dr Shetty or Mr Manghat should be reduced on the basis of this
         consideration. In response to it, the JA Claimants advanced an argument of general
         principle and a contention specific to this case, and I am persuaded by both.

853. The general argument is that fraudulent conduct of the kind with which I am concerned
         would be actionable under any developed system of law, and liability of this kind is
         unsurprising and by no means peculiar to the ADGM. In In Re Howard Holdings Inc [1998]
         BCC 549, the defendant directors argued in answer to claims of wrongful trading under
         the s.214 of the UK Insolvency Act that “it is inappropriate … for foreign directors of a
         foreign company to be judged in accordance with principles of English common law;
         principles with which the task that they were engaged had no connection at the time … a
         declaration under s.214 would be to impose on the directors a liability to the company
         which was alien to the duties which they undertook when they became directors” (at
         p.554F). Chadwick J rejected that argument, saying “For my part, I find it difficult to
         envisage any developed system of corporate law which does not impose some obligation
         on those charged with the responsibility [of] management of a company’s affairs to pay
         regard to the question whether or not it is, from time to time, solvent and, if insolvent, to
         consider what should be done about it” (at p.555A). This reasoning, which I would adopt,
         applies a fortiori to a claim of fraudulent trading.

854. The JA Claimants’ specific point is that the ADGM fraudulent trading provision is
         materially the same as that in the UK Insolvency Act 1986, which has extra-territorial
         effect: Bilta (UK) Ltd (In Liquidation) v Nazir [2015] UKSC 23. As executives and officers
         of NMC plc, Dr Shetty and Mr Manghat were potentially liable in fraudulent trading in
         respect of the fraud. I see no reason that their contributions should be reduced because
         their liability is technically in respect of their involvement with NMCH rather than their
         involvement with NMC plc.

855. I add that Dr Shetty submitted a proof of debt and thereby submitted to the jurisdiction of
         the ADGM and its insolvency regime: see the judgment of the Court of Appeal on the
         Preliminary Issues, [2024] ADGMCA 0001 at para. 106.

Interest

856. The JA Claimants ask for statutory interest in respect of contributions ordered on the
         Insolvency Claims. In my judgment, this is not appropriate, but that the amount of
         compensation ordered should be uplifted to include a quasi-interest element. This was
         the approach of Patten J in Morris v Bank of India (loc cit) at para. 135: “It seems to me
         that I have jurisdiction to include an element of interest in the contribution which I order
         …”.

The JA Claimants’ Civil Claims

The fraud claims

857. I have concluded that Dr Shetty and Mr Manghat were parties to the fraud throughout the
         Relevant Period. NMCH, Holding and the ADGM companies suffered harm, and there was
         a causal link between that harm and the fraud and Dr Shetty’s and Mr Manghat’s
         participation in it. It follows that they are liable under article 282 of the Civil Code.

858. Dr Shetty and Mr Manghat are both also liable on the basis of my findings against them
         under article 285 of the Civil Code. The fraud involved deception, by way of participation
         in the production of misleading financial statements and concealing the true financial
         position of the NMC Group. There was a causal link between the deception and
         concealment and the harm suffered by NMCH, Holding and the ADGM Companies.

859. The JA Claimants have also proved their case that their fraudulent conduct means that
         they are both liable under the CCL 1984 and the CCL 2015.

The non-fraud claims

860. Had I not concluded that Dr Shetty and Mr Manghat knowingly participated in the fraud, I
         should still have concluded that they were liable under article 282. Their conduct was
         wrongful because they were in breach of their duties under the CCL 1984 or, later, the
         CCL 2015, and because it amounted to taqseer or khataa jaseem in that they grossly
         failed to act as a director should have done, and could reasonably be expected to have
         done, so as to protect the assets of the NMC Group and properly to present its financial
         position in the statements. The fraud could not have been perpetrated if either of them
         had fulfilled his duties.

861. Mr Manghat appeared to acknowledge at times when he was cross-examined that he had
         not been properly diligent, and Mr Hennessey did not advance any distinct argument in
         response to this part of the JA Claimants’ case.

862. Mr Wandowicz submitted that, if the fraud allegations against Dr Shetty is rejected, then
         no alternative claim under the CCL 1984 or CCL 2015 should be accepted because:

         a. No claim is properly pleaded;

         b. No complaint short of fraud was properly put to Dr Shetty when he was cross-
                   examined; and

         c. No complaint short of fraud was proved.

         I am not persuaded by any of these points.

LISTED IN SCHEDULE 1 TO THE ADMINISTRATION APPLICATION
ADGMCFI-2022-299 – NMC HEALTHCARE LIMITED (IN ADMINISTRATION) (SUBJECT TO A DEED OF COMPANY ARRANGEMENT) AND OTHERS V BAVAGUTHU RAGHURAM
SHETTY AND OTHERS

                                                                                                                                                                     203

                                                                                       05 November 2026 04:43 PM

863. Although Mr Wandowicz cited plentiful authority in an attempt to support these
         complaints, the essential point in all of the cases that he cited was that a claimant must
         give a defendant proper notice of the case against him so as to give him a fair chance to
         answer it. I need refer to only two of them. In Fowler v Lanning [1959] 1QB 426, Diplock J
         made clear (at p.440) that his criticism of the Statement of Claim was fact-specific (in
         that, for example, it did not state the weapon with which the claimant was said to have
         been shot). Diplock J expressly declined to engage with the pleading requirements in a
         case of res ipsa loquitur. In Standard Life Assurance Ltd v Building Design Partnership
         Ltd [2021] EWCA Civ 1793, Coulson LJ said that the “critical issue” was whether or not
         the defendants “knew the case they have to meet” (at para. 62).

864. In my judgment, the nature of the JA Claimants’ case is sufficiently explained in the
         Particulars of Claim. For example, in support of the case against Dr Shetty, the JA
         Claimants refer to his duties as a director or manager with regard to safeguarding its best
         interest of NMCH, Holding and the ADGM Companies, transacting payments on their
         accounts for the purpose of achieving their objectives, proper management of their
         affairs and supervision of their employees; and his responsibilities as a signatory on
         accounts.

865. Mr Wandowicz properly observed that Mr Thanki put this part of NMC’s case to Dr Shetty
         in general terms. It was readily understandable that he did so, and to my mind it was
         appropriate given the time-limits on cross-examination that case management required.
         It was also practically inevitable, given the difficulty in conceiving how Dr Shetty could
         realistically have known as little as he claimed about the NMC Group’s affairs, his own
         bank accounts and his business affairs generally. Dr Shetty’s involvement in particular
         dealings were examined in detail when he was cross-examined, and it is unrealistic to
         suggest that more detailed cross-examination of the non-fraud case would have been
         useful.

866. The only submission that was advanced on behalf of Dr Shetty in answer to the substance
         of the non-fraud claims under article 282 was this “as a diligent person, in relation to the
         financial aspects of NMC’s business he reasonably placed reliance upon those who had
         the requisite qualifications, experience and reasonably appeared to him to be trustworthy
         and followed their advice”. I cannot accept that a person in Dr Shetty’s position, either as
         CEO of NMC plc or later, was entitled to abdicate (or delegate) responsibility in respect
         of financial matters, for example by way of entering into bank facilities and use of his
         aligning authority on accounts to the extent that this submission suggests: see Madoff
         Securities International Ltd (in liquidation) v Raven, (cit sup) at para. 90.

Unjust enrichment

867. As I have said, a claim for unjust enrichment under articles 318 and 319 of the Civil Code
         requires a claimant to show a gain on the part of the defendant, a corresponding loss on
         his part and that no lawful cause justified the gain or the impoverishment. Neither Dr

COURT OF FIRST INSTANCE JUDGMENT
ADGMCFI-2022-111 – ABU DHABI COMMERCIAL BANK PJSC V PRASANTH MANGHAT
ADGMCFI-2020-020 - IN THE MATTER OF NMC HEALTHCARE LIMITED (IN ADMINISTRATION) (SUBJECT TO A DEED OF COMPANY ARRANGEMENT) AND THE COMPANIES
LISTED IN SCHEDULE 1 TO THE ADMINISTRATION APPLICATION
ADGMCFI-2022-299 – NMC HEALTHCARE LIMITED (IN ADMINISTRATION) (SUBJECT TO A DEED OF COMPANY ARRANGEMENT) AND OTHERS V BAVAGUTHU RAGHURAM
SHETTY AND OTHERS

                                                                                                                                                                     204

                                                                                       05 November 2026 04:43 PM

         Shetty nor Mr Manghat developed any submission of UAE law that disputed this, and they
         did not suggest that there is any other ingredient to a claim. No wrongdoing on the part
         of the defendant is required.

868. However Mr Wandowicz submitted that, if money from the NMC Group passed through
         Dr Shetty’s bank account without him being aware of it, the JA Claimants’ case in unjust
         enrichment should be rejected. He cited no judicial or other authority in support of this
         proposition, but he contended that otherwise the UAE law would work unjustly. The
         argument was that, in these circumstances, Dr Shetty would be in a position analogous
         to the following scenarios: (i) where a person had a suitcase stolen and a thief used it to
         store cash; or (ii) where fraudsters opened a bank account in the name of a victim of
         identity theft and used it to receive money that the fraudsters then withdrew. I am not
         impressed by these examples: in neither case could it realistically be said that the owner
         of the bag or the person in whose name the fraudsters opened the account received a
         benefit: that the cash in the suitcase or the money in the bank account came into what
         the Commentary terms the “patrimony” of the owner of the suitcase or the victim of
         identity theft. In Dr Shetty’s case, when money was paid into his bank account, he was
         enriched by having the debt owed to him by the bank increased (or if the account were in
         debit, his debt to the bank reduced).

869. Another example given by Mr Wandowicz was a case where a fraudster gains access to a
         bank account through a banking application on a stolen mobile telephone and uses it to
         channel through the account money defrauded from another victim. This example might
         have come closer to the position of Dr Shetty, if indeed another (or others) had gained
         control of his bank accounts and he had not known that they were operating them. As I
         see it, in each case, a factual question would arise as to whether Dr Shetty or the owner
         of the stolen telephone had “patrimony” of the money transferred into the bank account,
         so as truly to have been enriched. Dr Shetty submitted that “enrichment is an objective
         fact” to be determined on the facts of the particular case. I do not understand the JA
         Claimants to dispute this, and I shall assess the case against Dr Shetty on this basis.

870. In view of my findings that Dr Shetty was well aware of the Direct Shetty Payments and
         the Indirect Shetty Payments, this argument will not assist him. I therefore need not, and
         shall not, engage with the question whether UAE law might conclude that, if funds pass
         through a person’s bank account without his involvement or knowledge, he might be said
         to have had “patrimony” of the funds at any time, and not truly to have been enriched:
         see para. 315 above. Those were not the facts of this case.

871. No other answer was put forward by Dr Shetty to the unjust enrichment claim against him
         and I uphold it.

872. Mr Manghat advanced no distinct defence to the unjust enrichment claim against him,
         and the JA Claimants are entitled to judgment on it.

                                                                                       05 November 2026 04:43 PM

Quantum of damages

873. The JA Claimants claim damages in respect of the Civil Claims by way of:

         a. The total amount of the Unlawful Payments (sc. the Direct Shetty Payments, the
                   Indirect Shetty Payments, the Direct Manghat Payments, the Manghat GGCM
                   Payments and the payments to the Bin Buttis and their companies); and

         b. Advisory and professional fees and disbursements that would not have been
                   incurred but for the fraud.

874. In principle, the JA Claimants are entitled to recover the amount of the Unlawful
         Payments as damages. There is little dispute about the amount: Mr Bezant calculated
         them at US$ 1,589 million, and Mr Grantham at US$ 1,587 million. I invite further
         submissions about this difference if the JA Claimants are minded to press for judgment
         reflecting Mr Bezant’s full amount, and about whether the sum awarded should be
         adjusted to reflect the findings in this Judgment.

875. I also invite further submissions about the claim for advisory and professional fees and
         disbursements. The JA Claimants’ pleaded claim here is for US$ 278.7 million, and it is
         based on JA’s trading account for NMCH to 19 August 2025, which was appended to their
         10th Progress Report. The claim includes some US$ 109.5 million by way of “Legal
         Fees/Disbursements”. This raises the same questions as ADCB’s claim in respect of
         costs of NMC plc’s administration: see para. 808 above.

Conclusions

876. In brief summary, I conclude:

         a. That ADCB succeeds in its claim against Mr Manghat, and it is entitled to damages
                   (at least) in respect of NMCH’s outstanding liabilities and loss of profits.

         b. That NMCH and its JAs succeed in their Insolvency Claims against both Dr Shetty
                   and Mr Manghat, and have established that I should declare that Dr Shetty and Mr
                   Manghat are liable to make contributions assessed on the basis that they were
                   knowingly parties to fraudulent trading from 30 April 2012 and throughout the
                   Relevant Period thereafter.

         c. That the starting point for assessing the contributions that I order that Dr Shetty
                   and Mr Manghat pay should be US$ 5,397 million, but I shall finally determine the
                   amounts that I order against each of them after hearing any further relevant
                   submissions that I permit.

         d. That the JA Claimants succeed in their Civil Claims against Dr Shetty and Mr
                   Manghat, and they are entitled to damages (provided that proper provision is

         a. To consider consequential matters, including claims for statutory interest and
                 costs.
         b. To consider the claims made by Dr Shetty and Baroda against the other Defendants:
                 see para. 4 above.
         c. To consider any applications or submissions about the various matters in respect
                 of which I have said in this Judgment that I would entertain them: see paras. 262,
                 474,775, 798, 803, 807, 818, 874, and 875 above.
         d. To settle the terms of the orders that I should make to give effect to my Judgment.
878. I should be grateful if the JA Claimants and ADCB would prepare draft orders for
         consideration at the further hearing.
